# Section I: Humanities & Social Sciences

## Chapter 1: Indian History

### Q001 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Who led the excavation of the Harappan site in 1921?

A. Daya Ram Sahni
B. Rakhaldas Banerjee
C. S. R. Rao
D. B. B. Lal

**Answer:** A. Daya Ram Sahni
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Daya Ram Sahni (Correct):** Daya Ram Sahni led the archaeological excavation at Harappa in 1921, marking the formal discovery of the Indus Valley Civilization.
- **B — Rakhaldas Banerjee:** Discovered Mohenjo-daro in 1922 on the banks of the Indus River.
- **C — S. R. Rao:** Excavated the Harappan port city of Lothal in Gujarat.
- **D — B. B. Lal:** Directed excavations at Kalibangan in Rajasthan along with B. K. Thapar.
- **Exam Extra:** Harappa is situated on the banks of the Ravi River in present-day Punjab, Pakistan.

### Q002 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

At which Indus Valley Civilization site was the famous 'Great Bath' discovered?

A. Harappa
B. Mohenjo-daro
C. Kalibangan
D. Dholavira

**Answer:** B. Mohenjo-daro
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Mohenjo-daro (Correct):** The Great Bath, a large waterproofed rectangular tank made of baked bricks and gypsum mortar, was unearthed in the citadel mound of Mohenjo-daro.
- **A — Harappa:** Famous for six granaries in a row and a red sandstone male torso.
- **C — Kalibangan:** Known for ploughed agricultural fields, fire altars, and mud-brick fortifications.
- **D — Dholavira:** Renowned for stone reservoirs, a three-part town layout, and a 10-sign Indus inscription.
- **Exam Extra:** Mohenjo-daro also yielded the famous bronze 'Dancing Girl' figurine and the steatite bust of the 'Priest-King'.

### Q003 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Which Indus Valley site is famous for having an artificial brick dockyard?

A. Banawali
B. Rakhigarhi
C. Lothal
D. Chanhudaro

**Answer:** C. Lothal
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Lothal (Correct):** Lothal in Gujarat featured an artificial baked-brick tidal dockyard connected to the Sabarmati/Bhogava river system for maritime trade.
- **A — Banawali:** Located in Haryana, notable for terracotta plough models and absence of systematic street drainage.
- **B — Rakhigarhi:** One of the largest Harappan settlements, located in Hisar district of Haryana.
- **D — Chanhudaro:** Known as an industrial hub for bead-making and shell crafts, and the only Harappan city without a citadel.
- **Exam Extra:** Excavations at Lothal also uncovered bead-making workshops, Persian Gulf seals, and evidence of rice husks.

### Q004 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Which Harappan site in Gujarat was inscribed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2021 for its water conservation system and three-tier town planning?

A. Surkotada
B. Rangpur
C. Ropar
D. Dholavira

**Answer:** D. Dholavira
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Dholavira (Correct):** Dholavira in Gujarat's Kutch district was inscribed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2021 for its elaborate water harvesting reservoirs and unique three-tier city layout.
- **A — Surkotada:** Located in Kutch, Gujarat, noted for stone fortifications and controversial horse bone finds.
- **B — Rangpur:** A Harappan and post-Harappan site in Gujarat known for rice cultivation evidence.
- **C — Ropar:** The first Harappan site excavated in independent India, situated on the Sutlej River in Punjab.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike most Harappan cities divided into two parts, Dholavira had a Citadel, Middle Town, and Lower Town.

### Q005 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Evidence of the earliest ploughed agricultural field in the Harappan Civilization was found at which site?

A. Kalibangan
B. Alamgirpur
C. Daimabad
D. Manda

**Answer:** A. Kalibangan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Kalibangan (Correct):** Kalibangan in Rajasthan revealed the world's earliest known ploughed agricultural field with a grid of perpendicular furrow marks from the Pre-Harappan phase.
- **B — Alamgirpur:** The easternmost site of the Indus Valley Civilization, located on the Hindon River in Uttar Pradesh.
- **C — Daimabad:** The southernmost Harappan site, located on the Pravara River in Maharashtra.
- **D — Manda:** The northernmost Harappan site, situated on the Chenab River in Jammu and Kashmir.
- **Exam Extra:** Sutkagen-dor in Balochistan marks the westernmost extent of the Indus Valley Civilization.

### Q006 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Which metal was NOT known to the people of the Indus Valley Civilization?

A. Copper
B. Iron
C. Bronze
D. Silver

**Answer:** B. Iron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Iron (Correct):** The Indus Valley Civilization belonged to the Bronze Age, and iron metallurgy was unknown to Harappans; iron appeared in India later around 1000 BCE.
- **A — Copper:** Widely used for tools and utensils, sourced mainly from the Khetri mines of Rajasthan.
- **C — Bronze:** Alloy of copper and tin used extensively for tools, weapons, and sculptures like the Dancing Girl.
- **D — Silver:** Imported and crafted into ornaments, vessels, and beads by Harappan artisans.
- **Exam Extra:** In Later Vedic texts, iron was referred to as 'Shyama Ayas' or 'Krishna Ayas' (black metal).

### Q007 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

Which is the oldest among the four Vedas?

A. Samaveda
B. Yajurveda
C. Rigveda
D. Atharvaveda

**Answer:** C. Rigveda
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Rigveda (Correct):** The Rigveda is the oldest of the four Vedas, composed during the Early Vedic Period (c. 1500–1000 BCE).
- **A — Samaveda:** Known as the 'Book of Chants', containing liturgical melodies derived largely from the Rigveda.
- **B — Yajurveda:** A Later Vedic compilation of prose mantras and sacrificial formulas for rituals.
- **D — Atharvaveda:** A Later Vedic text dealing with charms, spells, daily life, and early medicine.
- **Exam Extra:** The Rigveda contains 1,028 hymns (suktas) organized into 10 books called Mandalas.

### Q008 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

The famous Gayatri Mantra, dedicated to Savitr, is found in which Mandala of the Rigveda?

A. First Mandala
B. Seventh Mandala
C. Tenth Mandala
D. Third Mandala

**Answer:** D. Third Mandala
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Third Mandala (Correct):** The Gayatri Mantra, addressed to the solar deity Savitr, appears in Mandala 3 (Hymn 62, Verse 10) of the Rigveda.
- **A — First Mandala:** A diverse collection of hymns attributed to sages such as Dirghatamas and Gotama.
- **B — Seventh Mandala:** Composed by sage Vasishtha and describes the Battle of Ten Kings (Dasarajna).
- **C — Tenth Mandala:** Contains the Purusha Sukta, which mentions the fourfold varna system, and the Nasadiya Sukta.
- **Exam Extra:** The Gayatri Mantra was composed by sage Vishvamitra in the Gayatri poetic meter.

### Q009 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

From which Upanishad is India's national motto "Satyameva Jayate" taken?

A. Mundaka Upanishad
B. Katha Upanishad
C. Brihadaranyaka Upanishad
D. Chandogya Upanishad

**Answer:** A. Mundaka Upanishad
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Mundaka Upanishad (Correct):** The national motto of India, "Satyameva Jayate" (Truth Alone Triumphs), is taken from the Mundaka Upanishad (3.1.6).
- **B — Katha Upanishad:** Famous for the philosophical dialogue between Nachiketa and Yama, Lord of Death.
- **C — Brihadaranyaka Upanishad:** One of the oldest and largest Upanishads, featuring the teachings of sage Yajnavalkya.
- **D — Chandogya Upanishad:** Known for the Vedantic Mahavakya "Tat Tvam Asi" (That Thou Art).
- **Exam Extra:** "Satyameva Jayate" is inscribed in Devanagari script below the Lion Capital of Ashoka on the State Emblem of India.

### Q010 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

In the Rigveda, which modern river was known as 'Vitasta'?

A. Chenab
B. Jhelum
C. Ravi
D. Beas

**Answer:** B. Jhelum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Jhelum (Correct):** In Rigvedic geography, the Jhelum River was called 'Vitasta', which later became 'Hydaspes' in Greek accounts.
- **A — Chenab:** Referred to as 'Asikni' in the Rigvedic hymns.
- **C — Ravi:** Referred to as 'Parushni', along whose banks the Battle of Ten Kings was fought.
- **D — Beas:** Referred to as 'Vipasha' in Vedic literature.
- **Exam Extra:** In the Rigveda, the Sutlej was called 'Shutudri' and the Indus was called 'Sindhu'.

### Q011 · Indus Valley & Vedic Period

During the Early Vedic Period, what were 'Sabha' and 'Samiti'?

A. Types of land taxes
B. Military divisions
C. Tribal political assemblies
D. Sacrificial altars

**Answer:** C. Tribal political assemblies
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Tribal political assemblies (Correct):** Sabha (council of elders) and Samiti (general tribal assembly) were key Early Vedic political assemblies that checked the power of the king (Rajan).
- **A — Types of land taxes:** Early Vedic voluntary tribute paid to the chief was known as 'Bali', not Sabha or Samiti.
- **B — Military divisions:** Tribal fighting units in the Rigveda were called Vrata, Gana, Grama, and Sardha.
- **D — Sacrificial altars:** Vedic fire altars constructed for rituals were known as 'Vedi' or 'Chiti'.
- **Exam Extra:** The Atharvaveda describes Sabha and Samiti as the "twin daughters of Prajapati."

### Q012 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Who was the 24th and last Tirthankara of Jainism?

A. Rishabhanatha
B. Parshvanatha
C. Neminatha
D. Vardhamana Mahavira

**Answer:** D. Vardhamana Mahavira
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Vardhamana Mahavira (Correct):** Vardhamana Mahavira was the 24th and last Tirthankara of Jainism, who systematized and spread Jain teachings in the 6th century BCE.
- **A — Rishabhanatha:** The 1st Tirthankara and traditional founder of Jainism, associated with the bull emblem.
- **B — Parshvanatha:** The 23rd Tirthankara, born in Varanasi, who preached four main vows (excluding celibacy).
- **C — Neminatha:** The 22nd Tirthankara in Jain tradition, symbolized by a conch shell.
- **Exam Extra:** Mahavira was born at Kundagrama near Vaishali and attained Kaivalya (supreme knowledge) at Jimbhikagrama.

### Q013 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Where did Gautama Buddha deliver his first sermon, known as Dharmachakrapravartana?

A. Sarnath
B. Bodh Gaya
C. Lumbini
D. Kushinagar

**Answer:** A. Sarnath
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Sarnath (Correct):** Gautama Buddha delivered his first sermon (Dharmachakrapravartana — Turning of the Wheel of Law) to five ascetics at Sarnath near Varanasi.
- **B — Bodh Gaya:** The place in Bihar where Siddhartha attained Enlightenment (Nirvana) under the Bodhi tree.
- **C — Lumbini:** The birthplace of Siddhartha Gautama in present-day Nepal.
- **D — Kushinagar:** The place in Uttar Pradesh where Gautama Buddha attained Mahaparinirvana.
- **Exam Extra:** The five ascetics became Buddha's first disciples and formed the core of the Buddhist Sangha at the Deer Park in Sarnath.

### Q014 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Under whose royal patronage was the First Buddhist Council held at Rajagriha?

A. Bimbisara
B. Ajatashatru
C. Kalashoka
D. Kanishka

**Answer:** B. Ajatashatru
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Ajatashatru (Correct):** King Ajatashatru of the Haryanka dynasty patronized the First Buddhist Council at Rajagriha around 483 BCE, shortly after Buddha's Mahaparinirvana.
- **A — Bimbisara:** Father of Ajatashatru and a contemporary patron of Buddha, who ruled before Ajatashatru.
- **C — Kalashoka:** Patronized the Second Buddhist Council at Vaishali around 383 BCE.
- **D — Kanishka:** Patronized the Fourth Buddhist Council in Kashmir, where Buddhism split into Hinayana and Mahayana.
- **Exam Extra:** The First Buddhist Council was presided over by Mahakassapa, and the Vinaya Pitaka and Sutta Pitaka were recited there.

### Q015 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

In which language were the early Buddhist canonical texts, the Tripitakas, originally compiled?

A. Sanskrit
B. Prakrit
C. Pali
D. Apabhramsa

**Answer:** C. Pali
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Pali (Correct):** The early Buddhist canonical scriptures, collectively called the Tripitakas (Three Baskets), were originally compiled in the Pali language.
- **A — Sanskrit:** Used predominantly in later Mahayana Buddhist texts and classical Hindu literature.
- **B — Prakrit:** Used in early Jain canonical Agamas and most of Ashoka's rock and pillar edicts.
- **D — Apabhramsa:** A later Indo-Aryan literary dialect that bridged Prakrit and modern North Indian languages.
- **Exam Extra:** The three baskets are Vinaya Pitaka (monastic discipline), Sutta Pitaka (sermons), and Abhidhamma Pitaka (philosophy).

### Q016 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Which of the following is NOT one of the 'Triratnas' (Three Jewels) of Jainism?

A. Right Faith (Samyak Darshana)
B. Right Knowledge (Samyak Jnana)
C. Right Conduct (Samyak Charitra)
D. Right Livelihood (Samyak Ajiva)

**Answer:** D. Right Livelihood (Samyak Ajiva)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Right Livelihood (Samyak Ajiva) (Correct):** Right Livelihood (Samyak Ajiva) is a step in Buddhism's Noble Eightfold Path, whereas Jainism's Triratnas are Right Faith, Right Knowledge, and Right Conduct.
- **A — Right Faith (Samyak Darshana):** One of the three Jain Triratnas, signifying firm belief in the Tirthankaras' truth.
- **B — Right Knowledge (Samyak Jnana):** One of the three Jain Triratnas, denoting accurate understanding of Jain philosophy.
- **C — Right Conduct (Samyak Charitra):** One of the three Jain Triratnas, involving ethical living and non-violence.
- **Exam Extra:** Jainism also prescribes five Mahavratas (Great Vows): Ahimsa, Satya, Asteya, Aparigraha, and Brahmacharya.

### Q017 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Where was the First Jain Council held, during which the twelve Angas were compiled?

A. Pataliputra
B. Vallabhi
C. Pavapuri
D. Shravanabelagola

**Answer:** A. Pataliputra
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Pataliputra (Correct):** The First Jain Council was held at Pataliputra around 300 BCE under the leadership of Sthulabhadra, resulting in the compilation of the 12 Angas.
- **B — Vallabhi:** Venue of the Second Jain Council in Gujarat (512 CE), presided over by Devardhi Kshamasramana.
- **C — Pavapuri:** The sacred site in Bihar where Lord Mahavira attained Nirvana.
- **D — Shravanabelagola:** Major Digambara Jain centre in Karnataka housing the colossal statue of Bahubali.
- **Exam Extra:** The Shvetambara and Digambara schism deepened after a severe famine in Magadha led Bhadrabahu's followers to migrate south.

### Q018 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

What was the earliest capital of the Magadha Mahajanapada before Pataliputra?

A. Vaishali
B. Rajagriha (Girivraja)
C. Champa
D. Kaushambi

**Answer:** B. Rajagriha (Girivraja)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Rajagriha (Girivraja) (Correct):** Rajagriha (modern Rajgir), also known as Girivraja and surrounded by five hills, served as the earliest capital of Magadha.
- **A — Vaishali:** Capital of the Vajji confederacy before being annexed by Ajatashatru.
- **C — Champa:** Capital city of the Anga Mahajanapada in eastern Bihar.
- **D — Kaushambi:** Capital city of the Vatsa Mahajanapada on the banks of the Yamuna.
- **Exam Extra:** Haryanka ruler Udayin later shifted the capital of Magadha permanently from Rajagriha to Pataliputra.

### Q019 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Which ancient Buddhist text provides the most prominent list of the sixteen Mahajanapadas?

A. Digha Nikaya
B. Jataka Tales
C. Anguttara Nikaya
D. Milinda Panha

**Answer:** C. Anguttara Nikaya
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Anguttara Nikaya (Correct):** The Buddhist Pali text Anguttara Nikaya contains the most celebrated list of the sixteen Mahajanapadas (great kingdoms/republics) of 6th-century BCE India.
- **A — Digha Nikaya:** Collection of long discourses of Buddha, including the Mahaparinibbana Sutta.
- **B — Jataka Tales:** Collection of stories narrating the previous births of Gautama Buddha.
- **D — Milinda Panha:** A Pali dialogue between the Indo-Greek king Menander I and Buddhist monk Nagasena.
- **Exam Extra:** Among Jain scriptures, the 'Bhagavati Sutra' also provides a list of sixteen Mahajanapadas.

### Q020 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

Who founded the Nanda Dynasty in Magadha?

A. Dhana Nanda
B. Shishunaga
C. Kalashoka
D. Mahapadma Nanda

**Answer:** D. Mahapadma Nanda
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Mahapadma Nanda (Correct):** Mahapadma Nanda founded the Nanda Dynasty in Magadha in the 4th century BCE and expanded it into a vast northern Indian empire.
- **A — Dhana Nanda:** The last Nanda king, overthrown by Chandragupta Maurya with Chanakya's help.
- **B — Shishunaga:** Founder of the Shishunaga dynasty that preceded the Nandas in Magadha.
- **C — Kalashoka:** Shishunaga ruler who convened the Second Buddhist Council at Vaishali.
- **Exam Extra:** The Puranas describe Mahapadma Nanda as 'Sarvakshatrantaka' (destroyer of all Kshatriyas) and 'Ekarat' (sole sovereign).

### Q021 · Buddhism, Jainism & Mahajanapadas

In 326 BCE, Alexander the Great fought the Battle of the Hydaspes against King Porus on the banks of which river?

A. Jhelum
B. Indus
C. Beas
D. Ravi

**Answer:** A. Jhelum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Jhelum (Correct):** In 326 BCE, Alexander the Great defeated King Porus in the Battle of the Hydaspes fought on the banks of the Jhelum River.
- **B — Indus:** Alexander crossed the Indus at Ohind before advancing into Taxila.
- **C — Beas:** The easternmost river reached by Alexander's army before his troops refused to march further.
- **D — Ravi:** Crossed by Alexander after the Battle of the Hydaspes during his Punjab campaign.
- **Exam Extra:** Impressed by Porus's courage, Alexander restored his kingdom and appointed him as an allied satrap.

### Q022 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who founded the Maurya Empire?

A. Bindusara
B. Chandragupta Maurya
C. Ashoka
D. Brihadratha

**Answer:** B. Chandragupta Maurya
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Chandragupta Maurya (Correct):** Chandragupta Maurya founded the Maurya Empire in 322 BCE after overthrowing the Nanda dynasty with the strategic guidance of Chanakya.
- **A — Bindusara:** Son and successor of Chandragupta Maurya, known to Greek writers as Amitrochates.
- **C — Ashoka:** Grandson of Chandragupta Maurya, famous for the Kalinga War and spreading Dhamma.
- **D — Brihadratha:** The last Mauryan emperor, assassinated by Pushyamitra Shunga in 185 BCE.
- **Exam Extra:** Chandragupta Maurya defeated Seleucus I Nicator around 305 BCE and later embraced Jainism at Shravanabelagola.

### Q023 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who wrote the Arthashastra?

A. Megasthenes
B. Vishakhadatta
C. Kautilya (Chanakya)
D. Patanjali

**Answer:** C. Kautilya (Chanakya)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Kautilya (Chanakya) (Correct):** Kautilya (also known as Chanakya or Vishnugupta), chief advisor to Chandragupta Maurya, authored the Arthashastra on statecraft, economics, and military strategy.
- **A — Megasthenes:** Greek ambassador at Chandragupta Maurya's court who authored the account 'Indica'.
- **B — Vishakhadatta:** Sanskrit dramatist who wrote 'Mudrarakshasa' depicting Chandragupta's rise to power.
- **D — Patanjali:** Author of the 'Mahabhashya' (commentary on Panini's grammar) and compiler of the 'Yoga Sutras'.
- **Exam Extra:** The Arthashastra is organized into 15 books (Adhikaranas) and outlines the Saptanga theory of the state.

### Q024 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who authored 'Indica', the Greek account of Mauryan administration and society?

A. Fa-Hien
B. Hiuen Tsang
C. Pliny
D. Megasthenes

**Answer:** D. Megasthenes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Megasthenes (Correct):** Megasthenes, the Greek ambassador sent by Seleucus I Nicator to Chandragupta Maurya's court at Pataliputra, authored Indica.
- **A — Fa-Hien:** Chinese Buddhist pilgrim who visited India during the Gupta reign of Chandragupta II.
- **B — Hiuen Tsang:** Chinese traveller who visited India during Harshavardhana's reign in the 7th century CE.
- **C — Pliny:** Roman writer who authored 'Naturalis Historia', criticizing the outflow of Roman gold to India.
- **Exam Extra:** Although the original Indica is lost, extensive fragments survive in later works by Strabo, Arrian, and Diodorus.

### Q025 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

The Kalinga War is associated with which ruler?

A. Ashoka
B. Chandragupta Maurya
C. Bindusara
D. Dasharatha

**Answer:** A. Ashoka
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Ashoka (Correct):** Emperor Ashoka fought the bloody Kalinga War around 261 BCE, after which the immense suffering led him to renounce aggressive warfare and embrace Dhamma.
- **B — Chandragupta Maurya:** Founder of the Maurya Empire who unified northern and western India.
- **C — Bindusara:** Father of Ashoka, who extended Mauryan power deep into southern India.
- **D — Dasharatha:** Grandson of Ashoka who donated caves in the Nagarjuni Hills to the Ajivika sect.
- **Exam Extra:** Details of the Kalinga War and Ashoka's remorse are recorded in his Major Rock Edict XIII.

### Q026 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who first deciphered the Brahmi script of Ashoka's edicts in 1837?

A. Alexander Cunningham
B. James Prinsep
C. William Jones
D. John Marshall

**Answer:** B. James Prinsep
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — James Prinsep (Correct):** James Prinsep, an officer of the East India Company and secretary of the Asiatic Society, deciphered the Brahmi script of Ashoka's edicts in 1837.
- **A — Alexander Cunningham:** First Director-General of the Archaeological Survey of India, called the father of Indian archaeology.
- **C — William Jones:** Founded the Asiatic Society of Bengal in 1784 to study Asian languages and antiquities.
- **D — John Marshall:** Director-General of ASI when Harappa and Mohenjo-daro were excavated in the 1920s.
- **Exam Extra:** In most inscriptions, Ashoka refers to himself by the royal title 'Devanampiya Piyadasi' (Beloved of the Gods).

### Q027 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who founded the Shunga Dynasty after overthrowing the last Mauryan ruler?

A. Agnimitra
B. Vasudeva Kanva
C. Pushyamitra Shunga
D. Simuka

**Answer:** C. Pushyamitra Shunga
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Pushyamitra Shunga (Correct):** Pushyamitra Shunga, the Mauryan commander-in-chief, assassinated the last Mauryan ruler Brihadratha in 185 BCE and founded the Shunga Dynasty.
- **A — Agnimitra:** Son of Pushyamitra Shunga and the hero of Kalidasa's play 'Malavikagnimitram'.
- **B — Vasudeva Kanva:** Minister who killed the last Shunga king Devabhuti and established the Kanva dynasty.
- **D — Simuka:** Founder of the Satavahana dynasty in the Deccan region.
- **Exam Extra:** Pushyamitra Shunga performed two Ashvamedha sacrifices and was a contemporary of the Sanskrit grammarian Patanjali.

### Q028 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Which ruler is credited with starting the Saka Era in 78 CE?

A. Vikramaditya
B. Rudradaman I
C. Menander I
D. Kanishka

**Answer:** D. Kanishka
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Kanishka (Correct):** Kushana emperor Kanishka is credited with initiating the Saka Era in 78 CE, which now serves as the official Indian National Calendar.
- **A — Vikramaditya:** Traditionally associated with the Vikrama Era beginning in 57 BCE.
- **B — Rudradaman I:** Western Kshatrapa king known for the Sanskrit Junagadh rock inscription and repairing Sudarshana Lake.
- **C — Menander I:** Prominent Indo-Greek king, remembered as Milinda in the Buddhist text 'Milinda Panha'.
- **Exam Extra:** Kanishka's primary capital was Purushapura (Peshawar), with Mathura serving as a major secondary centre.

### Q029 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Which Satavahana ruler defeated Saka king Nahapana and is eulogized in the Nasik Prasasti?

A. Gautamiputra Satakarni
B. Simuka
C. Hala
D. Vasishthiputra Pulumavi

**Answer:** A. Gautamiputra Satakarni
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Gautamiputra Satakarni (Correct):** Gautamiputra Satakarni, the greatest Satavahana ruler, defeated the Western Kshatrapa ruler Nahapana and is celebrated in the Nasik Prasasti composed by his mother Gautami Balashri.
- **B — Simuka:** Founder of the Satavahana dynasty in the 1st century BCE.
- **C — Hala:** The 17th Satavahana ruler who compiled the Prakrit poetry anthology 'Gatha Saptashati'.
- **D — Vasishthiputra Pulumavi:** Son and successor of Gautamiputra Satakarni who expanded Satavahana influence in Andhra.
- **Exam Extra:** The Satavahanas were the earliest Indian rulers known to grant tax-free land to Brahmans and Buddhist monks and issue lead coins extensively.

### Q030 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who was the founder of the Gupta Dynasty?

A. Chandragupta I
B. Sri Gupta
C. Samudragupta
D. Ghatotkacha

**Answer:** B. Sri Gupta
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sri Gupta (Correct):** Sri Gupta founded the Gupta Dynasty in the late 3rd century CE, laying the foundation for the Imperial Guptas.
- **A — Chandragupta I:** First Gupta sovereign to adopt the title 'Maharajadhiraja' and initiate the Gupta Era in 319–320 CE.
- **C — Samudragupta:** Son of Chandragupta I, famed for his pan-Indian military campaigns.
- **D — Ghatotkacha:** Son of Sri Gupta and father of Chandragupta I.
- **Exam Extra:** The Gupta age is often called the 'Golden Age of India' for breakthroughs in astronomy, mathematics, literature, and temple architecture.

### Q031 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Which Gupta emperor is famously known as the 'Napoleon of India'?

A. Chandragupta I
B. Chandragupta II
C. Samudragupta
D. Skandagupta

**Answer:** C. Samudragupta
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Samudragupta (Correct):** Historian V. A. Smith called Samudragupta the 'Napoleon of India' because of his extensive and undefeated military campaigns recorded on the Allahabad Pillar.
- **A — Chandragupta I:** Consolidated the Gupta kingdom through a marriage alliance with the Lichchhavis.
- **B — Chandragupta II:** Assumed the title 'Vikramaditya' and defeated the Western Satraps (Sakas).
- **D — Skandagupta:** Successfully defended the Gupta Empire against fierce Huna invasions.
- **Exam Extra:** Samudragupta's court poet Harishena composed the Prayag Prasasti (Allahabad Pillar Inscription) in Sanskrit.

### Q032 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

During the reign of which Gupta ruler did the Chinese pilgrim Fa-Hien visit India?

A. Samudragupta
B. Kumaragupta I
C. Skandagupta
D. Chandragupta II

**Answer:** D. Chandragupta II
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Chandragupta II (Correct):** The Chinese Buddhist pilgrim Fa-Hien (Faxian) visited India between 399 and 414 CE during the reign of Gupta Emperor Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya).
- **A — Samudragupta:** Predecessor of Chandragupta II, who granted Sri Lankan king Meghavarman permission to build a monastery at Bodh Gaya.
- **B — Kumaragupta I:** Son of Chandragupta II, credited with founding Nalanda Mahavihara.
- **C — Skandagupta:** Last major Gupta emperor who repelled Huna attacks and repaired the Sudarshana Lake.
- **Exam Extra:** Fa-Hien documented his travels and observations of Gupta society in 'Fo-Kwo-Ki' (A Record of Buddhistic Kingdoms).

### Q033 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Which Gupta ruler founded the renowned Nalanda University?

A. Kumaragupta I
B. Chandragupta II
C. Samudragupta
D. Vishnugupta

**Answer:** A. Kumaragupta I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Kumaragupta I (Correct):** Kumaragupta I (Shakraditya) established Nalanda Mahavihara (Nalanda University) in Bihar during the 5th century CE.
- **B — Chandragupta II:** Renowned for patronizing the Navaratnas (Nine Gems) at his Ujjain court.
- **C — Samudragupta:** Known as 'Kaviraja' (King of Poets) and depicted playing the veena on gold coins.
- **D — Vishnugupta:** The last recognized ruler of the Imperial Gupta dynasty.
- **Exam Extra:** Nalanda flourished as a premier residential centre of Buddhist and secular learning until it was destroyed by Bakhtiyar Khilji around 1193–1200 CE.

### Q034 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who authored the Sanskrit classics 'Abhijnanashakuntalam' and 'Meghaduta' as one of the Navaratnas in Chandragupta II's court?

A. Banabhatta
B. Kalidasa
C. Bhavabhuti
D. Bharavi

**Answer:** B. Kalidasa
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Kalidasa (Correct):** Kalidasa, one of the Navaratnas in Chandragupta II's court, authored classical Sanskrit masterpieces including Abhijnanashakuntalam, Meghaduta, and Raghuvamsha.
- **A — Banabhatta:** Court poet of Harshavardhana in the 7th century CE who wrote 'Harshacharita' and 'Kadambari'.
- **C — Bhavabhuti:** 8th-century Sanskrit playwright famous for 'Uttararamacharita' and 'Malatimadhava'.
- **D — Bharavi:** 6th-century Sanskrit poet celebrated for the Mahakavya 'Kiratarjuniya'.
- **Exam Extra:** Other famous Navaratnas in Chandragupta II's court included astronomer Varahamihira, physician Dhanvantari, and lexicographer Amarasimha.

### Q035 · Mauryas, Post-Mauryan & Guptas

Who wrote 'Harshacharita', the biography of Emperor Harshavardhana?

A. Hiuen Tsang
B. Ravikirti
C. Banabhatta
D. Harishena

**Answer:** C. Banabhatta
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Banabhatta (Correct):** Banabhatta served as the court poet (Asthana Kavi) of Emperor Harshavardhana and wrote his biography Harshacharita in Sanskrit prose.
- **A — Hiuen Tsang:** Chinese pilgrim who wrote 'Si-Yu-Ki' recounting his travels in Harshavardhana's empire.
- **B — Ravikirti:** Court poet of Chalukya king Pulakeshin II who composed the Aihole Inscription.
- **D — Harishena:** Court poet of Samudragupta who composed the Allahabad Pillar Inscription.
- **Exam Extra:** Harshavardhana himself was an accomplished playwright who authored 'Ratnavali', 'Priyadarsika', and 'Nagananda'.

### Q036 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

In which year was the Second Battle of Tarain fought between Muhammad Ghori and Prithviraj Chauhan?

A. 1191 CE
B. 1194 CE
C. 1206 CE
D. 1192 CE

**Answer:** D. 1192 CE
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 1192 CE (Correct):** In 1192 CE, Muhammad Ghori defeated Prithviraj Chauhan in the Second Battle of Tarain, opening northern India to Ghurid and Delhi Sultanate rule.
- **A — 1191 CE:** Year of the First Battle of Tarain, won by Prithviraj Chauhan.
- **B — 1194 CE:** Year of the Battle of Chandawar, where Muhammad Ghori defeated Jaichandra of Kannauj.
- **C — 1206 CE:** Year Qutb-ud-din Aibak established the Delhi Sultanate after Muhammad Ghori's death.
- **Exam Extra:** In 1191 CE, Prithviraj Chauhan had defeated Muhammad Ghori in the First Battle of Tarain.

### Q037 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Who founded the Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty of the Delhi Sultanate in 1206 CE?

A. Qutb-ud-din Aibak
B. Iltutmish
C. Ghiyas-ud-din Balban
D. Razia Sultana

**Answer:** A. Qutb-ud-din Aibak
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Qutb-ud-din Aibak (Correct):** Qutb-ud-din Aibak founded the Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty in 1206 CE following the death of Muhammad Ghori, initially ruling from Lahore.
- **B — Iltutmish:** Son-in-law and successor of Aibak, regarded as the real consolidator of the Delhi Sultanate.
- **C — Ghiyas-ud-din Balban:** Introduced strict court etiquette (Sijda and Paibos) and the 'Blood and Iron' policy.
- **D — Razia Sultana:** Daughter of Iltutmish and the only woman ruler of the Delhi Sultanate.
- **Exam Extra:** Qutb-ud-din Aibak earned the title 'Lakh Baksh' (giver of lakhs) for his generosity and died in 1210 CE while playing Chaugan (polo).

### Q038 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which Delhi Sultan completed the Qutub Minar and introduced the 'Iqta' system along with silver 'Tanka' and copper 'Jital' coins?

A. Qutb-ud-din Aibak
B. Iltutmish
C. Alauddin Khilji
D. Firoz Shah Tughlaq

**Answer:** B. Iltutmish
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Iltutmish (Correct):** Iltutmish completed the Qutub Minar started by Aibak, institutionalized the Iqta land-assignment system, and introduced standard silver Tanka and copper Jital coins.
- **A — Qutb-ud-din Aibak:** Initiated construction of the Qutub Minar in honour of Sufi saint Qutbuddin Bakhtiyar Kaki.
- **C — Alauddin Khilji:** Built the Alai Darwaza gateway near the Qutub Minar complex.
- **D — Firoz Shah Tughlaq:** Repaired and added the top storeys of the Qutub Minar after lightning damage.
- **Exam Extra:** Iltutmish also organized an elite council of forty Turkish nobles known as 'Turkan-i-Chahalgani'.

### Q039 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Who was the first and only woman Sultan to rule the Delhi Sultanate?

A. Chand Bibi
B. Nur Jahan
C. Razia Sultana
D. Ahilyabai Holkar

**Answer:** C. Razia Sultana
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Razia Sultana (Correct):** Razia Sultana, daughter of Sultan Iltutmish, ruled from 1236 to 1240 CE as the first and only female monarch of the Delhi Sultanate.
- **A — Chand Bibi:** 16th-century regent of Ahmadnagar who bravely defended her kingdom against Mughal forces.
- **B — Nur Jahan:** Influential Mughal empress and consort of Emperor Jahangir in the early 17th century.
- **D — Ahilyabai Holkar:** 18th-century Maratha ruler of Malwa celebrated for administration and temple restoration.
- **Exam Extra:** Razia discarded the traditional veil (purdah), wore male royal attire, and held open court before facing rebellion from Turkish nobles.

### Q040 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which Delhi Sultan introduced the court customs of 'Sijda' and 'Paibos'?

A. Iltutmish
B. Kaiqubad
C. Jalal-ud-din Khilji
D. Ghiyas-ud-din Balban

**Answer:** D. Ghiyas-ud-din Balban
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ghiyas-ud-din Balban (Correct):** Ghiyas-ud-din Balban introduced the Persian court rituals of Sijda (prostration) and Paibos (kissing the Sultan's feet) to assert supreme royal authority.
- **A — Iltutmish:** Made Delhi the permanent capital of the Sultanate and secured investiture from the Abbasid Caliph.
- **B — Kaiqubad:** Grandson of Balban and the weak final ruler before the Khilji takeover.
- **C — Jalal-ud-din Khilji:** Founder of the Khilji Dynasty in 1290 CE.
- **Exam Extra:** Balban also introduced the Persian New Year festival 'Nauroz' and established the military department 'Diwan-i-Arz'.

### Q041 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which Delhi Sultan introduced strict market price control regulations along with the 'Dagh' and 'Chehra' military reforms?

A. Alauddin Khilji
B. Muhammad bin Tughlaq
C. Balban
D. Sikandar Lodi

**Answer:** A. Alauddin Khilji
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Alauddin Khilji (Correct):** Alauddin Khilji enforced strict market price controls to maintain a large standing army and introduced 'Dagh' (branding of horses) and 'Chehra' (descriptive soldier rolls).
- **B — Muhammad bin Tughlaq:** Famous for shifting the capital to Daulatabad and launching token currency.
- **C — Balban:** Reorganized the army under Diwan-i-Arz and pursued the 'Blood and Iron' policy.
- **D — Sikandar Lodi:** Founded Agra in 1504 CE and introduced the 'Gazz-i-Sikandari' unit of land measurement.
- **Exam Extra:** To supervise market regulations, Alauddin Khilji created 'Diwan-i-Riyasat' and appointed officers called 'Shahna-i-Mandi'.

### Q042 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which Delhi Sultan shifted his capital from Delhi to Daulatabad and introduced token currency?

A. Ghiyas-ud-din Tughlaq
B. Muhammad bin Tughlaq
C. Firoz Shah Tughlaq
D. Nasir-ud-din Mahmud

**Answer:** B. Muhammad bin Tughlaq
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Muhammad bin Tughlaq (Correct):** Muhammad bin Tughlaq shifted his capital from Delhi to Devagiri (renamed Daulatabad) in 1327 CE and later introduced bronze/copper token currency.
- **A — Ghiyas-ud-din Tughlaq:** Founder of the Tughlaq Dynasty in 1320 CE and builder of Tughlaqabad Fort.
- **C — Firoz Shah Tughlaq:** Established 'Diwan-i-Khairat' (charity bureau) and built major irrigation canals.
- **D — Nasir-ud-din Mahmud:** The Tughlaq Sultan during whose reign Timur invaded and sacked Delhi in 1398 CE.
- **Exam Extra:** Moroccan traveller Ibn Battuta visited India during Muhammad bin Tughlaq's reign and wrote the travelogue 'Rihla'.

### Q043 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which medieval poet and musician of the Delhi Sultanate was famously known as 'Tuti-e-Hind' (Parrot of India)?

A. Ziauddin Barani
B. Al-Biruni
C. Amir Khusrau
D. Abul Fazl

**Answer:** C. Amir Khusrau
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Amir Khusrau (Correct):** Amir Khusrau, the celebrated Persian-Hindavi poet, historian, and musician at the Delhi Sultanate court, was popularly titled 'Tuti-e-Hind' (Parrot of India).
- **A — Ziauddin Barani:** Prominent Delhi Sultanate historian who wrote 'Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi' and 'Fatwa-i-Jahandari'.
- **B — Al-Biruni:** 11th-century polymath who came with Mahmud of Ghazni and authored 'Kitab-ul-Hind'.
- **D — Abul Fazl:** 16th-century Mughal court historian who authored 'Akbarnama' and 'Ain-i-Akbari'.
- **Exam Extra:** A disciple of Sufi saint Nizamuddin Auliya, Amir Khusrau is associated with the development of Qawwali and Khayal music.

### Q044 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which Lodi Sultan founded the city of Agra in 1504 CE?

A. Bahlol Lodi
B. Ibrahim Lodi
C. Daulat Khan Lodi
D. Sikandar Lodi

**Answer:** D. Sikandar Lodi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sikandar Lodi (Correct):** Sikandar Lodi founded the city of Agra in 1504 CE and made it his capital in 1506 CE to tighten control over eastern Rajasthan and the Doab.
- **A — Bahlol Lodi:** Founder of the Lodi Dynasty in 1451 CE, the first Afghan dynasty to rule Delhi.
- **B — Ibrahim Lodi:** Last Sultan of Delhi, defeated and killed by Babur in the First Battle of Panipat (1526).
- **C — Daulat Khan Lodi:** Governor of Punjab who invited Babur to invade India against Ibrahim Lodi.
- **Exam Extra:** Sikandar Lodi also composed Persian poetry under the pen name 'Gulrukhi'.

### Q045 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Who founded the Vijayanagara Empire in 1336 CE?

A. Harihara I and Bukka Raya I
B. Krishnadevaraya and Devaraya II
C. Saluva Narasimha and Tirumala
D. Rama Raya and Venkata II

**Answer:** A. Harihara I and Bukka Raya I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Harihara I and Bukka Raya I (Correct):** Brothers Harihara I and Bukka Raya I founded the Vijayanagara Empire (Sangama Dynasty) in 1336 CE on the banks of the Tungabhadra River.
- **B — Krishnadevaraya and Devaraya II:** Later illustrious emperors of the Tuluva and Sangama dynasties respectively, not the founders.
- **C — Saluva Narasimha and Tirumala:** Founders of the later Saluva and Aravidu dynasties of Vijayanagara.
- **D — Rama Raya and Venkata II:** Late Vijayanagara leaders; Rama Raya fell at the Battle of Talikota in 1565.
- **Exam Extra:** They established the kingdom with the spiritual guidance of sage Vidyaranya, with Hampi (Vijayanagara) as its capital.

### Q046 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

Which famous Telugu literary work on statecraft was authored by Krishnadevaraya?

A. Manucharitram
B. Amuktamalyada
C. Panduranga Mahatyam
D. Prabhavati Pradyumnamu

**Answer:** B. Amuktamalyada
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Amuktamalyada (Correct):** Vijayanagara Emperor Krishnadevaraya composed the acclaimed Telugu poetic treatise on statecraft and devotion titled 'Amuktamalyada'.
- **A — Manucharitram:** Famous Telugu prabandha authored by Allasani Peddana, chief poet among the Ashtadiggajas.
- **C — Panduranga Mahatyam:** Celebrated Telugu work written by Tenali Ramakrishna.
- **D — Prabhavati Pradyumnamu:** 16th-century Telugu classic composed by Pingali Surana.
- **Exam Extra:** Krishnadevaraya belonged to the Tuluva Dynasty and patronized eight eminent Telugu poets known as the 'Ashtadiggajas'.

### Q047 · Delhi Sultanate & Vijayanagara

In which year was the Battle of Talikota fought, leading to the decline of the Vijayanagara Empire?

A. 1526
B. 1556
C. 1565
D. 1576

**Answer:** C. 1565
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1565 (Correct):** The Battle of Talikota (Rakshasi-Tangadi) was fought in 1565 CE, when a coalition of Deccan Sultanates defeated the Vijayanagara army under Aliya Rama Raya.
- **A — 1526:** Year of the First Battle of Panipat between Babur and Ibrahim Lodi.
- **B — 1556:** Year of the Second Battle of Panipat between Akbar's forces and Hemu.
- **D — 1576:** Year of the Battle of Haldighati between Mughal forces and Maharana Pratap.
- **Exam Extra:** The victorious Deccan Sultanate alliance comprised Bijapur, Ahmadnagar, Golconda, and Bidar.

### Q048 · Mughals & Marathas

Who founded the Mughal Empire in India?

A. Humayun
B. Akbar
C. Sher Shah Suri
D. Babur

**Answer:** D. Babur
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Babur (Correct):** Zahir-ud-din Muhammad Babur founded the Mughal Empire in India in 1526 after defeating Ibrahim Lodi at Panipat.
- **A — Humayun:** Son and successor of Babur, who briefly lost the empire to Sher Shah Suri before restoring Mughal rule in 1555.
- **B — Akbar:** Grandson of Babur who consolidated and expanded the Mughal Empire across northern and central India.
- **C — Sher Shah Suri:** Afghan ruler who founded the Sur Empire (1540–1555) after defeating Humayun.
- **Exam Extra:** Babur recorded his life and Indian observations in his Chagatai Turkish autobiography, 'Tuzuk-i-Baburi' (Baburnama).

### Q049 · Mughals & Marathas

The First Battle of Panipat was fought in which year?

A. 1526
B. 1527
C. 1556
D. 1761

**Answer:** A. 1526
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1526 (Correct):** The First Battle of Panipat was fought on 21 April 1526 between Babur and Ibrahim Lodi, establishing Mughal rule in Delhi.
- **B — 1527:** Year of the Battle of Khanwa, in which Babur defeated Rana Sanga of Mewar.
- **C — 1556:** Year of the Second Battle of Panipat between Akbar's army and Hemu.
- **D — 1761:** Year of the Third Battle of Panipat between Ahmad Shah Abdali and the Marathas.
- **Exam Extra:** Babur's effective use of gunpowder artillery and the 'Tulughma' flanking tactic proved decisive against Ibrahim Lodi's larger force.

### Q050 · Mughals & Marathas

Which medieval ruler introduced the silver coin 'Rupiya' and built the Grand Trunk Road (Sadak-i-Azam)?

A. Alauddin Khilji
B. Sher Shah Suri
C. Akbar
D. Jahangir

**Answer:** B. Sher Shah Suri
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sher Shah Suri (Correct):** Sher Shah Suri introduced the standardized silver coin called 'Rupiya' and rebuilt the Grand Trunk Road (Sadak-i-Azam) linking Bengal to the Indus region.
- **A — Alauddin Khilji:** Known for Delhi market price controls and the Dagh-Chehra military reforms.
- **C — Akbar:** Introduced the Mansabdari system and Todar Mal's Dahsala land revenue system.
- **D — Jahangir:** Remembered for installing the 'Chain of Justice' (Zanjir-i-Adl) at Agra Fort.
- **Exam Extra:** Sher Shah Suri's birth name was Farid Khan, and his octagonal tomb stands at Sasaram in Bihar.

### Q051 · Mughals & Marathas

Whom did Akbar's forces under Bairam Khan defeat in the Second Battle of Panipat in 1556?

A. Ibrahim Lodi
B. Rana Sanga
C. Hemu (Hem Chandra Vikramaditya)
D. Sikandar Shah Suri

**Answer:** C. Hemu (Hem Chandra Vikramaditya)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Hemu (Hem Chandra Vikramaditya) (Correct):** In the Second Battle of Panipat (5 November 1556), Akbar's forces led by his regent Bairam Khan defeated Hemu (Hem Chandra Vikramaditya).
- **A — Ibrahim Lodi:** Last Lodi Sultan defeated by Babur in the First Battle of Panipat (1526).
- **B — Rana Sanga:** Rajput ruler of Mewar defeated by Babur at the Battle of Khanwa (1527).
- **D — Sikandar Shah Suri:** Sur ruler defeated by Humayun at the Battle of Sirhind in 1555.
- **Exam Extra:** Victory at Panipat secured the Mughal throne for 13-year-old Akbar, who ruled under Bairam Khan's regency until 1560.

### Q052 · Mughals & Marathas

In the Battle of Haldighati (1576), Maharana Pratap fought against Mughal forces led by whom?

A. Bairam Khan
B. Todar Mal
C. Shaista Khan
D. Raja Man Singh I

**Answer:** D. Raja Man Singh I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Raja Man Singh I (Correct):** In the Battle of Haldighati (June 1576), Maharana Pratap of Mewar fought against Mughal forces commanded by Raja Man Singh I of Amber.
- **A — Bairam Khan:** Akbar's regent during the Second Battle of Panipat (1556); assassinated in 1561.
- **B — Todar Mal:** Akbar's finance minister who designed the Dahsala land revenue system.
- **C — Shaista Khan:** Aurangzeb's maternal uncle and Mughal governor surprised by Shivaji in Pune in 1663.
- **Exam Extra:** Maharana Pratap's loyal horse Chetak famously carried him out of danger despite being fatally wounded.

### Q053 · Mughals & Marathas

Which Mughal emperor founded the syncretic religious order 'Din-i-Ilahi' in 1582?

A. Akbar
B. Babur
C. Shah Jahan
D. Aurangzeb

**Answer:** A. Akbar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Akbar (Correct):** Mughal Emperor Akbar promulgated the syncretic spiritual order 'Din-i-Ilahi' (Tauhid-i-Ilahi) in 1582 to promote religious harmony.
- **B — Babur:** Founder of the Mughal Empire who did not establish any syncretic faith.
- **C — Shah Jahan:** Celebrated primarily for Mughal architecture such as the Taj Mahal and Jama Masjid.
- **D — Aurangzeb:** Followed orthodox Islamic policies and reimposed the Jizya tax in 1679.
- **Exam Extra:** Birbal was the only Hindu notable to accept Din-i-Ilahi; Akbar also built the 'Ibadat Khana' at Fatehpur Sikri for interfaith dialogues.

### Q054 · Mughals & Marathas

Who was Akbar's revenue minister who introduced the 'Dahsala' land revenue system?

A. Abul Fazl
B. Raja Todar Mal
C. Birbal
D. Faizi

**Answer:** B. Raja Todar Mal
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Raja Todar Mal (Correct):** Raja Todar Mal, Akbar's revenue minister (Diwan-i-Ashraf), devised the Zabt or Dahsala land revenue system in 1580.
- **A — Abul Fazl:** Akbar's chief historian who authored 'Akbarnama' and 'Ain-i-Akbari'.
- **C — Birbal:** Akbar's trusted courtier and advisor, originally named Mahesh Das.
- **D — Faizi:** Poet laureate (Malik-ush-Shuara) at Akbar's court and elder brother of Abul Fazl.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the Dahsala system, state revenue was fixed at one-third of the average produce and prices over the previous ten years.

### Q055 · Mughals & Marathas

Which English ambassador visited Emperor Jahangir's court in 1615 and secured trade privileges for the British East India Company?

A. Captain William Hawkins
B. Sir Francis Drake
C. Sir Thomas Roe
D. Job Charnock

**Answer:** C. Sir Thomas Roe
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Sir Thomas Roe (Correct):** Sir Thomas Roe arrived at Jahangir's court in 1615 as King James I's ambassador and secured imperial farmans for English East India Company trade and factories.
- **A — Captain William Hawkins:** Visited Jahangir's court in 1608–1609 and got a mansab, but failed to obtain a permanent Surat factory grant.
- **B — Sir Francis Drake:** 16th-century English navigator who circumnavigated the globe, not an envoy to Jahangir.
- **D — Job Charnock:** East India Company administrator linked with the founding of Calcutta in 1690.
- **Exam Extra:** Jahangir's reign is also renowned as the Golden Age of Mughal Painting, led by artists like Ustad Mansur and Abul Hasan.

### Q056 · Mughals & Marathas

Which Mughal emperor commissioned the Taj Mahal, the Red Fort in Delhi, and the Peacock Throne?

A. Akbar
B. Jahangir
C. Humayun
D. Shah Jahan

**Answer:** D. Shah Jahan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Shah Jahan (Correct):** Shah Jahan commissioned the Taj Mahal in Agra, the Red Fort (Lal Qila) and Jama Masjid in Delhi, and the Peacock Throne (Takht-i-Taus).
- **A — Akbar:** Built Agra Fort, Fatehpur Sikri, and the Buland Darwaza.
- **B — Jahangir:** Completed Akbar's tomb at Sikandra and developed the Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir.
- **C — Humayun:** His tomb in Delhi (commissioned by Bega Begum) served as an architectural precursor to the Taj Mahal.
- **Exam Extra:** The chief architect of the Taj Mahal, built in memory of Mumtaz Mahal, was Ustad Ahmad Lahauri.

### Q057 · Mughals & Marathas

Which Mughal emperor reimposed the Jizya tax in 1679 and was popularly called 'Zinda Pir'?

A. Aurangzeb
B. Bahadur Shah I
C. Shah Alam II
D. Farrukhsiyar

**Answer:** A. Aurangzeb
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Aurangzeb (Correct):** Aurangzeb (Alamgir) banned court music, reimposed the Jizya tax on non-Muslims in 1679, and was called 'Zinda Pir' (Living Saint) for his austere personal lifestyle.
- **B — Bahadur Shah I:** Son and successor of Aurangzeb (1707–1712) who adopted conciliatory policies toward Rajputs and Marathas.
- **C — Shah Alam II:** Later Mughal emperor who granted the Diwani rights of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa to the British in 1765.
- **D — Farrukhsiyar:** Issued the 1717 royal Farman granting duty-free trade privileges to the East India Company in Bengal.
- **Exam Extra:** Aurangzeb constructed the Moti Masjid inside Delhi's Red Fort, while Bibi Ka Maqbara at Aurangabad was built in memory of his wife Rabia-ud-Daurani.

### Q058 · Mughals & Marathas

In which year was Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj formally crowned at Raigad Fort?

A. 1664
B. 1674
C. 1680
D. 1659

**Answer:** B. 1674
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1674 (Correct):** Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj was formally crowned as sovereign Maratha ruler at Raigad Fort in June 1674.
- **A — 1664:** Year Shivaji carried out his first raid on the Mughal port of Surat.
- **C — 1680:** Year Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj passed away at Raigad Fort.
- **D — 1659:** Year Shivaji defeated and killed Bijapur commander Afzal Khan at Pratapgad.
- **Exam Extra:** Shivaji governed with a council of eight ministers known as the 'Ashtapradhan', headed by the Peshwa.

### Q059 · Mughals & Marathas

Between whom was the Treaty of Purandar signed in 1665?

A. Shivaji and Aurangzeb directly
B. Baji Rao I and Nizam-ul-Mulk
C. Shivaji and Jai Singh I
D. Balaji Vishwanath and Sayyid Brothers

**Answer:** C. Shivaji and Jai Singh I
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Shivaji and Jai Singh I (Correct):** The Treaty of Purandar (June 1665) was signed between Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj and Mughal commander Mirza Raja Jai Singh I of Amber.
- **A — Shivaji and Aurangzeb directly:** Shivaji visited Aurangzeb's court at Agra in 1666, not during the Treaty of Purandar.
- **B — Baji Rao I and Nizam-ul-Mulk:** Signed the Treaty of Mungi-Shevgaon in 1728 after the Battle of Palkhed.
- **D — Balaji Vishwanath and Sayyid Brothers:** Concluded a 1719 pact securing Maratha Chauth and Sardeshmukhi rights in the Deccan.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the Treaty of Purandar, Shivaji ceded 23 forts to the Mughals while retaining 12 forts.

### Q060 · Mughals & Marathas

Who defeated the Marathas in the Third Battle of Panipat in 1761?

A. Nadir Shah
B. Babur
C. Lord Lake
D. Ahmad Shah Abdali

**Answer:** D. Ahmad Shah Abdali
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ahmad Shah Abdali (Correct):** Afghan ruler Ahmad Shah Abdali (Durrani) defeated the Maratha forces in the Third Battle of Panipat on 14 January 1761.
- **A — Nadir Shah:** Persian ruler who invaded India in 1739, defeated Muhammad Shah at Karnal, and carried away the Peacock Throne.
- **B — Babur:** Defeated Ibrahim Lodi in the First Battle of Panipat in 1526.
- **C — Lord Lake:** British general who captured Delhi from Maratha control in 1803 during the Second Anglo-Maratha War.
- **Exam Extra:** Sadashivrao Bhau led the Maratha forces in the battle during the Peshwaship of Balaji Baji Rao (Nana Saheb).

### Q061 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

In which year did Portuguese navigator Vasco da Gama arrive at Calicut in India?

A. 1498
B. 1492
C. 1510
D. 1600

**Answer:** A. 1498
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1498 (Correct):** Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama reached Calicut (Kozhikode) on the Malabar Coast on 20 May 1498, opening the direct sea route from Europe to India.
- **B — 1492:** Year Christopher Columbus reached the Americas while searching for a westward sea route to India.
- **C — 1510:** Year Afonso de Albuquerque captured Goa from the Sultanate of Bijapur.
- **D — 1600:** Year the English East India Company received its royal charter from Queen Elizabeth I.
- **Exam Extra:** Vasco da Gama was received at Calicut by the local Hindu ruler known as the Zamorin (Samudri).

### Q062 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Which Nawab of Bengal was defeated by the British in the Battle of Plassey in 1757?

A. Mir Qasim
B. Siraj-ud-Daulah
C. Alivardi Khan
D. Shuja-ud-Daulah

**Answer:** B. Siraj-ud-Daulah
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Siraj-ud-Daulah (Correct):** In the Battle of Plassey (23 June 1757), British East India Company forces under Robert Clive defeated Siraj-ud-Daulah, the Nawab of Bengal.
- **A — Mir Qasim:** Fought the British at the Battle of Buxar (1764) in alliance with Shuja-ud-Daulah and Shah Alam II.
- **C — Alivardi Khan:** Maternal grandfather and predecessor of Siraj-ud-Daulah, who died in 1756.
- **D — Shuja-ud-Daulah:** Nawab of Awadh who joined the anti-British coalition at the Battle of Buxar (1764).
- **Exam Extra:** Betrayal by Siraj-ud-Daulah's commander Mir Jafar secured the British victory, after which Mir Jafar was made Nawab.

### Q063 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Who introduced the Permanent Settlement in Bengal?

A. Warren Hastings
B. Lord Dalhousie
C. Lord Cornwallis
D. Lord Wellesley

**Answer:** C. Lord Cornwallis
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Lord Cornwallis (Correct):** Lord Cornwallis introduced the Permanent Settlement (Zamindari System) in Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa in 1793, fixing land revenue permanently with Zamindars.
- **A — Warren Hastings:** First Governor-General of Bengal (1773) who abolished the Dual System and introduced the Ijaradari system.
- **B — Lord Dalhousie:** Known for the Doctrine of Lapse and introducing railways, telegraph, and postal reforms in India.
- **D — Lord Wellesley:** Introduced the Subsidiary Alliance system in 1798 to subordinate Indian princely states.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the Permanent Settlement, Zamindars retained 1/11th of the collected revenue and paid 10/11ths to the East India Company.

### Q064 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Who founded the Brahmo Samaj in Calcutta in 1828?

A. Swami Dayananda Saraswati
B. Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
C. Jyotirao Phule
D. Raja Ram Mohan Roy

**Answer:** D. Raja Ram Mohan Roy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Raja Ram Mohan Roy (Correct):** Raja Ram Mohan Roy founded the Brahmo Samaj (initially Brahmo Sabha) in Calcutta in 1828 to promote monotheism and social reform.
- **A — Swami Dayananda Saraswati:** Founded the Arya Samaj in Bombay in 1875 with the call "Go back to the Vedas."
- **B — Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar:** Championed women's education and spearheaded the Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act of 1856.
- **C — Jyotirao Phule:** Founded the Satyashodhak Samaj in Pune in 1873 and authored 'Gulamgiri'.
- **Exam Extra:** Raja Ram Mohan Roy's campaign led Governor-General Lord William Bentinck to abolish Sati via Regulation XVII in 1829.

### Q065 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

From which military station did the Revolt of 1857 break out on 10 May 1857?

A. Meerut
B. Barrackpore
C. Jhansi
D. Kanpur

**Answer:** A. Meerut
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Meerut (Correct):** The Revolt of 1857 broke out as a full-scale sepoy uprising at Meerut on 10 May 1857 before the soldiers marched to Delhi.
- **B — Barrackpore:** Site where Mangal Pandey rebelled on 29 March 1857 prior to the general outbreak.
- **C — Jhansi:** Major stronghold of the uprising led by Rani Lakshmibai.
- **D — Kanpur:** Key centre of the revolt led by Nana Saheb and Tatya Tope.
- **Exam Extra:** Upon reaching Delhi on 11 May 1857, the rebel sepoys proclaimed Mughal Emperor Bahadur Shah II (Zafar) as their leader.

### Q066 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Who founded the Indian National Congress in 1885?

A. W. C. Bonnerjee
B. A. O. Hume
C. Dadabhai Naoroji
D. Surendranath Banerjee

**Answer:** B. A. O. Hume
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — A. O. Hume (Correct):** Retired British civil servant Allan Octavian Hume (A. O. Hume) played the pivotal role in founding the Indian National Congress in December 1885.
- **A — W. C. Bonnerjee:** Served as the first President of the Indian National Congress at its 1885 Bombay session.
- **C — Dadabhai Naoroji:** Known as the 'Grand Old Man of India'; presided over INC sessions in 1886, 1893, and 1906.
- **D — Surendranath Banerjee:** Co-founded the Indian Association in 1876, an important precursor to the INC.
- **Exam Extra:** The inaugural INC session met at Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College in Bombay (28–31 December 1885) with 72 delegates.

### Q067 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

In which year did Lord Curzon execute the Partition of Bengal?

A. 1903
B. 1907
C. 1905
D. 1911

**Answer:** C. 1905
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1905 (Correct):** Viceroy Lord Curzon carried out the Partition of Bengal in 1905 (effective 16 October 1905), sparking the Swadeshi and Boycott Movements.
- **A — 1903:** Year the initial proposal to partition Bengal was announced publicly.
- **B — 1907:** Year of the Surat Split between the Moderate and Extremist factions of the INC.
- **D — 1911:** Year the Partition of Bengal was annulled under Lord Hardinge II.
- **Exam Extra:** The Partition of Bengal was later annulled in 1911 at the Delhi Durbar, when the British capital was shifted from Calcutta to Delhi.

### Q068 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

On which date did the Jallianwala Bagh Massacre take place in Amritsar?

A. 13 March 1919
B. 10 April 1919
C. 18 April 1919
D. 13 April 1919

**Answer:** D. 13 April 1919
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 13 April 1919 (Correct):** On 13 April 1919 (Baisakhi day), Brigadier-General Reginald Dyer ordered troops to fire on an unarmed gathering at Jallianwala Bagh in Amritsar.
- **A — 13 March 1919:** Not the massacre date; Udham Singh later assassinated Michael O'Dwyer in London on 13 March 1940.
- **B — 10 April 1919:** Date when nationalist leaders Dr. Saifuddin Kitchlew and Dr. Satyapal were arrested in Amritsar.
- **C — 18 April 1919:** Date when Mahatma Gandhi suspended the Rowlatt Satyagraha following violence.
- **Exam Extra:** Rabindranath Tagore renounced his British Knighthood in protest, and the Hunter Commission was formed to inquire into the massacre.

### Q069 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

The Non-Cooperation Movement began in which year?

A. 1920
B. 1919
C. 1922
D. 1930

**Answer:** A. 1920
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1920 (Correct):** The Non-Cooperation Movement began in 1920 under Mahatma Gandhi's leadership, formally launched on 1 August 1920 and ratified at the Nagpur INC session.
- **B — 1919:** Year of the Rowlatt Act, Jallianwala Bagh Massacre, and Government of India Act (1919).
- **C — 1922:** Year the Non-Cooperation Movement was called off following Chauri Chaura.
- **D — 1930:** Year the Civil Disobedience Movement was launched with the Dandi March.
- **Exam Extra:** Mahatma Gandhi suspended the Non-Cooperation Movement in February 1922 after the violent Chauri Chaura incident in Gorakhpur.

### Q070 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

At which INC session in December 1929 was the 'Purna Swaraj' (Complete Independence) resolution passed?

A. Calcutta Session
B. Lahore Session
C. Karachi Session
D. Lucknow Session

**Answer:** B. Lahore Session
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Lahore Session (Correct):** At the Lahore Session of the Indian National Congress in December 1929, presided over by Jawaharlal Nehru, the historic 'Purna Swaraj' (Complete Independence) resolution was adopted.
- **A — Calcutta Session:** In 1928, presided over by Motilal Nehru, issuing a one-year ultimatum for Dominion Status.
- **C — Karachi Session:** In 1931, presided over by Vallabhbhai Patel, adopting resolutions on Fundamental Rights and National Economic Programme.
- **D — Lucknow Session:** In 1916, presided over by A. C. Mazumdar, famous for the Lucknow Pact and Moderate-Extremist reunion.
- **Exam Extra:** Following the Lahore resolution, 26 January 1930 was celebrated across India as the first Independence Day (Purna Swaraj Day).

### Q071 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

The Dandi March took place in which year?

A. 1928
B. 1929
C. 1930
D. 1932

**Answer:** C. 1930
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1930 (Correct):** The Dandi March (Salt Satyagraha) took place in 1930, beginning on 12 March from Sabarmati Ashram and concluding at Dandi on 6 April.
- **A — 1928:** Year of the anti-Simon Commission protests and the Bardoli Satyagraha led by Sardar Patel.
- **B — 1929:** Year the Purna Swaraj resolution was passed at the Lahore INC Session.
- **D — 1932:** Year of the Communal Award and the Poona Pact between Mahatma Gandhi and Dr. B. R. Ambedkar.
- **Exam Extra:** Mahatma Gandhi marched roughly 240 miles with 78 chosen followers to break the British salt monopoly by picking up natural salt at Dandi.

### Q072 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

The Dandi March was associated with which movement?

A. Swadeshi Movement
B. Non-Cooperation Movement
C. Quit India Movement
D. Civil Disobedience Movement

**Answer:** D. Civil Disobedience Movement
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Civil Disobedience Movement (Correct):** The Dandi March (Salt Satyagraha) in 1930 inaugurated the nationwide Civil Disobedience Movement against British salt laws and colonial authority.
- **A — Swadeshi Movement:** Launched in 1905 in response to the Partition of Bengal.
- **B — Non-Cooperation Movement:** Mass movement conducted by Gandhi and the INC from 1920 to 1922.
- **C — Quit India Movement:** Final mass movement launched in August 1942 demanding immediate British withdrawal.
- **Exam Extra:** In Tamil Nadu, C. Rajagopalachari led a parallel Salt March from Tiruchirappalli to Vedaranyam in April 1930.

### Q073 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Who gave the slogan “Do or Die” during the freedom movement?

A. Mahatma Gandhi
B. Subhas Chandra Bose
C. Bal Gangadhar Tilak
D. Bhagat Singh

**Answer:** A. Mahatma Gandhi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Mahatma Gandhi (Correct):** Mahatma Gandhi gave the iconic call "Do or Die" (Karo ya Maro) on 8 August 1942 while launching the Quit India Movement.
- **B — Subhas Chandra Bose:** Popularized the slogans "Give me blood, and I shall give you freedom" and "Delhi Chalo."
- **C — Bal Gangadhar Tilak:** Gave the famous declaration "Swaraj is my birthright, and I shall have it!"
- **D — Bhagat Singh:** Popularized the revolutionary slogan "Inquilab Zindabad" (coined by Hasrat Mohani).
- **Exam Extra:** Gandhi delivered the "Do or Die" address at Gowalia Tank Maidan (now August Kranti Maidan) in Bombay.

### Q074 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Where did Subhas Chandra Bose establish the Provisional Government of Free India (Azad Hind) in October 1943?

A. Tokyo
B. Singapore
C. Rangoon
D. Berlin

**Answer:** B. Singapore
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Singapore (Correct):** Subhas Chandra Bose proclaimed the Provisional Government of Free India (Arzi Hukumat-e-Azad Hind) in Singapore on 21 October 1943.
- **A — Tokyo:** Capital of Japan where Bose met Prime Minister Hideki Tojo to seek military backing.
- **C — Rangoon:** City in Burma to which the Azad Hind headquarters was later shifted in January 1944.
- **D — Berlin:** Where Bose earlier formed the Free India Centre and the Indian Legion in 1941–1942.
- **Exam Extra:** The Indian National Army (INA) was initially raised by Captain Mohan Singh and Rash Behari Bose before Subhas Chandra Bose took supreme command in 1943.

### Q075 · Europeans, 1857 Revolt, Reform Movements, INC & Freedom Struggle

Who chaired the Drafting Committee of the Constituent Assembly?

A. Dr. Rajendra Prasad
B. Jawaharlal Nehru
C. Dr. B. R. Ambedkar
D. B. N. Rau

**Answer:** C. Dr. B. R. Ambedkar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Dr. B. R. Ambedkar (Correct):** Dr. Bhimrao Ramji Ambedkar served as the Chairman of the seven-member Drafting Committee of the Constituent Assembly of India.
- **A — Dr. Rajendra Prasad:** Permanent President of the Constituent Assembly and later India's first President.
- **B — Jawaharlal Nehru:** Chaired the Union Powers Committee, Union Constitution Committee, and States Committee.
- **D — B. N. Rau:** Served as the Constitutional Advisor to the Constituent Assembly.
- **Exam Extra:** The Drafting Committee was set up on 29 August 1947 and included members such as Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar and K. M. Munshi.

---

## Chapter 2: Geography & Census 2011

### Q076 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which planet in the Solar System is known as the “Morning Star” and “Evening Star”?

A. Mercury
B. Mars
C. Venus
D. Jupiter

**Answer:** C. Venus
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Venus (Correct):** Venus is the brightest planet in the night sky due to its thick, reflective sulfuric acid clouds and proximity to Earth, appearing prominently before sunrise and after sunset.
- **A — Mercury:** The smallest and innermost planet, completing an orbit around the Sun in about 88 Earth days.
- **B — Mars:** Known as the Red Planet because iron oxide on its surface gives it a reddish appearance.
- **D — Jupiter:** The largest planet in the Solar System, famous for its Great Red Spot storm.
- **Exam Extra:** Venus rotates from east to west (retrograde rotation) and has the hottest surface temperature (~465 °C) in the Solar System due to a runaway greenhouse effect.

### Q077 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which layer of Earth’s atmosphere contains the ozone layer that absorbs harmful ultraviolet radiation?

A. Stratosphere
B. Troposphere
C. Mesosphere
D. Thermosphere

**Answer:** A. Stratosphere
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Stratosphere (Correct):** The stratosphere (roughly 12–50 km altitude) houses the ozone layer, which absorbs most solar UV-B and UV-C radiation and causes temperature to rise with altitude.
- **B — Troposphere:** The lowest atmospheric layer where almost all weather phenomena, clouds, and precipitation occur.
- **C — Mesosphere:** The middle layer above the stratosphere where temperatures drop sharply and most meteors burn up.
- **D — Thermosphere:** A high-altitude layer containing the ionosphere, where auroras occur and the International Space Station orbits.
- **Exam Extra:** Ozone concentration is measured in Dobson Units (DU), and the Montreal Protocol (1987) was adopted to phase out ozone-depleting substances like CFCs.

### Q078 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

What is the approximate time taken by sunlight to reach Earth?

A. 4 minutes 20 seconds
B. 12 minutes 40 seconds
C. 16 minutes 10 seconds
D. 8 minutes 20 seconds

**Answer:** D. 8 minutes 20 seconds
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 8 minutes 20 seconds (Correct):** Light travels at ~299,792 km/s across the average Earth–Sun distance of ~149.6 million km (1 AU), taking approximately 499 seconds, or 8 minutes 20 seconds.
- **A — 4 minutes 20 seconds:** Close to the light-travel time from the Sun to Mercury, not Earth.
- **B — 12 minutes 40 seconds:** Approximately the light-travel time from the Sun to Mars at its average orbital distance.
- **C — 16 minutes 10 seconds:** Nearly double the actual light-travel time between the Sun and Earth.
- **Exam Extra:** The average distance between Earth and the Sun (~1.496 × 10^8 km) defines 1 Astronomical Unit (AU).

### Q079 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which imaginary line divides Earth into the Northern Hemisphere and the Southern Hemisphere?

A. Prime Meridian
B. Equator
C. Tropic of Cancer
D. International Date Line

**Answer:** B. Equator
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Equator (Correct):** The Equator (0° latitude) is the great circle perpendicular to Earth’s rotational axis that divides the planet into the Northern and Southern Hemispheres.
- **A — Prime Meridian:** The 0° longitude line passing through Greenwich, dividing Earth into Eastern and Western Hemispheres.
- **C — Tropic of Cancer:** The 23.5° N parallel marking the northernmost latitude where the Sun can appear directly overhead.
- **D — International Date Line:** The roughly 180° longitude line across the Pacific Ocean where the calendar date changes by one day.
- **Exam Extra:** At the Equator, day and night remain nearly equal (about 12 hours each) throughout the year, and 1° of latitude equals roughly 111 km.

### Q080 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

On which date does the Summer Solstice occur in the Northern Hemisphere, marking the longest day of the year there?

A. 21 March
B. 23 September
C. 21 June
D. 22 December

**Answer:** C. 21 June
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 21 June (Correct):** Around 21 June, the North Pole tilts maximally toward the Sun and solar rays fall vertically on the Tropic of Cancer (23.5° N), giving the Northern Hemisphere its longest day.
- **A — 21 March:** The Vernal (Spring) Equinox, when the Sun is directly overhead at the Equator and day and night are equal globally.
- **B — 23 September:** The Autumnal Equinox, when day and night are again nearly equal across the globe.
- **D — 22 December:** The Winter Solstice in the Northern Hemisphere, marking its shortest day when the Sun is overhead at the Tropic of Capricorn.
- **Exam Extra:** Earth reaches perihelion (closest to the Sun) around 3 January and aphelion (farthest from the Sun) around 4 July.

### Q081 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which gas constitutes the second-largest share of dry air in Earth’s atmosphere?

A. Oxygen
B. Nitrogen
C. Argon
D. Carbon dioxide

**Answer:** A. Oxygen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Oxygen (Correct):** Dry atmospheric air consists of ~78.08% nitrogen, ~20.95% oxygen (second largest), ~0.93% argon, and ~0.04% carbon dioxide.
- **B — Nitrogen:** The most abundant gas in Earth’s atmosphere, accounting for roughly 78% of dry air.
- **C — Argon:** The third most abundant gas in dry air (~0.93%) and the most common noble gas in the atmosphere.
- **D — Carbon dioxide:** A trace greenhouse gas comprising around 0.04% of the atmosphere.
- **Exam Extra:** Nitrogen and oxygen together make up nearly 99% of Earth’s dry atmosphere by volume.

### Q082 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which planet has the largest number of confirmed moons in the Solar System?

A. Jupiter
B. Uranus
C. Neptune
D. Saturn

**Answer:** D. Saturn
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Saturn (Correct):** Saturn holds the record for the most confirmed moons in the Solar System (over 145 confirmed moons, with Titan being its largest).
- **A — Jupiter:** Holds the second-highest number of confirmed moons (95), including the four large Galilean moons (Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto).
- **B — Uranus:** An ice giant with 28 known moons, named largely after characters from Shakespeare and Alexander Pope.
- **C — Neptune:** The outermost major planet, with 16 known moons, the largest being Triton.
- **Exam Extra:** Ganymede (a moon of Jupiter) is the largest moon in the Solar System, exceeding even the planet Mercury in diameter.

### Q083 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

What is the boundary separating Earth’s crust from the underlying mantle called?

A. Gutenberg Discontinuity
B. Mohorovičić Discontinuity
C. Conrad Discontinuity
D. Lehmann Discontinuity

**Answer:** B. Mohorovičić Discontinuity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Mohorovičić Discontinuity (Correct):** The Mohorovičić Discontinuity (Moho), identified by Andrija Mohorovičić in 1909, marks the sharp increase in seismic wave velocity between Earth’s crust and upper mantle.
- **A — Gutenberg Discontinuity:** Separates the lower mantle from the liquid outer core at a depth of about 2,900 km.
- **C — Conrad Discontinuity:** A seismic boundary within the continental crust separating the upper granitic (sial) layer from the lower basaltic (sima) layer.
- **D — Lehmann Discontinuity:** Separates the liquid outer core from the solid inner core at roughly 5,150 km depth.
- **Exam Extra:** Earth’s crust is dominated by oxygen (~46.6%) and silicon (~27.7%) by weight, whereas silicon and magnesium dominate the lower crust and mantle.

### Q084 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which instrument is used to measure atmospheric pressure?

A. Hygrometer
B. Anemometer
C. Barometer
D. Pyrometer

**Answer:** C. Barometer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Barometer (Correct):** A barometer (invented by Evangelista Torricelli in 1643) measures atmospheric pressure, typically expressed in millibars, hectopascals, or millimetres of mercury.
- **A — Hygrometer:** Measures relative humidity or moisture content in the air.
- **B — Anemometer:** Measures wind speed and air current velocity.
- **D — Pyrometer:** Measures very high temperatures, such as inside industrial furnaces, without direct contact.
- **Exam Extra:** A sudden drop in barometric pressure usually signals an approaching cyclonic storm or low-pressure depression.

### Q085 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which type of tide occurs when the Sun, Moon, and Earth align in a straight line during a full moon or new moon?

A. Spring tide
B. Neap tide
C. Ebb tide
D. Diurnal tide

**Answer:** A. Spring tide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Spring tide (Correct):** Spring tides produce the greatest tidal range (highest high tides and lowest low tides) because the gravitational pulls of the Sun and Moon reinforce each other during full and new moons.
- **B — Neap tide:** Occurs during the first and third quarter moons when the Sun and Moon are at right angles, causing the smallest tidal range.
- **C — Ebb tide:** Refers to the receding phase of a tide as seawater flows back out from high tide to low tide.
- **D — Diurnal tide:** A tidal pattern featuring only one high tide and one low tide during each lunar day.
- **Exam Extra:** The Bay of Fundy in Canada experiences the highest tidal range in the world, exceeding 15 metres.

### Q086 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Which seismic waves travel the fastest through Earth’s interior and are the first to be recorded on a seismograph?

A. S-waves (Secondary waves)
B. Love waves
C. Rayleigh waves
D. P-waves (Primary waves)

**Answer:** D. P-waves (Primary waves)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — P-waves (Primary waves) (Correct):** P-waves are longitudinal compressional body waves that travel fastest through solids, liquids, and gases, arriving first at seismic stations.
- **A — S-waves (Secondary waves):** Transverse shear waves that travel slower than P-waves and cannot propagate through liquids.
- **B — Love waves:** Horizontal-motion surface waves that cause intense side-to-side shaking near Earth’s surface.
- **C — Rayleigh waves:** Rolling surface waves moving in a vertical elliptical pattern, slower than body waves.
- **Exam Extra:** The inability of S-waves to pass through the outer core provided key evidence that Earth’s outer core is liquid.

### Q087 · Solar System, Earth & Atmosphere

Indian Standard Time (IST) is based on which meridian passing near Mirzapur in Uttar Pradesh?

A. 75°30′ E
B. 82°30′ E
C. 88°30′ E
D. 90°00′ E

**Answer:** B. 82°30′ E
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 82°30′ E (Correct):** The 82°30′ E longitude serves as the Standard Meridian of India, placing Indian Standard Time (IST) exactly 5 hours 30 minutes ahead of Greenwich Mean Time (UTC+05:30).
- **A — 75°30′ E:** Passes through western-central India and corresponds to roughly UTC+05:02, not IST.
- **C — 88°30′ E:** Passes near Kolkata in West Bengal, corresponding to roughly UTC+05:54.
- **D — 90°00′ E:** Corresponds to UTC+06:00 (used by Bangladesh Standard Time), passing across eastern India and Bangladesh.
- **Exam Extra:** The 82°30′ E meridian passes through five Indian states: Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha, and Andhra Pradesh.

### Q088 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

The Tropic of Cancer passes through how many Indian states?

A. Six
B. Seven
C. Eight
D. Nine

**Answer:** C. Eight
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Eight (Correct):** The Tropic of Cancer (23°30′ N) passes through eight Indian states from west to east: Gujarat, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, West Bengal, Tripura, and Mizoram.
- **A — Six:** Understates the count by omitting two of the eight states crossed by the 23°30′ N parallel.
- **B — Seven:** Often confused with the 'Seven Sisters' of Northeast India, though only Tripura and Mizoram are crossed in that region.
- **D — Nine:** Overstates the count; neighbouring states such as Bihar, Odisha, and Manipur lie outside the Tropic of Cancer.
- **Exam Extra:** The Mahi River is the only river in India that crosses the Tropic of Cancer twice—once in Madhya Pradesh and again in Gujarat.

### Q089 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which is the highest mountain peak located wholly within India?

A. Kanchenjunga
B. Nanda Devi
C. Kamet
D. Saltoro Kangri

**Answer:** B. Nanda Devi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nanda Devi (Correct):** Nanda Devi (7,816 m) in Uttarakhand’s Garhwal Himalayas is the highest mountain peak situated entirely within Indian territory.
- **A — Kanchenjunga:** At 8,586 m it is the highest peak in India overall, but it straddles the India (Sikkim)–Nepal international border.
- **C — Kamet:** At 7,756 m in Uttarakhand, it is the second-highest peak located wholly within India after Nanda Devi.
- **D — Saltoro Kangri:** Located at 7,742 m in the Saltoro/Karakoram range near the Actual Ground Position Line in Ladakh.
- **Exam Extra:** K2 (Godwin-Austen, 8,611 m) is the world’s second-highest peak and lies in Pakistan-occupied Kashmir (Gilgit-Baltistan).

### Q090 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which Himalayan pass connects Srinagar with Leh?

A. Zoji La
B. Banihal Pass
C. Shipki La
D. Nathu La

**Answer:** A. Zoji La
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Zoji La (Correct):** Zoji La (elevation ~3,528 m) in the western Himalayas links the Kashmir Valley (Srinagar) with Kargil and Leh in Ladakh along NH-1.
- **B — Banihal Pass:** Connects Jammu with Srinagar across the Pir Panjal range via the Jawahar Tunnel.
- **C — Shipki La:** Located in Himachal Pradesh on the India–Tibet border, where the Sutlej River enters India.
- **D — Nathu La:** Located in Sikkim, linking India with Tibet’s Chumbi Valley along the historic Silk Route.
- **Exam Extra:** The all-weather Zoji La Tunnel is under construction to keep the Srinagar–Leh route open throughout winter.

### Q091 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which Indian state has the longest coastline?

A. Maharashtra
B. Andhra Pradesh
C. Tamil Nadu
D. Gujarat

**Answer:** D. Gujarat
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Gujarat (Correct):** Gujarat has the longest coastline among Indian states (~1,214.7 km; ~1,600 km including creeks and islands), largely due to the deeply indented Kathiawar and Kachchh peninsulas.
- **A — Maharashtra:** Has a coastline of about 652.6 km along the Konkan coast.
- **B — Andhra Pradesh:** Has the second-longest coastline among Indian states (~973.7 km) along the eastern coast.
- **C — Tamil Nadu:** Has a coastline of roughly 906.9 km along the Coromandel coast and Gulf of Mannar.
