- **Exam Extra:** Among all States and Union Territories combined, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands have the longest coastline (~1,962 km), bringing India’s total coastline to 7,516.6 km.

### Q092 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which plateau in eastern India is rich in mineral resources such as coal, iron ore, and mica?

A. Malwa Plateau
B. Deccan Plateau
C. Chota Nagpur Plateau
D. Bundelkhand Plateau

**Answer:** C. Chota Nagpur Plateau
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Chota Nagpur Plateau (Correct):** The Chota Nagpur Plateau (centred in Jharkhand and extending into Odisha, West Bengal, and Chhattisgarh) is known as the “Ruhr of India” for its rich deposits of coal, iron ore, mica, copper, and bauxite.
- **A — Malwa Plateau:** A volcanic basalt plateau in western-central India (Madhya Pradesh and Rajasthan) drained by the Chambal and Betwa rivers.
- **B — Deccan Plateau:** The large triangular basaltic plateau covering most of southern India south of the Narmada River.
- **D — Bundelkhand Plateau:** An ancient granitic upland spanning southern Uttar Pradesh and northern Madhya Pradesh.
- **Exam Extra:** The Damodar River flows through a rift valley across the Chota Nagpur Plateau, flanked by major coalfields such as Jharia and Raniganj.

### Q093 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

What are the longitudinal valleys lying between the Lesser Himalayas and the Shiwaliks called?

A. Duns
B. Karewas
C. Bhabar
D. Terai

**Answer:** A. Duns
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Duns (Correct):** Duns (such as Dehradun, Kotli Dun, and Patli Dun) are flat-bottomed longitudinal structural valleys situated between the Lesser Himalayas and the Shiwalik hills.
- **B — Karewas:** Thick lacustrine (lake-bed) deposits in the Kashmir Valley famous for saffron (Zafran) cultivation.
- **C — Bhabar:** A narrow, porous belt of pebbles and boulders along the Shiwalik foothills where streams disappear underground.
- **D — Terai:** A marshy, densely forested belt south of Bhabar where subterranean streams re-emerge at the surface.
- **Exam Extra:** Dehradun is the largest and best-known Dun valley in India, extending roughly 35–45 km in length.

### Q094 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which is the highest peak of the Aravalli Range?

A. Anaimudi
B. Guru Shikhar
C. Doddabetta
D. Dhupgarh

**Answer:** B. Guru Shikhar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Guru Shikhar (Correct):** Guru Shikhar (1,722 m), located near Mount Abu in Rajasthan, is the highest summit of the ancient fold Aravalli Range.
- **A — Anaimudi:** At 2,695 m in Kerala’s Anaimalai Hills, it is the highest peak of the Western Ghats and all of Peninsular India.
- **C — Doddabetta:** At 2,637 m near Ooty in Tamil Nadu, it is the highest peak of the Nilgiri Hills.
- **D — Dhupgarh:** At 1,350 m near Pachmarhi in Madhya Pradesh, it is the highest peak of the Satpura Range.
- **Exam Extra:** The Aravalli Range is one of the oldest fold mountain systems in the world, stretching roughly 692 km from Gujarat to Delhi.

### Q095 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which water channel separates the Andaman Islands from the Nicobar Islands?

A. Eight Degree Channel
B. Nine Degree Channel
C. Duncan Passage
D. Ten Degree Channel

**Answer:** D. Ten Degree Channel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ten Degree Channel (Correct):** The Ten Degree Channel (along 10° N latitude) is a ~150-km-wide sea channel separating Little Andaman from Car Nicobar.
- **A — Eight Degree Channel:** Separates Minicoy Island (India) from the Maldives.
- **B — Nine Degree Channel:** Separates Minicoy Island from the main Lakshadweep archipelago.
- **C — Duncan Passage:** Separates South Andaman (Rutland Island) from Little Andaman.
- **Exam Extra:** Indira Point, the southernmost point of India (6°45′ N), is located on Great Nicobar Island.

### Q096 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which is the only active volcano in India?

A. Barren Island
B. Narcondam Island
C. Dhinodhar Hill
D. Tosham Hill

**Answer:** A. Barren Island
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Barren Island (Correct):** Barren Island, situated in the Andaman Sea about 135 km northeast of Port Blair, is India’s and South Asia’s only confirmed active volcano.
- **B — Narcondam Island:** A dormant volcanic island in the Andaman Sea, not currently active.
- **C — Dhinodhar Hill:** An extinct volcanic plug located in the Kachchh district of Gujarat.
- **D — Tosham Hill:** An ancient igneous ring complex (extinct volcano) located in Bhiwani district, Haryana.
- **Exam Extra:** Narcondam Island, northeast of Barren Island, is a dormant volcanic island in the Andaman Sea.

### Q097 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which mountain pass in Himachal Pradesh connects Manali with the Lahaul–Spiti Valley toward Leh?

A. Bomdi La
B. Jelep La
C. Rohtang Pass
D. Lipulekh Pass

**Answer:** C. Rohtang Pass
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Rohtang Pass (Correct):** Rohtang Pass (3,978 m) in the Pir Panjal range of Himachal Pradesh connects the Kullu Valley (Manali) with Lahaul and Spiti on the route toward Leh.
- **A — Bomdi La:** A mountain pass in Arunachal Pradesh connecting Tawang with Tezpur in Assam.
- **B — Jelep La:** A pass in Sikkim linking India with Lhasa in Tibet via the Chumbi Valley.
- **D — Lipulekh Pass:** A tri-junction pass in Uttarakhand near India, Nepal, and China used for the Kailash Mansarovar Yatra.
- **Exam Extra:** The 9.02-km Atal Tunnel beneath Rohtang Pass is the world’s longest single-tube highway tunnel above 3,000 m altitude.

### Q098 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

What is the southernmost point of the Indian mainland?

A. Indira Point
B. Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin)
C. Indira Col
D. Kibithu

**Answer:** B. Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin) (Correct):** Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin) in Tamil Nadu at 8°4′ N latitude is the southernmost tip of the Indian mainland, where the Arabian Sea, Indian Ocean, and Bay of Bengal converge.
- **A — Indira Point:** The southernmost point of the entire Republic of India (6°45′ N), located on Great Nicobar Island rather than the mainland.
- **C — Indira Col:** The northernmost point of India, located in the Siachen Muztagh of the Karakoram range.
- **D — Kibithu:** The easternmost town of India, situated in Anjaw district of Arunachal Pradesh.
- **Exam Extra:** Guhar Moti in the Kachchh district of Gujarat is the westernmost point of India.

### Q099 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which hills form the meeting point of the Eastern Ghats and the Western Ghats?

A. Cardamom Hills
B. Shevaroy Hills
C. Javadi Hills
D. Nilgiri Hills

**Answer:** D. Nilgiri Hills
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Nilgiri Hills (Correct):** The Nilgiri Hills, spanning the tri-junction of Tamil Nadu, Kerala, and Karnataka, form the physiographic meeting point of the Western Ghats and the Eastern Ghats.
- **A — Cardamom Hills:** Located in southeastern Kerala and southwestern Tamil Nadu, forming the southernmost part of the Western Ghats.
- **B — Shevaroy Hills:** A detached hill range of the Eastern Ghats near Salem in Tamil Nadu.
- **C — Javadi Hills:** An extension of the Eastern Ghats situated in northern Tamil Nadu.
- **Exam Extra:** Established in 1986, the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve was the first biosphere reserve created in India.

### Q100 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which Indian state shares the longest international border with Bangladesh?

A. Assam
B. Tripura
C. West Bengal
D. Meghalaya

**Answer:** C. West Bengal
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — West Bengal (Correct):** West Bengal shares a ~2,216.7 km border with Bangladesh, the longest international land border shared by any Indian state with a neighbouring country.
- **A — Assam:** Shares roughly 263 km of border with Bangladesh across the Brahmaputra and Barak valleys.
- **B — Tripura:** Shares an ~856 km border with Bangladesh, surrounded by Bangladesh on three sides.
- **D — Meghalaya:** Shares roughly 443 km of border with Bangladesh along its southern hills.
- **Exam Extra:** Among all neighbours, India shares its longest international land border with Bangladesh (4,096.7 km), followed by China (3,488 km) and Pakistan (3,323 km).

### Q101 · Indian Physiography, Mountains & Passes

Which island in the Brahmaputra River of Assam is the world’s largest inhabited river island?

A. Majuli
B. Umananda
C. Sagar Island
D. Divar Island

**Answer:** A. Majuli
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Majuli (Correct):** Majuli, nestled in the Brahmaputra River in Assam, is recognized by Guinness World Records as the world’s largest inhabited river island and became India’s first island district in 2016.
- **B — Umananda:** Known as the smallest inhabited river island in the Brahmaputra River, located near Guwahati.
- **C — Sagar Island:** A coastal island in the Sundarbans delta at the mouth of the Hooghly River in West Bengal.
- **D — Divar Island:** An island located in the Mandovi River in Goa.
- **Exam Extra:** Majuli is a major centre of Neo-Vaishnavite culture in Assam, famous for its traditional monasteries called Satras founded by Srimanta Sankardeva.

### Q102 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

The Sundarbans are chiefly associated with which river delta?

A. Godavari–Krishna delta
B. Mahanadi delta
C. Kaveri delta
D. Ganga–Brahmaputra delta

**Answer:** D. Ganga–Brahmaputra delta
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ganga–Brahmaputra delta (Correct):** The Sundarbans occupy the vast tidal Ganga–Brahmaputra–Meghna delta spanning West Bengal and Bangladesh, forming the world’s largest contiguous mangrove forest.
- **A — Godavari–Krishna delta:** A fertile east-coast delta in Andhra Pradesh housing the Coringa mangroves and Kolleru Lake.
- **B — Mahanadi delta:** Located in coastal Odisha, supporting the Bhitarkanika mangrove ecosystem.
- **C — Kaveri delta:** Known as the 'Granary of South India' in Tamil Nadu, renowned for intensive paddy cultivation.
- **Exam Extra:** Named after the Sundari tree (Heritiera fomes), the Sundarbans are a UNESCO World Heritage Site and home to the Royal Bengal Tiger.

### Q103 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which river is known as the “Sorrow of Bihar”?

A. Gandak
B. Kosi
C. Son
D. Ghaghara

**Answer:** B. Kosi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Kosi (Correct):** Originating in Tibet/Nepal and carrying immense Himalayan silt, the Kosi River frequently shifts its braided course across northern Bihar, causing devastating floods and earning the title “Sorrow of Bihar.”
- **A — Gandak:** A major left-bank tributary of the Ganga entering Bihar near Valmikinagar.
- **C — Son:** A major right-bank tributary of the Ganga originating near Amarkantak and joining near Patna.
- **D — Ghaghara:** Known as Karnali in Nepal; the largest tributary of the Ganga by water volume.
- **Exam Extra:** Similarly, the Damodar River was historically called the “Sorrow of Bengal” prior to the construction of the Damodar Valley Corporation (DVC) dams.

### Q104 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which is the largest brackish-water lagoon in India?

A. Chilika Lake
B. Pulicat Lake
C. Vembanad Lake
D. Kolleru Lake

**Answer:** A. Chilika Lake
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Chilika Lake (Correct):** Chilika Lake in Odisha (spanning roughly 1,100 sq km) is India’s largest brackish-water lagoon and the second-largest coastal lagoon in the world.
- **B — Pulicat Lake:** The second-largest brackish-water lagoon in India, straddling the Andhra Pradesh–Tamil Nadu border near Sriharikota.
- **C — Vembanad Lake:** India’s longest lake (~96.5 km), a coastal backwater lagoon in Kerala.
- **D — Kolleru Lake:** One of India’s largest freshwater lakes, situated between the Godavari and Krishna deltas in Andhra Pradesh.
- **Exam Extra:** Chilika Lake and Keoladeo National Park were designated in 1981 as India’s first Ramsar Wetlands of International Importance.

### Q105 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which is the longest river that flows entirely within India?

A. Ganga
B. Krishna
C. Godavari
D. Narmada

**Answer:** C. Godavari
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Godavari (Correct):** Flowing 1,465 km from Trimbakeshwar (Maharashtra) to the Bay of Bengal (Andhra Pradesh), the Godavari is the longest river flowing wholly within India and is called “Dakshin Ganga.”
- **A — Ganga:** At 2,525 km it is India’s longest river overall, but its lower course (Padma) flows through Bangladesh.
- **B — Krishna:** The second-longest peninsular river (~1,400 km), originating near Mahabaleshwar in Maharashtra.
- **D — Narmada:** At 1,312 km, the longest west-flowing peninsular river draining into the Arabian Sea.
- **Exam Extra:** Major tributaries of the Godavari include the Penganga, Wardha, Wainganga (Pranhita), Indravati, and Manjira.

### Q106 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which pair of major peninsular rivers flows westward into the Arabian Sea through rift valleys?

A. Mahanadi and Godavari
B. Narmada and Tapi
C. Krishna and Kaveri
D. Brahmani and Baitarani

**Answer:** B. Narmada and Tapi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Narmada and Tapi (Correct):** Unlike most peninsular rivers that drain eastward, the Narmada (between the Vindhya and Satpura ranges) and the Tapi (south of Satpura) flow westward through structural rift valleys into the Arabian Sea.
- **A — Mahanadi and Godavari:** East-flowing peninsular rivers that build large fertile deltas along the Bay of Bengal.
- **C — Krishna and Kaveri:** East-flowing rivers draining across Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu into the Bay of Bengal.
- **D — Brahmani and Baitarani:** East-flowing rivers of Odisha emptying into the Bay of Bengal near Dhamra.
- **Exam Extra:** West-flowing rivers like the Narmada and Tapi form estuaries rather than deltas due to steep gradients, hard rock beds, and strong tidal currents.

### Q107 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

The Sardar Sarovar Dam is constructed across which river in Gujarat?

A. Sabarmati
B. Mahi
C. Tapi
D. Narmada

**Answer:** D. Narmada
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Narmada (Correct):** The Sardar Sarovar Dam at Kevadia in Gujarat is a concrete gravity dam across the Narmada River supplying irrigation, drinking water, and hydroelectricity to four states.
- **A — Sabarmati:** Flows past Ahmedabad and Gandhinagar into the Gulf of Khambhat; hosts the Dharoi Dam.
- **B — Mahi:** Flows through Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan, and Gujarat; hosts the Kadana and Mahi Bajaj Sagar dams.
- **C — Tapi:** Flows across central India into Gujarat; hosts the Ukai and Kakrapar dams.
- **Exam Extra:** The 182-metre Statue of Unity stands on Sadhu Bet island facing the Sardar Sarovar Dam.

### Q108 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which is the highest gravity dam in India, built on the Sutlej River?

A. Bhakra Dam
B. Tehri Dam
C. Hirakud Dam
D. Nagarjuna Sagar Dam

**Answer:** A. Bhakra Dam
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Bhakra Dam (Correct):** The Bhakra Dam (226 m high) on the Sutlej River in Himachal Pradesh is India’s highest straight gravity dam, impounding the Gobind Sagar reservoir.
- **B — Tehri Dam:** At 260.5 m on the Bhagirathi River in Uttarakhand, it is India’s tallest dam overall, but it is a rock-fill embankment dam rather than a gravity dam.
- **C — Hirakud Dam:** Built on the Mahanadi River in Odisha; the longest major earthen dam in the world (~25.8 km including dykes).
- **D — Nagarjuna Sagar Dam:** A large masonry dam built across the Krishna River on the Telangana–Andhra Pradesh border.
- **Exam Extra:** Jawaharlal Nehru famously called the Bhakra Nangal project the “New Temple of Resurgent India.”

### Q109 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

At which of the Panch Prayags do the Alaknanda and Bhagirathi rivers unite to form the Ganga?

A. Vishnuprayag
B. Karnaprayag
C. Rudraprayag
D. Devprayag

**Answer:** D. Devprayag
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Devprayag (Correct):** At Devprayag in Uttarakhand, the Bhagirathi River (rising from Gangotri/Gaumukh) meets the Alaknanda River (from Satopanth Glacier) to officially form the Ganga.
- **A — Vishnuprayag:** The confluence of the Alaknanda and Dhauliganga rivers.
- **B — Karnaprayag:** The confluence of the Alaknanda and Pindar rivers.
- **C — Rudraprayag:** The confluence of the Alaknanda and Mandakini rivers.
- **Exam Extra:** The fifth confluence of the Panch Prayag is Nandaprayag, where the Nandakini River joins the Alaknanda.

### Q110 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

By what name is the Brahmaputra River known in Tibet before entering India?

A. Dihang
B. Jamuna
C. Yarlung Tsangpo
D. Padma

**Answer:** C. Yarlung Tsangpo
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Yarlung Tsangpo (Correct):** Originating near the Chemayungdung Glacier in southwestern Tibet, the river flows eastward as the Yarlung Tsangpo before cutting around Namcha Barwa into Arunachal Pradesh.
- **A — Dihang:** The name of the river (also called Siang) as it enters and flows through Arunachal Pradesh.
- **B — Jamuna:** The name given to the Brahmaputra after it enters Bangladesh.
- **D — Padma:** The name given to the Ganga River after it enters Bangladesh.
- **Exam Extra:** In Bangladesh, the Jamuna (Brahmaputra) merges with the Padma (Ganga) and then joins the Meghna before emptying into the Bay of Bengal.

### Q111 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which is the largest freshwater lake in India?

A. Dal Lake
B. Wular Lake
C. Loktak Lake
D. Sambhar Lake

**Answer:** B. Wular Lake
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Wular Lake (Correct):** Wular Lake in Bandipora district of Jammu & Kashmir, fed by the Jhelum River, is India’s largest freshwater lake, formed by tectonic activity.
- **A — Dal Lake:** A famous urban freshwater lake in Srinagar known for houseboats and shikaras, smaller than Wular Lake.
- **C — Loktak Lake:** The largest freshwater lake in Northeast India (Manipur), famous for floating biomass islands called phumdis.
- **D — Sambhar Lake:** India’s largest inland saltwater lake, located in Rajasthan.
- **Exam Extra:** Keibul Lamjao National Park on Loktak Lake is the world’s only floating national park and habitat of the endangered Sangai deer.

### Q112 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Kunchikal Falls, the highest waterfall in India, is formed by which river in Karnataka?

A. Varahi River
B. Sharavati River
C. Kaveri River
D. Mandovi River

**Answer:** A. Varahi River
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Varahi River (Correct):** Kunchikal Falls in Shivamogga district, Karnataka, drops ~455 m in tiered cascades along the Varahi River, making it India’s tallest waterfall.
- **B — Sharavati River:** Forms Jog (Gersoppa) Falls (~253 m) in Karnataka, renowned as a high single-drop waterfall on a major river.
- **C — Kaveri River:** Forms Shivanasamudra Falls in Karnataka and Hogenakkal Falls on the Karnataka–Tamil Nadu border.
- **D — Mandovi River:** Forms the four-tiered Dudhsagar Falls on the Goa–Karnataka border.
- **Exam Extra:** Nohkalikai Falls (~340 m) near Cherrapunji in Meghalaya is India’s tallest single plunge-type waterfall.

### Q113 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

The Mettur Dam in Tamil Nadu is built across which river?

A. Vaigai
B. Palar
C. Kaveri
D. Periyar

**Answer:** C. Kaveri
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Kaveri (Correct):** Built in 1934 at Mettur in Salem district, the Mettur Dam (impounding Stanley Reservoir) is one of India’s oldest large dams built across the Kaveri River.
- **A — Vaigai:** Flows past Madurai in southern Tamil Nadu and hosts the Vaigai Dam.
- **B — Palar:** Flows through Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, and northern Tamil Nadu into the Bay of Bengal.
- **D — Periyar:** Kerala’s longest river, supporting the Idukki and Mullaperiyar dams.
- **Exam Extra:** Originating at Talakaveri in Karnataka’s Brahmagiri Hills, the Kaveri receives rainfall from both the Southwest and Northeast monsoons.

### Q114 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Under the Indus Waters Treaty (1960), India was granted unrestricted use of which three rivers?

A. Indus, Jhelum, Chenab
B. Ravi, Beas, Sutlej
C. Jhelum, Chenab, Ravi
D. Chenab, Beas, Sutlej

**Answer:** B. Ravi, Beas, Sutlej
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Ravi, Beas, Sutlej (Correct):** Under the 1960 Indus Waters Treaty brokered by the World Bank, India received unrestricted rights over the three eastern rivers—Ravi, Beas, and Sutlej.
- **A — Indus, Jhelum, Chenab:** The three western rivers allocated primarily to Pakistan, with limited non-consumptive use permitted to India.
- **C — Jhelum, Chenab, Ravi:** Mixes two western rivers (Jhelum and Chenab) with one eastern river (Ravi).
- **D — Chenab, Beas, Sutlej:** Incorrectly substitutes the western river Chenab in place of Ravi.
- **Exam Extra:** The Sutlej enters India from Tibet through the Shipki La pass and is the longest of the five Punjab tributaries of the Indus.

### Q115 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which major peninsular river originates near the Amarkantak Plateau in Madhya Pradesh?

A. Godavari
B. Krishna
C. Mahanadi
D. Narmada

**Answer:** D. Narmada
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Narmada (Correct):** The Narmada River (along with the Son River) originates from the Amarkantak Plateau in the Maikal Hills of Anuppur district, Madhya Pradesh.
- **A — Godavari:** Originates at Trimbakeshwar in Nashik district, Maharashtra.
- **B — Krishna:** Originates near Mahabaleshwar in Satara district, Maharashtra.
- **C — Mahanadi:** Originates near Sihawa in Dhamtari district, Chhattisgarh.
- **Exam Extra:** Amarkantak exhibits a classic radial drainage pattern, feeding the Narmada (westward), Son (northward), and Johila rivers.

### Q116 · Rivers, Dams, Lakes & Waterfalls

Which river is called the “Lifeline of Goa” and forms Dudhsagar Falls?

A. Mandovi
B. Zuari
C. Terekhol
D. Sal

**Answer:** A. Mandovi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Mandovi (Correct):** The Mandovi (called Mahadayi in Karnataka) is known as the “Lifeline of Goa,” passing Panaji and forming Dudhsagar Falls along the Goa–Karnataka border.
- **B — Zuari:** Goa’s longest tidal river entirely within the state, joining the Mandovi estuary near Mormugao.
- **C — Terekhol:** Flows along Goa’s northern boundary separating the state from Maharashtra.
- **D — Sal:** A coastal river flowing through Salcete (Margao) in southern Goa.
- **Exam Extra:** The Cumbarjua Canal connects the Mandovi and Zuari rivers, enabling inland iron-ore barge transport in Goa.

### Q117 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which soil is especially suitable for cotton cultivation in India?

A. Alluvial soil
B. Red soil
C. Black soil
D. Laterite soil

**Answer:** C. Black soil
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Black soil (Correct):** Black soil (Regur soil), derived from Deccan Trap basaltic lava, retains moisture well and forms deep cracks when dry for natural aeration, making it ideal for cotton cultivation.
- **A — Alluvial soil:** The most extensive and fertile soil of India’s northern plains, best suited for wheat, rice, and sugarcane.
- **B — Red soil:** Coloured by diffused iron oxide; well suited to millets, pulses, tobacco, and groundnuts.
- **D — Laterite soil:** Formed under intense leaching in high-rainfall tropical zones; suited for tea, coffee, and cashew.
- **Exam Extra:** Black soil is rich in calcium carbonate, magnesium, potash, and lime, though generally poor in phosphorus, nitrogen, and organic matter.

### Q118 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

What are the newly deposited, fertile floodplain alluvial soils of northern India called?

A. Bhangar
B. Khadar
C. Bhabar
D. Kallar

**Answer:** B. Khadar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Khadar (Correct):** Khadar refers to newer, fine-textured alluvial soil replenished annually by river floods along low-lying floodplains, making it more fertile than older alluvium.
- **A — Bhangar:** Older alluvium situated on higher river terraces above flood levels, containing calcareous nodules called kankar.
- **C — Bhabar:** Coarse, porous pebble-and-boulder deposits along the Shiwalik foothills where rivers sink underground.
- **D — Kallar:** Saline and alkaline infertile soil patches (also called Usar or Reh) found in arid or over-irrigated tracts.
- **Exam Extra:** Alluvial soils cover roughly 40% of India’s total geographical area, making them the largest soil group in the country.

### Q119 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which monsoon branch brings the first major monsoon rainfall to Kerala around early June?

A. Arabian Sea branch
B. Bay of Bengal branch
C. Retreating Monsoon
D. Western Disturbances

**Answer:** A. Arabian Sea branch
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Arabian Sea branch (Correct):** The Arabian Sea branch of the Southwest Monsoon strikes the Western Ghats along the Malabar Coast (Kerala) around 1 June, triggering the onset of India’s monsoon.
- **B — Bay of Bengal branch:** Advances across Northeast India and deflects westward up the Ganga plains after hitting the Himalayan foothills.
- **C — Retreating Monsoon:** The Northeast Monsoon (October–December), which brings primary winter rain to coastal Tamil Nadu.
- **D — Western Disturbances:** Extratropical cyclones from the Mediterranean region bringing vital winter rain to northwestern India.
- **Exam Extra:** Mawsynram in the East Khasi Hills of Meghalaya receives the highest average annual rainfall in the world (~11,872 mm).

### Q120 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which pre-monsoon showers in Kerala and coastal Karnataka assist in the early ripening of mangoes?

A. Kalbaisakhi
B. Loo
C. Blossom Showers
D. Mango Showers

**Answer:** D. Mango Showers
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Mango Showers (Correct):** Mango Showers are pre-monsoon convectional thunderstorms in April–May over Kerala and coastal Karnataka that aid the early ripening of mangoes.
- **A — Kalbaisakhi:** Violent evening Nor’wester thunderstorms in West Bengal and Assam during spring, beneficial for tea, jute, and rice.
- **B — Loo:** Hot, dry, gusty daytime winds blowing across northwestern and northern Indian plains during May–June.
- **C — Blossom Showers:** Pre-monsoon rains in Kerala and adjoining hills that stimulate coffee flower blossoming.
- **Exam Extra:** In Assam, violent spring thunderstorms akin to Bengal’s Kalbaisakhi are locally known as Bordoisila.

### Q121 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which of the following is a Rabi crop sown during winter in India?

A. Rice (Paddy)
B. Wheat
C. Cotton
D. Maize

**Answer:** B. Wheat
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Wheat (Correct):** Wheat is a premier Rabi crop sown in winter (October–December) and harvested in spring (April–June), thriving in cool growing seasons and bright ripening weather.
- **A — Rice (Paddy):** A major Kharif crop sown at the onset of the southwest monsoon in June–July.
- **C — Cotton:** A Kharif fibre crop requiring high temperatures and frost-free bright sunshine during boll maturation.
- **D — Maize:** Primarily a Kharif coarse cereal crop grown during the rainy monsoon months.
- **Exam Extra:** Winter showers from Western Disturbances significantly boost Rabi wheat yields in Punjab, Haryana, and western Uttar Pradesh.

### Q122 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which Indian state is the largest producer of coffee?

A. Kerala
B. Tamil Nadu
C. Karnataka
D. Assam

**Answer:** C. Karnataka
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Karnataka (Correct):** Karnataka accounts for around 70% of India’s total coffee output, concentrated in Kodagu (Coorg), Chikkamagaluru, and Hassan districts.
- **A — Kerala:** The second-largest coffee-producing state (~20%), dominated by Robusta varieties in Wayanad.
- **B — Tamil Nadu:** Ranks third in Indian coffee production, primarily in the Nilgiris and Palani hills.
- **D — Assam:** India’s largest producer of tea (over half of national tea production), not coffee.
- **Exam Extra:** Coffee cultivation in India originated in the Baba Budan Hills of Chikkamagaluru, Karnataka, in the 17th century.

### Q123 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which mineral is the Jaduguda mine in Jharkhand primarily famous for?

A. Uranium
B. Copper
C. Gold
D. Diamond

**Answer:** A. Uranium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Uranium (Correct):** The Jaduguda (Jadugora) mine in East Singhbhum district, Jharkhand, operated by Uranium Corporation of India Limited (UCIL), was India’s first uranium mine (started in 1967).
- **B — Copper:** Major Indian copper mines are located at Khetri (Rajasthan), Malanjkhand (Madhya Pradesh), and Ghatshila (Jharkhand).
- **C — Gold:** India’s principal gold mines include Hutti (Karnataka) and the historic Kolar Gold Fields.
- **D — Diamond:** India’s primary diamond-producing mine is at Panna (Majhgawan) in Madhya Pradesh.
- **Exam Extra:** Thorium, another key nuclear resource, occurs abundantly in the monazite sands along the Kerala coast.

### Q124 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which Indian state is the leading producer of bauxite, the principal ore of aluminium?

A. Jharkhand
B. Chhattisgarh
C. Gujarat
D. Odisha

**Answer:** D. Odisha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Odisha (Correct):** Odisha holds more than half of India’s bauxite reserves and production, led by deposits in Kalahandi, Koraput (Panchpatmali), and Rayagada.
- **A — Jharkhand:** Major producer of coal, iron ore, copper, and mica, with a much smaller share of bauxite than Odisha.
- **B — Chhattisgarh:** Holds notable bauxite deposits on the Maikal and Surguja plateaus, ranking well behind Odisha.
- **C — Gujarat:** A significant coastal bauxite producer (especially in Jamnagar and Devbhoomi Dwarka), ranking behind Odisha.
- **Exam Extra:** Bauxite deposits in India are mainly formed by the weathering of aluminium-rich rocks into lateritic cappings on plateaus.

### Q125 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which Indian state is the largest producer of natural rubber?

A. Tamil Nadu
B. Kerala
C. Karnataka
D. Tripura

**Answer:** B. Kerala
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Kerala (Correct):** Kerala accounts for roughly 70–75% of India’s natural rubber production, supported by its warm, humid climate and well-drained lateritic slopes.
- **A — Tamil Nadu:** Produces natural rubber mainly in Kanyakumari district, representing a small fraction of national output.
- **C — Karnataka:** Grows rubber in Dakshina Kannada and Kodagu, far behind Kerala’s output.
- **D — Tripura:** India’s second-largest natural rubber producer, leading cultivation in the Northeast.
- **Exam Extra:** The headquarters of the Rubber Board of India is situated in Kottayam, Kerala.

### Q126 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which variety of coal has the highest carbon content (80–95%) and burns with minimal smoke?

A. Peat
B. Lignite
C. Anthracite
D. Bituminous

**Answer:** C. Anthracite
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Anthracite (Correct):** Anthracite is the highest-rank, hardest metamorphic coal, containing 80–95% carbon and burning with high heat and minimal smoke.
- **A — Peat:** The earliest stage of coal formation, containing less than 40% carbon and high moisture content.
- **B — Lignite:** Low-grade brown coal (40–55% carbon); India’s largest deposits are mined at Neyveli in Tamil Nadu.
- **D — Bituminous:** Medium-to-high grade coal (60–80% carbon) and the most widely used commercial coal in India.
- **Exam Extra:** About 98% of India’s coal reserves belong to Gondwana bituminous deposits, while small anthracite reserves occur in Jammu & Kashmir (Kalakot).

### Q127 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

Which oilfield in Assam is the oldest continuously producing oilfield in India?

A. Digboi
B. Ankleshwar
C. Bombay High
D. Naharkatiya

**Answer:** A. Digboi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Digboi (Correct):** Digboi in Tinsukia district, Assam (discovered in 1889), is India’s oldest continuously producing oilfield and the site of Asia’s first oil refinery (1901).
- **B — Ankleshwar:** A major onshore oilfield in Bharuch district, Gujarat, discovered in 1958.
- **C — Bombay High:** Bombay High (Mumbai High): India’s largest offshore oilfield in the Arabian Sea, discovered in 1974.
- **D — Naharkatiya:** Another major upper-Assam oilfield southwest of Digboi developed in the 1950s.
- **Exam Extra:** Nearly 60% of India’s domestic crude oil historically comes from offshore fields led by Mumbai High.

### Q128 · Climate, Soils, Crops & Minerals

According to Köppen’s climate classification, most of the Ganga Plains experience which climate type?

A. Aw (Tropical savannah)
B. BWhw (Hot desert)
C. ET (Tundra)
D. Cwg (Monsoon with dry winter)

**Answer:** D. Cwg (Monsoon with dry winter)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cwg (Monsoon with dry winter) (Correct):** In Köppen’s classification, Cwg denotes a monsoon-type subtropical climate with dry winters and hot summers, covering most of the Ganga Plains.
- **A — Aw (Tropical savannah):** Covers most of Peninsular India south of the Tropic of Cancer.
- **B — BWhw (Hot desert):** Characterizes extreme western Rajasthan (the Thar Desert).
- **C — ET (Tundra):** Occurs in the high-altitude Himalayan zones of Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, and Uttarakhand.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s west coast south of Goa falls under Köppen’s Amw (tropical monsoon with short dry winter) climate type.

### Q129 · World Geography

Which strait separates Asia from North America?

A. Strait of Gibraltar
B. Bering Strait
C. Malacca Strait
D. Palk Strait

**Answer:** B. Bering Strait
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Bering Strait (Correct):** The Bering Strait (~85 km wide) separates Russia (Asia) from Alaska, USA (North America), connecting the Arctic Ocean to the Bering Sea.
- **A — Strait of Gibraltar:** Separates Europe (Spain) from Africa (Morocco), linking the Atlantic Ocean and the Mediterranean Sea.
- **C — Malacca Strait:** Separates the Malay Peninsula from Sumatra (Indonesia), connecting the Indian Ocean to the South China Sea.
- **D — Palk Strait:** Separates Tamil Nadu (India) from northern Sri Lanka.
- **Exam Extra:** The International Date Line passes between Big Diomede (Russia) and Little Diomede (USA) islands inside the Bering Strait.

### Q130 · World Geography

Which is the deepest known oceanic trench on Earth?

A. Puerto Rico Trench
B. Java Trench
C. Mariana Trench
D. Tonga Trench

**Answer:** C. Mariana Trench
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Mariana Trench (Correct):** The Challenger Deep in the Mariana Trench (western Pacific Ocean) reaches a depth of ~10,984–11,034 m, making it Earth’s deepest oceanic point.
- **A — Puerto Rico Trench:** The deepest trench in the Atlantic Ocean (~8,376 m at Milwaukee Deep).
- **B — Java Trench:** Also called the Sunda Trench; the deepest trench in the Indian Ocean (~7,290 m).
- **D — Tonga Trench:** The second-deepest oceanic trench (~10,882 m), located in the southwestern Pacific Ocean.
- **Exam Extra:** The deepest continental depression on dry land is the shoreline of the Dead Sea (~430 m below sea level).

### Q131 · World Geography

Which is the longest continental mountain range above sea level in the world?

A. Andes
B. Himalayas
C. Rockies
D. Alps

**Answer:** A. Andes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Andes (Correct):** Stretching roughly 7,000 km along South America’s western edge across seven countries, the Andes form the world’s longest continental mountain range.
- **B — Himalayas:** The highest mountain system in the world, extending ~2,400 km across South Asia.
- **C — Rockies:** Extend roughly 4,800 km across western North America from Canada to New Mexico.
- **D — Alps:** The highest and most extensive mountain range located wholly within Europe (~1,200 km).
- **Exam Extra:** Mount Aconcagua (6,961 m) in Argentina is the highest peak of the Andes and the tallest mountain outside Asia.

### Q132 · World Geography

Which is the largest hot desert in the world?

A. Gobi Desert
B. Kalahari Desert
C. Atacama Desert
D. Sahara Desert

**Answer:** D. Sahara Desert
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sahara Desert (Correct):** Covering roughly 9.2 million sq km across North Africa, the Sahara is the largest hot (subtropical) desert on Earth.
- **A — Gobi Desert:** A cold winter desert spanning northern China and southern Mongolia.
- **B — Kalahari Desert:** A large semi-arid sandy basin extending across Botswana, Namibia, and South Africa.
- **C — Atacama Desert:** Located along Chile’s Pacific coast; Earth’s driest non-polar desert.
- **Exam Extra:** Including polar deserts, Antarctica (~14 million sq km) is technically the world’s largest desert overall due to extremely low precipitation.

### Q133 · World Geography

Which international boundary line was demarcated in 1893 separating British India (now Pakistan/India) and Afghanistan?

A. Radcliffe Line
B. Durand Line
C. McMahon Line
D. Maginot Line

**Answer:** B. Durand Line
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Durand Line (Correct):** Demarcated in 1893 by Sir Mortimer Durand, the Durand Line forms the ~2,640-km boundary between Afghanistan and Pakistan (and India’s 106-km border with Afghanistan in Ladakh).
- **A — Radcliffe Line:** Drawn in 1947 by Sir Cyril Radcliffe to demarcate India and Pakistan (including erstwhile East Pakistan/Bangladesh).
- **C — McMahon Line:** Demarcated at the 1914 Shimla Convention as the boundary between Tibet/China and Northeast India.
- **D — Maginot Line:** A pre-World War II defensive fortification line built by France along its German border.
- **Exam Extra:** The 38th Parallel divides North Korea and South Korea, while the 49th Parallel separates the United States and Canada.

### Q134 · World Geography

Which artificial waterway connects the Mediterranean Sea with the Red Sea?

A. Suez Canal
B. Panama Canal
C. Kiel Canal
D. Corinth Canal

**Answer:** A. Suez Canal
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Suez Canal (Correct):** Opened in 1869 across Egypt’s Isthmus of Suez, the 193-km sea-level Suez Canal links the Mediterranean Sea to the Red Sea, shortening Europe–Asia maritime trade.
- **B — Panama Canal:** A lock-operated waterway across Panama linking the Atlantic Ocean (Caribbean Sea) and the Pacific Ocean.
- **C — Kiel Canal:** A 98-km canal across northern Germany connecting the North Sea to the Baltic Sea.
- **D — Corinth Canal:** A narrow canal in Greece cutting the Isthmus of Corinth between the Gulf of Corinth and the Saronic Gulf.
- **Exam Extra:** The Suez Canal requires no locks because the Mediterranean and Red Seas lie at virtually the same mean sea level.

### Q135 · World Geography

Which river crosses the Equator twice?

A. Nile River
B. Amazon River
C. Congo River
D. Zambezi River

**Answer:** C. Congo River
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Congo River (Correct):** The Congo (Zaire) River in Central Africa curves northward across the Equator and then bends southwestward, crossing the Equator twice before emptying into the Atlantic Ocean.
- **A — Nile River:** The longest river in Africa (~6,650 km), flowing northward into the Mediterranean Sea.
- **B — Amazon River:** The world’s largest river by water discharge, flowing eastward across South America without crossing the Equator twice.
- **D — Zambezi River:** Flows eastward across southern Africa into the Indian Ocean, famous for Victoria Falls.
- **Exam Extra:** Similarly, Africa’s Limpopo River crosses the Tropic of Capricorn twice, while India’s Mahi River crosses the Tropic of Cancer twice.

### Q136 · World Geography

Which is the only continent through which the Equator, the Tropic of Cancer, and the Tropic of Capricorn all pass?

A. South America
B. Asia
C. Australia
D. Africa

**Answer:** D. Africa
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Africa (Correct):** Spanning ~37° N to ~35° S latitude, Africa is the only continent crossed by all three major parallels: Tropic of Cancer, Equator, and Tropic of Capricorn.
- **A — South America:** Crossed by the Equator and the Tropic of Capricorn, but lies entirely south of the Tropic of Cancer.
- **B — Asia:** Crossed by the Tropic of Cancer and the Equator (through Indonesian islands), but not the Tropic of Capricorn.
- **C — Australia:** Crossed only by the Tropic of Capricorn.
- **Exam Extra:** Africa is also traversed by the Prime Meridian (0° longitude), which passes through Algeria, Mali, Burkina Faso, and Ghana.

### Q137 · World Geography

Which is the largest landlocked country in the world by area?

A. Mongolia
B. Kazakhstan
C. Bolivia
D. Chad

**Answer:** B. Kazakhstan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Kazakhstan (Correct):** Covering ~2.72 million sq km in Central Asia, Kazakhstan is the world’s largest landlocked country (bordering only the landlocked Caspian Sea).
- **A — Mongolia:** The second-largest landlocked country in the world (~1.56 million sq km), situated between Russia and China.
- **C — Bolivia:** A landlocked country in South America sharing Lake Titicaca with Peru.
- **D — Chad:** The largest landlocked country in Africa (~1.28 million sq km).
- **Exam Extra:** Only two countries in the world are doubly landlocked (surrounded solely by other landlocked countries): Liechtenstein and Uzbekistan.

### Q138 · World Geography

What are the temperate grasslands of Argentina and Uruguay in South America called?

A. Pampas
B. Prairies
C. Steppes
D. Velds

**Answer:** A. Pampas
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Pampas (Correct):** The Pampas are fertile temperate grasslands covering central-eastern Argentina and Uruguay, famous for wheat cultivation and cattle ranching.
- **B — Prairies:** Temperate grasslands of North America across the United States and Canada.
- **C — Steppes:** Temperate grasslands stretching across Eastern Europe and Central Asia.
- **D — Velds:** Temperate grasslands situated in South Africa.
- **Exam Extra:** Temperate grasslands are known as Downs in Australia and Canterbury Plains in New Zealand.

### Q139 · World Geography

Which is the largest island in the world (excluding continental landmasses)?

A. Madagascar
B. New Guinea
C. Greenland
D. Borneo

**Answer:** C. Greenland
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Greenland (Correct):** Greenland (~2.166 million sq km), an autonomous territory of Denmark between the North Atlantic and Arctic oceans, is the world’s largest non-continental island.
- **A — Madagascar:** The fourth-largest island in the world (~587,041 sq km), located off southeastern Africa.
- **B — New Guinea:** The second-largest island in the world (~785,753 sq km), shared by Indonesia and Papua New Guinea.
- **D — Borneo:** The third-largest island in the world (~748,168 sq km), divided among Indonesia, Malaysia, and Brunei.
- **Exam Extra:** Indonesia is the world’s largest island country by number of islands, comprising more than 17,000 islands.

### Q140 · World Geography

Which warm Atlantic ocean current helps keep Western European ports largely ice-free during winter?

A. Labrador Current
B. Humboldt (Peru) Current
C. Benguela Current
D. Gulf Stream

**Answer:** D. Gulf Stream
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Gulf Stream (Correct):** Originating in the Gulf of Mexico, the warm Gulf Stream (continuing as the North Atlantic Drift) carries tropical heat northeastward, keeping Western European ports largely ice-free in winter.
- **A — Labrador Current:** A cold Arctic current flowing south along eastern Canada, bringing icebergs and fog.
- **B — Humboldt (Peru) Current:** A cold, nutrient-rich Pacific current flowing north along western South America.
- **C — Benguela Current:** A cold Atlantic current flowing north along the southwestern coast of Africa.
- **Exam Extra:** The meeting of the warm Gulf Stream and the cold Labrador Current near Newfoundland’s Grand Banks fosters dense plankton and rich fisheries.

### Q141 · Census of India 2011

What was the official slogan of the Census of India 2011?

A. Our Census, Our Future
B. One Nation, One Count
C. Count Every Citizen
D. Census for Progress

**Answer:** A. Our Census, Our Future
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Our Census, Our Future (Correct):** The 15th National Census of India (2011), conducted under Registrar General C. Chandramouli, carried the official motto “Our Census, Our Future.”
- **B — One Nation, One Count:** A fabricated phrase never used as an official Census of India motto.
- **C — Count Every Citizen:** A generic distractor; not the official slogan of the 2011 Census.
- **D — Census for Progress:** Not an official motto used by the Office of the Registrar General of India.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s first synchronous decennial census was conducted in 1881 under W.C. Plowden, following the first non-synchronous count in 1872 under Lord Mayo.

### Q142 · Census of India 2011

According to Census 2011, what was India’s overall population density?

A. 325 persons/sq km
B. 382 persons/sq km
C. 412 persons/sq km
D. 445 persons/sq km

**Answer:** B. 382 persons/sq km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 382 persons/sq km (Correct):** India’s population density rose from 325 persons/sq km in 2001 to 382 persons/sq km in Census 2011.
- **A — 325 persons/sq km:** India’s national population density recorded in the 2001 Census.
- **C — 412 persons/sq km:** Overstates the 2011 national average by 30 persons/sq km.
- **D — 445 persons/sq km:** Significantly higher than India’s actual 2011 population density.
- **Exam Extra:** In Census 2011, Bihar had the highest state population density (1,106 persons/sq km), while Arunachal Pradesh had the lowest (17 persons/sq km).

### Q143 · Census of India 2011

Which Indian state recorded the highest literacy rate in Census 2011?

A. Mizoram
B. Goa
C. Kerala
D. Tripura

**Answer:** C. Kerala
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Kerala (Correct):** Kerala topped all Indian states and Union Territories in Census 2011 with an overall literacy rate of 94.00% (male 96.11%, female 92.07%).
- **A — Mizoram:** Ranked second among Indian states in literacy at 91.33% (just behind Lakshadweep among UTs at 91.85%).
- **B — Goa:** Recorded an 88.70% literacy rate in Census 2011, ranking fourth among states.
- **D — Tripura:** Recorded an 87.22% literacy rate in Census 2011.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s overall literacy rate in 2011 was 74.04% in provisional figures (72.99% final), with males at ~82.14% and females at ~65.46%.

### Q144 · Census of India 2011

According to Census 2011, which Indian state had the highest overall sex ratio (females per 1,000 males)?

A. Tamil Nadu
B. Andhra Pradesh
C. Chhattisgarh
D. Kerala

**Answer:** D. Kerala
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Kerala (Correct):** Kerala recorded the highest overall sex ratio in India at 1,084 females per 1,000 males in Census 2011.
- **A — Tamil Nadu:** Ranked second among Indian states with a sex ratio of 996 females per 1,000 males.
- **B — Andhra Pradesh:** Undivided Andhra Pradesh recorded 993 females per 1,000 males in 2011.
- **C — Chhattisgarh:** Recorded 991 females per 1,000 males, ranking among the top five states.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s overall sex ratio in 2011 stood at 943 females per 1,000 males, while Puducherry topped Union Territories at 1,037.

### Q145 · Census of India 2011

Which Indian state recorded the lowest overall sex ratio in Census 2011?

A. Haryana
B. Punjab
C. Rajasthan
D. Uttar Pradesh

**Answer:** A. Haryana
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Haryana (Correct):** Among all Indian states in Census 2011, Haryana recorded the lowest sex ratio at 879 females per 1,000 males.
- **B — Punjab:** Recorded a sex ratio of 895 females per 1,000 males in 2011.
- **C — Rajasthan:** Recorded a sex ratio of 928 females per 1,000 males in 2011.
- **D — Uttar Pradesh:** Recorded a sex ratio of 912 females per 1,000 males in 2011.
- **Exam Extra:** Among all States and Union Territories combined, Daman & Diu recorded the lowest sex ratio (618) due to heavy male migrant labour.

### Q146 · Census of India 2011

Which was the only Indian state to register a negative decadal population growth rate in Census 2011?

A. Sikkim
B. Nagaland
C. Goa
D. Manipur

**Answer:** B. Nagaland
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nagaland (Correct):** Nagaland was the only Indian state to record a negative decadal population growth rate (-0.58%) between 2001 and 2011, largely attributed to corrected enumeration overcounts from 2001.
- **A — Sikkim:** Recorded a positive decadal growth rate of 12.89%, though it remained India’s least populous state.
- **C — Goa:** Recorded a positive decadal growth rate of 8.23% during 2001–2011.
- **D — Manipur:** Recorded a positive decadal growth rate of 24.50% during 2001–2011.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s overall decadal population growth rate during 2001–2011 was 17.7%, while Meghalaya recorded the highest state growth rate (27.95%).

### Q147 · Census of India 2011

Which was the most populous state in India according to Census 2011?

A. Maharashtra
B. Bihar
C. Uttar Pradesh
D. West Bengal

**Answer:** C. Uttar Pradesh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Uttar Pradesh (Correct):** With 19.98 crore inhabitants (199,812,341), Uttar Pradesh was India’s most populous state in Census 2011, accounting for ~16.51% of the national population of 121.08 crore.
- **A — Maharashtra:** Ranked second in population (~11.24 crore, or 9.28% of India’s population).
- **B — Bihar:** Ranked third in population (~10.41 crore, or 8.60% of India’s population).
- **D — West Bengal:** Ranked fourth in population (~9.13 crore, or 7.54% of India’s population).
- **Exam Extra:** Sikkim was the least populous state (~6.10 lakh), while Lakshadweep was the least populous Union Territory (~64,473) in 2011.

### Q148 · Census of India 2011

Which Indian state recorded the highest population density (1,106 persons/sq km) in Census 2011?

A. West Bengal
B. Kerala
C. Uttar Pradesh
D. Bihar

**Answer:** D. Bihar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Bihar (Correct):** Bihar overtook West Bengal in Census 2011 to record the highest population density among Indian states at 1,106 persons per sq km.
- **A — West Bengal:** Ranked second among Indian states with a population density of 1,028 persons/sq km.
- **B — Kerala:** Ranked third among Indian states at 860 persons/sq km.
- **C — Uttar Pradesh:** Ranked fourth among major states at 829 persons/sq km.
- **Exam Extra:** Among Union Territories, the NCT of Delhi recorded the highest population density in India at 11,320 persons/sq km.

### Q149 · Census of India 2011

Which Indian state recorded the highest child sex ratio (0–6 years) in Census 2011?

A. Arunachal Pradesh
B. Kerala
C. Mizoram
D. Meghalaya

**Answer:** A. Arunachal Pradesh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Arunachal Pradesh (Correct):** Arunachal Pradesh recorded the highest child sex ratio (0–6 years) among all Indian states at 972 girls per 1,000 boys in Census 2011.
- **B — Kerala:** Despite topping the overall sex ratio (1,084), Kerala recorded a child sex ratio of 964.
- **C — Mizoram:** Recorded 970 girls per 1,000 boys in the 0–6 age group, just below Arunachal Pradesh.
- **D — Meghalaya:** Also recorded a child sex ratio of 970 girls per 1,000 boys in 2011.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s national child sex ratio (0–6 years) dropped to 919 in 2011 (from 927 in 2001), with Haryana lowest among states at 834.

### Q150 · Census of India 2011

Which Indian state had the highest percentage of urban population in Census 2011?

A. Maharashtra
B. Tamil Nadu
C. Goa
D. Gujarat

**Answer:** C. Goa
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Goa (Correct):** In Census 2011, Goa was India’s most urbanized state by percentage share, with 62.17% of its total population residing in urban areas.
- **A — Maharashtra:** Had the largest absolute urban population (~5.08 crore), though its urban percentage was 45.22%.
- **B — Tamil Nadu:** Ranked first among large states by urban percentage (48.40%), behind Goa and Mizoram overall.
- **D — Gujarat:** Recorded an urban population share of 42.60% in Census 2011.
- **Exam Extra:** India’s overall urbanization rate stood at 31.16% in 2011, while Himachal Pradesh was the least urbanized state at 10.03%.

---

## Chapter 3: Indian Polity & Constitution

### Q151 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Which British Act ended the East India Company's monopoly over trade with India, except for trade in tea and trade with China?

A. Regulating Act of 1773
B. Pitt's India Act of 1784
C. Charter Act of 1813
D. Charter Act of 1833

**Answer:** C. Charter Act of 1813
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Charter Act of 1813 (Correct):** The Charter Act of 1813 ended the East India Company's trade monopoly in India, throwing Indian trade open to all British merchants, while retaining the Company's monopoly only over trade in tea and trade with China.
- **A — Regulating Act of 1773:** Laid the foundation of central administration in India and designated the Governor of Bengal as the Governor-General of Bengal.
- **B — Pitt's India Act of 1784:** Distinguished between the commercial and political functions of the East India Company and created the Board of Control.
- **D — Charter Act of 1833:** Ended all commercial activities of the East India Company (including tea and China trade) and made it a purely administrative body.
- **Exam Extra:** The Charter Act of 1813 also allocated ₹1 lakh annually for the promotion of education among Indians and allowed Christian missionaries to enter India.

### Q152 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Which Act designated the Governor-General of Bengal as the Governor-General of India?

A. Regulating Act of 1773
B. Charter Act of 1833
C. Charter Act of 1853
D. Government of India Act 1858

**Answer:** B. Charter Act of 1833
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Charter Act of 1833 (Correct):** The Charter Act of 1833 elevated the Governor-General of Bengal to the Governor-General of India and vested in him all civil and military powers, making Lord William Bentinck the first Governor-General of India.
- **A — Regulating Act of 1773:** Created the office of the Governor-General of Bengal, with Warren Hastings as its first incumbent.
- **C — Charter Act of 1853:** Separated the legislative and executive functions of the Governor-General's Council and introduced open competition for civil services.
- **D — Government of India Act 1858:** Abolished the East India Company and replaced the Governor-General of India with the title of Viceroy of India (Lord Canning).
- **Exam Extra:** The Charter Act of 1833 also added a Law Member (Lord Macaulay) to the Governor-General's Council and began the centralization of legislative powers.

### Q153 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Which Act introduced dyarchy in the provinces and bicameralism at the Centre?

A. Indian Councils Act 1892
B. Indian Councils Act 1909
C. Government of India Act 1919
D. Government of India Act 1935

**Answer:** C. Government of India Act 1919
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Government of India Act 1919 (Correct):** The Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms) introduced dyarchy in the provinces (dividing provincial subjects into Reserved and Transferred) and replaced the Indian Legislative Council with a bicameral legislature at the Centre.
- **A — Indian Councils Act 1892:** Increased the number of non-official members in legislatures and allowed them to discuss the budget and ask questions.
- **B — Indian Councils Act 1909:** Known as the Morley-Minto Reforms; introduced a system of communal representation by providing separate electorates for Muslims.
- **D — Government of India Act 1935:** Abolished dyarchy in the provinces and introduced provincial autonomy in its place.
- **Exam Extra:** The bicameral legislature introduced by the 1919 Act consisted of the Council of State (Upper House) and the Legislative Assembly (Lower House).

### Q154 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Which Act introduced provincial autonomy and proposed an All-India Federation?

A. Indian Councils Act 1909
B. Government of India Act 1919
C. Indian Independence Act 1947
D. Government of India Act 1935

**Answer:** D. Government of India Act 1935
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Government of India Act 1935 (Correct):** The Government of India Act 1935 abolished provincial dyarchy, introduced provincial autonomy, and provided for the establishment of an All-India Federation consisting of provinces and princely states (though the federation never came into being).
- **A — Indian Councils Act 1909:** Introduced separate electorates for Muslims and allowed Satyendra Prasad Sinha to join the Viceroy's Executive Council as the first Indian Law Member.
- **B — Government of India Act 1919:** Created the office of the High Commissioner for India in London and provided for the establishment of a Public Service Commission.
- **C — Indian Independence Act 1947:** Partitioned British India into the two independent dominions of India and Pakistan and ended British rule on 15 August 1947.
- **Exam Extra:** The Government of India Act 1935 also provided for the establishment of a Federal Court (1937) and the Reserve Bank of India (1935), and served as the largest structural blueprint for India's Constitution.

### Q155 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Under which plan was the Constituent Assembly of India constituted in November 1946?

A. August Offer (1940)
B. Cabinet Mission Plan (1946)
C. Cripps Mission (1942)
D. Mountbatten Plan (1947)

**Answer:** B. Cabinet Mission Plan (1946)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Cabinet Mission Plan (1946) (Correct):** The Constituent Assembly of India was constituted in November 1946 under the scheme formulated by the Cabinet Mission Plan (1946), which had three British cabinet members: Lord Pethick-Lawrence, Sir Stafford Cripps, and A.V. Alexander.
- **A — August Offer (1940):** Proposed by Viceroy Linlithgow, promising Dominion status and a constituent assembly after World War II, which the Congress rejected.
- **C — Cripps Mission (1942):** Headed by Sir Stafford Cripps to secure Indian cooperation in World War II by offering Dominion status; Mahatma Gandhi called it a 'post-dated cheque'.
- **D — Mountbatten Plan (1947):** Proposed the partition of British India into India and Pakistan on 3 June 1947.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the Cabinet Mission Plan, the total strength of the Constituent Assembly was fixed at 389 seats (296 for British India and 93 for princely states), roughly one seat per million population.

### Q156 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Who served as the temporary President of the Constituent Assembly at its first meeting on 9 December 1946?

A. Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha
B. Dr. Rajendra Prasad
C. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar
D. H.C. Mukherjee

**Answer:** A. Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha (Correct):** At the first sitting of the Constituent Assembly on 9 December 1946, Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha, the oldest member, was elected as the temporary (interim) President following the French practice.
- **B — Dr. Rajendra Prasad:** Elected as the permanent President of the Constituent Assembly on 11 December 1946 and later became the first President of India.
- **C — Dr. B.R. Ambedkar:** Served as the Chairman of the seven-member Drafting Committee of the Constituent Assembly, appointed on 29 August 1947.
- **D — H.C. Mukherjee:** Served as one of the Vice-Presidents of the Constituent Assembly and later as the Governor of West Bengal.
- **Exam Extra:** Two days later, on 11 December 1946, Dr. Rajendra Prasad was elected the permanent President of the Constituent Assembly, with H.C. Mukherjee and V.T. Krishnamachari as Vice-Presidents.

### Q157 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Who moved the historic 'Objectives Resolution' in the Constituent Assembly on 13 December 1946?

A. Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
B. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar
C. Jawaharlal Nehru
D. Dr. Rajendra Prasad

**Answer:** C. Jawaharlal Nehru
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Jawaharlal Nehru (Correct):** Jawaharlal Nehru moved the historic 'Objectives Resolution' in the Constituent Assembly on 13 December 1946, laying down the fundamental philosophy and structure of the future Constitution.
- **A — Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel:** Chaired the Advisory Committee on Fundamental Rights, Minorities, and Tribal and Excluded Areas, as well as the Provincial Constitution Committee.
- **B — Dr. B.R. Ambedkar:** Chaired the Drafting Committee and piloted the Draft Constitution through the Constituent Assembly.
- **D — Dr. Rajendra Prasad:** Presided over the Constituent Assembly and chaired the Steering Committee and Rules of Procedure Committee.
- **Exam Extra:** The Objectives Resolution was unanimously adopted by the Constituent Assembly on 22 January 1947, and its modified version forms the present Preamble of the Constitution.

### Q158 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Who was appointed as the Constitutional Advisor (Legal Advisor) to the Constituent Assembly?

A. K.M. Munshi
B. Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar
C. N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar
D. B.N. Rau

**Answer:** D. B.N. Rau
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — B.N. Rau (Correct):** Sir B.N. Rau (Benegal Narsing Rau), a distinguished jurist and civil servant, served as the Constitutional Advisor (Legal Advisor) to the Constituent Assembly and prepared the initial draft of the Constitution.
- **A — K.M. Munshi:** A prominent member of the Drafting Committee and Chairman of the Order of Business Committee.
- **B — Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar:** A distinguished jurist and member of the Drafting Committee of the Constituent Assembly.
- **C — N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar:** A member of the Drafting Committee who played a key role in drafting provisions for Jammu and Kashmir and the Union-State relations.
- **Exam Extra:** B.N. Rau later served as a judge of the International Court of Justice at The Hague (1952–1953) and also helped draft the Constitution of Burma (Myanmar).

### Q159 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

Who chaired the Union Powers Committee and the Union Constitution Committee of the Constituent Assembly?

A. Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
B. Jawaharlal Nehru
C. Dr. Rajendra Prasad
D. J.B. Kripalani

**Answer:** B. Jawaharlal Nehru
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Jawaharlal Nehru (Correct):** Jawaharlal Nehru chaired both the Union Powers Committee and the Union Constitution Committee, as well as the States Committee (Committee for Negotiating with States).
- **A — Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel:** Chaired the Provincial Constitution Committee and the Advisory Committee on Fundamental Rights, Minorities, and Tribal Areas.
- **C — Dr. Rajendra Prasad:** Chaired the Rules of Procedure Committee and the Steering Committee.
- **D — J.B. Kripalani:** Chaired the Fundamental Rights Sub-Committee under the Advisory Committee.
- **Exam Extra:** Out of the eight major committees of the Constituent Assembly, Jawaharlal Nehru chaired three, while Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel and Dr. Rajendra Prasad chaired two each, and Dr. B.R. Ambedkar chaired one (Drafting Committee).

### Q160 · Historical Acts & Making of the Constitution

On which date was the Constitution of India formally adopted by the Constituent Assembly?

A. 15 August 1947
B. 24 January 1950
C. 26 November 1949
D. 26 January 1950

**Answer:** C. 26 November 1949
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 26 November 1949 (Correct):** The Constituent Assembly formally adopted, enacted, and gave to themselves the Constitution of India on 26 November 1949, which is celebrated annually as Constitution Day (Samvidhan Divas).
- **A — 15 August 1947:** Date on which India attained independence from British rule under the Indian Independence Act 1947.
- **B — 24 January 1950:** Final session of the Constituent Assembly, when members signed the Constitution and elected Dr. Rajendra Prasad as India's first President.
- **D — 26 January 1950:** Date of commencement of the Constitution of India, celebrated every year as Republic Day.
- **Exam Extra:** The Constitution took 2 years, 11 months, and 18 days to draft across 11 sessions, and came into full force on 26 January 1950 to commemorate Purna Swaraj Day (1930).

### Q161 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

From which country's Constitution did India borrow the concept of 'Procedure Established by Law'?

A. United States
B. United Kingdom
C. Japan
D. Ireland

**Answer:** C. Japan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Japan (Correct):** The principle of 'Procedure Established by Law' in Article 21 of the Indian Constitution was borrowed from the Constitution of Japan.
- **A — United States:** Source of Fundamental Rights, Judicial Review, independence of the judiciary, impeachment of the President, and the Vice-President's office.
- **B — United Kingdom:** Source of Parliamentary government, Rule of Law, single citizenship, bicameralism, and prerogative writs.
- **D — Ireland:** Source of the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP), nomination of members to the Rajya Sabha, and the method of Presidential election.
- **Exam Extra:** In the Maneka Gandhi case (1978), the Supreme Court expanded 'Procedure Established by Law' under Article 21 to incorporate the American concept of 'Due Process of Law', requiring any procedure to be fair, just, and reasonable.

### Q162 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

From which country's Constitution was the Concurrent List in the Seventh Schedule borrowed?

A. Canada
B. Australia
C. Germany
D. South Africa

**Answer:** B. Australia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Australia (Correct):** The Concurrent List in the Seventh Schedule, along with freedom of trade, commerce, and intercourse, and the provision for a joint sitting of the two Houses of Parliament, was borrowed from the Constitution of Australia.
- **A — Canada:** Source of the federation with a strong Centre, vesting of residuary powers in the Centre, and appointment of state Governors by the Centre.
- **C — Germany:** Source of the suspension of Fundamental Rights during an Emergency (borrowed from the Weimar Constitution of Germany).
- **D — South Africa:** Source of the procedure for amendment of the Constitution and the election of members of the Rajya Sabha.
- **Exam Extra:** Originally containing 47 subjects, the Concurrent List currently has 52 subjects, including education, forests, and weights and measures (moved from the State List by the 42nd Amendment).

### Q163 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which three words were inserted into the Preamble by the 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1976?

A. Socialist, Secular, and Integrity
B. Sovereign, Democratic, and Republic
C. Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity
D. Justice, Liberty, and Secular

**Answer:** A. Socialist, Secular, and Integrity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Socialist, Secular, and Integrity (Correct):** The 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1976 amended the Preamble for the first and only time, adding the three words 'Socialist', 'Secular', and 'Integrity'.
- **B — Sovereign, Democratic, and Republic:** Terms that were already present in the original Preamble adopted in 1949.
- **C — Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity:** Ideals present in the original Preamble, inspired by the French Revolution.
- **D — Justice, Liberty, and Secular:** 'Justice' and 'Liberty' were in the original Preamble, whereas only 'Secular' was added in 1976.
- **Exam Extra:** The ideals of Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity in the Preamble of the Indian Constitution were inspired by the French Revolution (1789), while Social, Economic, and Political Justice were drawn from the Russian Revolution (1917).

### Q164 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

In which landmark judgment did the Supreme Court rule that the Preamble is an integral part of the Constitution and subject to the Basic Structure doctrine?

A. Berubari Union case (1960)
B. Golaknath case (1967)
C. Kesavananda Bharati case (1973)
D. Minerva Mills case (1980)

**Answer:** C. Kesavananda Bharati case (1973)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Kesavananda Bharati case (1973) (Correct):** In the Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala case (1973), a 13-judge bench of the Supreme Court overruled the Berubari Union opinion and held that the Preamble is an integral part of the Constitution and can be amended without destroying its 'Basic Structure'.
- **A — Berubari Union case (1960):** The Supreme Court held that the Preamble is a key to the minds of the makers of the Constitution, but ruled that it was NOT a part of the Constitution.
- **B — Golaknath case (1967):** Ruled that Parliament could not amend Fundamental Rights under Article 368, treating constitutional amendments as 'law' under Article 13.
- **D — Minerva Mills case (1980):** Reaffirmed the Basic Structure doctrine and struck down clauses of the 42nd Amendment that gave DPSPs absolute primacy over Fundamental Rights and barred judicial review of amendments.
- **Exam Extra:** The 13-judge bench in the Kesavananda Bharati case (7:6 majority) remains the largest constitutional bench ever constituted in the history of the Supreme Court of India.

### Q165 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Schedule of the Indian Constitution deals with the allocation of seats in the Rajya Sabha to States and Union Territories?

A. Third Schedule
B. Fifth Schedule
C. Sixth Schedule
D. Fourth Schedule

**Answer:** D. Fourth Schedule
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Fourth Schedule (Correct):** The Fourth Schedule of the Indian Constitution specifies the allocation of seats in the Rajya Sabha (Council of States) to each State and Union Territory.
- **A — Third Schedule:** Contains the forms of oaths or affirmations for Union Ministers, MPs, Judges, CAG, State Ministers, and MLAs (excluding the President, Vice-President, and Governors).
- **B — Fifth Schedule:** Deals with the administration and control of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in states other than Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram.
- **C — Sixth Schedule:** Deals with the administration of Tribal Areas specifically in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike the US Senate, where every state has two seats, Rajya Sabha seats in India are allocated on the basis of population; Uttar Pradesh has the highest representation with 31 seats.

### Q166 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Schedule contains provisions for the administration of Tribal Areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram?

A. Fifth Schedule
B. Sixth Schedule
C. Seventh Schedule
D. Ninth Schedule

**Answer:** B. Sixth Schedule
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sixth Schedule (Correct):** The Sixth Schedule (under Articles 244(2) and 275(1)) provides special provisions for the administration of Tribal Areas in the four northeastern states of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram through Autonomous District Councils.
- **A — Fifth Schedule:** Governs Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes across 10 other states through Tribes Advisory Councils.
- **C — Seventh Schedule:** Divides legislative powers between the Union and the States into three lists—Union, State, and Concurrent.
- **D — Ninth Schedule:** Added by the 1st Amendment (1951) to protect land reform and other specified laws from judicial scrutiny.
- **Exam Extra:** Currently, there are 10 Autonomous District Councils under the Sixth Schedule across these four states (3 each in Assam, Meghalaya, and Mizoram, and 1 in Tripura).

### Q167 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Schedule of the Indian Constitution lists the 22 officially recognized languages?

A. Seventh Schedule
B. Tenth Schedule
C. Eighth Schedule
D. Eleventh Schedule

**Answer:** C. Eighth Schedule
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Eighth Schedule (Correct):** The Eighth Schedule of the Constitution lists the 22 officially recognized languages of India (originally 14 languages at the time of commencement in 1950).
- **A — Seventh Schedule:** Contains the Union List, State List, and Concurrent List distributing legislative powers.
- **B — Tenth Schedule:** Contains provisions regarding disqualification of legislators on the grounds of defection (Anti-Defection Law).
- **D — Eleventh Schedule:** Lists 29 functional items placed under the purview of Panchayati Raj Institutions.
- **Exam Extra:** Sindhi was added by the 21st Amendment (1967); Konkani, Manipuri, and Nepali by the 71st Amendment (1992); and Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, and Santhali by the 92nd Amendment (2003).

### Q168 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Schedule, added by the 52nd Constitutional Amendment Act (1985), contains provisions regarding disqualification on the grounds of defection?

A. Ninth Schedule
B. Tenth Schedule
C. Eleventh Schedule
D. Twelfth Schedule

**Answer:** B. Tenth Schedule
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Tenth Schedule (Correct):** The Tenth Schedule, popularly known as the Anti-Defection Law, was inserted into the Constitution by the 52nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1985 to curb political defections by MPs and MLAs.
- **A — Ninth Schedule:** Protects specified central and state laws (originally zamindari abolition laws) from being challenged for violating Fundamental Rights.
- **C — Eleventh Schedule:** Added by the 73rd Amendment Act (1992), specifying the powers, authority, and responsibilities of Panchayats.
- **D — Twelfth Schedule:** Added by the 74th Amendment Act (1992), specifying 18 functional items for Urban Local Bodies (Municipalities).
- **Exam Extra:** Under the 91st Amendment Act (2003), the exception for a one-third 'split' in a legislature party was deleted; only a 'merger' supported by at least two-thirds of the members is now protected from disqualification.

### Q169 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Part of the Indian Constitution deals with the Amendment of the Constitution?

A. Part XV
B. Part XVII
C. Part XVIII
D. Part XX

**Answer:** D. Part XX
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Part XX (Correct):** Part XX of the Indian Constitution consists of a single Article—Article 368—which outlines the power of Parliament to amend the Constitution and the procedure thereof.
- **A — Part XV:** Deals with Elections and the Election Commission of India (Articles 324 to 329A).
- **B — Part XVII:** Deals with Official Language of the Union, Regional Languages, and Language of the Courts (Articles 343 to 351).
- **C — Part XVIII:** Contains Emergency Provisions—National, State, and Financial Emergencies (Articles 352 to 360).
- **Exam Extra:** Article 368 provides two special amendment procedures (special majority of Parliament, and special majority plus ratification by half the states), while simple-majority amendments lie outside Article 368.

### Q170 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Part of the Indian Constitution deals with the Official Language?

A. Part XIV
B. Part XVII
C. Part XV
D. Part XVI

**Answer:** B. Part XVII
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Part XVII (Correct):** Part XVII of the Constitution (Articles 343 to 351) deals with Official Language, designating Hindi in Devanagari script as the official language of the Union under Article 343.
- **A — Part XIV:** Deals with Services under the Union and the States, including All-India Services and Public Service Commissions (Articles 308 to 323).
- **C — Part XV:** Deals with Elections and the Election Commission of India (Articles 324 to 329A).
- **D — Part XVI:** Contains Special Provisions relating to Certain Classes such as SCs, STs, and backward classes (Articles 330 to 342A).
- **Exam Extra:** Article 343(2) originally allowed English to continue for official Union purposes for 15 years (until 1965), which was later extended indefinitely by the Official Languages Act, 1963.

### Q171 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Part of the Indian Constitution contains provisions relating to Citizenship?

A. Part I
B. Part II
C. Part III
D. Part IV

**Answer:** B. Part II
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Part II (Correct):** Part II of the Indian Constitution (Articles 5 to 11) deals with Citizenship, specifying who became citizens of India at the commencement of the Constitution on 26 January 1950.
- **A — Part I:** Deals with the Union and its Territory, including admission and formation of new states (Articles 1 to 4).
- **C — Part III:** Contains Fundamental Rights guaranteed to citizens and persons (Articles 12 to 35).
- **D — Part IV:** Contains the Directive Principles of State Policy (Articles 36 to 51).
- **Exam Extra:** India follows the system of single citizenship (borrowed from the United Kingdom), meaning there is no separate state citizenship unlike in the United States.

### Q172 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Under the Citizenship Act, 1955, how many ways are prescribed for acquiring Indian citizenship?

A. Three
B. Four
C. Five
D. Six

**Answer:** C. Five
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Five (Correct):** The Citizenship Act, 1955 prescribes five modes of acquiring Indian citizenship: by birth, by descent, by registration, by naturalisation, and by incorporation of territory.
- **A — Three:** The number of modes prescribed under the Citizenship Act, 1955 for losing Indian citizenship, not acquiring it.
- **B — Four:** Incorrect count; naturalisation and incorporation of territory are distinct statutory modes alongside birth, descent, and registration.
- **D — Six:** Incorrect count; Overseas Citizen of India (OCI) registration is a status for foreign citizens of Indian origin, not a sixth mode of full citizenship.
- **Exam Extra:** The same Citizenship Act, 1955 prescribes three ways of losing Indian citizenship: renunciation, termination, and deprivation.

### Q173 · Preamble, Sources, Parts, Schedules & Citizenship

Which Article empowers Parliament to regulate the right of citizenship by law?

A. Article 5
B. Article 9
C. Article 10
D. Article 11

**Answer:** D. Article 11
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Article 11 (Correct):** Article 11 explicitly empowers Parliament to make any provision with respect to the acquisition and termination of citizenship and all other matters relating to citizenship.
- **A — Article 5:** Defines citizenship at the commencement of the Constitution on 26 January 1950 based on domicile.
- **B — Article 9:** States that a person who voluntarily acquires the citizenship of any foreign state ceases to be an Indian citizen.
- **C — Article 10:** Guarantees the continuance of citizenship rights subject to the provisions of any law made by Parliament.
- **Exam Extra:** Exercising its power under Article 11, Parliament enacted the Citizenship Act, 1955, which has been amended several times, including in 1986, 1992, 2003, 2005, 2015, and 2019.

### Q174 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Indian Constitution guarantees equality before the law?

A. Article 14
B. Article 15
C. Article 19
D. Article 21

**Answer:** A. Article 14
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 14 (Correct):** Article 14 states that the State shall not deny to any person equality before the law or the equal protection of the laws within the territory of India.
- **B — Article 15:** Prohibits discrimination by the State against any citizen on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth.
- **C — Article 19:** Guarantees six fundamental freedoms, including freedom of speech and expression, to Indian citizens.
