- **D — Article 21:** Protects life and personal liberty against arbitrary deprivation.
- **Exam Extra:** 'Equality before law' is a British concept (negative concept implying absence of special privileges), while 'Equal protection of the laws' is an American concept (positive concept implying equal treatment in equal circumstances).

### Q175 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article prohibits discrimination by the State on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth?

A. Article 14
B. Article 15
C. Article 16
D. Article 18

**Answer:** B. Article 15
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 15 (Correct):** Article 15 prohibits the State from discriminating against any citizen on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth, and ensures equal access to public places.
- **A — Article 14:** Guarantees equality before the law and equal protection of the laws to all persons.
- **C — Article 16:** Guarantees equality of opportunity in matters of public employment under the State.
- **D — Article 18:** Abolishes all titles except military and academic distinctions.
- **Exam Extra:** While Article 15 prohibits discrimination generally, clauses (3) to (6) allow the State to make special provisions for women, children, SCs, STs, SEBCs, and Economically Weaker Sections (added by the 103rd Amendment).

### Q176 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Indian Constitution abolishes untouchability?

A. Article 16
B. Article 18
C. Article 17
D. Article 23

**Answer:** C. Article 17
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 17 (Correct):** Article 17 abolishes 'untouchability' and forbids its practice in any form, making the enforcement of any disability arising out of untouchability an offence punishable in accordance with law.
- **A — Article 16:** Guarantees equality of opportunity for all citizens in matters relating to employment or appointment to any office under the State.
- **B — Article 18:** Abolishes titles and prohibits citizens from accepting titles from foreign states.
- **D — Article 23:** Prohibits traffic in human beings, begar (forced labour), and other similar forms of forced labour.
- **Exam Extra:** To enforce Article 17, Parliament enacted the Untouchability (Offences) Act, 1955, which was later amended and renamed the Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955 in 1976.

### Q177 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Constitution abolishes titles except military and academic distinctions?

A. Article 15
B. Article 17
C. Article 19
D. Article 18

**Answer:** D. Article 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Article 18 (Correct):** Article 18 abolishes titles and prohibits the State from conferring any title (not being a military or academic distinction) upon any person, whether a citizen or a foreigner.
- **A — Article 15:** Prohibits discrimination on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth.
- **B — Article 17:** Abolishes untouchability and forbids its practice in any form.
- **C — Article 19:** Protects six fundamental freedoms of speech, assembly, association, movement, residence, and profession.
- **Exam Extra:** In Balaji Raghavan v. Union of India (1996), the Supreme Court upheld the constitutional validity of National Awards like Bharat Ratna and Padma Awards, clarifying they are decorations and not 'titles' under Article 18.

### Q178 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article guarantees six fundamental freedoms, including freedom of speech and expression, to citizens of India?

A. Article 19
B. Article 20
C. Article 21
D. Article 22

**Answer:** A. Article 19
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 19 (Correct):** Article 19 guarantees six fundamental freedoms exclusively to citizens of India: speech and expression, peaceful assembly, forming associations/unions, free movement, residence/settlement, and profession/occupation.
- **B — Article 20:** Grants protection against arbitrary and excessive punishment to an accused person, including protection against double jeopardy and self-incrimination.
- **C — Article 21:** Guarantees protection of life and personal liberty to both citizens and foreigners.
- **D — Article 22:** Provides protection against arbitrary arrest and detention in certain cases.
- **Exam Extra:** Originally, Article 19 contained seven freedoms; the Right to acquire, hold, and dispose of property under Article 19(1)(f) was deleted by the 44th Amendment Act, 1978 and moved to Article 300A as a legal right.

### Q179 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Constitution protects life and personal liberty?

A. Article 19
B. Article 20
C. Article 21
D. Article 22

**Answer:** C. Article 21
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 21 (Correct):** Article 21 states that no person shall be deprived of his life or personal liberty except according to procedure established by law, applying equally to both citizens and non-citizens.
- **A — Article 19:** Protects six fundamental freedoms available only to citizens of India.
- **B — Article 20:** Protects persons against ex-post-facto laws, double jeopardy, and self-incrimination.
- **D — Article 22:** Grants rights to arrested persons (to be informed of grounds, consult a lawyer, and be produced before a magistrate within 24 hours) and regulates preventive detention.
- **Exam Extra:** Over decades, the Supreme Court has interpreted Article 21 expansively to include the right to privacy (Puttaswamy case, 2017), right to a clean environment, right to shelter, and right to marry a person of one's choice.

### Q180 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article guarantees the Right to Education as a Fundamental Right for children aged 6 to 14 years?

A. Article 21
B. Article 21A
C. Article 23
D. Article 24

**Answer:** B. Article 21A
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 21A (Correct):** Article 21A declares that the State shall provide free and compulsory education to all children of the age of six to fourteen years in such manner as the State may determine.
- **A — Article 21:** Protects life and personal liberty generally; prior to 2002, the Supreme Court had inferred the right to education under it in Unni Krishnan (1993).
- **C — Article 23:** Prohibits human trafficking and begar (forced labour).
- **D — Article 24:** Prohibits employment of children below 14 years of age in factories, mines, or other hazardous occupations.
- **Exam Extra:** Article 21A was inserted by the 86th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2002, and operationalized by Parliament through the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education (RTE) Act, 2009.

### Q181 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article prohibits the employment of children below 14 years of age in factories, mines, or hazardous work?

A. Article 21A
B. Article 23
C. Article 24
D. Article 25

**Answer:** C. Article 24
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 24 (Correct):** Article 24 prohibits the employment of any child below the age of fourteen years to work in any factory or mine or to be engaged in any other hazardous employment.
- **A — Article 21A:** Guarantees free and compulsory education to children aged 6 to 14 years.
- **B — Article 23:** Prohibits traffic in human beings and forced labour (begar) across all age groups.
- **D — Article 25:** Guarantees freedom of conscience and the right to freely profess, practice, and propagate religion.
- **Exam Extra:** Under the Child Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Amendment Act, 2016, employment of children below 14 is prohibited in all occupations (except helping in non-hazardous family enterprises after school hours).

### Q182 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article protects the right of any section of citizens to conserve their distinct language, script, or culture?

A. Article 25
B. Article 28
C. Article 29: No, wait — let's keep options clean!
D. Article 29

**Answer:** D. Article 29
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Article 29 (Correct):** Article 29 provides that any section of the citizens residing in India having a distinct language, script, or culture of its own shall have the right to conserve the same.
- **A — Article 25:** Protects freedom of conscience and free profession, practice, and propagation of religion.
- **B — Article 28:** Prohibits religious instruction in educational institutions wholly maintained out of State funds.
- **C — Article 29: No, wait — let's keep options clean!:** Article 30: Grants all religious and linguistic minorities the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice.
- **Exam Extra:** The Supreme Court has clarified that the scope of Article 29 is not necessarily restricted to religious or linguistic minorities alone, as it uses the phrase 'any section of the citizens'.

### Q183 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Constitution provides the right to constitutional remedies?

A. Article 32
B. Article 136
C. Article 226
D. Article 356

**Answer:** A. Article 32
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 32 (Correct):** Article 32 guarantees the Right to Constitutional Remedies, enabling citizens to move the Supreme Court directly for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights through five prerogative writs.
- **B — Article 136:** Grants the Supreme Court discretionary power to grant special leave to appeal from any judgment or order of any court or tribunal in India.
- **C — Article 226:** Empowers High Courts to issue writs not only for Fundamental Rights but also for any other legal right.
- **D — Article 356:** Authorizes the imposition of President's Rule in a State upon failure of constitutional machinery.
- **Exam Extra:** Dr. B.R. Ambedkar described Article 32 as the 'very soul of the Constitution and the very heart of it', and it itself is a Fundamental Right and part of the Basic Structure.

### Q184 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which writ is issued by a court commanding a public official to perform an official duty that they have failed or refused to perform?

A. Habeas Corpus
B. Mandamus
C. Quo Warranto
D. Certiorari

**Answer:** B. Mandamus
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Mandamus (Correct):** Mandamus (meaning 'we command') is a writ issued by a court to a public official, public body, corporation, inferior court, or government asking them to perform a public duty that they have failed or refused to perform.
- **A — Habeas Corpus:** Meaning 'to have the body of'; issued to examine the legality of a person's detention and secure release if detained unlawfully.
- **C — Quo Warranto:** Meaning 'by what authority'; issued to enquire into the legality of a person's claim to hold a public office.
- **D — Certiorari:** Meaning 'to be certified'; issued by a higher court to a lower court or tribunal to transfer a pending case or quash an order made without jurisdiction.
- **Exam Extra:** A writ of Mandamus cannot be issued against a private individual, the President of India, or State Governors in their official capacities.

### Q185 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which writ is issued by a court to enquire into the legality of a person's claim to a public office?

A. Prohibition
B. Certiorari
C. Mandamus
D. Quo Warranto

**Answer:** D. Quo Warranto
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Quo Warranto (Correct):** Quo Warranto (meaning 'by what authority or warrant') is issued by a court to enquire into the legality of a claim of a person to a public office, preventing illegal usurpation of public office.
- **A — Prohibition:** Issued by a higher court to a lower court or tribunal to prevent it from exceeding its jurisdiction or usurping a jurisdiction it does not possess.
- **B — Certiorari:** Issued to quash an order already passed by an inferior court or tribunal acting without jurisdiction or violating natural justice.
- **C — Mandamus:** Commands a public authority to perform a mandatory public duty that it has neglected.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike most other writs where only the aggrieved person can apply, a writ of Quo Warranto can be sought by any interested person, not necessarily the aggrieved party.

### Q186 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which pair of Fundamental Rights Articles cannot be suspended even during a National Emergency?

A. Articles 14 and 19
B. Articles 19 and 21
C. Articles 20 and 21
D. Articles 21 and 22

**Answer:** C. Articles 20 and 21
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Articles 20 and 21 (Correct):** Under Article 359 (as amended by the 44th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1978), the President cannot suspend the right to move any court for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights guaranteed by Articles 20 and 21 even during a National Emergency.
- **A — Articles 14 and 19:** Article 19 is suspended during external emergencies under Article 358, and Article 14 enforcement can be suspended by Presidential order under Article 359.
- **B — Articles 19 and 21:** Article 19 can be suspended during war or external aggression, unlike Article 21.
- **D — Articles 21 and 22:** Enforcement of Article 22 (protection against arrest and detention) can be suspended by Presidential order under Article 359.
- **Exam Extra:** While Article 19 is automatically suspended under Article 358 only when a National Emergency is declared on grounds of war or external aggression (not armed rebellion), Articles 20 and 21 remain protected in all emergencies.

### Q187 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

The Directive Principles of State Policy are contained in which Part of the Indian Constitution?

A. Part III
B. Part IVA
C. Part V
D. Part IV

**Answer:** D. Part IV
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Part IV (Correct):** The Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP) are contained in Part IV of the Indian Constitution, spanning Articles 36 to 51.
- **A — Part III:** Contains Fundamental Rights (Articles 12 to 35), which are justiciable in courts.
- **B — Part IVA:** Contains Fundamental Duties (Article 51A), added by the 42nd Amendment Act, 1976.
- **C — Part V:** Deals with the Union Government—Executive, Parliament, Judiciary, and CAG (Articles 52 to 151).
- **Exam Extra:** The DPSPs were borrowed from the Irish Constitution (1937) and are non-justiciable in nature under Article 37, though they are declared fundamental in the governance of the country.

### Q188 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article directs the State to take steps to organise village panchayats as units of self-government?

A. Article 39A
B. Article 40
C. Article 44
D. Article 50

**Answer:** B. Article 40
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 40 (Correct):** Article 40 directs the State to take steps to organise village panchayats and endow them with such powers and authority as may be necessary to enable them to function as units of self-government.
- **A — Article 39A:** Added by the 42nd Amendment Act, 1976; directs the State to promote equal justice and provide free legal aid to the poor.
- **C — Article 44:** Directs the State to endeavour to secure for citizens a Uniform Civil Code throughout India.
- **D — Article 50:** Directs the State to take steps to separate the judiciary from the executive in the public services of the State.
- **Exam Extra:** Article 40 reflects Gandhian principles within the Directive Principles and was given constitutional teeth decades later by the 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1992.

### Q189 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Article of the Directive Principles directs the State to secure a Uniform Civil Code throughout India?

A. Article 43
B. Article 44
C. Article 48
D. Article 51

**Answer:** B. Article 44
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 44 (Correct):** Article 44 of the Constitution directs that the State shall endeavour to secure for the citizens a Uniform Civil Code throughout the territory of India.
- **A — Article 43:** Directs the State to endeavour to secure a living wage, decent standard of life, and promote cottage industries.
- **C — Article 48:** Directs the State to organise agriculture and animal husbandry on modern lines and prohibit the slaughter of cows and calves.
- **D — Article 51:** Directs the State to promote international peace and security and maintain just and honourable relations between nations.
- **Exam Extra:** Goa is currently the only Indian state with a Uniform Civil Code (the Portuguese Civil Code of 1867), while Uttarakhand enacted its own Uniform Civil Code in 2024.

### Q190 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Fundamental Duties are listed in which Part of the Indian Constitution?

A. Part III
B. Part IV
C. Part IVA
D. Part V

**Answer:** C. Part IVA
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Part IVA (Correct):** Fundamental Duties are listed in Part IVA of the Indian Constitution under a single Article—Article 51A.
- **A — Part III:** Contains Fundamental Rights (Articles 12 to 35).
- **B — Part IV:** Contains the Directive Principles of State Policy (Articles 36 to 51).
- **D — Part V:** Deals with the Union Executive, Parliament, Supreme Court, and Comptroller and Auditor General.
- **Exam Extra:** Originally, 10 Fundamental Duties were listed under Article 51A in 1976; an 11th duty (parents/guardians providing education opportunities to children aged 6–14) was added in 2002 by the 86th Amendment.

### Q191 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

Which Constitutional Amendment Act added Fundamental Duties to the Indian Constitution?

A. 24th Amendment Act
B. 44th Amendment Act
C. 86th Amendment Act
D. 42nd Amendment Act

**Answer:** D. 42nd Amendment Act
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 42nd Amendment Act (Correct):** The 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1976 added Part IVA (Article 51A) containing 10 Fundamental Duties to the Constitution, inspired by the Constitution of the USSR.
- **A — 24th Amendment Act:** Affirmed Parliament's power to amend any part of the Constitution including Fundamental Rights, and made Presidential assent to constitutional amendment bills obligatory.
- **B — 44th Amendment Act:** Reversed several distortions of the 42nd Amendment, made the right to property a legal right (Article 300A), and strengthened emergency safeguards.
- **C — 86th Amendment Act:** Inserted Article 21A (Right to Education) and added the 11th Fundamental Duty under Article 51A(k).
- **Exam Extra:** The 42nd Amendment Act, 1976 made such sweeping changes to the Constitution that it is often referred to as the 'Mini-Constitution'.

### Q192 · Fundamental Rights, Writs, DPSPs & Fundamental Duties

On the recommendation of which committee were Fundamental Duties incorporated into the Indian Constitution?

A. Sarkaria Commission
B. Swaran Singh Committee
C. Verma Committee
D. Balwant Rai Mehta Committee

**Answer:** B. Swaran Singh Committee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Swaran Singh Committee (Correct):** Fundamental Duties were incorporated into the Constitution on the recommendation of the Sardar Swaran Singh Committee, set up by the government in 1976.
- **A — Sarkaria Commission:** Constituted in 1983 to examine Centre-State relations in India and submitted its report in 1988.
- **C — Verma Committee:** Constituted in 1999 to identify existing legal provisions for the operationalization and enforcement of Fundamental Duties.
- **D — Balwant Rai Mehta Committee:** Appointed in 1957 to examine the Community Development Programme and recommended a three-tier Panchayati Raj system.
- **Exam Extra:** Although the Swaran Singh Committee had recommended 8 Fundamental Duties and suggested imposing penalties for non-compliance, the 42nd Amendment included 10 duties without statutory punishments.

### Q193 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Who is the constitutional head of the Union executive in India?

A. The President of India
B. The Prime Minister of India
C. The Vice-President of India
D. The Chief Justice of India

**Answer:** A. The President of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — The President of India (Correct):** The President of India is the constitutional (nominal/de jure) head of the Union executive, in whom the executive power of the Union is vested under Article 53.
- **B — The Prime Minister of India:** Serves as the head of government and the real (de facto) executive authority of the Union.
- **C — The Vice-President of India:** Occupies the second-highest constitutional office and serves as the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha.
- **D — The Chief Justice of India:** Serves as the head of the Indian judiciary and the Supreme Court of India.
- **Exam Extra:** While the President is the constitutional head of state, the Prime Minister and the Council of Ministers exercise real (de facto) executive authority under Article 74.

### Q194 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Which Article specifies the procedure for the impeachment of the President of India?

A. Article 56
B. Article 60
C. Article 61
D. Article 72

**Answer:** C. Article 61
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 61 (Correct):** Article 61 lays down the quasi-judicial procedure for the impeachment of the President of India for 'violation of the Constitution', which can be initiated in either House of Parliament.
- **A — Article 56:** Specifies the five-year term of office of the President and grounds for resignation or removal.
- **B — Article 60:** Prescribes the oath or affirmation taken by the President before entering office.
- **D — Article 72:** Confers pardoning powers on the President of India.
- **Exam Extra:** To pass an impeachment resolution against the President, it must be approved by a special majority of two-thirds of the total membership of each House—higher than any other parliamentary majority.

### Q195 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Which Article empowers the President of India to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment?

A. Article 61
B. Article 72
C. Article 123
D. Article 161

**Answer:** B. Article 72
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 72 (Correct):** Article 72 empowers the President of India to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or to suspend, remit, or commute sentences in cases including court-martial and death sentences.
- **A — Article 61:** Deals with the procedure for impeachment of the President of India.
- **C — Article 123:** Empowers the President to promulgate ordinances during the recess of Parliament.
- **D — Article 161:** Grants pardoning powers to the Governor of a State for offences against state laws (excluding death sentences and court-martials).
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike a State Governor under Article 161, only the President of India has the authority to pardon a death sentence or sentences inflicted by a court-martial.

### Q196 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Who administers the oath of office to the President of India?

A. The Prime Minister of India
B. The Vice-President of India
C. The Chief Justice of India
D. The Speaker of the Lok Sabha

**Answer:** C. The Chief Justice of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The Chief Justice of India (Correct):** Under Article 60, the oath or affirmation of office to the President of India is administered by the Chief Justice of India, and in their absence, by the seniormost judge of the Supreme Court available.
- **A — The Prime Minister of India:** Advises the President on appointments and heads the Council of Ministers, but does not administer the President's oath.
- **B — The Vice-President of India:** Takes oath before the President and receives the President's resignation, but does not administer the President's oath.
- **D — The Speaker of the Lok Sabha:** Presides over the Lok Sabha and joint sittings of Parliament, but does not administer oaths to the President.
- **Exam Extra:** The President of India submits their letter of resignation to the Vice-President of India, who must immediately communicate it to the Speaker of the Lok Sabha.

### Q197 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Under which Article can the President of India promulgate an ordinance when Parliament is not in session?

A. Article 110
B. Article 112
C. Article 123
D. Article 213

**Answer:** C. Article 123
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 123 (Correct):** Article 123 empowers the President to promulgate ordinances when both Houses of Parliament (or either House) are not in session and immediate action is needed.
- **A — Article 110:** Defines what constitutes a 'Money Bill' in Parliament.
- **B — Article 112:** Mandates the laying of the Annual Financial Statement (Union Budget) before Parliament.
- **D — Article 213:** Empowers the Governor of a State to promulgate ordinances during the recess of the State Legislature.
- **Exam Extra:** An ordinance promulgated under Article 123 must be approved by Parliament within six weeks of its reassembly, making the maximum possible life of an ordinance 6 months and 6 weeks without parliamentary approval.

### Q198 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Who is the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha?

A. The President of India
B. The Vice-President of India
C. The Prime Minister of India
D. The Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha

**Answer:** B. The Vice-President of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — The Vice-President of India (Correct):** Under Articles 64 and 89(1), the Vice-President of India is the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha (Council of States) and presides over its proceedings.
- **A — The President of India:** Summons and prorogues Parliament and addresses both Houses, but does not preside over either House.
- **C — The Prime Minister of India:** Serves as the Leader of the House in whichever House of Parliament they belong to (usually Lok Sabha).
- **D — The Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha:** Elected by the Rajya Sabha from amongst its own members to preside in the absence of the Chairman.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike the Speaker of the Lok Sabha, the Vice-President is not a member of the Rajya Sabha, and draws salary in the capacity of Chairman of the Rajya Sabha rather than as Vice-President.

### Q199 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

What is the minimum age required to be eligible for election as the President or Vice-President of India?

A. 25 years
B. 30 years
C. 35 years
D. 40 years

**Answer:** C. 35 years
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 35 years (Correct):** Under Articles 58 and 66, a citizen of India must have completed the age of 35 years to be eligible for election as President or Vice-President of India.
- **A — 25 years:** Minimum age required to contest elections to the Lok Sabha and State Legislative Assemblies.
- **B — 30 years:** Minimum age required to become a member of the Rajya Sabha or a State Legislative Council.
- **D — 40 years:** Not a constitutional age threshold for any legislative or executive office in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The same minimum age requirement of 35 years applies to appointment as the Governor of a State under Article 157.

### Q200 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Which Article states that there shall be a Council of Ministers with the Prime Minister at the head to aid and advise the President?

A. Article 74
B. Article 75
C. Article 76
D. Article 78

**Answer:** A. Article 74
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 74 (Correct):** Article 74(1) provides that there shall be a Council of Ministers with the Prime Minister at the head to aid and advise the President, who shall act in accordance with such advice.
- **B — Article 75:** Deals with the appointment of the Prime Minister and other Ministers, their tenure, and collective responsibility to the Lok Sabha.
- **C — Article 76:** Provides for the office of the Attorney General for India.
- **D — Article 78:** Defines the duties of the Prime Minister with respect to furnishing information to the President.
- **Exam Extra:** The 42nd Amendment (1976) made ministerial advice binding on the President, while the 44th Amendment (1978) allowed the President to return the advice once for reconsideration.

### Q201 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

To whom is the Union Council of Ministers collectively responsible?

A. The President of India
B. The Rajya Sabha
C. The Lok Sabha
D. The Prime Minister

**Answer:** C. The Lok Sabha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The Lok Sabha (Correct):** Under Article 75(3), the Union Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha (House of the People), meaning the entire Ministry must resign if a no-confidence motion is passed in the Lok Sabha.
- **A — The President of India:** Ministers hold office individually during the pleasure of the President, but collective responsibility is to the Lok Sabha.
- **B — The Rajya Sabha:** Ministers can be members of the Rajya Sabha and answer questions there, but a no-confidence motion cannot be moved in the Rajya Sabha.
- **D — The Prime Minister:** Ministers work under the leadership of the Prime Minister, who is part of the Council of Ministers itself.
- **Exam Extra:** While Ministers are collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha under Article 75(3), they are individually responsible to the President under Article 75(2), holding office during the President's pleasure.

### Q202 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Who is the highest law officer of the Government of India, appointed under Article 76?

A. Solicitor General of India
B. Advocate General of India
C. Attorney General of India
D. Chief Justice of India

**Answer:** C. Attorney General of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Attorney General of India (Correct):** The Attorney General for India, appointed by the President under Article 76, is the highest law officer of the country and advises the Government of India on legal matters.
- **A — Solicitor General of India:** A statutory/subordinate law officer who assists the Attorney General; not a constitutional post under Article 76.
- **B — Advocate General of India:** Incorrect title; each State has an Advocate General (under Article 165) as the highest law officer of that State.
- **D — Chief Justice of India:** Heads the judicial branch (Supreme Court) and is not a law officer of the government.
- **Exam Extra:** Under Article 88, the Attorney General has the right to speak and take part in the proceedings of both Houses of Parliament and their committees, though without the right to vote.

### Q203 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Who appoints the Governor of an Indian State?

A. The Chief Minister of the state
B. The Chief Justice of the High Court
C. The Prime Minister of India
D. The President of India

**Answer:** D. The President of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The President of India (Correct):** Under Article 155, the Governor of a State is appointed by the President of India by warrant under his hand and seal, and holds office during the pleasure of the President (Article 156).
- **A — The Chief Minister of the state:** Appointed by the Governor; has no constitutional role in appointing the Governor, though consultation is a convention.
- **B — The Chief Justice of the High Court:** Administers the oath of office to the Governor under Article 159, but does not appoint the Governor.
- **C — The Prime Minister of India:** Advises the President on the appointment, while the formal constitutional appointment is made by the President.
- **Exam Extra:** By the 7th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1956, the same person can be appointed as Governor for two or more states simultaneously.

### Q204 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Which Article empowers the Governor of a State to grant pardons or commute sentences under state law?

A. Article 72
B. Article 153
C. Article 161
D. Article 213

**Answer:** C. Article 161
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 161 (Correct):** Article 161 empowers the Governor of a State to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or to suspend, remit, or commute sentences for offences against laws relating to matters within the State's executive power.
- **A — Article 72:** Confers pardoning powers upon the President of India, not the Governor.
- **B — Article 153:** Provides that there shall be a Governor for each State.
- **D — Article 213:** Empowers the Governor to promulgate ordinances when the State Legislature is not in session.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike the President under Article 72, a Governor cannot pardon a death sentence (though they can suspend, remit, or commute it) and has no jurisdiction over court-martial sentences.

### Q205 · Union & State Executive (President, VP, PM, Governor)

Under the 91st Constitutional Amendment Act (2003), the Union Council of Ministers (including the Prime Minister) cannot exceed what percentage of the total strength of the Lok Sabha?

A. 10 percent
B. 12 percent
C. 15 percent
D. 20 percent

**Answer:** C. 15 percent
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 15 percent (Correct):** Inserted by the 91st Constitutional Amendment Act, 2003, Article 75(1A) mandates that the total number of Ministers, including the Prime Minister, in the Union Council of Ministers shall not exceed 15% of the total strength of the Lok Sabha.
- **A — 10 percent:** Incorrect percentage; sometimes confused with the 10% quorum required for parliamentary sittings under Article 100(3).
- **B — 12 percent:** Incorrect percentage; 12 is the minimum number of ministers prescribed for a State Council of Ministers, not a percentage cap.
- **D — 20 percent:** Incorrect percentage; exceeds the constitutional limit established by the 91st Amendment.
- **Exam Extra:** The 91st Amendment also imposed the same 15% cap on State Councils of Ministers relative to the Legislative Assembly strength, with a minimum floor of 12 ministers (including the Chief Minister) for smaller states.

### Q206 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Who presides over a joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament?

A. The President of India
B. The Vice-President of India
C. The Speaker of the Lok Sabha
D. The Prime Minister of India

**Answer:** C. The Speaker of the Lok Sabha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — The Speaker of the Lok Sabha (Correct):** Under Article 118(4), the Speaker of the Lok Sabha presides over a joint sitting of the two Houses of Parliament; in their absence, the Deputy Speaker of the Lok Sabha presides, followed by the Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha.
- **A — The President of India:** Summons the joint sitting of both Houses under Article 108, but never presides over it.
- **B — The Vice-President of India:** Serves as Chairman of the Rajya Sabha and never presides over a joint sitting of Parliament.
- **D — The Prime Minister of India:** Heads the government and participates as an MP, without presiding over parliamentary sessions.
- **Exam Extra:** A joint sitting is summoned by the President under Article 108 and has been held only three times in Indian history: Dowry Prohibition Bill (1961), Banking Service Commission Repeal Bill (1978), and POTA Bill (2002).

### Q207 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Which Article provides for a joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament in case of a deadlock over an ordinary bill?

A. Article 105
B. Article 108
C. Article 110
D. Article 112

**Answer:** B. Article 108
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 108 (Correct):** Article 108 provides for a joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament to resolve a deadlock over an ordinary bill or a financial bill (other than a Money Bill or Constitutional Amendment Bill).
- **A — Article 105:** Deals with the powers, privileges, and immunities of Parliament and its members.
- **C — Article 110:** Defines what constitutes a 'Money Bill'.
- **D — Article 112:** Mandates the presentation of the Annual Financial Statement (Union Budget).
- **Exam Extra:** A joint sitting cannot be convened for a Money Bill (Lok Sabha has overriding authority) or a Constitutional Amendment Bill (each House must pass it separately by special majority).

### Q208 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

A Money Bill can be introduced only in which House of Parliament?

A. Rajya Sabha
B. Lok Sabha
C. Either House of Parliament
D. Joint sitting of both Houses

**Answer:** B. Lok Sabha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Lok Sabha (Correct):** Under Article 109(1), a Money Bill shall not be introduced in the Council of States (Rajya Sabha); it can be introduced only in the Lok Sabha, and only with the prior recommendation of the President.
- **A — Rajya Sabha:** Has no power to initiate, amend, or reject a Money Bill; it can only make recommendations within 14 days.
- **C — Either House of Parliament:** Applies to Ordinary Bills and Constitutional Amendment Bills, not Money Bills.
- **D — Joint sitting of both Houses:** Not permitted for Money Bills; a joint sitting is used only to resolve deadlocks on ordinary or certain financial bills.
- **Exam Extra:** Because a Money Bill requires the President's prior recommendation before introduction in the Lok Sabha, the President cannot return a Money Bill to Parliament for reconsideration.

### Q209 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Who has the final authority to certify whether a bill is a Money Bill or not?

A. The President of India
B. The Union Finance Minister
C. The Chairman of the Rajya Sabha
D. The Speaker of the Lok Sabha

**Answer:** D. The Speaker of the Lok Sabha
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The Speaker of the Lok Sabha (Correct):** Under Article 110(3), if any question arises whether a Bill is a Money Bill or not, the decision of the Speaker of the Lok Sabha thereon shall be final.
- **A — The President of India:** Grants prior recommendation to introduce a Money Bill and gives assent after passage, but does not certify whether it is a Money Bill.
- **B — The Union Finance Minister:** Typically introduces Money Bills in the Lok Sabha, but has no authority to certify their constitutional status.
- **C — The Chairman of the Rajya Sabha:** Presides over the Upper House and receives the certified Money Bill, without power to alter its classification.
- **Exam Extra:** The Speaker endorses a certificate on every Money Bill before transmitting it to the Rajya Sabha and before presenting it to the President for assent.

### Q210 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

For a maximum of how many days can the Rajya Sabha delay a Money Bill passed by the Lok Sabha?

A. 14 days
B. 30 days
C. 45 days
D. 60 days

**Answer:** A. 14 days
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 14 days (Correct):** Under Article 109(2) and (5), the Rajya Sabha must return a Money Bill to the Lok Sabha with or without recommendations within 14 days from the date of its receipt; otherwise, it is deemed to have been passed by both Houses.
- **B — 30 days:** Not a constitutional deadline for Money Bills; used in certain statutory or committee rules.
- **C — 45 days:** Incorrect; exceeds the 14-day constitutional limit for the Rajya Sabha on Money Bills.
- **D — 60 days:** The period of continuous absence from a House without permission after which an MP's seat may be declared vacant under Article 101(4).
- **Exam Extra:** The Lok Sabha is not bound to accept any of the recommendations made by the Rajya Sabha on a Money Bill and may accept or reject them all.

### Q211 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Which Article of the Constitution refers to the Annual Financial Statement, commonly known as the Union Budget?

A. Article 110
B. Article 112
C. Article 114
D. Article 280

**Answer:** B. Article 112
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 112 (Correct):** Article 112 uses the term 'Annual Financial Statement' for the document commonly known as the Union Budget, laying out estimated receipts and expenditure of the Government of India for each financial year.
- **A — Article 110:** Defines Money Bills relating to taxation, borrowing, and the Consolidated Fund of India.
- **C — Article 114:** Deals with Appropriation Bills, authorizing withdrawal of money from the Consolidated Fund of India.
- **D — Article 280:** Provides for the constitution of the Finance Commission every five years.
- **Exam Extra:** The word 'Budget' is nowhere mentioned in the Indian Constitution; the financial year runs from 1 April to 31 March.

### Q212 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

What is the quorum required to constitute a sitting of either House of Parliament?

A. One-fourth of total membership
B. One-fifth of total membership
C. One-tenth of total membership
D. One-third of total membership

**Answer:** C. One-tenth of total membership
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — One-tenth of total membership (Correct):** Under Article 100(3), the quorum to constitute a meeting of either House of Parliament is one-tenth of the total number of members of the House (including the presiding officer).
- **A — One-fourth of total membership:** Incorrect fraction; not prescribed for parliamentary quorum.
- **B — One-fifth of total membership:** Incorrect fraction; neither House uses a 20% quorum.
- **D — One-third of total membership:** Incorrect fraction; one-third of Rajya Sabha members retire every two years, which is unrelated to quorum.
- **Exam Extra:** With a total strength of 543 in the Lok Sabha and 245 in the Rajya Sabha, the quorum is 55 members for the Lok Sabha and 25 members for the Rajya Sabha.

### Q213 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

What is the maximum permissible gap between two sessions of Parliament?

A. Three months
B. Four months
C. Six months
D. Nine months

**Answer:** C. Six months
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Six months (Correct):** Article 85(1) mandates that six months shall not intervene between the last sitting of Parliament in one session and the date appointed for its first sitting in the next session.
- **A — Three months:** Shorter than the constitutional limit; the typical gap between the Monsoon and Winter sessions is around three months by convention.
- **B — Four months:** Not a constitutional maximum gap for parliamentary sessions.
- **D — Nine months:** Exceeds the six-month ceiling fixed by Article 85(1).
- **Exam Extra:** Because the gap between two sessions cannot exceed six months, Parliament must meet at least twice a year; by convention, it holds three sessions: Budget, Monsoon, and Winter.

### Q214 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Under which Article is the Contingency Fund of India established at the disposal of the President?

A. Article 265
B. Article 266
C. Article 267
D. Article 280

**Answer:** C. Article 267
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 267 (Correct):** Article 267 authorizes Parliament to establish the Contingency Fund of India by law, placing it at the disposal of the President to meet unforeseen expenditure pending parliamentary authorization.
- **A — Article 265:** Declares that no tax shall be levied or collected except by authority of law.
- **B — Article 266:** Establishes the Consolidated Fund of India and the Public Account of India (and of the States).
- **D — Article 280:** Provides for the constitution of the Finance Commission to recommend tax devolution.
- **Exam Extra:** The Contingency Fund of India Act, 1950 established the fund, which is held by the Finance Secretary on behalf of the President and currently has a corpus of ₹30,000 crore.

### Q215 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Which parliamentary committee consists of 22 members (15 from Lok Sabha and 7 from Rajya Sabha) and examines the CAG audit reports?

A. Estimates Committee
B. Public Accounts Committee
C. Committee on Public Undertakings
D. Business Advisory Committee

**Answer:** B. Public Accounts Committee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Public Accounts Committee (Correct):** The Public Accounts Committee (PAC) consists of 22 members (15 from the Lok Sabha and 7 from the Rajya Sabha) and examines the annual audit reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.
- **A — Estimates Committee:** Consists of 30 members exclusively from the Lok Sabha and examines budget estimates to suggest economies in public expenditure.
- **C — Committee on Public Undertakings:** Consists of 22 members (15 LS + 7 RS) and examines reports and accounts of Public Sector Undertakings.
- **D — Business Advisory Committee:** Regulates the timetable and allocation of time for legislative and other business of the House.
- **Exam Extra:** Set up in 1921 under the Government of India Act 1919, the PAC has followed the convention since 1967 of having a member from the opposition as its Chairman.

### Q216 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Which is the largest parliamentary committee, consisting of 30 members drawn exclusively from the Lok Sabha?

A. Public Accounts Committee
B. Estimates Committee
C. Committee on Public Undertakings
D. Rules Committee

**Answer:** B. Estimates Committee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Estimates Committee (Correct):** The Estimates Committee is the largest parliamentary committee, comprising 30 members elected exclusively from the Lok Sabha, with no representation from the Rajya Sabha.
- **A — Public Accounts Committee:** Consists of 22 members drawn from both Houses (15 from Lok Sabha and 7 from Rajya Sabha).
- **C — Committee on Public Undertakings:** Consists of 22 members drawn from both Houses (15 from Lok Sabha and 7 from Rajya Sabha).
- **D — Rules Committee:** Consists of 15 members in the Lok Sabha (16 in Rajya Sabha), chaired ex-officio by the Speaker or Chairman respectively.
- **Exam Extra:** The Estimates Committee is also called the 'Continuous Economy Committee' because it examines how money is laid out in budget estimates and suggests administrative improvements.

### Q217 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

What is the minimum age required to become a member of the Rajya Sabha?

A. 21 years
B. 25 years
C. 30 years
D. 35 years

**Answer:** C. 30 years
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 30 years (Correct):** Under Article 84(b), a person must be not less than 30 years of age to be qualified to fill a seat in the Rajya Sabha (Council of States).
- **A — 21 years:** Minimum age required to contest Panchayat and Municipal elections under Parts IX and IXA.
- **B — 25 years:** Minimum age required to become a member of the Lok Sabha or a State Legislative Assembly.
- **D — 35 years:** Minimum age required for election as President or Vice-President, or appointment as Governor.
- **Exam Extra:** The Rajya Sabha is a permanent body not subject to dissolution, and one-third of its members retire every second year after completing a six-year term.

### Q218 · Parliament, Bills, Funds & Committees

Who was the first Speaker of the Lok Sabha?

A. M. Ananthasayanam Ayyangar
B. G.V. Mavalankar
C. Hukam Singh
D. Neelam Sanjiva Reddy

**Answer:** B. G.V. Mavalankar
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — G.V. Mavalankar (Correct):** Ganesh Vasudev (G.V.) Mavalankar served as the first Speaker of the Lok Sabha from 1952 until his death in 1956, having earlier served as Speaker of the Central Legislative Assembly and Provisional Parliament.
- **A — M. Ananthasayanam Ayyangar:** Served as the first Deputy Speaker of the Lok Sabha and succeeded G.V. Mavalankar as the second Speaker in 1956.
- **C — Hukam Singh:** Served as the third Speaker of the Lok Sabha (1962–1967).
- **D — Neelam Sanjiva Reddy:** Served twice as Speaker of the Lok Sabha before being elected the sixth President of India unopposed in 1977.
- **Exam Extra:** G.V. Mavalankar was affectionately called 'Dadasaheb' and was hailed by Jawaharlal Nehru as the 'Father of the Lok Sabha'.

### Q219 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

Which Article provides for the establishment and constitution of the Supreme Court of India?

A. Article 124
B. Article 129
C. Article 137
D. Article 143

**Answer:** A. Article 124
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 124 (Correct):** Article 124 provides for the establishment and constitution of the Supreme Court of India, including the appointment, qualifications, and removal procedure of its judges.
- **B — Article 129:** Declares the Supreme Court to be a 'Court of Record' with the power to punish for contempt of itself.
- **C — Article 137:** Empowers the Supreme Court to review any judgment pronounced or order made by it.
- **D — Article 143:** Authorizes the President to seek the advisory opinion of the Supreme Court on questions of law or fact.
- **Exam Extra:** The Supreme Court of India was inaugurated on 28 January 1950, succeeding the Federal Court of India established under the Government of India Act 1935.

### Q220 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

At what age does a Judge of the Supreme Court of India retire?

A. 60 years
B. 62 years
C. 65 years
D. 68 years

**Answer:** C. 65 years
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 65 years (Correct):** Under Article 124(2), a Judge of the Supreme Court of India holds office until attaining the age of 65 years.
- **A — 60 years:** Original retirement age of High Court judges prior to the 15th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1963.
- **B — 62 years:** Current retirement age of High Court judges under Article 217.
- **D — 68 years:** Incorrect age; not the constitutional retirement age for any court in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The Constitution does not fix a minimum age for appointment as a Supreme Court or High Court judge, nor does it prescribe a fixed tenure in years.

### Q221 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

Under which Article can the President seek the advisory opinion of the Supreme Court on a question of law or fact of public importance?

A. Article 131
B. Article 136
C. Article 141
D. Article 143

**Answer:** D. Article 143
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Article 143 (Correct):** Article 143 confers advisory jurisdiction on the Supreme Court, allowing the President to refer any question of law or fact of public importance (or pre-constitutional treaty disputes) for its opinion.
- **A — Article 131:** Grants original and exclusive jurisdiction to the Supreme Court in federal disputes between the Centre and States or between States.
- **B — Article 136:** Empowers the Supreme Court to grant Special Leave Petitions (SLP) to appeal against orders of any court or tribunal (except military tribunals).
- **C — Article 141:** Declares that the law declared by the Supreme Court shall be binding on all courts within India.
- **Exam Extra:** The advisory opinion tendered by the Supreme Court under Article 143 is not binding on the President, nor is the Court bound to answer in every case (except pre-constitutional treaty/agreement disputes under Article 143(2)).

### Q222 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

Which Article empowers a High Court to issue writs for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights as well as for any other legal purpose?

A. Article 32
B. Article 214
C. Article 226
D. Article 227

**Answer:** C. Article 226
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 226 (Correct):** Article 226 empowers every High Court to issue writs (Habeas Corpus, Mandamus, Prohibition, Quo Warranto, Certiorari) for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights as well as 'for any other purpose' (ordinary legal rights).
- **A — Article 32:** Grants writ jurisdiction specifically to the Supreme Court, limited to the enforcement of Fundamental Rights.
- **B — Article 214:** Mandates that there shall be a High Court for each State.
- **D — Article 227:** Grants every High Court the power of superintendence over all courts and tribunals throughout its territorial jurisdiction.
- **Exam Extra:** Because High Courts can issue writs for both Fundamental Rights and ordinary legal rights, the writ jurisdiction of a High Court under Article 226 is wider than that of the Supreme Court under Article 32.

### Q223 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

At what age does a Judge of a High Court in India retire?

A. 60 years
B. 62 years
C. 65 years
D. 67 years

**Answer:** B. 62 years
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 62 years (Correct):** Under Article 217(1), a Judge of a High Court holds office until attaining the age of 62 years.
- **A — 60 years:** The original retirement age of High Court judges before it was raised in 1963.
- **C — 65 years:** The constitutional retirement age of Judges of the Supreme Court of India.
- **D — 67 years:** Not the retirement age for either High Court or Supreme Court judges in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The retirement age of High Court judges was raised from 60 years to 62 years by the 15th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1963.

### Q224 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

Which Article declares the Supreme Court to be a 'Court of Record' with the power to punish for contempt of itself?

A. Article 124
B. Article 129
C. Article 131
D. Article 141

**Answer:** B. Article 129
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 129 (Correct):** Article 129 declares that the Supreme Court shall be a 'Court of Record' and shall have all the powers of such a court, including the power to punish for contempt of itself.
- **A — Article 124:** Deals with the establishment, composition, and appointment of judges of the Supreme Court.
- **C — Article 131:** Defines the original jurisdiction of the Supreme Court in Centre-State and inter-State disputes.
- **D — Article 141:** Establishes that law declared by the Supreme Court is binding on all courts in India.
- **Exam Extra:** Similarly, Article 215 declares every High Court to be a 'Court of Record' with the power to punish for contempt of itself.

### Q225 · Judiciary (Supreme Court & High Courts)

Who was the first woman Judge of the Supreme Court of India?

A. Justice Leila Seth
B. Justice Anna Chandy
C. Justice M. Fathima Beevi
D. Justice Ruma Pal

**Answer:** C. Justice M. Fathima Beevi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Justice M. Fathima Beevi (Correct):** Justice M. Fathima Beevi became the first woman Judge of the Supreme Court of India when she was appointed in October 1989.
- **A — Justice Leila Seth:** First woman Chief Justice of a High Court in India (Himachal Pradesh High Court, 1991) and first woman judge of the Delhi High Court.
- **B — Justice Anna Chandy:** First female judge in India (1937) and the first woman to become a High Court judge in India (Kerala High Court, 1959).
- **D — Justice Ruma Pal:** Served as a Judge of the Supreme Court (2000–2006) and was the longest-serving woman Supreme Court judge before Justice Indu Malhotra/Indira Banerjee era.
- **Exam Extra:** Justice Fathima Beevi was also the first Muslim woman to be appointed to the higher judiciary in India and later served as the Governor of Tamil Nadu.

### Q226 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which body conducts elections to Parliament and State Legislatures in India?

A. Union Public Service Commission
B. Election Commission of India
C. Finance Commission of India
D. Delimitation Commission of India

**Answer:** B. Election Commission of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Election Commission of India (Correct):** The Election Commission of India, established under Article 324, is an autonomous constitutional body responsible for conducting elections to Parliament, State Legislatures, and the offices of President and Vice-President.
- **A — Union Public Service Commission:** Constitutional body under Article 315 that conducts examinations for recruitment to All-India Services and Central Civil Services.
- **C — Finance Commission of India:** Constitutional body under Article 280 that recommends distribution of tax revenues between the Union and the States.
- **D — Delimitation Commission of India:** Statutory body appointed to redraw boundaries of parliamentary and assembly constituencies based on census data.
- **Exam Extra:** Elections to Panchayats and Municipalities are conducted by separate State Election Commissions (under Articles 243K and 243ZA), not by the Election Commission of India.

### Q227 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which body audits the accounts of the Union and State Governments in India?

A. Finance Commission
B. NITI Aayog
C. Comptroller and Auditor General of India
D. Public Accounts Committee

**Answer:** C. Comptroller and Auditor General of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Comptroller and Auditor General of India (Correct):** The Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) of India, appointed under Article 148, audits all receipts and expenditures of the Union Government and State Governments as the guardian of the public purse.
- **A — Finance Commission:** Recommends financial transfers and grants-in-aid between the Centre and States; it does not audit government accounts.
- **B — NITI Aayog:** Public policy think tank of the Government of India that replaced the Planning Commission in 2015.
- **D — Public Accounts Committee:** A parliamentary committee that scrutinizes the audit reports already prepared and submitted by the CAG.
- **Exam Extra:** Under Article 151, the CAG submits audit reports relating to Union accounts to the President and those relating to State accounts to the respective Governor, to be laid before the legislatures.

### Q228 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Under which Article is the Finance Commission constituted by the President every five years?

A. Article 280
B. Article 315
C. Article 324
D. Article 338

**Answer:** A. Article 280
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 280 (Correct):** Article 280 mandates that the President of India constitute a Finance Commission every five years (or earlier) to recommend the distribution of net tax proceeds between the Union and the States and grants-in-aid to States.
- **B — Article 315:** Provides for the establishment of the Union Public Service Commission and State Public Service Commissions.
- **C — Article 324:** Establishes the Election Commission of India for superintendence, direction, and control of elections.
- **D — Article 338:** Establishes the National Commission for Scheduled Castes.
- **Exam Extra:** The First Finance Commission (1951) was chaired by K.C. Neogy, while the Sixteenth Finance Commission (constituted in December 2023) is chaired by Dr. Arvind Panagariya.

### Q229 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which Article provides for the establishment of the Union Public Service Commission and State Public Service Commissions?

A. Article 280
B. Article 312
C. Article 315
D. Article 324

**Answer:** C. Article 315
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 315 (Correct):** Article 315 provides for a Public Service Commission for the Union (UPSC) and a Public Service Commission for each State (SPSC), as well as Joint State Public Service Commissions if requested by two or more states.
- **A — Article 280:** Deals with the constitution and functions of the Finance Commission.
- **B — Article 312:** Empowers the Rajya Sabha to authorize Parliament by a two-thirds resolution to create new All-India Services.
- **D — Article 324:** Vests the superintendence, direction, and control of elections in the Election Commission of India.
- **Exam Extra:** While UPSC members are appointed and removed by the President, SPSC members are appointed by the Governor of the State but can only be removed by the President.

### Q230 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Under which Article can the President proclaim a National Emergency on grounds of war, external aggression, or armed rebellion?

A. Article 352
B. Article 356
C. Article 360
D. Article 365

**Answer:** A. Article 352
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Article 352 (Correct):** Article 352 empowers the President to proclaim a National Emergency if the security of India or any part of it is threatened by war, external aggression, or armed rebellion.
- **B — Article 356:** Authorizes the imposition of President's Rule in a State when constitutional machinery fails.
- **C — Article 360:** Empowers the President to declare a Financial Emergency when financial stability or credit is threatened.
- **D — Article 365:** States that failure of a State to comply with Union directions is a valid ground for invoking President's Rule under Article 356.
- **Exam Extra:** National Emergency has been proclaimed three times in India (1962, 1971, and 1975); the 44th Amendment (1978) replaced the term 'internal disturbance' with 'armed rebellion'.

### Q231 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which Article deals with President's Rule due to the failure of constitutional machinery in a State?

A. Article 352
B. Article 356
C. Article 358
D. Article 360

**Answer:** B. Article 356
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Article 356 (Correct):** Article 356 empowers the President to impose President's Rule (State Emergency) in a State if satisfied, on receipt of a Governor's report or otherwise, that the State government cannot be carried on in accordance with the Constitution.
- **A — Article 352:** Deals with National Emergency due to war, external aggression, or armed rebellion.
- **C — Article 358:** Provides for the automatic suspension of the six freedoms under Article 19 during a National Emergency declared on grounds of war or external aggression.
- **D — Article 360:** Deals with Financial Emergency.
- **Exam Extra:** A proclamation of President's Rule must be approved by both Houses of Parliament within two months and can remain in force for six months at a time, up to a maximum of three years.

### Q232 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which Article provides for Financial Emergency, which has never been imposed in India?

A. Article 352
B. Article 356
C. Article 359
D. Article 360

**Answer:** D. Article 360
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Article 360 (Correct):** Article 360 empowers the President to proclaim a Financial Emergency if the financial stability or credit of India or any part of its territory is threatened; it has never been imposed in India.
- **A — Article 352:** Deals with National Emergency, which has been proclaimed three times (1962, 1971, 1975).
- **B — Article 356:** Deals with President's Rule in States, which has been invoked over 100 times across various states.
- **C — Article 359:** Authorizes the President to suspend the right to move courts for enforcement of specified Fundamental Rights (except Articles 20 and 21) during a National Emergency.
- **Exam Extra:** During a Financial Emergency, the executive authority of the Union extends to directing States to observe canons of financial propriety, including reducing salaries of all Union and State officials and judges.

### Q233 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which state was the first to inaugurate the Panchayati Raj system in India on 2 October 1959?

A. Andhra Pradesh
B. Rajasthan
C. Gujarat
D. Maharashtra

**Answer:** B. Rajasthan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Rajasthan (Correct):** Rajasthan became the first state to establish Panchayati Raj in independent India when Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru inaugurated it in Nagaur district on 2 October 1959.
- **A — Andhra Pradesh:** Was the second state in India to implement Panchayati Raj, launching it nine days after Rajasthan.
- **C — Gujarat:** Adopted Panchayati Raj in 1963 following the recommendations of the Rasiklal Parikh Committee.
- **D — Maharashtra:** Adopted Panchayati Raj in 1962 based on the Vasantrao Naik Committee recommendations.
- **Exam Extra:** Andhra Pradesh became the second state to launch the Panchayati Raj system shortly thereafter on 11 October 1959.

### Q234 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which Constitutional Amendment Act gave constitutional status to Panchayati Raj Institutions by adding Part IX and the Eleventh Schedule?

A. 42nd Amendment Act
B. 73rd Amendment Act
C. 74th Amendment Act
D. 86th Amendment Act

**Answer:** B. 73rd Amendment Act
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 73rd Amendment Act (Correct):** The 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1992 granted constitutional status to Panchayati Raj Institutions by inserting Part IX (Articles 243 to 243O) and the Eleventh Schedule.
- **A — 42nd Amendment Act:** Added Fundamental Duties, modified the Preamble, and introduced tribunals, without establishing constitutional status for Panchayats.
- **C — 74th Amendment Act:** Gave constitutional status to Urban Local Bodies (Municipalities) by adding Part IXA and the Twelfth Schedule.
- **D — 86th Amendment Act:** Made elementary education a Fundamental Right under Article 21A.
- **Exam Extra:** The 73rd Amendment Act came into force on 24 April 1993, which is celebrated annually across India as National Panchayati Raj Day.

### Q235 · Constitutional Bodies, Emergency, Panchayati Raj & Amendments

Which Constitutional Amendment Act lowered the voting age from 21 years to 18 years for Lok Sabha and State Assembly elections?

A. 44th Amendment Act
B. 52nd Amendment Act
C. 61st Amendment Act
D. 69th Amendment Act

**Answer:** C. 61st Amendment Act
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 61st Amendment Act (Correct):** The 61st Constitutional Amendment Act, 1988 (effective 28 March 1989) amended Article 326 to lower the voting age for Lok Sabha and State Legislative Assembly elections from 21 years to 18 years.
- **A — 44th Amendment Act:** Replaced 'internal disturbance' with 'armed rebellion' in Article 352 and shifted the Right to Property to Article 300A.
- **B — 52nd Amendment Act:** Enacted the Anti-Defection Law by adding the Tenth Schedule in 1985.
- **D — 69th Amendment Act:** Accorded special status to Delhi as the National Capital Territory (NCT) and created its Legislative Assembly and Council of Ministers in 1991.
- **Exam Extra:** Universal adult suffrage was guaranteed by the original Constitution under Article 326, initially setting the minimum voting age at 21 years before the 1989 revision.
