## Chapter 4: Economics & Banking

### Q236 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

Which type of good exhibits a positive relationship between price and quantity demanded, violating the law of demand?

A. Normal good
B. Inferior good
C. Giffen good
D. Complementary good

**Answer:** C. Giffen good
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Giffen good (Correct):** A Giffen good is a rare type of strongly inferior good whose demand rises when its price increases and falls when its price decreases, creating an upward-sloping demand curve that violates the law of demand.
- **A — Normal good:** Demand increases as consumer income rises and decreases when its price rises, obeying the law of demand.
- **B — Inferior good:** Demand decreases when consumer income increases, though it still generally obeys the law of demand unless the income effect outweighs the substitution effect.
- **D — Complementary good:** A good consumed jointly with another good (such as petrol and cars), so a price rise in one lowers demand for the other.
- **Exam Extra:** Named after Scottish economist Sir Robert Giffen; the classic example cited by Alfred Marshall was bread among low-income nineteenth-century workers.

### Q237 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

When the quantity demanded of a commodity does not change at all despite a change in its price, what is its price elasticity of demand called?

A. Perfectly inelastic demand
B. Perfectly elastic demand
C. Unitary elastic demand
D. Relatively elastic demand

**Answer:** A. Perfectly inelastic demand
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Perfectly inelastic demand (Correct):** Perfectly inelastic demand (Ed = 0) occurs when the quantity demanded remains completely unchanged regardless of any change in price, represented graphically by a vertical demand curve.
- **B — Perfectly elastic demand:** Even an infinitesimally small price rise causes quantity demanded to drop to zero (Ed = infinity), shown as a horizontal line.
- **C — Unitary elastic demand:** The percentage change in quantity demanded equals the percentage change in price (Ed = 1), keeping total expenditure constant.
- **D — Relatively elastic demand:** The percentage change in quantity demanded exceeds the percentage change in price (Ed > 1), common for luxury goods.
- **Exam Extra:** Essential life-saving medicines such as insulin closely approximate perfectly inelastic demand in the short run.

### Q238 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

What is the economic term for the value of the next best alternative foregone when making a decision?

A. Marginal cost
B. Sunk cost
C. Fixed cost
D. Opportunity cost

**Answer:** D. Opportunity cost
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Opportunity cost (Correct):** Opportunity cost is the value of the next best alternative foregone when a scarce resource is committed to a particular choice.
- **A — Marginal cost:** The additional cost incurred in producing one extra unit of output.
- **B — Sunk cost:** A historical expenditure that has already been incurred and cannot be recovered, so it should not affect future decisions.
- **C — Fixed cost:** Expenditure on inputs such as factory rent or machinery that does not vary with the level of output in the short run.
- **Exam Extra:** Opportunity cost is zero only for free goods (like abundant sunlight or air) that have no alternative uses or scarcity.

### Q239 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

According to Engel's Law, as a family's income increases, the proportion of income spent on which item decreases?

A. Education
B. Food
C. Healthcare
D. Recreation

**Answer:** B. Food
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Food (Correct):** Engel's Law, formulated by German statistician Ernst Engel in 1857, states that as a household's income increases, the percentage share of income spent on food decreases, even though absolute spending on food may rise.
- **A — Education:** As household income rises, the share of expenditure devoted to education typically increases rather than decreases.
- **C — Healthcare:** Spending on medical care and wellness generally rises in proportion as households move into higher income brackets.
- **D — Recreation:** Leisure and entertainment are income-elastic items whose budget share tends to expand with rising income.
- **Exam Extra:** The income elasticity of demand for food is generally positive but less than 1 (between 0 and 1).

### Q240 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

Which graphical curve is used to measure the degree of income or wealth inequality in an economy?

A. Phillips curve
B. Laffer curve
C. Lorenz curve
D. Kuznets curve

**Answer:** C. Lorenz curve
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Lorenz curve (Correct):** The Lorenz curve, developed by Max O. Lorenz in 1905, plots the cumulative percentage of total income or wealth against the cumulative percentage of population to visually represent inequality.
- **A — Phillips curve:** Shows the short-run inverse relationship between the rate of inflation and the rate of unemployment.
- **B — Laffer curve:** Illustrates the relationship between statutory tax rates and total government tax revenue.
- **D — Kuznets curve:** Depicts an inverted-U relationship between per capita economic development and income inequality.
- **Exam Extra:** The Gini coefficient is derived from the Lorenz curve as the ratio of the area between the line of equality and the Lorenz curve to the total area under the line of equality.

### Q241 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

The Laffer curve illustrates the relationship between which two economic variables?

A. Tax rates and tax revenue
B. Inflation rate and unemployment rate
C. Per capita income and inequality
D. Money supply and interest rates

**Answer:** A. Tax rates and tax revenue
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Tax rates and tax revenue (Correct):** The Laffer curve, popularized by supply-side economist Arthur Laffer, demonstrates that tax revenue is zero at both 0% and 100% tax rates and peaks at an intermediate optimal rate.
- **B — Inflation rate and unemployment rate:** This relationship is represented by the Phillips curve, not the Laffer curve.
- **C — Per capita income and inequality:** This relationship is represented by the Kuznets curve.
- **D — Money supply and interest rates:** This relationship is represented by liquidity preference / LM analysis rather than the Laffer curve.
- **Exam Extra:** Raising tax rates beyond the revenue-maximizing point on the Laffer curve can reduce total tax collection by discouraging work and encouraging tax evasion.

### Q242 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

What does the short-run Phillips curve depict an inverse relationship between?

A. Interest rate and investment
B. Inflation rate and unemployment rate
C. Government expenditure and GDP
D. Exports and exchange rate

**Answer:** B. Inflation rate and unemployment rate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Inflation rate and unemployment rate (Correct):** Proposed by A.W. Phillips in 1958, the short-run Phillips curve shows a trade-off (inverse relationship) between the rate of inflation and the rate of unemployment.
- **A — Interest rate and investment:** Investment generally varies inversely with the real interest rate along the Marginal Efficiency of Capital schedule.
- **C — Government expenditure and GDP:** Higher government spending shifts aggregate demand outward, raising equilibrium output through the fiscal multiplier.
- **D — Exports and exchange rate:** Currency depreciation generally boosts exports and discourages imports under the Marshall-Lerner condition.
- **Exam Extra:** In the long run, Monetarists like Milton Friedman showed that the Phillips curve is vertical at the Natural Rate of Unemployment (NAIRU).

### Q243 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

Which market structure is characterized by a few large firms with mutual interdependence and high barriers to entry?

A. Perfect competition
B. Monopolistic competition
C. Monopsony
D. Oligopoly

**Answer:** D. Oligopoly
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Oligopoly (Correct):** Oligopoly is a market structure dominated by a few large firms whose pricing and output decisions are mutually interdependent, often involving non-price competition and barriers to entry.
- **A — Perfect competition:** Features a very large number of buyers and sellers trading homogeneous products with free entry and exit and no price-setting power.
- **B — Monopolistic competition:** Consists of many firms selling differentiated products with easy entry and some control over price.
- **C — Monopsony:** A market structure characterized by a single dominant buyer facing many sellers, often seen in specialized labour markets.
- **Exam Extra:** Paul Sweezy's kinked demand curve model explains price rigidity in oligopolistic markets.

### Q244 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

Agriculture, forestry, fishing, and mining belong to which sector of the economy?

A. Primary sector
B. Secondary sector
C. Tertiary sector
D. Quaternary sector

**Answer:** A. Primary sector
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Primary sector (Correct):** The primary sector encompasses economic activities directly concerned with extracting or harvesting natural resources, including agriculture, forestry, fishing, and mining.
- **B — Secondary sector:** Converts raw materials from the primary sector into finished or semi-finished goods through manufacturing, construction, and utilities.
- **C — Tertiary sector:** Provides intangible services such as banking, transport, trade, healthcare, and education.
- **D — Quaternary sector:** A knowledge-based sub-sector involving information technology, R&D, consultancy, and data analysis.
- **Exam Extra:** In India, the primary sector employs the largest share of the workforce, whereas the tertiary (services) sector contributes the largest share of Gross Value Added (GVA).

### Q245 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

According to the Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility, what is the value of marginal utility when total utility reaches its maximum?

A. Positive
B. Maximum
C. Zero
D. Negative

**Answer:** C. Zero
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Zero (Correct):** Total utility rises as long as marginal utility is positive; at the exact point of satiety where total utility reaches its maximum, marginal utility becomes zero.
- **A — Positive:** While marginal utility remains positive, total utility is still increasing rather than at its maximum.
- **B — Maximum:** Marginal utility peaks at the point of inflection before diminishing returns set in, not where total utility is maximized.
- **D — Negative:** Negative marginal utility causes total utility to fall below its maximum level.
- **Exam Extra:** If consumption continues past the point of satiety, marginal utility turns negative and total utility starts to decline.

### Q246 · Microeconomics, Curves & Sectors

According to the Kuznets curve hypothesis, how does income inequality behave as a country's per capita income rises?

A. Continuously decreases throughout development
B. First increases and then decreases
C. Continuously increases throughout development
D. Remains constant across all stages

**Answer:** B. First increases and then decreases
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — First increases and then decreases (Correct):** Simon Kuznets' inverted-U hypothesis states that as an economy develops from agrarian to industrial, income inequality initially rises and later declines once industrialization matures and benefits spread.
- **A — Continuously decreases throughout development:** This contradicts the early industrialization phase where urban-rural wage gaps widen.
- **C — Continuously increases throughout development:** Kuznets argued that social welfare policies and wider education eventually compress inequality at high income levels.
- **D — Remains constant across all stages:** Structural transformation shifts labour across sectors of differing productivity, changing inequality over time.
- **Exam Extra:** A similar inverted-U pattern called the Environmental Kuznets Curve (EKC) links per capita income with environmental degradation.

### Q247 · National Income & Inflation

What does inflation mean in economics?

A. A persistent fall in the general price level
B. A sustained rise in the general price level
C. A temporary slowdown in the rate of price rise
D. A simultaneous rise in inflation and unemployment

**Answer:** B. A sustained rise in the general price level
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — A sustained rise in the general price level (Correct):** Inflation refers to a persistent, sustained rise in the general price level of goods and services over time, resulting in a fall in the purchasing power of money.
- **A — A persistent fall in the general price level:** This defines deflation, where prices drop continuously and the purchasing power of money rises.
- **C — A temporary slowdown in the rate of price rise:** This defines disinflation, where prices still increase but at a slower pace.
- **D — A simultaneous rise in inflation and unemployment:** This defines stagflation, combining stagnant growth with rising prices.
- **Exam Extra:** In India, the Reserve Bank of India targets Consumer Price Index (CPI-Combined) inflation at 4% with a tolerance band of +/- 2% (2% to 6%).

### Q248 · National Income & Inflation

Which formula correctly converts Gross Domestic Product (GDP) into Gross National Product (GNP)?

A. GDP minus Depreciation
B. GDP minus Indirect Taxes plus Subsidies
C. GDP plus Net Factor Income from Abroad
D. GDP plus Net Exports

**Answer:** C. GDP plus Net Factor Income from Abroad
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — GDP plus Net Factor Income from Abroad (Correct):** Gross National Product (GNP) measures the total value of goods and services produced by a country's residents, obtained by adding Net Factor Income from Abroad (NFIA) to GDP.
- **A — GDP minus Depreciation:** Subtracting depreciation (consumption of fixed capital) from GDP yields Net Domestic Product (NDP).
- **B — GDP minus Indirect Taxes plus Subsidies:** Adjusting GDP at market price by subtracting net indirect taxes yields GDP at factor cost.
- **D — GDP plus Net Exports:** Net exports (X - M) are already a component of GDP under the expenditure method rather than a conversion to GNP.
- **Exam Extra:** NFIA equals factor income earned by residents abroad minus factor income earned by non-residents within the domestic territory.

### Q249 · National Income & Inflation

Which macroeconomic aggregate is traditionally defined as National Income?

A. Gross Domestic Product at Market Price
B. Gross National Product at Market Price
C. Net Domestic Product at Market Price
D. Net National Product at Factor Cost

**Answer:** D. Net National Product at Factor Cost
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Net National Product at Factor Cost (Correct):** Net National Product at Factor Cost (NNP at FC) represents the net factor income earned by normal residents of a country after deducting depreciation and net indirect taxes, and is conventionally termed National Income.
- **A — Gross Domestic Product at Market Price:** Measures total domestic production including depreciation and net indirect taxes, without adjusting for NFIA.
- **B — Gross National Product at Market Price:** Includes depreciation and net indirect taxes, overstating true factor earnings of residents.
- **C — Net Domestic Product at Market Price:** Excludes depreciation, but reflects domestic rather than national income and includes net indirect taxes.
- **Exam Extra:** Dividing NNP at Factor Cost by the total population gives Per Capita Income.

### Q250 · National Income & Inflation

Who made the first scientific estimate of National Income in India for the year 1931–32?

A. Dadabhai Naoroji
B. V.K.R.V. Rao
C. P.C. Mahalanobis
D. William Digby

**Answer:** B. V.K.R.V. Rao
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — V.K.R.V. Rao (Correct):** Professor V.K.R.V. Rao made the first scientific estimate of India's National Income for 1931-32 by dividing the economy into sectors and combining the product and income methods.
- **A — Dadabhai Naoroji:** Made the first estimate of national and per capita income for 1867-68, but it lacked modern statistical methodology.
- **C — P.C. Mahalanobis:** Chaired the National Income Committee set up by the Government of India in 1949 after independence.
- **D — William Digby:** Published early estimates of Indian national income in 1899 during British rule, prior to scientific sector-based accounting.
- **Exam Extra:** Dadabhai Naoroji made the earliest pioneering (though non-scientific) estimate of India's per capita income at Rs 20 in 1867-68 in 'Poverty and Un-British Rule in India'.

### Q251 · National Income & Inflation

Which formula represents the GDP Deflator?

A. (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) x 100
B. (Real GDP / Nominal GDP) x 100
C. (GNP / GDP) x 100
D. (NNP / NDP) x 100

**Answer:** A. (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) x 100
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) x 100 (Correct):** The GDP deflator is a broad measure of price inflation across all domestically produced goods and services, calculated as (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) x 100.
- **B — (Real GDP / Nominal GDP) x 100:** Inverts the ratio and does not give the price index relative to the base year.
- **C — (GNP / GDP) x 100:** Compares national versus domestic output, reflecting Net Factor Income from Abroad rather than price changes.
- **D — (NNP / NDP) x 100:** Compares net national output to net domestic output rather than measuring inflation.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike CPI and WPI, the GDP deflator has no fixed basket of goods; its weights change automatically with the composition of current output.

### Q252 · National Income & Inflation

Real GDP differs from Nominal GDP because Real GDP is adjusted for which factor?

A. Population growth
B. Depreciation
C. Price level changes (inflation)
D. Net factor income from abroad

**Answer:** C. Price level changes (inflation)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Price level changes (inflation) (Correct):** Real GDP is valued at constant base-year prices, thereby removing the distortion of inflation (price level changes) that affects Nominal GDP measured at current prices.
- **A — Population growth:** Adjusting total GDP by population gives Per Capita GDP, not Real GDP.
- **B — Depreciation:** Deducting depreciation converts a Gross measure (GDP) into a Net measure (NDP).
- **D — Net factor income from abroad:** Adding NFIA converts a Domestic measure (GDP) into a National measure (GNP).
- **Exam Extra:** When prices rise after the base year, Nominal GDP exceeds Real GDP and the GDP deflator is greater than 100.

### Q253 · National Income & Inflation

Which economic situation is characterized by stagnant growth, high unemployment, and high inflation occurring simultaneously?

A. Deflation
B. Disinflation
C. Reflation
D. Stagflation

**Answer:** D. Stagflation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Stagflation (Correct):** Stagflation combines economic stagnation (slow or negative GDP growth and high unemployment) with persistent high inflation, posing a severe policy dilemma.
- **A — Deflation:** A sustained decline in the general price level of goods and services, often accompanied by weak demand.
- **B — Disinflation:** A reduction in the rate of inflation where prices still rise, just more slowly than before.
- **C — Reflation:** Deliberate fiscal or monetary policy stimulus aimed at restoring output and raising prices out of a deflationary slump.
- **Exam Extra:** The term 'stagflation' was popularized in the 1970s during global oil price shocks when both unemployment and prices rose simultaneously.

### Q254 · National Income & Inflation

Which price index is used by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) as the nominal anchor for inflation targeting?

A. Wholesale Price Index (WPI)
B. Consumer Price Index (Combined)
C. Index of Industrial Production (IIP)
D. Producer Price Index (PPI)

**Answer:** B. Consumer Price Index (Combined)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Consumer Price Index (Combined) (Correct):** Following the Urjit Patel Committee recommendations, the RBI adopted the Consumer Price Index (CPI-Combined) published by the NSO as its nominal anchor for inflation targeting.
- **A — Wholesale Price Index (WPI):** Used by RBI as the primary inflation gauge before 2014; it tracks bulk-level prices of goods only and excludes services.
- **C — Index of Industrial Production (IIP):** Measures short-term changes in the volume of industrial production across mining, manufacturing, and electricity, not price inflation.
- **D — Producer Price Index (PPI):** Measures average price changes received by domestic producers, and is not currently the official RBI policy anchor in India.
- **Exam Extra:** WPI is published monthly by the Office of the Economic Adviser (Ministry of Commerce and Industry) and covers only goods, excluding services.

### Q255 · National Income & Inflation

Which components are excluded from headline inflation to calculate Core Inflation?

A. Food and fuel prices
B. Housing and education prices
C. Clothing and footwear prices
D. Healthcare and transport services

**Answer:** A. Food and fuel prices
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Food and fuel prices (Correct):** Core inflation excludes volatile food and fuel (energy) prices from headline inflation to capture underlying, persistent price pressures in the economy.
- **B — Housing and education prices:** These relatively sticky service components remain included inside core inflation.
- **C — Clothing and footwear prices:** Manufactured consumer items remain part of core inflation calculations.
- **D — Healthcare and transport services:** Non-fuel healthcare and service charges are retained in core inflation to gauge structural demand.
- **Exam Extra:** Food and beverages carry the largest weight (about 45.86%) in India's CPI-Combined basket (2012 base).

### Q256 · National Income & Inflation

Inflation driven by rising costs of production inputs such as wages and raw materials is known as:

A. Demand-pull inflation
B. Creeping inflation
C. Cost-push inflation
D. Galloping inflation

**Answer:** C. Cost-push inflation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Cost-push inflation (Correct):** Cost-push inflation occurs when rising costs of production inputs—such as wages, raw materials, or energy—force producers to raise output prices, shifting the aggregate supply curve leftward.
- **A — Demand-pull inflation:** Occurs when aggregate demand exceeds aggregate supply ('too much money chasing too few goods').
- **B — Creeping inflation:** Refers to a mild, slow rate of price rise (typically under 3% annually), categorized by speed rather than cause.
- **D — Galloping inflation:** Refers to very high inflation running into double or triple digits per year, categorized by severity rather than cost origin.
- **Exam Extra:** Sharp spikes in global crude oil prices are a classic trigger of cost-push inflation in oil-importing economies like India.

### Q257 · National Income & Inflation

Which organization under MoSPI is responsible for compiling and releasing India's official GDP estimates?

A. Reserve Bank of India (RBI)
B. NITI Aayog
C. Department of Economic Affairs
D. National Statistical Office (NSO)

**Answer:** D. National Statistical Office (NSO)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — National Statistical Office (NSO) (Correct):** The National Statistical Office (NSO), formed in May 2019 under the Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MoSPI) by merging the CSO and NSSO, compiles India's GDP estimates.
- **A — Reserve Bank of India (RBI):** Manages monetary policy, currency issuance, and banking regulation, rather than compiling official GDP accounts.
- **B — NITI Aayog:** Serves as the Government of India's public policy think tank, not the statutory national accounts compiler.
- **C — Department of Economic Affairs:** Formulates macroeconomic policy and prepares the Union Budget within the Ministry of Finance.
- **Exam Extra:** Prior to the 2019 merger, the Central Statistics Office (CSO) compiled National Accounts while the NSSO conducted large-scale sample surveys.

### Q258 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

In which type of unemployment is the marginal productivity of additional workers zero, commonly seen in Indian agriculture?

A. Frictional unemployment
B. Cyclical unemployment
C. Disguised unemployment
D. Structural unemployment

**Answer:** C. Disguised unemployment
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Disguised unemployment (Correct):** Disguised (hidden) unemployment occurs when more workers are engaged in an activity than needed, so removing extra workers does not reduce total output—meaning their marginal productivity is zero.
- **A — Frictional unemployment:** Short-term unemployment occurring when workers voluntarily switch jobs or enter the labour market for the first time.
- **B — Cyclical unemployment:** Caused by downturns or recessions in the business cycle when aggregate demand falls.
- **D — Structural unemployment:** Arises from a mismatch between workers' skills or locations and the requirements of available jobs.
- **Exam Extra:** Disguised unemployment is widespread in Indian subsistence agriculture due to high population pressure and limited non-farm rural jobs.

### Q259 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Temporary unemployment experienced by workers while transitioning between jobs is called:

A. Frictional unemployment
B. Seasonal unemployment
C. Disguised unemployment
D. Cyclical unemployment

**Answer:** A. Frictional unemployment
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Frictional unemployment (Correct):** Frictional unemployment is temporary unemployment arising as workers move between jobs, search for better opportunities, or enter the labour force.
- **B — Seasonal unemployment:** Occurs when demand for labour drops during off-peak seasons, common in agriculture and tourism.
- **C — Disguised unemployment:** Exists when surplus labour has zero marginal productivity, so output does not fall if workers leave.
- **D — Cyclical unemployment:** Results from macroeconomic recessions and insufficient aggregate demand across the economy.
- **Exam Extra:** Even an economy operating at 'full employment' still experiences natural frictional and structural unemployment.

### Q260 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Which expert group recommended moving away from calorie-only norms to include private expenditure on health and education in India's poverty line in 2009?

A. Y.K. Alagh Committee
B. Suresh Tendulkar Committee
C. D.T. Lakdawala Committee
D. C. Rangarajan Committee

**Answer:** B. Suresh Tendulkar Committee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Suresh Tendulkar Committee (Correct):** The Suresh Tendulkar Committee (submitted 2009) moved away from purely calorie-based norms, adopted a uniform poverty line basket across rural and urban India, and explicitly included private expenditure on health and education.
- **A — Y.K. Alagh Committee:** Y.K. Alagh Committee (1979): Defined the poverty line strictly using minimum daily calorie requirements of 2,400 kcal (rural) and 2,100 kcal (urban).
- **C — D.T. Lakdawala Committee:** D.T. Lakdawala Committee (1993): Retained Alagh's calorie norms while disaggregating poverty lines state-wise using CPI-AL and CPI-IW price indices.
- **D — C. Rangarajan Committee:** C. Rangarajan Committee (2014): Reviewed Tendulkar's methodology later and proposed separate rural/urban baskets with higher normative calorie, protein, and fat thresholds.
- **Exam Extra:** Using the Tendulkar methodology, the Planning Commission estimated India's poverty ratio at 21.9% for 2011-12 (Rs 816 rural / Rs 1,000 urban monthly per capita).

### Q261 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Which economic growth model formed the basis of India's First Five-Year Plan (1951–1956)?

A. Harrod-Domar model
B. Mahalanobis model
C. Gadgil formula
D. Bombay Plan

**Answer:** A. Harrod-Domar model
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Harrod-Domar model (Correct):** India's First Five-Year Plan (1951-1956), drafted primarily by K.N. Raj, was based on the Harrod-Domar growth model, emphasizing savings, investment, and agricultural development.
- **B — Mahalanobis model:** Formed the theoretical backbone of the Second Five-Year Plan (1956-1961), prioritizing heavy and basic industries.
- **C — Gadgil formula:** A formula adopted in 1969 (Fourth Plan) to allocate central financial assistance to states, not a growth model for the First Plan.
- **D — Bombay Plan:** A 1944-45 private-sector proposal drafted by eight leading Indian industrialists before independence.
- **Exam Extra:** The First Five-Year Plan achieved an average annual GDP growth rate of about 3.6%, exceeding its target of 2.1%.

### Q262 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Who designed the growth strategy emphasizing rapid industrialization and heavy industries for India's Second Five-Year Plan?

A. K.N. Raj
B. John Mathai
C. P.C. Mahalanobis
D. D.R. Gadgil

**Answer:** C. P.C. Mahalanobis
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — P.C. Mahalanobis (Correct):** Statistician Prasanta Chandra (P.C.) Mahalanobis formulated the strategy for India's Second Five-Year Plan (1956-1961), shifting focus toward rapid industrialization and capital-goods heavy industries.
- **A — K.N. Raj:** Contributed significantly to drafting India's First Five-Year Plan (1951-1956), not the Second Plan's heavy-industry strategy.
- **B — John Mathai:** Served as India's Finance Minister in 1949-1950 and opposed creating the Planning Commission, resigning in 1950.
- **D — D.R. Gadgil:** Authored the Gadgil formula for centre-state plan resource transfers ahead of the Fourth Five-Year Plan.
- **Exam Extra:** During the Second Five-Year Plan, major steel plants were established at Bhilai (with USSR), Durgapur (with UK), and Rourkela (with West Germany).

### Q263 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

During which period did India observe a 'Plan Holiday' with three consecutive Annual Plans?

A. 1961–1964
B. 1966–1969
C. 1978–1980
D. 1990–1992

**Answer:** B. 1966–1969
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1966–1969 (Correct):** Following the 1962 and 1965 wars, severe drought, and foreign exchange crisis after the Third Plan, India observed a 'Plan Holiday' from 1966 to 1969, implementing three Annual Plans instead.
- **A — 1961–1964:** Covered the initial three years of the Third Five-Year Plan (1961-1966), not a Plan Holiday.
- **C — 1978–1980:** Marked the 'Rolling Plan' period introduced by the Janata Party government after terminating the Fifth Plan early.
- **D — 1990–1992:** Featured two Annual Plans preceding the Eighth Five-Year Plan due to political instability and the balance-of-payments crisis.
- **Exam Extra:** The Green Revolution in Indian agriculture was launched during this 1966-1969 Annual Plan period using high-yielding variety (HYV) seeds.

### Q264 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Which was India's final Five-Year Plan (2012–2017), launched with the theme 'Faster, More Inclusive and Sustainable Growth'?

A. Ninth Five-Year Plan
B. Tenth Five-Year Plan
C. Eleventh Five-Year Plan
D. Twelfth Five-Year Plan

**Answer:** D. Twelfth Five-Year Plan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Twelfth Five-Year Plan (Correct):** The Twelfth Five-Year Plan (2012-2017) was India's final Five-Year Plan, carrying the theme 'Faster, More Inclusive and Sustainable Growth' before NITI Aayog replaced the Planning Commission.
- **A — Ninth Five-Year Plan:** Covered 1997-2002 with the focus on 'Growth with Social Justice and Equality'.
- **B — Tenth Five-Year Plan:** Covered 2002-2007, aiming for an 8% GDP growth rate and introducing monitorable social targets.
- **C — Eleventh Five-Year Plan:** Covered 2007-2012 under the theme 'Towards Faster and More Inclusive Growth', lacking the 'Sustainable' element of the Twelfth Plan.
- **Exam Extra:** The Eleventh Five-Year Plan (2007-2012) immediately preceded it with the theme 'Towards Faster and More Inclusive Growth'.

### Q265 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Which Five-Year Plan had 'Removal of Poverty (Garibi Hatao)' and attainment of self-reliance as its two main objectives?

A. Third Five-Year Plan
B. Fourth Five-Year Plan
C. Fifth Five-Year Plan
D. Sixth Five-Year Plan

**Answer:** C. Fifth Five-Year Plan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Fifth Five-Year Plan (Correct):** The Fifth Five-Year Plan (1974-1979) formally adopted 'Removal of Poverty (Garibi Hatao)' and 'Attainment of Self-Reliance' as its two principal objectives.
- **A — Third Five-Year Plan:** Third Five-Year Plan (1961–1966): Focused on making India a 'self-reliant and self-generating economy' with balanced agricultural and industrial growth.
- **B — Fourth Five-Year Plan:** Fourth Five-Year Plan (1969–1974): Focused on 'Growth with Stability' and progressive achievement of self-reliance, prior to the formal Fifth Plan poverty programme.
- **D — Sixth Five-Year Plan:** Sixth Five-Year Plan (1980–1985): Marked the start of early economic liberalization and introduced direct anti-poverty schemes like IRDP and NREP.
- **Exam Extra:** The Fifth Five-Year Plan was terminated one year early in 1978 by the newly elected Janata Party government, which introduced a Rolling Plan.

### Q266 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Who was the Union Finance Minister of India when the 1991 New Economic Policy (LPG reforms) was introduced?

A. Dr. Manmohan Singh
B. P. Chidambaram
C. Yashwant Sinha
D. Pranab Mukherjee

**Answer:** A. Dr. Manmohan Singh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Dr. Manmohan Singh (Correct):** Dr. Manmohan Singh served as Union Finance Minister under Prime Minister P.V. Narasimha Rao in July 1991 when India unveiled the New Economic Policy based on Liberalisation, Privatisation, and Globalisation (LPG).
- **B — P. Chidambaram:** Served as Commerce Minister in 1991 and later became Finance Minister in 1996, presenting the 'Dream Budget' in 1997.
- **C — Yashwant Sinha:** Served as Finance Minister briefly in 1990-91 before the Narasimha Rao government, and again from 1998 to 2002.
- **D — Pranab Mukherjee:** Served as Finance Minister in 1982-84 and 2009-2012, and as Deputy Chairman of the Planning Commission in 1991-1996.
- **Exam Extra:** India pledged gold reserves to the Bank of England and Bank of Japan and secured an IMF structural adjustment loan during the 1991 balance-of-payments crisis.

### Q267 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

On which date was NITI Aayog established to replace the Planning Commission of India?

A. 15 August 2014
B. 1 January 2015
C. 1 April 2015
D. 1 July 2017

**Answer:** B. 1 January 2015
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1 January 2015 (Correct):** NITI Aayog (National Institution for Transforming India) was constituted on 1 January 2015 via a Union Cabinet resolution, replacing the 65-year-old Planning Commission.
- **A — 15 August 2014:** Date on which Prime Minister Narendra Modi announced from the Red Fort that a new institution would replace the Planning Commission.
- **C — 1 April 2015:** Start of the 2015-16 financial year and the 14th Finance Commission award period, three months after NITI Aayog's creation.
- **D — 1 July 2017:** Date on which the Goods and Services Tax (GST) was rolled out across India.
- **Exam Extra:** The Prime Minister serves as the ex-officio Chairperson of NITI Aayog, which operates on the principle of cooperative federalism.

### Q268 · Poverty, Unemployment & Planning

Which organization jointly publishes the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) along with OPHI?

A. World Bank
B. International Monetary Fund (IMF)
C. World Trade Organization (WTO)
D. United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

**Answer:** D. United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) (Correct):** The Global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) is published annually by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) in partnership with the Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI).
- **A — World Bank:** Publishes the 'World Development Report' and tracks extreme monetary poverty using international purchasing-power-parity lines.
- **B — International Monetary Fund (IMF):** Publishes the 'World Economic Outlook' and 'Global Financial Stability Report'.
- **C — World Trade Organization (WTO):** Publishes the 'World Trade Report' and monitors multilateral trade rules.
- **Exam Extra:** The Global MPI evaluates deprivation across three dimensions—Health, Education, and Standard of Living—using 10 indicators.

### Q269 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

Which institution issues almost all currency notes in India?

A. State Bank of India
B. Ministry of Finance
C. Reserve Bank of India
D. Securities and Exchange Board of India

**Answer:** C. Reserve Bank of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Reserve Bank of India (Correct):** Under Section 22 of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, the RBI has the sole authority to issue bank notes of denominations Rs 2 and above, accounting for almost all currency notes in circulation in India.
- **A — State Bank of India:** India's largest public sector commercial bank, acting as an agent of the RBI in places without RBI offices rather than issuing currency.
- **B — Ministry of Finance:** Issues only one-rupee notes and coins under the Coinage Act, not the bulk of Indian currency notes.
- **D — Securities and Exchange Board of India:** Regulates India's securities and capital markets rather than currency issuance.
- **Exam Extra:** One-rupee currency notes and all coins are issued by the Ministry of Finance, bearing the signature of the Finance Secretary, while higher notes bear the RBI Governor's signature.

### Q270 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

Based on the recommendations of which commission was the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) established in 1935?

A. Hilton Young Commission
B. Narasimham Committee
C. Urjit Patel Committee
D. Malhotra Committee

**Answer:** A. Hilton Young Commission
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Hilton Young Commission (Correct):** The Reserve Bank of India was established on 1 April 1935 under the RBI Act, 1934, based on the recommendations of the Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance (1926), popularly known as the Hilton Young Commission.
- **B — Narasimham Committee:** Recommended wide-ranging banking sector reforms in 1991 and 1998, long after the RBI was founded.
- **C — Urjit Patel Committee:** Recommended adopting CPI-Combined inflation targeting and forming the Monetary Policy Committee in 2014.
- **D — Malhotra Committee:** Recommended reforms and private participation in the Indian insurance sector in 1993-1994, leading to IRDAI's creation.
- **Exam Extra:** Sir Osborne Smith served as the first Governor of the RBI (1935-1937), while C.D. Deshmukh was its first Indian Governor (1943-1949).

### Q271 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

In which year was the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) nationalised?

A. 1935
B. 1947
C. 1949
D. 1955

**Answer:** C. 1949
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1949 (Correct):** Originally set up as a privately owned shareholders' bank in 1935, the Reserve Bank of India was nationalised on 1 January 1949 under the Reserve Bank (Transfer to Public Ownership) Act, 1948.
- **A — 1935:** Year in which the RBI commenced operations (1 April 1935) as a privately owned central bank.
- **B — 1947:** Year of Indian independence, when RBI still functioned as a private shareholders' institution and briefly served Pakistan too until June 1948.
- **D — 1955:** Year in which the Imperial Bank of India was nationalised and renamed the State Bank of India (SBI).
- **Exam Extra:** The RBI's central office was initially located in Kolkata before moving permanently to Mumbai in 1937.

### Q272 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

In Indian monetary policy, what is the Repo Rate?

A. Interest rate at which RBI lends short-term funds to commercial banks against securities
B. Interest rate at which RBI absorbs liquidity from banks against collateral
C. Interest rate charged by RBI on long-term loans without collateral
D. Minimum share of deposits banks must keep in cash with the RBI

**Answer:** A. Interest rate at which RBI lends short-term funds to commercial banks against securities
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Interest rate at which RBI lends short-term funds to commercial banks against securities (Correct):** The Repo Rate (Repurchase Option Rate) is the policy interest rate at which the RBI lends short-term funds to commercial banks against government securities as collateral.
- **B — Interest rate at which RBI absorbs liquidity from banks against collateral:** This describes the Reverse Repo Rate (and the SDF rate without collateral).
- **C — Interest rate charged by RBI on long-term loans without collateral:** Corresponds broadly to the Bank Rate for long-term penal/rediscounting credit rather than repo operations.
- **D — Minimum share of deposits banks must keep in cash with the RBI:** This describes the Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR), which is a reserve requirement rather than a lending rate.
- **Exam Extra:** When the RBI raises the Repo Rate, borrowing becomes costlier for banks, tightening liquidity and helping curb inflation.

### Q273 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

Under the Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR), in what form must scheduled commercial banks maintain a mandated percentage of their NDTL?

A. Gold reserves kept with the Ministry of Finance
B. Government securities held by the banks themselves
C. Foreign currency deposits kept with overseas banks
D. Cash balances maintained with the Reserve Bank of India

**Answer:** D. Cash balances maintained with the Reserve Bank of India
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cash balances maintained with the Reserve Bank of India (Correct):** Under Section 42(1) of the RBI Act, 1934, scheduled commercial banks must hold the Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) strictly in the form of cash balances maintained with the RBI.
- **A — Gold reserves kept with the Ministry of Finance:** Gold is permissible for SLR (held by the bank itself), not for CRR balances with the RBI.
- **B — Government securities held by the banks themselves:** Unencumbered approved securities count toward the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR), not CRR.
- **C — Foreign currency deposits kept with overseas banks:** Foreign nostro balances do not satisfy domestic CRR requirements.
- **Exam Extra:** Commercial banks do not earn any interest from the RBI on the cash balances held under CRR.

### Q274 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

Which combination of assets is permitted for banks to maintain the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR)?

A. Only cash balances deposited with the RBI
B. Cash, gold, and unencumbered approved government securities
C. Only foreign exchange reserves and corporate bonds
D. Equity shares and mutual fund units

**Answer:** B. Cash, gold, and unencumbered approved government securities
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Cash, gold, and unencumbered approved government securities (Correct):** Under Section 24 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949, banks maintain the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR) in liquid assets—namely cash, gold, or unencumbered approved government securities.
- **A — Only cash balances deposited with the RBI:** Cash deposited exclusively with the RBI fulfills the Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR), not SLR.
- **C — Only foreign exchange reserves and corporate bonds:** Corporate bonds and foreign exchange are not eligible SLR instruments.
- **D — Equity shares and mutual fund units:** Risky market equities do not qualify as safe liquid SLR assets.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike CRR, banks earn interest on the government securities they hold to fulfill SLR requirements.

### Q275 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

How many members constitute India's statutory Monetary Policy Committee (MPC)?

A. 4 members
B. 5 members
C. 6 members
D. 8 members

**Answer:** C. 6 members
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 6 members (Correct):** Constituted in 2016 under Section 45ZB of the amended RBI Act, 1934, the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) has 6 members: 3 from the RBI and 3 external members appointed by the Central Government.
- **A — 4 members:** Incorrect; a 4-member body is not the statutory structure under the amended RBI Act.
- **B — 5 members:** Incorrect; RBI's earlier Technical Advisory Committee was advisory, whereas the statutory MPC has 6 members.
- **D — 8 members:** Incorrect; the MPC has 3 internal RBI members and 3 external government-appointed members, totaling 6.
- **Exam Extra:** The RBI Governor acts as the ex-officio Chairperson of the MPC and holds a second or casting vote in the event of a tie.

### Q276 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

How many major commercial banks were nationalised in the first phase of bank nationalisation on 19 July 1969?

A. 6 banks
B. 10 banks
C. 14 banks
D. 20 banks

**Answer:** C. 14 banks
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 14 banks (Correct):** On 19 July 1969, the Government of India nationalised 14 major private commercial banks each holding deposits of Rs 50 crore or more.
- **A — 6 banks:** Six banks were nationalised during the second phase in April 1980, not in July 1969.
- **B — 10 banks:** No bank nationalisation tranche in India involved 10 banks.
- **D — 20 banks:** Represents the combined total of the 1969 (14) and 1980 (6) nationalisation waves rather than the first phase alone.
- **Exam Extra:** In a second phase on 15 April 1980, 6 more private banks with deposits of Rs 200 crore or more were nationalised.

### Q277 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

On the recommendation of which committee was NABARD established in 1982?

A. B. Sivaraman Committee
B. M. Narasimham Committee
C. Raghuram Rajan Committee
D. P.J. Nayak Committee

**Answer:** A. B. Sivaraman Committee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — B. Sivaraman Committee (Correct):** NABARD (National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development) was established on 12 July 1982 on the recommendations of the B. Sivaraman Committee (CRAFICARD).
- **B — M. Narasimham Committee:** Recommended the establishment of Regional Rural Banks (1975) and later financial sector reforms (1991, 1998).
- **C — Raghuram Rajan Committee:** Submitted a 2008 report on financial sector reforms ('A Hundred Small Steps') and a 2013 index on state backwardness.
- **D — P.J. Nayak Committee:** Reviewed governance of boards of banks in India in 2014, recommending the Bank Board Bureau.
- **Exam Extra:** Headquartered in Mumbai, NABARD absorbed the agricultural credit functions of the RBI and the Agricultural Refinance and Development Corporation (ARDC).

### Q278 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

Where is the headquarters of the Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) located?

A. Mumbai
B. New Delhi
C. Kolkata
D. Lucknow

**Answer:** D. Lucknow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Lucknow (Correct):** The Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI), set up in 1990 as the apex institution for MSME financing and development, is headquartered in Lucknow, Uttar Pradesh.
- **A — Mumbai:** Hosts the headquarters of RBI, SEBI, NABARD, and EXIM Bank, not SIDBI.
- **B — New Delhi:** Hosts the headquarters of Punjab National Bank and IFCI, not SIDBI.
- **C — Kolkata:** Hosts the headquarters of UCO Bank and Bandhan Bank, and was the original 1935 office of the RBI.
- **Exam Extra:** SIDBI was established as a wholly owned subsidiary of IDBI under the SIDBI Act, 1989, before becoming independent.

### Q279 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

What is the statutory shareholding ratio among the Central Government, Sponsor Bank, and State Government in a Regional Rural Bank (RRB)?

A. 50% : 35% : 15%
B. 51% : 29% : 20%
C. 40% : 40% : 20%
D. 60% : 25% : 15%

**Answer:** A. 50% : 35% : 15%
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 50% : 35% : 15% (Correct):** The capital of a Regional Rural Bank (RRB) is jointly owned by the Central Government (50%), the Sponsor Bank (35%), and the concerned State Government (15%).
- **B — 51% : 29% : 20%:** Incorrect shareholding split; the Sponsor Bank holds 35% and the State Government holds 15%.
- **C — 40% : 40% : 20%:** Incorrect; the Central Government holds a majority 50% stake while the Sponsor Bank holds 35%.
- **D — 60% : 25% : 15%:** Incorrect; Central Government ownership in RRBs is fixed at 50% under the RRB Act, 1976.
- **Exam Extra:** India's first RRB, Prathama Bank, was established on 2 October 1975 in Moradabad, Uttar Pradesh, sponsored by Syndicate Bank.

### Q280 · RBI, Monetary Policy & Banking

In which year was the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) granted statutory powers through the SEBI Act?

A. 1988
B. 1992
C. 1990
D. 1995

**Answer:** B. 1992
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1992 (Correct):** Although SEBI was first set up as a non-statutory body in April 1988, it received statutory powers and autonomous status on 30 January 1992 through the SEBI Act, 1992.
- **A — 1988:** Year in which SEBI was initially established by an executive resolution without statutory powers.
- **C — 1990:** Year in which SIDBI began operations, not when SEBI obtained statutory status.
- **D — 1995:** Year in which the World Trade Organization (WTO) was established, three years after the SEBI Act.
- **Exam Extra:** Madhabi Puri Buch became the first woman Chairperson of SEBI in March 2022.

### Q281 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

Goods and Services Tax (GST) is primarily an example of which type of tax?

A. Direct tax
B. Indirect tax
C. Capital gains tax
D. Corporate tax

**Answer:** B. Indirect tax
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Indirect tax (Correct):** Goods and Services Tax (GST) is a destination-based, multi-stage indirect tax whose burden can be shifted from suppliers to final consumers through prices of goods and services.
- **A — Direct tax:** A tax (such as personal income tax) levied directly on an individual or entity whose burden cannot be shifted to others.
- **C — Capital gains tax:** A specific direct tax imposed on profits realized from selling capital assets like shares or real estate.
- **D — Corporate tax:** A direct tax levied on the net profits of registered companies.
- **Exam Extra:** GST subsumed multiple central and state indirect levies such as Central Excise Duty, Service Tax, VAT, and Octroi.

### Q282 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

Which body makes recommendations on the devolution of taxes between the Union and the States in India?

A. NITI Aayog
B. Goods and Services Tax Council
C. Finance Commission
D. Comptroller and Auditor General

**Answer:** C. Finance Commission
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Finance Commission (Correct):** The Finance Commission is a quasi-judicial constitutional body appointed every five years to recommend the vertical and horizontal distribution of net tax proceeds between the Union and the States.
- **A — NITI Aayog:** A non-constitutional executive policy think tank that does not decide constitutional tax devolution formulas.
- **B — Goods and Services Tax Council:** A federal body under Article 279A that decides GST tax rates, exemptions, and rules rather than general Union-State tax devolution.
- **D — Comptroller and Auditor General:** Audits Union and State government accounts rather than recommending tax-sharing formulas.
- **Exam Extra:** The 16th Finance Commission was constituted in December 2023 under the chairmanship of Dr. Arvind Panagariya for the 2026–2031 award period.

### Q283 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

Under which Article of the Indian Constitution is the Finance Commission constituted?

A. Article 266
B. Article 279A
C. Article 280
D. Article 360

**Answer:** C. Article 280
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Article 280 (Correct):** Article 280 of the Indian Constitution mandates the President to constitute a Finance Commission every five years (or earlier) consisting of a Chairperson and four other members.
- **A — Article 266:** Establishes the Consolidated Funds and Public Accounts of India and of the States.
- **B — Article 279A:** Provides for the constitution of the Goods and Services Tax (GST) Council by the President.
- **D — Article 360:** Empowers the President to proclaim a Financial Emergency in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The First Finance Commission was constituted in 1951 under the chairmanship of K.C. Neogy.

### Q284 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

On which date was the Goods and Services Tax (GST) rolled out across India?

A. 1 April 2016
B. 1 July 2017
C. 1 April 2017
D. 8 November 2016

**Answer:** B. 1 July 2017
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1 July 2017 (Correct):** GST was launched nationwide at midnight on 1 July 2017 during a special joint session in the Central Hall of Parliament under the slogan 'One Nation, One Tax'.
- **A — 1 April 2016:** Start of FY 2016-17, prior to passage of the 101st Constitutional Amendment Act in August-September 2016.
- **C — 1 April 2017:** Initial target date for GST rollout before being shifted by three months to 1 July 2017.
- **D — 8 November 2016:** Date on which Rs 500 and Rs 1,000 currency notes were demonetised in India.
- **Exam Extra:** France was the first country in the world to implement a GST/VAT system in 1954.

### Q285 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

Who serves as the ex-officio Chairperson of the Goods and Services Tax (GST) Council under Article 279A?

A. Prime Minister of India
B. Governor of the Reserve Bank of India
C. Comptroller and Auditor General
D. Union Finance Minister

**Answer:** D. Union Finance Minister
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Union Finance Minister (Correct):** Under Article 279A(2) of the Constitution, the Union Finance Minister serves as the ex-officio Chairperson of the Goods and Services Tax (GST) Council.
- **A — Prime Minister of India:** Chairs NITI Aayog and the Inter-State Council, not the GST Council.
- **B — Governor of the Reserve Bank of India:** Chairs the Monetary Policy Committee, not the GST Council.
- **C — Comptroller and Auditor General:** Serves as the constitutional auditor of public funds, without membership in the GST Council.
- **Exam Extra:** Decisions in the GST Council require a majority of at least three-fourths of weighted votes, where the Centre has one-third weightage and all States together have two-thirds.

### Q286 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

How is Fiscal Deficit defined in the Union Budget of India?

A. Total Expenditure minus Total Receipts excluding borrowings
B. Revenue Expenditure minus Revenue Receipts
C. Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments
D. Capital Expenditure minus Capital Receipts

**Answer:** A. Total Expenditure minus Total Receipts excluding borrowings
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Total Expenditure minus Total Receipts excluding borrowings (Correct):** Fiscal Deficit measures the total borrowing requirement of the government and equals Total Expenditure minus Total Receipts excluding borrowings (i.e., Revenue Receipts plus Non-debt Capital Receipts).
- **B — Revenue Expenditure minus Revenue Receipts:** Defines the Revenue Deficit, reflecting dissaving on the government's current account.
- **C — Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments:** Defines the Primary Deficit, showing borrowing requirements excluding past debt servicing.
- **D — Capital Expenditure minus Capital Receipts:** Reflects the imbalance on the capital account alone rather than overall fiscal borrowing.
- **Exam Extra:** The Union Budget was presented alongside the Railway Budget as a merged document starting from FY 2017-18 on the Bibek Debroy Committee's recommendation.

### Q287 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

What does Primary Deficit equal in government budgeting?

A. Revenue Deficit minus Interest Payments
B. Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments
C. Fiscal Deficit plus Interest Payments
D. Total Expenditure minus Revenue Receipts

**Answer:** B. Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments (Correct):** Primary Deficit equals Fiscal Deficit minus Interest Payments, indicating how much of the government's current borrowing is needed for non-interest expenditures.
- **A — Revenue Deficit minus Interest Payments:** Not a standard budget deficit aggregate; interest payments are part of revenue expenditure.
- **C — Fiscal Deficit plus Interest Payments:** Adding interest payments double-counts debt servicing, which is already included inside Fiscal Deficit.
- **D — Total Expenditure minus Revenue Receipts:** Ignores non-debt capital receipts such as disinvestment proceeds and loan recoveries.
- **Exam Extra:** A zero Primary Deficit implies that the government is borrowing solely to pay interest on past accumulated debt.

### Q288 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

In which year was the Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act enacted in India?

A. 1991
B. 2000
C. 2003
D. 2007

**Answer:** C. 2003
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 2003 (Correct):** The Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act was enacted in August 2003 to institutionalize fiscal discipline, reduce deficits, and improve macroeconomic management in India.
- **A — 1991:** Year of India's economic liberalisation (LPG) reforms, twelve years before the FRBM Act.
- **B — 2000:** Year in which the FRBM Bill was first introduced in Parliament before being enacted in 2003.
- **D — 2007:** Year just prior to the 2008 global financial crisis when India's fiscal consolidation targets were temporarily paused.
- **Exam Extra:** The N.K. Singh Committee (2017) reviewed the FRBM framework and recommended a combined government debt-to-GDP ceiling of 60% (40% Centre, 20% States).

### Q289 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

Which two international financial institutions are collectively known as the 'Bretton Woods Twins'?

A. IMF and World Trade Organization (WTO)
B. World Bank and Asian Development Bank
C. IMF and Bank for International Settlements
D. IMF and World Bank (IBRD)

**Answer:** D. IMF and World Bank (IBRD)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — IMF and World Bank (IBRD) (Correct):** The International Monetary Fund (IMF) and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD / World Bank) are called the 'Bretton Woods Twins' because both were created at the 1944 Bretton Woods Conference.
- **A — IMF and World Trade Organization (WTO):** The WTO was founded later in 1995 under the Marrakesh Agreement, not at Bretton Woods in 1944.
- **B — World Bank and Asian Development Bank:** The Asian Development Bank (ADB) was established much later in 1966 in Manila.
- **C — IMF and Bank for International Settlements:** The BIS was founded earlier in 1930 in Basel, Switzerland, prior to Bretton Woods.
- **Exam Extra:** Both the IMF and the World Bank are headquartered in Washington, D.C., United States.

### Q290 · Union Budget, GST & International Orgs

The World Trade Organization (WTO), established in 1995, replaced which earlier international trade arrangement?

A. General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT)
B. UNCTAD
C. International Trade Organization (ITO)
D. European Free Trade Association (EFTA)

**Answer:** A. General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT) (Correct):** Established on 1 January 1995 via the Marrakesh Agreement (1994), the World Trade Organization (WTO) replaced the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT), which had operated since 1948.
- **B — UNCTAD:** A permanent United Nations intergovernmental body founded in 1964 that continues to operate alongside the WTO.
- **C — International Trade Organization (ITO):** A proposed 1948 agency under the Havana Charter that never came into existence because the US Congress did not ratify it.
- **D — European Free Trade Association (EFTA):** A regional trade bloc of non-EU European countries established in 1960, unrelated to WTO's global predecessor.
- **Exam Extra:** India is a founding member of both GATT (1947) and the WTO (1995), whose headquarters is in Geneva, Switzerland.

# Section II: General Science

## Chapter 5: Physics

### Q291 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

What is the SI unit of force?

A. Joule
B. Watt
C. Newton
D. Pascal

**Answer:** C. Newton
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Newton (Correct):** Newton (N) is the SI derived unit of force, defined as the force required to accelerate a 1 kg mass at 1 m/s² (1 N = 1 kg·m/s²).
- **A — Joule:** Joule (J): SI unit of work, energy, and heat.
- **B — Watt:** Watt (W): SI unit of power (rate of doing work).
- **D — Pascal:** Pascal (Pa): SI unit of pressure (force per unit area, 1 N/m²).
- **Exam Extra:** In the CGS system, force is measured in Dyne (1 Newton = 10⁵ Dynes), and its dimensional formula is [MLT⁻²].

### Q292 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

What is the SI unit of power?

A. Joule
B. Watt
C. Newton
D. Coulomb

**Answer:** B. Watt
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Watt (Correct):** Watt (W) is the SI unit of power, defined as 1 Joule of work done or energy transferred per second (1 W = 1 J/s).
- **A — Joule:** Joule (J): SI unit of work and energy.
- **C — Newton:** Newton (N): SI unit of force.
- **D — Coulomb:** Coulomb (C): SI unit of electric charge.
- **Exam Extra:** 1 Horsepower (HP), commonly used to rate mechanical engines, equals 746 Watts (named after Scottish engineer James Watt).

### Q293 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

What is the SI unit of electric current?

A. Ampere
B. Volt
C. Ohm
D. Farad

**Answer:** A. Ampere
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Ampere (Correct):** Ampere (A) is one of the seven SI base units and measures electric current, equal to a flow of 1 Coulomb of electric charge per second.
- **B — Volt:** Volt (V): SI unit of electric potential difference and electromotive force.
- **C — Ohm:** Ohm (Ω): SI unit of electrical resistance.
- **D — Farad:** Farad (F): SI unit of electrical capacitance.
- **Exam Extra:** Electric current in a circuit is measured by an Ammeter connected in series; an ideal ammeter has zero resistance.

### Q294 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

What is the SI base unit of luminous intensity?

A. Candela
B. Lumen
C. Lux
D. Mole

**Answer:** A. Candela
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Candela (Correct):** Candela (cd) is the SI base unit of luminous intensity, measuring the wavelength-weighted power emitted by a light source in a particular direction.
- **B — Lumen:** Lumen (lm): SI derived unit of luminous flux (total visible light emitted).
- **C — Lux:** Lux (lx): SI derived unit of illuminance (1 lumen per square metre).
- **D — Mole:** Mole (mol): SI base unit of amount of substance.
- **Exam Extra:** The 7 SI base units are metre, kilogram, second, ampere, kelvin, mole, and candela.

### Q295 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which of the following is the largest practical unit used to measure astronomical distances?

A. Astronomical Unit
B. Light Year
C. Parsec
D. Fermi

**Answer:** C. Parsec
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Parsec (Correct):** A Parsec (parallax second) is the largest of these astronomical distance units, equal to approximately 3.26 light years (3.086 × 10¹⁶ m).
- **A — Astronomical Unit:** Astronomical Unit (AU): Mean distance between the Earth and the Sun (≈ 1.496 × 10¹¹ m).
- **B — Light Year:** Distance travelled by light in vacuum in one Julian year (≈ 9.46 × 10¹⁵ m).
- **D — Fermi:** Fermi (femtometre): Microscopic unit used for nuclear radii (10⁻¹⁵ m).
- **Exam Extra:** 1 Parsec equals approximately 3.26 light years or 206,265 Astronomical Units (AU).

### Q296 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

What is the SI unit of frequency?

A. Decibel
B. Hertz
C. Tesla
D. Weber

**Answer:** B. Hertz
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Hertz (Correct):** Hertz (Hz) is the SI derived unit of frequency, defined as one cycle or oscillation per second (1 Hz = 1 s⁻¹).
- **A — Decibel:** Decibel (dB): Logarithmic unit used to measure sound intensity level.
- **C — Tesla:** Tesla (T): SI unit of magnetic flux density (magnetic field strength).
- **D — Weber:** Weber (Wb): SI unit of magnetic flux.
- **Exam Extra:** Named after German physicist Heinrich Hertz; the normal human audible frequency range is 20 Hz to 20,000 Hz.

### Q297 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which physical quantity is measured in Pascal (Pa)?

A. Momentum
B. Torque
C. Viscosity
D. Pressure

**Answer:** D. Pressure
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Pressure (Correct):** Pressure is measured in Pascal (Pa), defined as 1 Newton of force acting perpendicular to 1 square metre of surface area (1 Pa = 1 N/m²).
- **A — Momentum:** Measured in kg·m/s or N·s.
- **B — Torque:** Measured in Newton-metre (N·m).
- **C — Viscosity:** Dynamic viscosity is measured in Pa·s (CGS unit: Poise).
- **Exam Extra:** 1 standard atmosphere (atm) equals 101,325 Pascals (or 760 mm of Hg); the dimensional formula of pressure is [ML⁻¹T⁻²].

### Q298 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which pair of physical quantities has the same dimensional formula?

A. Work and Torque
B. Force and Pressure
C. Impulse and Acceleration
D. Power and Energy

**Answer:** A. Work and Torque
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Work and Torque (Correct):** Both Work (Force × displacement) and Torque (Force × perpendicular distance) have the exact same dimensional formula [ML²T⁻²], though work is a scalar and torque is a vector.
- **B — Force and Pressure:** Force has dimensions [MLT⁻²], whereas Pressure has [ML⁻¹T⁻²].
- **C — Impulse and Acceleration:** Impulse has dimensions [MLT⁻¹], whereas Acceleration has [LT⁻²].
- **D — Power and Energy:** Power has dimensions [ML²T⁻³], whereas Energy has [ML²T⁻²].
- **Exam Extra:** Impulse and Linear Momentum also share identical dimensions: [MLT⁻¹].

### Q299 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which instrument is used to measure very high temperatures, such as those inside a furnace?

A. Thermistor
B. Pyrometer
C. Bolometer
D. Calorimeter

**Answer:** B. Pyrometer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Pyrometer (Correct):** An optical or radiation Pyrometer measures extremely high temperatures (such as inside industrial furnaces or stellar surfaces) without physical contact by detecting emitted thermal radiation.
- **A — Thermistor:** A temperature-sensitive semiconductor resistor used for moderate temperature measurement and control.
- **C — Bolometer:** Measures the power of incident electromagnetic radiation.
- **D — Calorimeter:** Measures the quantity of heat absorbed or released during a physical or chemical process.
- **Exam Extra:** Pyrometers operate on the principles of the Stefan-Boltzmann law (E ∝ T⁴) and Wien's displacement law.

### Q300 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which instrument is used to measure the depth of an ocean using ultrasonic waves?

A. Hydrometer
B. Odometer
C. Fathometer
D. Tachometer

**Answer:** C. Fathometer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Fathometer (Correct):** A Fathometer (an ultrasonic echo sounder) measures the depth of an ocean by timing ultrasonic pulses reflected from the seabed.
- **A — Hydrometer:** Measures the specific gravity (relative density) of liquids.
- **B — Odometer:** Measures the total distance travelled by a wheeled vehicle.
- **D — Tachometer:** Measures the rotational speed (RPM) of a shaft or disk, such as in an engine.
- **Exam Extra:** 1 Fathom equals 6 feet (1.8288 metres); SONAR stands for Sound Navigation and Ranging.

### Q301 · SI Units, Dimensions & Instruments

Which of the following is a vector quantity?

A. Electric Charge
B. Work
C. Speed
D. Momentum

**Answer:** D. Momentum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Momentum (Correct):** Momentum (p = mv) is a vector quantity because it possesses both magnitude and a definite direction (the same direction as velocity).
- **A — Electric Charge:** A scalar quantity having only magnitude and algebraic sign, not spatial direction.
- **B — Work:** A scalar quantity obtained from the dot product of force and displacement.
- **C — Speed:** A scalar quantity representing distance covered per unit time regardless of direction.
- **Exam Extra:** Electric current has magnitude and a direction of flow, yet it is a scalar quantity because it obeys algebraic addition rather than vector addition.

### Q302 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

A passenger leans backward when a stationary bus suddenly starts moving forward. This phenomenon is explained by:

A. Law of Inertia
B. Newton's Second Law
C. Conservation of Energy
D. Newton's Third Law

**Answer:** A. Law of Inertia
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Law of Inertia (Correct):** Newton's First Law of Motion (Law of Inertia) states that a body at rest tends to remain at rest; when the bus accelerates forward, the passenger's upper body stays behind due to inertia of rest.
- **B — Newton's Second Law:** Relates net external force to the rate of change of momentum (F = ma).
- **C — Conservation of Energy:** States that total energy of an isolated system remains constant.
- **D — Newton's Third Law:** States that every action has an equal and opposite reaction acting on different bodies.
- **Exam Extra:** Mass is the quantitative measure of inertia—the greater the mass of an object, the greater its inertia.

### Q303 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

Why does a cricketer pull his hands backward while catching a fast-moving ball?

A. To increase the momentum of the ball
B. To increase time and reduce impact force
C. To decrease the impulse on the ball
D. To apply an equal reaction force

**Answer:** B. To increase time and reduce impact force
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — To increase time and reduce impact force (Correct):** By pulling hands back, the fielder increases the time interval (Δt) over which the ball comes to rest; by Newton's Second Law (F = Δp/Δt), a longer stopping time reduces the average impact force on the hands.
- **A — To increase the momentum of the ball:** Catching brings the ball's momentum down to zero, not up.
- **C — To decrease the impulse on the ball:** Total impulse (Δp = mv) depends on mass and initial speed, so it remains unchanged.
- **D — To apply an equal reaction force:** Equal and opposite forces exist instantaneously by the Third Law and do not explain cushioning.
- **Exam Extra:** Impulse equals Force × Time (which equals total change in momentum), and its SI unit is Newton-second (N·s).

### Q304 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

Rocket propulsion works primarily on the principle of:

A. Bernoulli's Theorem
B. Archimedes' Principle
C. Conservation of Linear Momentum
D. Kepler's Third Law

**Answer:** C. Conservation of Linear Momentum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Conservation of Linear Momentum (Correct):** A rocket ejects hot exhaust gases downward at high speed; by Conservation of Linear Momentum (and Newton's Third Law), the rocket gains an equal and opposite upward momentum.
- **A — Bernoulli's Theorem:** Explains pressure drops in fast-flowing fluids, such as lift on an aeroplane wing.
- **B — Archimedes' Principle:** Explains buoyant upthrust on objects immersed in fluids.
- **D — Kepler's Third Law:** Relates the square of a planet's orbital period to the cube of its semi-major axis (T² ∝ r³).
- **Exam Extra:** Because rocket propulsion relies on momentum conservation rather than pushing against air, rockets accelerate efficiently even in the vacuum of space.

### Q305 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

Which force is responsible for separating cream from milk in a centrifugal cream separator?

A. Gravitational force
B. Electrostatic force
C. Frictional force
D. Centrifugal force

**Answer:** D. Centrifugal force
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Centrifugal force (Correct):** In the rotating frame of a cream separator, the outward apparent Centrifugal force acts more strongly on the denser skim milk, pushing it toward the wall while lighter cream collects near the centre.
- **A — Gravitational force:** Far too weak compared to high-speed rotational forces for rapid dairy separation.
- **B — Electrostatic force:** Acts between electric charges and does not drive mechanical centrifuge separation.
- **C — Frictional force:** Opposes relative motion between surfaces rather than separating fluids by density.
- **Exam Extra:** Centrifugal force is a pseudo-force directed radially outward in a rotating reference frame, equal in magnitude to centripetal force (mv²/r).

### Q306 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

Where on Earth is the value of acceleration due to gravity (g) minimum?

A. At the Equator
B. At the North Pole
C. At the South Pole
D. At 45° Latitude

**Answer:** A. At the Equator
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — At the Equator (Correct):** Acceleration due to gravity (g) is minimum at the Equator because Earth's equatorial radius is larger (equatorial bulge) and the outward centrifugal effect of Earth's rotation is maximum there.
- **B — At the North Pole:** g is maximum here (~9.83 m/s²) due to smaller polar radius and zero rotational effect.
- **C — At the South Pole:** g is also maximum at the South Pole for the same reasons as at the North Pole.
- **D — At 45° Latitude:** g takes an intermediate standard value (~9.80665 m/s²).
- **Exam Extra:** At the centre of the Earth, the value of g is zero, so a body becomes weightless there while its mass remains unchanged.

### Q307 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

What is the approximate escape velocity from the surface of the Earth?

A. 7.9 km/s
B. 11.2 km/s
C. 9.8 km/s
D. 2.4 km/s

**Answer:** B. 11.2 km/s
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 11.2 km/s (Correct):** Earth's escape velocity is approximately 11.2 km/s (vₑ = √(2gR)); an object projected at or above this speed escapes Earth's gravitational pull without further propulsion.
- **A — 7.9 km/s:** Approximate orbital velocity (v₀ = √(gR)) of a satellite orbiting very close to Earth's surface.
- **C — 9.8 km/s:** Confusion with the numerical value of acceleration due to gravity (9.8 m/s²).
- **D — 2.4 km/s:** Approximate escape velocity on the Moon (≈ 2.38 km/s).
- **Exam Extra:** Escape velocity is √2 times the near-surface orbital velocity (vₑ = √2 · v₀) and is independent of the mass of the escaping object.

### Q308 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

What is the time period of revolution of a geostationary satellite around the Earth?

A. 12 hours
B. 84 minutes
C. 24 hours
D. 365 days

**Answer:** C. 24 hours
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 24 hours (Correct):** A geostationary satellite orbits in the equatorial plane from west to east with a period of 24 hours, matching Earth's sidereal rotation period so it remains fixed over one point on the equator.
- **A — 12 hours:** Approximate orbital period of medium-Earth-orbit navigation satellites such as GPS.
- **B — 84 minutes:** Approximate orbital period of a satellite orbiting just above Earth's surface in low-Earth orbit.
- **D — 365 days:** Orbital period of the Earth revolving around the Sun.
- **Exam Extra:** Geostationary satellites orbit at an altitude of approximately 35,786 km (~36,000 km) above Earth's equator.

### Q309 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

If a simple pendulum is taken to the Moon, how will its time period change compared to that on Earth?

A. It becomes zero
B. It decreases
C. It remains unchanged
D. It increases

**Answer:** D. It increases
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — It increases (Correct):** Since time period T = 2π√(L/g) and gravity on the Moon is 1/6th of Earth's gravity, the time period increases by √6 (≈ 2.45) times, causing a pendulum clock to run slower.
- **A — It becomes zero:** Occurs only hypothetically if string length L = 0, not due to lower gravity.
- **B — It decreases:** Occurs when gravity g increases or pendulum length L decreases.
- **C — It remains unchanged:** Happens when the mass of the pendulum bob is changed, since T is independent of bob mass.
- **Exam Extra:** In summer, thermal expansion lengthens a metallic pendulum rod (L increases), so T increases and the pendulum clock loses time.

### Q310 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

Kepler's second law of planetary motion (law of equal areas) is a direct consequence of the conservation of:

A. Angular momentum
B. Linear momentum
C. Kinetic energy
D. Gravitational potential

**Answer:** A. Angular momentum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Angular momentum (Correct):** Because the Sun's gravitational pull on a planet is a purely central force (exerting zero torque), angular momentum (L = mvr) is conserved, keeping the areal velocity (dA/dt = L/2m) constant.
- **B — Linear momentum:** Continuously changes direction and magnitude along an elliptical orbit due to gravitational force.
- **C — Kinetic energy:** Varies along an elliptical orbit (maximum at perihelion, minimum at aphelion); total mechanical energy is conserved.
- **D — Gravitational potential:** Varies as the planet's distance from the Sun changes.
- **Exam Extra:** Kepler's three laws of planetary motion are: (1) Elliptical orbits, (2) Equal areas in equal times, and (3) T² ∝ a³ (Law of Periods).

### Q311 · Motion, Newton's Laws, Gravitation & Levers

A pair of scissors and a seesaw belong to which class of lever?

A. Class III lever
B. Class I lever
C. Class II lever
D. Compound pulley

**Answer:** B. Class I lever
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Class I lever (Correct):** In a Class I lever, the fulcrum is situated between the effort and the load (Effort–Fulcrum–Load), as seen in scissors, pliers, crowbars, and a seesaw.
- **A — Class III lever:** Effort lies between the fulcrum and the load (e.g., human forearm, tweezers, fishing rod).
- **C — Class II lever:** Load lies between the fulcrum and the effort (e.g., wheelbarrow, nutcracker, bottle opener).
- **D — Compound pulley:** A combination of fixed and movable pulleys rather than a rigid bar lever.
- **Exam Extra:** The mnemonic 'FLE' identifies what lies in the middle: Class I = Fulcrum, Class II = Load, Class III = Effort; Class II levers always give mechanical advantage > 1.

### Q312 · Fluid Mechanics

Hydraulic brakes and hydraulic lifts used in automobiles work on which scientific principle?

A. Archimedes' Principle
B. Bernoulli's Principle
C. Pascal's Law
D. Torricelli's Theorem

**Answer:** C. Pascal's Law
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Pascal's Law (Correct):** Pascal's Law states that pressure applied to any part of an enclosed incompressible fluid is transmitted equally and undiminished in all directions throughout the fluid.
- **A — Archimedes' Principle:** Governs buoyant force and explains why objects float or sink.
- **B — Bernoulli's Principle:** Relates fluid flow speed to pressure in steady streamline flow.
- **D — Torricelli's Theorem:** Gives the speed of efflux of liquid emerging from an orifice (v = √(2gh)).
- **Exam Extra:** In a hydraulic lift, a small force on a narrow piston produces a magnified force on a wide piston because P = F₁/A₁ = F₂/A₂.

### Q313 · Fluid Mechanics

Why does an iron needle sink in water while a large iron ship floats?

A. Water viscosity is higher under a ship
B. Surface tension supports the ship
C. Iron ships are made of lighter alloys
D. The ship displaces water equal to its own weight

**Answer:** D. The ship displaces water equal to its own weight
