**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — The ship displaces water equal to its own weight (Correct):** By Archimedes' Principle, a hollow ship has an average density lower than water, enabling it to displace a volume of water whose weight equals the total weight of the ship.
- **A — Water viscosity is higher under a ship:** Viscosity depends on the fluid and temperature, not on the size of the immersed object.
- **B — Surface tension supports the ship:** Surface tension can support tiny insects or a gently placed needle, never a heavy ship.
- **C — Iron ships are made of lighter alloys:** Ships are built of steel (denser than water); their hollow shape reduces average density.
- **Exam Extra:** A ship rises slightly when sailing from a freshwater river into seawater because seawater has higher density (~1.025 g/cm³) and exerts greater buoyant thrust.

### Q314 · Fluid Mechanics

A piece of ice is floating in a glass filled completely with water to the brim. What happens to the water level when the ice melts completely?

A. It remains the same
B. It overflows immediately
C. It drops slightly
D. It first rises and then drops

**Answer:** A. It remains the same
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — It remains the same (Correct):** Floating ice displaces a weight of water equal to its own weight; upon melting into water of identical mass, its volume exactly fills the displaced volume, so the water level remains unchanged.
- **B — It overflows immediately:** Would happen if pure ice were floating in a denser liquid like brine (saltwater), not in pure water.
- **C — It drops slightly:** Happens only if a denser object (such as a stone) was embedded in the floating ice and sinks upon melting.
- **D — It first rises and then drops:** Melting of pure ice in pure water maintains equilibrium continuously with no rise or drop.
- **Exam Extra:** Water reaches its maximum density of 1 g/cm³ (1000 kg/m³) at 4 °C and expands on freezing into ice (~0.917 g/cm³).

### Q315 · Fluid Mechanics

During a severe storm, tin roofs of houses are often blown off upward. This is mainly explained by:

A. Hooke's Law
B. Bernoulli's Principle
C. Boyle's Law
D. Stokes' Law

**Answer:** B. Bernoulli's Principle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Bernoulli's Principle (Correct):** High-speed wind over the roof creates a low-pressure zone above it by Bernoulli's Principle, while still air inside maintains higher pressure, pushing the roof upward.
- **A — Hooke's Law:** States that stress is proportional to strain within the elastic limit of a solid.
- **C — Boyle's Law:** States that pressure of a fixed gas mass is inversely proportional to volume at constant temperature.
- **D — Stokes' Law:** Gives the viscous drag force on a small sphere falling through a fluid (F = 6πηrv).
- **Exam Extra:** Bernoulli's Principle expresses conservation of energy for an ideal, incompressible, non-viscous fluid in steady streamline flow.

### Q316 · Fluid Mechanics

Why do small falling raindrops acquire a spherical shape?

A. Due to atmospheric pressure
B. Due to viscosity of air
C. Due to surface tension
D. Due to gravitational pull

**Answer:** C. Due to surface tension
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Due to surface tension (Correct):** Surface tension pulls a liquid surface into the shape with minimum surface area for a given volume, and a sphere has the smallest surface area for any given volume.
- **A — Due to atmospheric pressure:** Atmospheric pressure acts uniformly around the drop and does not minimize surface area.
- **B — Due to viscosity of air:** Air drag slows falling drops and flattens very large drops rather than making them spherical.
- **D — Due to gravitational pull:** Gravity pulls the drop downward rather than shaping it into a sphere.
- **Exam Extra:** Adding soap or detergent to water lowers its surface tension, enabling water to wet fabrics better and remove grease and dirt.

### Q317 · Fluid Mechanics

Oil rises through the wick of an oil lamp and ink is absorbed by blotting paper due to:

A. Osmosis
B. Diffusion
C. Buoyancy
D. Capillary action

**Answer:** D. Capillary action
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Capillary action (Correct):** Capillary action draws liquids upward through narrow pores (in a lamp wick or blotting paper) because adhesive forces between liquid and solid exceed cohesive forces within the liquid.
- **A — Osmosis:** Movement of solvent molecules across a semi-permeable membrane from lower to higher solute concentration.
- **B — Diffusion:** Net movement of particles from a region of higher concentration to lower concentration.
- **C — Buoyancy:** Upward thrust exerted by a fluid on a partially or fully immersed body.
- **Exam Extra:** Farmers plough fields after rainfall to break soil capillaries and prevent underground moisture from evaporating at the surface.

### Q318 · Fluid Mechanics

When the temperature of a liquid increases, what happens to its viscosity?

A. It decreases
B. It increases
C. It remains constant
D. It first increases then decreases

**Answer:** A. It decreases
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — It decreases (Correct):** In liquids, rising temperature increases molecular kinetic energy and weakens intermolecular cohesive forces, so viscosity decreases and the liquid flows more easily.
- **B — It increases:** Viscosity increases with temperature in gases (due to faster molecular momentum exchange), not in liquids.
- **C — It remains constant:** Viscosity of real liquids and gases depends strongly on temperature.
- **D — It first increases then decreases:** Not the behaviour of ordinary Newtonian liquids.
- **Exam Extra:** An ideal fluid has zero viscosity and zero compressibility; the terminal velocity of a falling raindrop is governed by air viscosity (Stokes' Law).

### Q319 · Fluid Mechanics

Which of the following materials is more elastic in physics?

A. Rubber
B. Steel
C. Glass
D. Copper

**Answer:** B. Steel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Steel (Correct):** In physics, elasticity is quantified by Young's modulus (stress/strain); steel requires much greater stress to produce the same strain as rubber, making steel far more elastic.
- **A — Rubber:** Deforms easily under small stress, giving it a much smaller Young's modulus than steel.
- **C — Glass:** Brittle solid with a Young's modulus (~70 GPa) lower than that of steel (~200 GPa).
- **D — Copper:** Has a Young's modulus (~110–130 GPa) lower than that of steel.
- **Exam Extra:** Quartz and phosphor bronze are nearly perfectly elastic materials, whereas putty and wet clay are close to perfectly plastic materials.

### Q320 · Heat & Thermodynamics

At which temperature do the Celsius and Fahrenheit scales show the exact same numerical reading?

A. 0°
B. 32°
C. -40°
D. -273°

**Answer:** C. -40°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — -40° (Correct):** From C/5 = (F − 32)/9, setting C = F gives 9C = 5C − 160, which solves to C = F = −40°.
- **A — 0°:** 0 °C corresponds to 32 °F.
- **B — 32°:** 32 °F corresponds to 0 °C (freezing point of water).
- **D — -273°:** Approximately −273.15 °C is Absolute Zero (0 K), equal to −459.67 °F.
- **Exam Extra:** Normal human body temperature is 37 °C, which corresponds to 98.6 °F or 310.15 K.

### Q321 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Which liquid is preferred in thermometers used in extremely cold polar regions?

A. Mercury
B. Water
C. Glycerine
D. Alcohol

**Answer:** D. Alcohol
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Alcohol (Correct):** Alcohol freezes at about −114 °C whereas mercury freezes at −39 °C, making alcohol thermometers suitable for extremely cold polar climates.
- **A — Mercury:** Freezes at −38.8 °C, solidifying in extreme polar winters.
- **B — Water:** Freezes at 0 °C and shows anomalous expansion between 0 °C and 4 °C.
- **C — Glycerine:** Freezes around +18 °C and is too viscous for rapid thermometric expansion.
- **Exam Extra:** Mercury is preferred in clinical and standard laboratory thermometers (up to ~357 °C) because it does not wet glass and expands uniformly.

### Q322 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Aquatic animals survive beneath frozen lakes during severe winter mainly because:

A. Water has maximum density at 4 °C
B. Ice is a good conductor of heat
C. Salt lowers the freezing point of all lakes
D. Water freezes from bottom to top

**Answer:** A. Water has maximum density at 4 °C
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Water has maximum density at 4 °C (Correct):** Water has its maximum density at 4 °C; as surface water cools from 4 °C to 0 °C, it becomes lighter and stays on top to freeze, leaving denser 4 °C liquid water at the bottom.
- **B — Ice is a good conductor of heat:** Ice is actually a poor thermal conductor, insulating the water beneath from freezing air.
- **C — Salt lowers the freezing point of all lakes:** Freshwater lakes lack high salinity; survival relies on water's anomalous expansion.
- **D — Water freezes from bottom to top:** Lakes freeze from top to bottom because ice is less dense than liquid water.
- **Exam Extra:** Between 0 °C and 4 °C, water contracts on heating and expands on cooling—known as the anomalous expansion of water.

### Q323 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Why does steam at 100 °C cause more severe burns than boiling water at 100 °C?

A. Steam travels faster than water
B. Steam possesses extra latent heat of vaporisation
C. Steam has higher specific heat capacity
D. Boiling water evaporates instantly on skin

**Answer:** B. Steam possesses extra latent heat of vaporisation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Steam possesses extra latent heat of vaporisation (Correct):** Steam at 100 °C carries about 540 cal/g (2260 J/g) of extra latent heat of vaporisation compared to boiling water at 100 °C, releasing all of it upon condensing on skin.
- **A — Steam travels faster than water:** Contact speed does not explain the huge thermal energy released per gram.
- **C — Steam has higher specific heat capacity:** Specific heat of liquid water (~4186 J/kg·K) is higher than that of steam (~2010 J/kg·K).
- **D — Boiling water evaporates instantly on skin:** Water cools when contacting cooler skin rather than releasing condensation latent heat.
- **Exam Extra:** Liquid water has the highest specific heat capacity among common substances (4186 J/kg·K), making it ideal as an engine radiator coolant.

### Q324 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Food cooks faster in a pressure cooker because:

A. Trapped steam reduces the boiling point of water
B. Heat cannot escape through the aluminium walls
C. Increased pressure raises the boiling point of water
D. Latent heat of water increases inside the cooker

**Answer:** C. Increased pressure raises the boiling point of water
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Increased pressure raises the boiling point of water (Correct):** Trapped steam raises the internal pressure inside the cooker (to ~2 atm), increasing the boiling point of water to about 120 °C so food cooks faster at a higher temperature.
- **A — Trapped steam reduces the boiling point of water:** Reducing pressure lowers the boiling point, which would slow down cooking.
- **B — Heat cannot escape through the aluminium walls:** Aluminium is a good thermal conductor, not a heat insulator.
- **D — Latent heat of water increases inside the cooker:** Latent heat of vaporisation actually decreases slightly as pressure and boiling temperature rise.
- **Exam Extra:** At high mountain altitudes, lower atmospheric pressure lowers the boiling point of water below 100 °C, slowing cooking in open pots.

### Q325 · Heat & Thermodynamics

By which mode of heat transfer does heat energy from the Sun reach the Earth?

A. Conduction
B. Convection
C. Advection
D. Radiation

**Answer:** D. Radiation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Radiation (Correct):** Radiation transfers heat via electromagnetic waves (primarily infrared and visible radiation) without needing any material medium, allowing solar heat to cross the vacuum of space.
- **A — Conduction:** Heat transfer through direct particle collisions in solids without bulk movement of matter.
- **B — Convection:** Heat transfer via bulk circulation of fluids (liquids and gases), driving land and sea breezes.
- **C — Advection:** Horizontal transport of heat or matter by wind or ocean currents.
- **Exam Extra:** A Thermos (Dewar) flask minimizes all three modes of heat transfer: vacuum stops conduction and convection, while silvered walls reflect radiation.

### Q326 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Which physical law explains why good absorbers of heat are also good emitters of heat?

A. Kirchhoff's Law of Thermal Radiation
B. Stefan-Boltzmann Law
C. Newton's Law of Cooling
D. Wien's Displacement Law

**Answer:** A. Kirchhoff's Law of Thermal Radiation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Kirchhoff's Law of Thermal Radiation (Correct):** Kirchhoff's Law of Thermal Radiation states that at thermal equilibrium, a body's emissivity equals its absorptivity—meaning good absorbers are also good emitters.
- **B — Stefan-Boltzmann Law:** States that total radiant power emitted per unit area is proportional to T⁴ (E = σT⁴).
- **C — Newton's Law of Cooling:** States that the rate of cooling of a body is proportional to the temperature difference with its surroundings.
- **D — Wien's Displacement Law:** States that the peak emitted wavelength of a blackbody is inversely proportional to its absolute temperature (λₘT = constant).
- **Exam Extra:** White clothes are worn in summer because they reflect most sunlight, whereas dark clothes absorb more radiant heat in winter.

### Q327 · Heat & Thermodynamics

Which law of thermodynamics states that heat cannot spontaneously flow from a colder body to a hotter body without external work?

A. Zeroth Law of Thermodynamics
B. Second Law of Thermodynamics
C. First Law of Thermodynamics
D. Third Law of Thermodynamics

**Answer:** B. Second Law of Thermodynamics
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Second Law of Thermodynamics (Correct):** The Clausius statement of the Second Law of Thermodynamics states that heat cannot flow spontaneously from a colder body to a hotter body without external work (the operating principle of a refrigerator).
- **A — Zeroth Law of Thermodynamics:** Defines thermal equilibrium and forms the basis of temperature measurement.
- **C — First Law of Thermodynamics:** Expresses conservation of energy (ΔQ = ΔU + ΔW).
- **D — Third Law of Thermodynamics:** States that the entropy of a perfect crystal approaches zero as temperature approaches 0 K.
- **Exam Extra:** Leaving a refrigerator door open inside a closed room actually heats the room overall because the compressor motor adds more heat than it removes.

### Q328 · Heat & Thermodynamics

What is the process called in which a solid changes directly into vapour without passing through the liquid state?

A. Condensation
B. Vaporisation
C. Sublimation
D. Regelation

**Answer:** C. Sublimation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Sublimation (Correct):** Sublimation is the direct phase transition from solid to gas without passing through the liquid phase, observed in camphor, naphthalene, iodine, and dry ice (solid CO₂).
- **A — Condensation:** Phase transition from gas (vapour) to liquid.
- **B — Vaporisation:** Phase transition from liquid to gas (either evaporation or boiling).
- **D — Regelation:** Melting of ice under increased pressure and refreezing once pressure is released.
- **Exam Extra:** Skating on ice relies on regelation and frictional melting creating a thin lubricating film of water beneath the skate blade.

### Q329 · Waves & Sound

Sound waves in air are classified as:

A. Transverse mechanical waves
B. Transverse electromagnetic waves
C. Longitudinal electromagnetic waves
D. Longitudinal mechanical waves

**Answer:** D. Longitudinal mechanical waves
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Longitudinal mechanical waves (Correct):** Sound waves require a material medium (mechanical) and air particles vibrate parallel to the direction of wave propagation as compressions and rarefactions (longitudinal).
- **A — Transverse mechanical waves:** Waves in a medium where particles vibrate perpendicular to wave travel (e.g., waves on a stretched string).
- **B — Transverse electromagnetic waves:** Light, radio waves, and X-rays, which propagate even through vacuum.
- **C — Longitudinal electromagnetic waves:** Electromagnetic waves in free space are strictly transverse, not longitudinal.
- **Exam Extra:** Sound cannot travel through a vacuum (such as outer space) because it needs an elastic material medium.

### Q330 · Waves & Sound

In which of the following media does sound travel with the highest speed?

A. Steel
B. Water
C. Air
D. Hydrogen

**Answer:** A. Steel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Steel (Correct):** Sound travels fastest in solids such as steel (~5960 m/s) because solids have much higher elasticity (bulk/Young's modulus) than liquids or gases.
- **B — Water:** Speed of sound in water is roughly 1480–1530 m/s, slower than in steel.
- **C — Air:** Speed of sound in dry air at 20 °C is about 343 m/s.
- **D — Hydrogen:** Fastest among gases (~1284 m/s), yet still much slower than in steel.
- **Exam Extra:** The general order of speed of sound is Solids > Liquids > Gases; in air, speed increases with temperature and humidity, and is independent of pressure at constant temperature.

### Q331 · Waves & Sound

What is the minimum distance required between a listener and a reflecting surface to hear a distinct echo in air at ~20 °C?

A. 10.5 m
B. 17.2 m
C. 34.4 m
D. 50.0 m

**Answer:** B. 17.2 m
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 17.2 m (Correct):** Human persistence of hearing is 0.1 s; at ~344 m/s, the round-trip distance in 0.1 s is 34.4 m, so the reflecting surface must be at least 34.4 / 2 = 17.2 m away.
- **A — 10.5 m:** Gives a round-trip time of ~0.06 s, too brief for the ear to separate the echo from the original sound.
- **C — 34.4 m:** Total round-trip distance travelled by sound in 0.1 s, not the one-way distance to the reflector.
- **D — 50.0 m:** Well above the minimum required distance of 17.2 m.
- **Exam Extra:** Multiple reflections inside large halls cause reverberation, which is reduced using sound-absorbing curtains, acoustic panels, and carpets.

### Q332 · Waves & Sound

Which type of sound waves do bats use to navigate and locate prey in the dark?

A. Infrasonic waves (< 20 Hz)
B. Audible waves (20 Hz – 20 kHz)
C. Ultrasonic waves (> 20 kHz)
D. Shock waves

**Answer:** C. Ultrasonic waves (> 20 kHz)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Ultrasonic waves (> 20 kHz) (Correct):** Bats emit high-frequency Ultrasonic waves (> 20 kHz) and analyse the reflected echoes (echolocation) to navigate and locate small prey in darkness.
- **A — Infrasonic waves (< 20 Hz):** Low-frequency waves generated by earthquakes, whales, and elephants; not used by bats for echolocation.
- **B — Audible waves (20 Hz – 20 kHz):** Human hearing range; their longer wavelengths cannot resolve tiny insects sharply.
- **D — Shock waves:** Pressure waves formed when an object moves faster than the speed of sound (sonic boom).
- **Exam Extra:** Ultrasonic waves are also used in medical ultrasonography, echocardiography, SONAR, and detecting internal flaws in metal blocks.

### Q333 · Waves & Sound

Which physical characteristic of a sound wave determines its pitch (shrillness)?

A. Amplitude
B. Waveform
C. Intensity
D. Frequency

**Answer:** D. Frequency
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Frequency (Correct):** Pitch depends on the frequency of vibration: higher frequency produces a shriller, higher-pitched sound, while lower frequency produces a deeper, bass sound.
- **A — Amplitude:** Determines the loudness of sound (loudness ∝ amplitude²).
- **B — Waveform:** Determines the quality or timbre of sound, distinguishing instruments playing the same note.
- **C — Intensity:** Sound energy crossing unit area per unit time (W/m²), governing loudness rather than pitch.
- **Exam Extra:** Noise levels above 80–85 dB are harmful to human hearing; normal conversation is around 60 dB and a whisper is ~30 dB.

### Q334 · Waves & Sound

An ambulance siren sounds higher in pitch as it approaches a stationary listener and lower as it moves away. This is due to:

A. Doppler Effect
B. Resonance
C. Diffraction of sound
D. Interference

**Answer:** A. Doppler Effect
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Doppler Effect (Correct):** The Doppler Effect is the apparent change in frequency (pitch) of a wave caused by relative motion between the wave source and the observer.
- **B — Resonance:** Large-amplitude oscillation when an external driving frequency matches a system's natural frequency.
- **C — Diffraction of sound:** Bending and spreading of sound waves around corners or obstacles.
- **D — Interference:** Superposition of coherent waves producing constructive and destructive patterns (beats in sound).
- **Exam Extra:** In astronomy, the optical Doppler effect (redshift of light from distant galaxies) provides key evidence for the expanding universe.

### Q335 · Waves & Sound

Why are soldiers ordered to break step while marching across a suspension bridge?

A. To reduce static friction on the bridge
B. To prevent mechanical resonance
C. To lower air resistance
D. To decrease the total weight on the bridge

**Answer:** B. To prevent mechanical resonance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — To prevent mechanical resonance (Correct):** If the rhythmic marching frequency matches the natural frequency of the bridge, mechanical resonance builds up dangerously large oscillations that can collapse the bridge.
- **A — To reduce static friction on the bridge:** Breaking step does not alter static friction meaningfully.
- **C — To lower air resistance:** Air resistance on marching soldiers is negligible compared to structural oscillations.
- **D — To decrease the total weight on the bridge:** The total weight of the soldiers remains the same whether marching in step or out of step.
- **Exam Extra:** Resonance is also the principle used when tuning a radio receiver to match a broadcast station's carrier frequency.

### Q336 · Waves & Sound

Which electromagnetic waves have the highest penetrating power and shortest wavelength?

A. Ultraviolet rays
B. X-rays
C. Gamma rays
D. Microwaves

**Answer:** C. Gamma rays
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Gamma rays (Correct):** Gamma rays (emitted during radioactive nuclear decay) have the highest frequency, highest photon energy, shortest wavelength (< 10⁻¹¹ m), and greatest penetrating power.
- **A — Ultraviolet rays:** Less energetic than X-rays; absorbed by the ozone layer and used in water sterilization.
- **B — X-rays:** Discovered by W. C. Röntgen; penetrate soft tissue for medical imaging but are less penetrating than gamma rays.
- **D — Microwaves:** Longer wavelength (mm to cm) used in radar, satellite links, and microwave ovens.
- **Exam Extra:** In order of increasing frequency (decreasing wavelength): Radio < Microwave < Infrared < Visible < Ultraviolet < X-ray < Gamma ray.

### Q337 · Optics

Which mirror is commonly used as a rear-view mirror in vehicles?

A. Concave mirror
B. Plane mirror
C. Parabolic mirror
D. Convex mirror

**Answer:** D. Convex mirror
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Convex mirror (Correct):** A Convex mirror curves outward and always forms a virtual, erect, and diminished image, providing a much wider field of view of traffic behind the vehicle.
- **A — Concave mirror:** Used in vehicle headlights, shaving mirrors, and dental mirrors to focus beams or magnify close objects.
- **B — Plane mirror:** Forms same-size virtual images with a much narrower field of view.
- **C — Parabolic mirror:** A specialised concave reflector used in searchlights and telescopes to eliminate spherical aberration.
- **Exam Extra:** Because convex mirrors form diminished images, vehicles appear farther away—hence the warning 'Objects in mirror are closer than they appear'.

### Q338 · Optics

Which colour of visible light has the longest wavelength?

A. Red
B. Violet
C. Blue
D. Green

**Answer:** A. Red
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Red (Correct):** Red light has the longest wavelength (~620–750 nm) and the lowest frequency in the visible spectrum (VIBGYOR), so it deviates the least when passing through a prism.
- **B — Violet:** Has the shortest wavelength (~380–450 nm) and highest frequency in the visible spectrum.
- **C — Blue:** Has a short wavelength (~450–495 nm) that is strongly scattered by Earth's atmosphere.
- **D — Green:** Lies near the middle of the visible spectrum (~495–570 nm), where human eyes have peak sensitivity.
- **Exam Extra:** Because red light has the longest wavelength, it undergoes the least Rayleigh scattering (∝ 1/λ⁴) by fog and smoke, making it ideal for danger signals.

### Q339 · Optics

Which mirror is used by dentists to see an enlarged image of a patient's teeth?

A. Convex mirror
B. Concave mirror
C. Plane mirror
D. Cylindrical mirror

**Answer:** B. Concave mirror
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Concave mirror (Correct):** When a tooth lies between the pole and principal focus of a Concave mirror, the mirror forms a virtual, erect, and magnified image for clear inspection.
- **A — Convex mirror:** Always forms diminished images, unsuitable for magnifying small teeth.
- **C — Plane mirror:** Always produces same-size images (magnification = +1).
- **D — Cylindrical mirror:** Distorts images along one axis rather than providing uniform magnification.
- **Exam Extra:** Concave mirrors are also used in solar furnaces to concentrate sunlight and in car headlights as reflectors to emit a parallel beam.

### Q340 · Optics

Optical fibres used in high-speed telecommunications work on the principle of:

A. Dispersion of light
B. Diffraction of light
C. Total Internal Reflection
D. Polarisation of light

**Answer:** C. Total Internal Reflection
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Total Internal Reflection (Correct):** Optical fibres guide light signals inside a high-refractive-index glass core surrounded by lower-index cladding through repeated Total Internal Reflection (TIR) with negligible loss.
- **A — Dispersion of light:** Splitting of white light into its constituent colours (e.g., by a glass prism).
- **B — Diffraction of light:** Bending and spreading of light waves around sharp edges or narrow apertures.
- **D — Polarisation of light:** Restricting transverse light vibrations to a single plane (used in polaroid sunglasses).
- **Exam Extra:** Desert mirages, the sparkling brilliance of cut diamonds, and medical endoscopes also operate on Total Internal Reflection.

### Q341 · Optics

The twinkling of stars at night is caused by:

A. Scattering of starlight
B. Total internal reflection in clouds
C. Dispersion by raindrops
D. Atmospheric refraction of starlight

**Answer:** D. Atmospheric refraction of starlight
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Atmospheric refraction of starlight (Correct):** Starlight travels through atmospheric layers of continuously changing temperature and density, causing fluctuating atmospheric refraction that shifts the apparent position and brightness of point-like stars.
- **A — Scattering of starlight:** Explains the blue daytime sky and reddish sunsets, not stellar twinkling.
- **B — Total internal reflection in clouds:** Clouds block starlight rather than causing twinkling.
- **C — Dispersion by raindrops:** Separates sunlight into colours to form rainbows.
- **Exam Extra:** Planets do not twinkle because they are much closer to Earth and appear as extended discs whose point-to-point fluctuations average out.

### Q342 · Optics

The sky appears blue during a clear day primarily due to:

A. Rayleigh scattering of sunlight
B. Refraction of sunlight by ozone
C. Reflection of ocean water
D. Dispersion by atmospheric dust

**Answer:** A. Rayleigh scattering of sunlight
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Rayleigh scattering of sunlight (Correct):** Atmospheric gas molecules (much smaller than light wavelengths) scatter shorter-wavelength blue/violet light far more strongly than red light (Rayleigh scattering, intensity ∝ 1/λ⁴).
- **B — Refraction of sunlight by ozone:** Atmospheric refraction causes slightly earlier sunrises and later sunsets, not blue sky colour.
- **C — Reflection of ocean water:** Common misconception; the sky looks blue even over vast inland deserts.
- **D — Dispersion by atmospheric dust:** Large dust particles scatter all wavelengths roughly equally (Mie scattering), making hazy skies look whitish.
- **Exam Extra:** To an astronaut in space (with no atmosphere to scatter sunlight), the sky appears completely black even during daytime.

### Q343 · Optics

Which lens is used to correct Myopia (nearsightedness), where distant objects appear blurry?

A. Convex lens
B. Concave lens
C. Bifocal lens
D. Cylindrical lens

**Answer:** B. Concave lens
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Concave lens (Correct):** In Myopia, distant light focuses in front of the retina; a diverging Concave lens spreads incoming rays slightly so that they focus sharply on the retina.
- **A — Convex lens:** Converging lens used to correct Hypermetropia (farsightedness).
- **C — Bifocal lens:** Combines upper concave and lower convex segments to correct Presbyopia.
- **D — Cylindrical lens:** Used to correct Astigmatism caused by uneven corneal curvature.
- **Exam Extra:** Power of a lens P = 1/f (in metres) is measured in Dioptre (D); concave lenses have negative power, while convex lenses have positive power.

### Q344 · Optics

A person who can see distant objects clearly but cannot see nearby objects distinctly suffers from Hypermetropia. Which lens corrects this defect?

A. Concave lens
B. Cylindrical lens
C. Convex lens
D. Plano-concave lens

**Answer:** C. Convex lens
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Convex lens (Correct):** In Hypermetropia (farsightedness), light from nearby objects focuses behind the retina; a converging Convex lens brings the focal point forward onto the retina.
- **A — Concave lens:** Diverging lens used to correct Myopia (nearsightedness).
- **B — Cylindrical lens:** Used to correct Astigmatism.
- **D — Plano-concave lens:** A diverging lens that would push the focus even farther behind the retina.
- **Exam Extra:** For a normal young adult human eye, the least distance of distinct vision (near point) is 25 cm and the far point is infinity.

### Q345 · Optics

Which part of the human eye controls the size of the pupil and regulates the amount of light entering the eye?

A. Cornea
B. Retina
C. Ciliary muscles
D. Iris

**Answer:** D. Iris
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Iris (Correct):** The Iris is the coloured muscular diaphragm behind the cornea that adjusts the diameter of the pupil to control how much light enters the eye.
- **A — Cornea:** Transparent front membrane where most refraction of incoming light takes place.
- **B — Retina:** Light-sensitive inner screen containing rods and cones where real, inverted images are formed.
- **C — Ciliary muscles:** Change the curvature and focal length of the eye lens during accommodation.
- **Exam Extra:** Cone cells in the retina detect colour in bright light, whereas Rod cells detect dim light; Vitamin A deficiency causes night blindness.

### Q346 · Optics

What is the speed of light in vacuum?

A. 3 × 10⁸ m/s
B. 3 × 10⁶ m/s
C. 3 × 10¹⁰ m/s
D. 1.5 × 10⁸ m/s

**Answer:** A. 3 × 10⁸ m/s
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 3 × 10⁸ m/s (Correct):** The speed of light in vacuum (c) is approximately 3 × 10⁸ m/s (precisely 299,792,458 m/s), the maximum possible speed of energy and information.
- **B — 3 × 10⁶ m/s:** Two orders of magnitude slower than the actual speed of light.
- **C — 3 × 10¹⁰ m/s:** Equivalent to 3 × 10¹⁰ cm/s, not m/s.
- **D — 1.5 × 10⁸ m/s:** Half of c; in typical glass (n = 1.5), light travels at 2.0 × 10⁸ m/s, and in diamond (n ≈ 2.42) at ~1.24 × 10⁸ m/s.
- **Exam Extra:** Sunlight takes about 8 minutes 20 seconds (500 s) to reach Earth, while light from the Moon takes about 1.28 seconds.

### Q347 · Optics

A rainbow is formed in the sky after rain due to which combination of optical phenomena in water droplets?

A. Diffraction, polarisation, and absorption
B. Refraction, dispersion, and internal reflection
C. Interference, scattering, and reflection
D. Polarisation, refraction, and scattering

**Answer:** B. Refraction, dispersion, and internal reflection
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Refraction, dispersion, and internal reflection (Correct):** Sunlight refracts and disperses upon entering a raindrop, undergoes internal reflection at the back surface of the drop, and refracts again on exiting toward the observer.
- **A — Diffraction, polarisation, and absorption:** Do not cause primary rainbow formation in raindrops.
- **C — Interference, scattering, and reflection:** Thin-film interference explains iridescent colours in soap bubbles and oil slicks, not rainbows.
- **D — Polarisation, refraction, and scattering:** Scattering explains blue skies and red sunsets rather than rainbows.
- **Exam Extra:** A rainbow always appears in the sky opposite the Sun; in a primary rainbow, red forms the outer arc and violet forms the inner arc.

### Q348 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

Which metal is commonly used to make the filament of an incandescent electric bulb?

A. Nichrome
B. Copper
C. Tungsten
D. Constantan

**Answer:** C. Tungsten
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Tungsten (Correct):** Tungsten (W) is used for incandescent bulb filaments because of its extremely high melting point (~3422 °C) and low rate of evaporation at white-hot temperatures.
- **A — Nichrome:** Nickel-chromium alloy used in heating coils (irons, toasters, geysers) due to high resistivity.
- **B — Copper:** Has very low resistivity and a lower melting point (~1085 °C), used for electrical wiring.
- **D — Constantan:** Copper-nickel alloy used in standard resistors due to its nearly zero temperature coefficient of resistance.
- **Exam Extra:** Incandescent bulbs are filled with chemically inert gases such as Argon and Nitrogen to retard evaporation of the tungsten filament.

### Q349 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

A safety electric fuse wire is always connected in series with the live wire and is made of a material having:

A. High resistance and high melting point
B. Low resistance and high melting point
C. Low resistance and low melting point
D. High resistance and low melting point

**Answer:** D. High resistance and low melting point
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — High resistance and low melting point (Correct):** A fuse wire (usually a lead-tin alloy) must have high resistance (to heat rapidly via I²Rt during overcurrent) and a low melting point (so it melts promptly and breaks the circuit).
- **A — High resistance and high melting point:** Characteristic of heating elements like Nichrome, which must stay intact while red-hot.
- **B — Low resistance and high melting point:** Would allow dangerously high currents to flow without melting.
- **C — Low resistance and low melting point:** Would not generate enough Joule heat rapidly enough to melt reliably on overload.
- **Exam Extra:** Household appliances are wired in parallel so every appliance receives the same mains voltage (220 V, 50 Hz in India) and operates independently.

### Q350 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

Which alloy is used as the heating element in electric irons, geysers, and room heaters?

A. Nichrome
B. Solder
C. Brass
D. Duralumin

**Answer:** A. Nichrome
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Nichrome (Correct):** Nichrome (an alloy of ~80% nickel and ~20% chromium) has high electrical resistivity, a high melting point (~1400 °C), and resists oxidation when heated red-hot.
- **B — Solder:** Low-melting alloy of lead and tin used for joining electronic joints and making fuse wires.
- **C — Brass:** Alloy of copper and zinc used in hardware, fittings, and musical instruments.
- **D — Duralumin:** Lightweight, high-strength aluminium alloy used in aircraft construction.
- **Exam Extra:** According to Joule's Law of Heating, heat generated in a resistor is H = I²Rt, directly proportional to the square of the current.

### Q351 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

A transformer works on the principle of mutual induction and is used to:

A. Convert AC into DC
B. Step up or step down AC voltage
C. Convert DC into AC
D. Store electrical energy

**Answer:** B. Step up or step down AC voltage
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Step up or step down AC voltage (Correct):** A transformer operates by mutual electromagnetic induction between primary and secondary coils to step up or step down alternating current (AC) voltage; it cannot function with steady DC.
- **A — Convert AC into DC:** Performed by a Rectifier using semiconductor diodes.
- **C — Convert DC into AC:** Performed by an Inverter.
- **D — Store electrical energy:** Performed by a Capacitor (in an electric field) or a Battery (as chemical energy).
- **Exam Extra:** Transformer cores are constructed from laminated soft iron sheets to minimize eddy current losses and hysteresis loss.

### Q352 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

An electric generator (dynamo) converts mechanical energy into electrical energy based on:

A. Joule's heating effect
B. Electrolysis
C. Electromagnetic induction
D. Thermionic emission

**Answer:** C. Electromagnetic induction
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Electromagnetic induction (Correct):** Discovered by Michael Faraday in 1831, electromagnetic induction generates an induced electromotive force (EMF) whenever the magnetic flux linked with a coil changes.
- **A — Joule's heating effect:** Converts electrical energy into heat energy in a resistor.
- **B — Electrolysis:** Uses electrical energy to drive non-spontaneous chemical decomposition.
- **D — Thermionic emission:** Release of electrons from a metal surface heated to high temperature.
- **Exam Extra:** Fleming's Right-Hand Rule gives the direction of induced current in a generator, while Fleming's Left-Hand Rule gives the force direction in an electric motor.

### Q353 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

Above the Curie temperature, a ferromagnetic substance such as iron turns into a:

A. Diamagnetic substance
B. Superconductor
C. Permanent magnet
D. Paramagnetic substance

**Answer:** D. Paramagnetic substance
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Paramagnetic substance (Correct):** Above the Curie temperature (770 °C for iron), thermal agitation disrupts aligned magnetic domains, converting a ferromagnetic substance into a paramagnetic substance.
- **A — Diamagnetic substance:** Weakly repelled by magnetic fields (e.g., bismuth, copper, water); ferromagnets do not turn diamagnetic above the Curie point.
- **B — Superconductor:** Exhibits zero electrical resistance and expels magnetic fields (Meissner effect) below a very low critical temperature.
- **C — Permanent magnet:** Made from hard ferromagnetic materials (such as Alnico or steel) below their Curie temperature.
- **Exam Extra:** Soft iron is chosen for electromagnet cores because of its high magnetic permeability and low retentivity (easily magnetised and demagnetised).

### Q354 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

To convert pure silicon into a p-type semiconductor, it is doped with a small amount of:

A. Trivalent impurity like Boron
B. Pentavalent impurity like Arsenic
C. Pentavalent impurity like Phosphorus
D. Inert gas like Argon

**Answer:** A. Trivalent impurity like Boron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Trivalent impurity like Boron (Correct):** Doping pure silicon with a Group 13 trivalent impurity (Boron, Aluminium, Gallium, or Indium) creates electron deficiencies ('holes') as majority charge carriers, yielding a p-type semiconductor.
- **B — Pentavalent impurity like Arsenic:** Group 15 donor impurity with 5 valence electrons; produces an n-type semiconductor.
- **C — Pentavalent impurity like Phosphorus:** Also a pentavalent donor impurity that forms an n-type semiconductor.
- **D — Inert gas like Argon:** Chemically inert and does not bond into the silicon crystal lattice.
- **Exam Extra:** Unlike metals whose resistance rises with temperature, the electrical resistance of semiconductors decreases as temperature increases.

### Q355 · Electricity, Magnetism & Nuclear Physics

The immense energy produced by the Sun and other stars comes from:

A. Nuclear fission of Uranium
B. Nuclear fusion of Hydrogen
C. Combustion of Hydrogen gas
D. Radioactive alpha decay

**Answer:** B. Nuclear fusion of Hydrogen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nuclear fusion of Hydrogen (Correct):** In the Sun's core (~15 million K), hydrogen nuclei (protons) undergo nuclear fusion to form helium nuclei, converting a small mass defect into immense energy via E = mc².
- **A — Nuclear fission of Uranium:** Splitting of heavy nuclei (U-235, Pu-239) used in nuclear power plants and atomic bombs.
- **C — Combustion of Hydrogen gas:** A chemical oxidation reaction incapable of powering stars for billions of years.
- **D — Radioactive alpha decay:** Spontaneous emission of helium nuclei by heavy unstable atoms, far weaker than solar fusion.
- **Exam Extra:** A hydrogen bomb operates on uncontrolled nuclear fusion (triggered by a fission primary), whereas an atomic bomb operates on uncontrolled nuclear fission.

---

## Chapter 6: Chemistry

### Q356 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

Dry ice is the solid form of which substance?

A. Carbon dioxide
B. Nitrogen
C. Ammonia
D. Methane

**Answer:** A. Carbon dioxide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Carbon dioxide (Correct):** Dry ice is solid carbon dioxide (CO₂). At standard atmospheric pressure, it sublimates directly from a solid to a gas at −78.5 °C without leaving a liquid residue.
- **B — Nitrogen:** Freezes into solid nitrogen at −210 °C under normal pressure and is used in liquid form (−196 °C) as a cryogenic coolant rather than dry ice.
- **C — Ammonia:** Forms a pungent liquid/solid (m.p. −77.7 °C) used primarily in industrial refrigeration and fertiliser production, not dry ice.
- **D — Methane:** A greenhouse and fuel gas (primary component of natural gas) that freezes at −182.5 °C and is not known as dry ice.
- **Exam Extra:** Dry ice is widely used for cold-chain refrigeration and theatrical fog effects because it is much colder than water ice and leaves no liquid melt.

### Q357 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

What is the direct transition of a substance from the solid state to the gaseous state without passing through the liquid state called?

A. Condensation
B. Sublimation
C. Deposition
D. Vaporisation

**Answer:** B. Sublimation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sublimation (Correct):** Sublimation is the endothermic phase transition in which a solid converts directly into a gas without passing through an intermediate liquid phase (e.g., camphor, naphthalene, and dry ice).
- **A — Condensation:** The phase change from a gas or vapour directly into a liquid upon cooling or compression.
- **C — Deposition:** The reverse of sublimation, where a gas transforms directly into a solid without becoming a liquid (e.g., frost formation).
- **D — Vaporisation:** The transition from the liquid state to the gaseous state, occurring via evaporation or boiling.
- **Exam Extra:** Iodine, ammonium chloride, and camphor are classic substances separated from non-volatile impurities in the laboratory by sublimation.

### Q358 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

Which gas law states that at constant temperature, the pressure of a given mass of gas is inversely proportional to its volume?

A. Charles's Law
B. Avogadro's Law
C. Boyle's Law
D. Gay-Lussac's Law

**Answer:** C. Boyle's Law
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Boyle's Law (Correct):** Boyle's Law (proposed by Robert Boyle in 1662) states that for a fixed mass of an ideal gas at constant temperature, pressure is inversely proportional to volume (PV = constant).
- **A — Charles's Law:** States that at constant pressure, the volume of a fixed mass of gas is directly proportional to its absolute temperature (V ∝ T).
- **B — Avogadro's Law:** States that equal volumes of all gases at the same temperature and pressure contain equal numbers of molecules (V ∝ n).
- **D — Gay-Lussac's Law:** States that at constant volume, the pressure of a fixed mass of gas is directly proportional to its absolute temperature (P ∝ T).
- **Exam Extra:** Scuba divers rely on Boyle's Law to understand why air pockets expand as water pressure decreases during ascent.

### Q359 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

Milk is an example of which type of colloidal system?

A. Aerosol
B. Sol
C. Gel
D. Emulsion

**Answer:** D. Emulsion
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Emulsion (Correct):** Milk is an oil-in-water emulsion, a colloidal system in which microscopic liquid fat globules (dispersed phase) are dispersed in an aqueous liquid (dispersion medium).
- **A — Aerosol:** A colloid in which solid particles (smoke) or liquid droplets (fog, mist) are dispersed in a gas.
- **B — Sol:** A colloid in which fine solid particles are dispersed in a liquid medium, such as milk of magnesia or muddy water.
- **C — Gel:** A semi-rigid colloidal system where a liquid is dispersed within a continuous solid network, such as cheese or fruit jelly.
- **Exam Extra:** Butter is the opposite type of emulsion (water-in-oil), where tiny water droplets are dispersed inside a continuous fat phase.

### Q360 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

What is the scattering of a beam of light by colloidal particles called?

A. Tyndall effect
B. Raman effect
C. Photoelectric effect
D. Doppler effect

**Answer:** A. Tyndall effect
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Tyndall effect (Correct):** The Tyndall effect is the scattering of a beam of visible light by colloidal particles (1–1000 nm in diameter), making the path of the light beam visible.
- **B — Raman effect:** The inelastic scattering of photons by molecules, resulting in a shift in the frequency/wavelength of scattered light (discovered by C.V. Raman).
- **C — Photoelectric effect:** The emission of electrons from a metal surface when electromagnetic radiation above a threshold frequency strikes it.
- **D — Doppler effect:** The apparent change in frequency or wavelength of a wave for an observer moving relative to the wave source.
- **Exam Extra:** The Tyndall effect distinguishes a true solution (which does not scatter light) from a colloidal dispersion and explains why sunlight beams are visible through mist in a forest.

### Q361 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

Which state of matter consists of super-energetic and super-excited ionised gases?

A. Bose-Einstein condensate
B. Plasma
C. Liquid crystal
D. Supercritical fluid

**Answer:** B. Plasma
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Plasma (Correct):** Plasma is the fourth state of matter, consisting of super-energetic and super-excited ionised gases containing free electrons and positive ions, as found in stars, lightning, and neon signs.
- **A — Bose-Einstein condensate:** The fifth state of matter formed when bosons of extremely low density are cooled to temperatures very close to absolute zero (0 K).
- **C — Liquid crystal:** An intermediate mesophase that flows like a liquid while retaining some long-range molecular order like a crystal (used in LCD screens).
- **D — Supercritical fluid:** A substance held above both its critical temperature and critical pressure where distinct liquid and gas phases cease to exist.
- **Exam Extra:** Fluorescent tubes glow because electricity ionises mercury vapour inside the tube to create glowing plasma, whose UV light excites the phosphor coating.

### Q362 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

According to Graham's Law of Diffusion, the rate of diffusion of a gas is inversely proportional to the square root of its:

A. Temperature
B. Pressure
C. Molar mass
D. Volume

**Answer:** C. Molar mass
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Molar mass (Correct):** Graham's Law of Diffusion states that at constant temperature and pressure, the rate of diffusion (or effusion) of a gas is inversely proportional to the square root of its molar mass (or vapour density).
- **A — Temperature:** Higher absolute temperature increases molecular kinetic energy and speeds up diffusion directly rather than inversely.
- **B — Pressure:** A larger pressure gradient increases the rate of effusion/flow rather than acting as an inverse square-root factor in Graham's law.
- **D — Volume:** Represents the space occupied by the gas container and does not appear as the intrinsic molecular property in Graham's rate ratio.
- **Exam Extra:** Because hydrogen has the lowest molar mass (2 g/mol), it diffuses four times faster than oxygen (32 g/mol) under identical conditions.

### Q363 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

What is the numerical value of Absolute Zero on the Celsius scale?

A. −100 °C
B. −173.15 °C
C. −373.15 °C
D. −273.15 °C

**Answer:** D. −273.15 °C
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — −273.15 °C (Correct):** Absolute zero is 0 Kelvin (0 K), the lowest theoretically attainable temperature where classical thermal motion ceases, which equals −273.15 °C on the Celsius scale.
- **A — −100 °C:** Corresponds to 173.15 K, well above absolute zero and warmer than dry ice sublimation at high pressures.
- **B — −173.15 °C:** Corresponds to exactly 100 K, the boiling region of liquid air components rather than absolute zero.
- **C — −373.15 °C:** Represents −100 K, which is physically impossible on the thermodynamic Kelvin scale.
- **Exam Extra:** To convert temperature from Celsius to Kelvin, add 273.15 (often approximated as 273 in SSC numericals); thus 25 °C equals 298.15 K.

### Q364 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

Which separation technique is used to separate two miscible liquids having a boiling point difference of less than 25 K?

A. Fractional distillation
B. Simple distillation
C. Centrifugation
D. Sublimation

**Answer:** A. Fractional distillation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Fractional distillation (Correct):** Fractional distillation uses a fractionating column to create repeated vaporisation-condensation cycles, separating miscible liquids whose boiling points differ by less than 25 K (such as ethanol and water, or petroleum fractions).
- **B — Simple distillation:** Used to separate liquids with a boiling point difference greater than 25 K, or to recover a volatile solvent from a non-volatile dissolved solid.
- **C — Centrifugation:** Separates suspended denser particles from lighter liquids by spinning at high speed (e.g., separating cream from milk).
- **D — Sublimation:** Separates a sublimable solid (like camphor or ammonium chloride) from a non-sublimable solid impurity (like common salt).
- **Exam Extra:** Crude petroleum is separated into petrol, diesel, kerosene, and LPG in oil refineries via fractional distillation.

### Q365 · States of Matter, Colloids & Gas Laws

What is the approximate numerical value of Avogadro's constant (particles per mole)?

A. 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹
B. 6.022 × 10²³
C. 6.626 × 10⁻³⁴
D. 8.314

**Answer:** B. 6.022 × 10²³
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 6.022 × 10²³ (Correct):** Avogadro's constant (Nₐ) defines the exact number of constituent particles (atoms, molecules, or ions) in one mole of a substance: 6.022 × 10²³ mol⁻¹.
- **A — 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹:** The magnitude of the elementary electric charge of a single electron or proton (in coulombs).
- **C — 6.626 × 10⁻³⁴:** The numerical value of Planck's constant (h) in joule-seconds (J·s), fundamental to quantum mechanics.
- **D — 8.314:** The numerical value of the universal ideal gas constant (R) in J·mol⁻¹·K⁻¹.
- **Exam Extra:** Since the 2019 SI redefinition, Avogadro's constant is fixed at exactly 6.02214076 × 10²³ mol⁻¹.

### Q366 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Who discovered the neutron in 1932?

A. J.J. Thomson
B. Ernest Rutherford
C. James Chadwick
D. Eugen Goldstein

**Answer:** C. James Chadwick
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — James Chadwick (Correct):** James Chadwick discovered the electrically neutral subatomic particle—the neutron—in 1932 by bombarding beryllium with alpha particles, earning the 1935 Nobel Prize in Physics.
- **A — J.J. Thomson:** Discovered the electron in 1897 using cathode-ray tube experiments and proposed the plum-pudding model.
- **B — Ernest Rutherford:** Discovered the dense, positively charged atomic nucleus in 1911 through the gold-foil alpha-scattering experiment.
- **D — Eugen Goldstein:** Discovered positively charged canal rays (anode rays) in gas-discharge tubes in 1886.
- **Exam Extra:** All atomic nuclei contain neutrons except ordinary hydrogen (protium, ¹H), whose nucleus consists of a single proton.

### Q367 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which isotope of carbon is used in radiocarbon dating to determine the age of archaeological fossils?

A. Carbon-11
B. Carbon-12
C. Carbon-13
D. Carbon-14

**Answer:** D. Carbon-14
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Carbon-14 (Correct):** Carbon-14 (¹⁴C) is a radioactive isotope of carbon with a half-life of about 5,730 years; measuring its remaining decay activity in organic remains allows archaeologists to date ancient fossils and artefacts.
- **A — Carbon-11:** A very short-lived artificial radioisotope (half-life ~20 minutes) used as a tracer in medical PET imaging, unsuitable for fossil dating.
- **B — Carbon-12:** The most abundant (~98.9%) stable isotope of carbon, used as the standard reference for defining atomic mass units.
- **C — Carbon-13:** A stable, non-radioactive isotope (~1.1% abundance) widely used in Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy.
- **Exam Extra:** Radiocarbon dating was developed by Willard Libby in the late 1940s, for which he received the 1960 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.

### Q368 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Atoms of different elements that have the same mass number (A) but different atomic numbers (Z) are called:

A. Isobars
B. Isotopes
C. Isotones
D. Isomers

**Answer:** A. Isobars
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Isobars (Correct):** Isobars are atoms of different chemical elements (different atomic numbers Z) that possess the same mass number A (total number of protons + neutrons), such as ⁴⁰Ar and ⁴⁰Ca.
- **B — Isotopes:** Atoms of the same element (same atomic number Z) having different mass numbers A due to differing neutron counts (e.g., ¹H, ²H, ³H).
- **C — Isotones:** Atoms of different elements that contain the exact same number of neutrons (N = A − Z), such as ¹⁴C and ¹⁶O (both have 8 neutrons).
- **D — Isomers:** Molecules having the same molecular formula but different structural arrangements (or nuclei in different excited energy states).
- **Exam Extra:** Because isobars have different atomic numbers (and thus different electron counts), they exhibit distinct chemical properties despite having the same mass number.

### Q369 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which radioactive isotope is commonly used in the treatment of cancer (radiotherapy)?

A. Iodine-131
B. Cobalt-60
C. Sodium-24
D. Uranium-238

**Answer:** B. Cobalt-60
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Cobalt-60 (Correct):** Cobalt-60 (⁶⁰Co) emits high-energy gamma rays and is widely used in external-beam radiotherapy to destroy malignant cancer cells.
- **A — Iodine-131:** A beta- and gamma-emitting radioisotope specifically absorbed by the thyroid gland, used to treat goitre and thyroid cancer.
- **C — Sodium-24:** A short-lived radioisotope injected as saline to trace blood circulation and detect arterial blockages or clots.
- **D — Uranium-238:** A long-lived naturally occurring isotope used in radiometric rock dating and as fertile material in breeder reactors.
- **Exam Extra:** Cobalt-60 is also used in food irradiation and industrial radiography to inspect metal welds for internal flaws.

### Q370 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which quantum number determines the shape of an atomic orbital?

A. Principal quantum number (n)
B. Magnetic quantum number (m)
C. Azimuthal quantum number (l)
D. Spin quantum number (s)

**Answer:** C. Azimuthal quantum number (l)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Azimuthal quantum number (l) (Correct):** The azimuthal (or orbital angular momentum) quantum number (l) defines the three-dimensional shape of an atomic orbital (l = 0 for spherical s, l = 1 for dumbbell p, l = 2 for cloverleaf d).
- **A — Principal quantum number (n):** Determines the main energy level (shell) and approximate size/radius of the orbital.
- **B — Magnetic quantum number (m):** Determines the spatial orientation of an orbital in a magnetic field (values from −l to +l).
- **D — Spin quantum number (s):** Specifies the intrinsic spin orientation of an electron (+½ or −½) rather than orbital geometry.
- **Exam Extra:** For a given principal shell n, the azimuthal quantum number l can take integer values from 0 up to (n − 1).

### Q371 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which subatomic particle carries a negative charge and negligible mass compared to a proton?

A. Proton
B. Neutron
C. Positron
D. Electron

**Answer:** D. Electron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Electron (Correct):** An electron carries a unit negative elementary charge (−1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C) and has a rest mass of only about 1/1836 of a proton's mass, making its mass negligible in atomic mass number calculations.
- **A — Proton:** A positively charged (+1) subatomic particle located inside the nucleus with a relative mass of ~1 u.
- **B — Neutron:** An electrically neutral (0 charge) nucleon inside the nucleus with a relative mass slightly greater than a proton (~1 u).
- **C — Positron:** The antimatter counterpart of the electron, having the same negligible mass but a positive (+1) electric charge.
- **Exam Extra:** Almost the entire mass of an atom is concentrated in its tiny central nucleus made up of protons and neutrons (nucleons).

### Q372 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

What is the maximum number of electrons that can be accommodated in the M-shell (n = 3) of an atom?

A. 18
B. 8
C. 32
D. 2

**Answer:** A. 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 18 (Correct):** By the Bohr-Bury 2n² rule, the maximum number of electrons in shell n is 2n²; for the M-shell (n = 3), 2 × 3² = 18 electrons.
- **B — 8:** The maximum electron capacity of the L-shell (n = 2, since 2 × 2² = 8).
- **C — 32:** The maximum electron capacity of the N-shell (n = 4, since 2 × 4² = 32).
- **D — 2:** The maximum electron capacity of the innermost K-shell (n = 1, since 2 × 1² = 2).
- **Exam Extra:** Even though the M-shell can hold up to 18 electrons, the outermost shell of a stable atom in the ground state cannot hold more than 8 electrons (octet rule).

### Q373 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which type of radiation has the highest penetrating power?

A. Alpha rays
B. Gamma rays
C. Beta rays
D. Cathode rays

**Answer:** B. Gamma rays
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Gamma rays (Correct):** Gamma rays are high-frequency, uncharged electromagnetic photons emitted from excited nuclei; lacking charge and rest mass, they interact weakly with matter and have the highest penetrating power.
- **A — Alpha rays:** Streams of doubly ionised helium nuclei (⁴He²⁺) with very high ionising power but the lowest penetration, stopped by a sheet of paper.
- **C — Beta rays:** Streams of fast-moving nuclear electrons or positrons with moderate penetration, stopped by a thin aluminium sheet.
- **D — Cathode rays:** Beams of electrons accelerated inside vacuum discharge tubes; they have low penetrating power and are not nuclear decay rays.
- **Exam Extra:** Thick lead blocks or several feet of concrete are required to shield effectively against gamma radiation.

### Q374 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Who discovered the phenomenon of natural radioactivity in 1896 using uranium salts?

A. Marie Curie
B. Pierre Curie
C. Henri Becquerel
D. Otto Hahn

**Answer:** C. Henri Becquerel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Henri Becquerel (Correct):** French physicist Henri Becquerel discovered natural radioactivity in 1896 when he observed that uranium salts spontaneously fogged wrapped photographic plates without exposure to sunlight.
- **A — Marie Curie:** Coined the term "radioactivity" and discovered the radioactive elements polonium and radium in 1898.
- **B — Pierre Curie:** Collaborated with Marie Curie on polonium and radium isolation and discovered the Curie temperature in magnetism.
- **D — Otto Hahn:** Discovered nuclear fission of heavy uranium nuclei upon neutron bombardment in 1938 along with Fritz Strassmann.
- **Exam Extra:** The SI unit of radioactivity—one nuclear decay per second—is named the becquerel (Bq) in his honour.

### Q375 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Heavy water, used as a moderator in nuclear reactors, is chemically:

A. Hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂)
B. Tritium oxide (T₂O)
C. Water containing dissolved calcium salts
D. Deuterium oxide (D₂O)

**Answer:** D. Deuterium oxide (D₂O)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Deuterium oxide (D₂O) (Correct):** Heavy water is deuterium oxide (D₂O or ²H₂O), in which ordinary hydrogen (protium) atoms are replaced by the heavier stable isotope deuterium; it slows down fast neutrons without absorbing many of them.
- **A — Hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂):** A pale blue liquid used as a mild antiseptic, oxidising agent, and bleaching agent, not a reactor moderator.
- **B — Tritium oxide (T₂O):** Super-heavy water containing radioactive tritium (³H); it is radioactive and not used as a standard commercial moderator.
- **C — Water containing dissolved calcium salts:** Known as "hard water," which prevents lathering with soap and forms boiler scale, unrelated to isotopic heavy water.
- **Exam Extra:** Heavy water was discovered by Harold Urey in 1931 and is used as both moderator and coolant in India's Pressurised Heavy Water Reactors (PHWRs).

### Q376 · Atomic Structure, Isotopes & Radioactivity

Which principle states that it is impossible to simultaneously determine the exact position and exact momentum of a microscopic particle like an electron?

A. Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle
B. Pauli Exclusion Principle
C. Hund's Rule
D. Aufbau Principle

**Answer:** A. Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle (Correct):** Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle (1927) states that the exact position and exact momentum of a quantum particle cannot be known simultaneously with arbitrary precision (Δx·Δp ≥ h/4π).
- **B — Pauli Exclusion Principle:** States that no two electrons in the same atom can have the identical set of all four quantum numbers.
- **C — Hund's Rule:** States that electrons occupy degenerate orbitals singly with parallel spins before pairing up.
- **D — Aufbau Principle:** States that in the ground state of an atom, electrons fill atomic orbitals in order of increasing energy.
- **Exam Extra:** Because of this principle, electrons in atoms are described by probabilistic orbital clouds rather than fixed circular Bohr trajectories.

### Q377 · Periodic Table & Trends

What is the chemical symbol for sodium?

A. S
B. Si
C. Na
D. K

**Answer:** C. Na
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Na (Correct):** The chemical symbol for sodium is Na, derived from its New Latin name "Natrium" (atomic number 11).
- **A — S:** The chemical symbol for Sulfur (atomic number 16), a yellow non-metal.
- **B — Si:** The chemical symbol for Silicon (atomic number 14), a metalloid widely used in semiconductors.
- **D — K:** The chemical symbol for Potassium (atomic number 19), derived from its Latin name Kalium.
- **Exam Extra:** Eleven elements have symbols derived from their Latin names—such as Na (Natrium), K (Kalium), Fe (Ferrum), Cu (Cuprum), Ag (Argentum), and Au (Aurum).

### Q378 · Periodic Table & Trends

According to the Modern Periodic Law formulated by Henry Moseley, the properties of elements are a periodic function of their:

A. Atomic mass
B. Atomic number
C. Mass number
D. Valency

**Answer:** B. Atomic number
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Atomic number (Correct):** In 1913, Henry Moseley showed through X-ray spectra that atomic number (Z), rather than atomic mass, is the fundamental property determining periodicity: properties of elements are a periodic function of their atomic numbers.
- **A — Atomic mass:** Used by Dmitri Mendeleev in 1869 for his original Periodic Law, which led to minor anomalies in element ordering.
- **C — Mass number:** The sum of protons and neutrons in a specific isotope; it varies among isotopes of the same element.
- **D — Valency:** The combining capacity of an element, which is itself a periodic property determined by valence electrons rather than the ordering basis.
- **Exam Extra:** Using atomic number resolved Mendeleev's inverted pairs—such as Argon (Z=18, mass 39.95) before Potassium (Z=19, mass 39.10).

### Q379 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which element has the highest electronegativity on the Pauling scale?

A. Oxygen
B. Chlorine
C. Fluorine
D. Nitrogen

**Answer:** C. Fluorine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Fluorine (Correct):** Fluorine (F) has the highest electronegativity of all elements, assigned a value of 3.98 (often rounded to 4.0) on the Pauling scale due to its small atomic radius and high effective nuclear charge.
- **A — Oxygen:** The second most electronegative element (3.44 on the Pauling scale), forming hydrogen bonds and oxides with nearly all elements.
- **B — Chlorine:** Has an electronegativity of 3.16 on the Pauling scale (lower than fluorine), though it holds the highest electron gain enthalpy.
- **D — Nitrogen:** Has an electronegativity of 3.04 on the Pauling scale, important in ammonia and biological amino acids.
- **Exam Extra:** Caesium (Cs) and Francium (Fr) at the bottom-left of the periodic table have the lowest electronegativity (~0.79 and ~0.7).

### Q380 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which element exhibits the highest electron gain enthalpy (electron affinity) in the periodic table?

A. Fluorine
B. Bromine
C. Iodine
D. Chlorine

**Answer:** D. Chlorine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Chlorine (Correct):** Chlorine (Cl) has the highest negative electron gain enthalpy (electron affinity) in the periodic table because incoming electrons experience less inter-electronic repulsion in its larger 3p subshell compared to fluorine's compact 2p subshell.
- **A — Fluorine:** Has slightly lower electron gain enthalpy than chlorine because adding an electron into its tiny 2p orbital causes strong repulsion.
- **B — Bromine:** A reddish-brown liquid halogen whose electron gain enthalpy is lower than both chlorine and fluorine due to larger atomic size.
- **C — Iodine:** Has the lowest electron affinity among the stable halogens because its valence 5p shell lies farthest from the nucleus.
- **Exam Extra:** Despite chlorine having higher electron affinity, fluorine remains the stronger oxidising agent in aqueous solution due to its low F–F bond dissociation energy and high hydration enthalpy.

### Q381 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which group of the Modern Periodic Table is known as the Noble Gases?

A. Group 18
B. Group 17
C. Group 16
D. Group 1

**Answer:** A. Group 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Group 18 (Correct):** Group 18 of the Modern Periodic Table comprises the Noble Gases (He, Ne, Ar, Kr, Xe, Rn, Og), characterised by completely filled valence shells and extreme chemical stability.
- **B — Group 17:** Known as the Halogens (F, Cl, Br, I, At), reactive non-metals that form salts with metals.
- **C — Group 16:** Known as the Chalcogens (O, S, Se, Te, Po), meaning ore-forming elements.
- **D — Group 1:** Known as the Alkali Metals (Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs, Fr, excluding hydrogen), highly reactive metals with +1 valency.
- **Exam Extra:** Because noble gases are monoatomic and unreactive under normal conditions, they were formerly called "inert gases" (Group 0 in Mendeleev's later table).

### Q382 · Periodic Table & Trends

Who proposed the Law of Octaves, where every eighth element shared properties similar to the first when arranged by atomic mass?

A. Dmitri Mendeleev
B. John Newlands
C. Johann Dobereiner
D. Lothar Meyer

**Answer:** B. John Newlands
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — John Newlands (Correct):** English chemist John Newlands proposed the Law of Octaves in 1865, noting that when elements were arranged by increasing atomic mass, every eighth element repeated properties similar to the first, like musical notes.
- **A — Dmitri Mendeleev:** Formulated the Periodic Table in 1869 and famously left blank spaces predicting undiscovered elements like gallium (eka-aluminium) and germanium (eka-silicon).
- **C — Johann Dobereiner:** Proposed the Law of Triads (1817–1829), grouping three similar elements where the middle element's atomic mass was roughly the average of the other two.
- **D — Lothar Meyer:** Independently plotted atomic volume against atomic weight in 1869, demonstrating periodic trends graphically.
- **Exam Extra:** Newlands' Law of Octaves worked well only up to Calcium (Ca) and did not accommodate the later-discovered noble gases.

### Q383 · Periodic Table & Trends

Moving from left to right across a period in the Modern Periodic Table, the atomic radius of main-group elements generally:

A. Increases
B. Remains constant
C. Decreases
D. Doubles

**Answer:** C. Decreases
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Decreases (Correct):** Moving left to right across a period, electrons are added to the same valence shell while nuclear charge (protons) increases, pulling the electron cloud closer and causing atomic radius to decrease.
- **A — Increases:** Atomic radius increases down a group from top to bottom as new electron shells are added, not across a period.
- **B — Remains constant:** Effective nuclear charge rises across a period, preventing the atomic radius from staying constant.
- **D — Doubles:** No periodic trend causes atomic radius to double uniformly across a period.
- **Exam Extra:** Down a group, atomic radius increases because new principal electron shells are added, outweighing the increased nuclear charge.

### Q384 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which non-metal is liquid at standard room temperature?

A. Chlorine
B. Iodine
C. Sulfur
D. Bromine

**Answer:** D. Bromine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Bromine (Correct):** Bromine (Br, atomic number 35) is the only non-metal that exists as a reddish-brown liquid at standard room temperature (m.p. −7.2 °C, b.p. 58.8 °C).
- **A — Chlorine:** A greenish-yellow gas at standard room temperature and pressure (b.p. −34 °C).
- **B — Iodine:** A dark violet-black crystalline solid at room temperature that sublimes readily upon gentle heating.
- **C — Sulfur:** A brittle, yellow non-metallic solid at room temperature (m.p. 115.2 °C).
- **Exam Extra:** The name "bromine" comes from the Greek word "bromos," meaning stench, referring to its sharp, irritating odour.

### Q385 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which element is represented by the chemical symbol W?

A. Tungsten
B. Tin
C. Titanium
D. Thorium

**Answer:** A. Tungsten
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Tungsten (Correct):** The chemical symbol W (atomic number 74) stands for Tungsten, taken from its older German/mineral name "Wolfram" (wolframite ore).
- **B — Tin:** Represented by the symbol Sn (from Latin "Stannum", atomic number 50).
- **C — Titanium:** Represented by the symbol Ti (atomic number 22), a strong lightweight transition metal.
- **D — Thorium:** Represented by the symbol Th (atomic number 90), a weakly radioactive actinide abundant in India's monazite sands.
- **Exam Extra:** Tungsten has the highest melting point of all pure metals (3,422 °C), making it ideal for incandescent light bulb filaments.

### Q386 · Periodic Table & Trends

Elements belonging to Group 17 of the Modern Periodic Table are collectively called:

A. Chalcogens
B. Halogens
C. Pnictogens
D. Alkaline earth metals

**Answer:** B. Halogens
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Halogens (Correct):** Group 17 elements (F, Cl, Br, I, At, Ts) are called Halogens—meaning "salt-producers"—because they readily react with metals to form ionic halide salts such as NaCl and KBr.
- **A — Chalcogens:** The elements of Group 16 (oxygen, sulfur, selenium, tellurium, polonium).
- **C — Pnictogens:** The elements of Group 15 (nitrogen, phosphorus, arsenic, antimony, bismuth).
- **D — Alkaline earth metals:** The elements of Group 2 (beryllium, magnesium, calcium, strontium, barium, radium).
- **Exam Extra:** Astatine (At) is the rarest naturally occurring halogen and is radioactive.

### Q387 · Periodic Table & Trends

Which noble gas is the most abundant in Earth's dry atmosphere?

A. Helium
B. Neon
C. Argon
D. Krypton

**Answer:** C. Argon
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Argon (Correct):** Argon (Ar) makes up about 0.93% of Earth's dry atmosphere by volume, making it the third most abundant atmospheric gas overall (after N₂ and O₂) and the most abundant noble gas.
- **A — Helium:** Accounts for only ~0.0005% of Earth's atmosphere because it is light enough to escape Earth's gravity, though it is the second most abundant element in the universe.
- **B — Neon:** Makes up only ~0.0018% of Earth's dry atmosphere and is famous for glowing reddish-orange in discharge signs.
- **D — Krypton:** A trace noble gas present at ~0.00011% in Earth's atmosphere, used in high-speed flash photography and specialised lamps.
- **Exam Extra:** Most terrestrial argon (Argon-40) is formed over geological time by the radioactive decay of Potassium-40 in Earth's crust.

### Q388 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

A solution with a pH below 7 at 25 °C is generally:

A. Alkaline
B. Neutral
C. Amphoteric
D. Acidic

**Answer:** D. Acidic
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Acidic (Correct):** On the pH scale (0–14 at 25 °C), values below 7 indicate an excess of hydrogen/hydronium ions (H⁺ / H₃O⁺) over hydroxide ions (OH⁻), making the solution acidic.
- **A — Alkaline:** Describes a basic aqueous solution with a pH greater than 7 at 25 °C, where [OH⁻] exceeds [H⁺].
- **B — Neutral:** Describes a solution with an exact pH of 7 at 25 °C (such as pure water), where [H⁺] equals [OH⁻].
- **C — Amphoteric:** Describes a substance (such as Al₂O₃ or ZnO) capable of reacting chemically as both an acid and a base, rather than a pH range below 7.
- **Exam Extra:** The pH scale was introduced by Danish biochemist Søren Sørensen in 1909, where pH = −log₁₀[H⁺].

### Q389 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which gas is released when an acid reacts with a carbonate?

A. Carbon dioxide
B. Hydrogen
C. Oxygen
D. Nitrogen

**Answer:** A. Carbon dioxide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Carbon dioxide (Correct):** When an acid reacts with a metal carbonate or bicarbonate, it produces a salt, water, and carbon dioxide (CO₂) gas with brisk effervescence.
- **B — Hydrogen:** Released when dilute acids react with active metals (such as Zn + 2HCl → ZnCl₂ + H₂), burning with a characteristic "pop" sound.
- **C — Oxygen:** Released during photosynthesis and hydrogen peroxide decomposition, not during acid-carbonate reactions.
- **D — Nitrogen:** An inert diatomic gas making up ~78% of air, not generated when acids react with carbonates.
- **Exam Extra:** Passing this evolved CO₂ gas through freshly prepared limewater (Ca(OH)₂) turns it milky due to insoluble calcium carbonate (CaCO₃) formation.

### Q390 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

What is the pH value of pure water at 25 °C?

A. 5
B. 7
C. 9
D. 0

**Answer:** B. 7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 7 (Correct):** Pure water undergoes slight self-ionisation at 25 °C such that [H⁺] = [OH⁻] = 10⁻⁷ M, giving it a neutral pH of 7.
- **A — 5:** Strongly to moderately acidic; rainwater becomes acidic (~5.6 or below) due to dissolved CO₂ or pollutants.
- **C — 9:** Alkaline; typical of mild bases such as aqueous baking soda (NaHCO₃ ≈ 8.3) or dilute ammonia.
- **D — 0:** Extremely acidic, corresponding to a 1 M solution of a strong monoprotic acid such as hydrochloric acid (HCl).
- **Exam Extra:** Human blood is slightly alkaline with a tightly regulated normal pH range of 7.35 to 7.45.

### Q391 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which acid is predominantly present in vinegar?

A. Citric acid
B. Tartaric acid
C. Acetic acid
D. Oxalic acid

**Answer:** C. Acetic acid
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Acetic acid (Correct):** Vinegar is a dilute aqueous solution (typically 5–8%) of acetic acid (ethanoic acid, CH₃COOH), produced by the fermentation of ethanol by acetic acid bacteria.
- **A — Citric acid:** A weak tricarboxylic acid naturally found in citrus fruits like lemons, limes, and oranges.
- **B — Tartaric acid:** An organic acid naturally present in tamarind, unripe mangoes, and grapes, also used in baking powder.
- **D — Oxalic acid:** A dicarboxylic acid found abundantly in tomatoes and spinach.
- **Exam Extra:** Pure, water-free acetic acid freezes at 16.6 °C into ice-like crystals and is therefore called "glacial acetic acid."

### Q392 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which acid is injected into the skin during an ant sting?

A. Lactic acid
B. Butyric acid
C. Malic acid
D. Methanoic acid

**Answer:** D. Methanoic acid
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Methanoic acid (Correct):** An ant sting (as well as stinging nettle leaves) injects methanoic acid (commonly known as formic acid, HCOOH), causing sharp pain and irritation.
- **A — Lactic acid:** Present in sour milk/curd and accumulates in human muscles during intense anaerobic exercise, causing cramps.
- **B — Butyric acid:** The fatty acid responsible for the characteristic smell of rancid butter.
- **C — Malic acid:** The natural organic acid primarily found in apples and pears.
- **Exam Extra:** Rubbing a mild base like baking soda (NaHCO₃) or calamine solution (zinc carbonate) on an ant sting neutralises the methanoic acid and relieves pain.

### Q393 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Aqua regia, used to dissolve gold and platinum, is a freshly prepared mixture of concentrated hydrochloric acid and concentrated nitric acid in the ratio of:

A. 3 : 1
B. 1 : 3
C. 2 : 1
D. 1 : 2

**Answer:** A. 3 : 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 3 : 1 (Correct):** Aqua regia ("royal water") is a freshly prepared mixture of concentrated hydrochloric acid (HCl) and concentrated nitric acid (HNO₃) in a 3 : 1 volume/molar ratio, capable of dissolving noble metals like gold and platinum.
- **B — 1 : 3:** Reverses the required proportion; excess nitric acid without 3 parts HCl cannot supply enough chloride ions to dissolve gold efficiently.
- **C — 2 : 1:** Incorrect stoichiometric ratio that fails to match the 3 HCl : 1 HNO₃ balance needed for optimal aqua regia reaction.
- **D — 1 : 2:** Incorrect inverted ratio lacking sufficient hydrochloric acid for chloroaurate complex formation.
- **Exam Extra:** Neither acid alone can dissolve gold; together they generate nitrosyl chloride (NOCl) and nascent chlorine that oxidise and complex gold into soluble chloroauric acid (HAuCl₄).

### Q394 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

What is the chemical formula of Plaster of Paris?

A. CaSO₄ · 2H₂O
B. CaSO₄ · ½H₂O
C. CaCO₃
D. CaOCl₂

**Answer:** B. CaSO₄ · ½H₂O
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
