- **B — CaSO₄ · ½H₂O (Correct):** Plaster of Paris is calcium sulfate hemihydrate (CaSO₄ · ½H₂O), prepared by heating gypsum at 373 K (100 °C) so that it loses three-quarters of its water of crystallisation.
- **A — CaSO₄ · 2H₂O:** The chemical formula of Gypsum (calcium sulfate dihydrate), the raw mineral from which Plaster of Paris is made.
- **C — CaCO₃:** The chemical formula of calcium carbonate, found naturally as limestone, chalk, and marble.
- **D — CaOCl₂:** The chemical formula of bleaching powder (calcium oxychloride).
- **Exam Extra:** When mixed with water, Plaster of Paris sets rapidly back into a hard mass of gypsum and is used for setting fractured bones, making casts, and smoothing walls.

### Q395 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Washing soda is chemically known as:

A. Sodium hydrogen carbonate
B. Sodium hydroxide
C. Sodium carbonate decahydrate
D. Calcium sulfate

**Answer:** C. Sodium carbonate decahydrate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Sodium carbonate decahydrate (Correct):** Washing soda is sodium carbonate decahydrate (Na₂CO₃ · 10H₂O), an alkaline salt obtained by recrystallising anhydrous sodium carbonate (soda ash) from water.
- **A — Sodium hydrogen carbonate:** Known as baking soda (NaHCO₃), used in cooking, antacids, and soda-acid fire extinguishers.
- **B — Sodium hydroxide:** Known as caustic soda (NaOH), a strong alkali used in soap-making, paper, and textiles.
- **D — Calcium sulfate:** The parent salt of gypsum and Plaster of Paris, not washing soda.
- **Exam Extra:** Washing soda is used to remove permanent hardness of water by precipitating dissolved calcium and magnesium ions as insoluble carbonates.

### Q396 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which chemical compound is commonly known as Bleaching Powder?

A. Sodium hypochlorite (NaOCl)
B. Calcium chloride (CaCl₂)
C. Calcium hydroxide [Ca(OH)₂]
D. Calcium oxychloride (CaOCl₂)

**Answer:** D. Calcium oxychloride (CaOCl₂)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Calcium oxychloride (CaOCl₂) (Correct):** Bleaching powder is calcium oxychloride (CaOCl₂), manufactured by passing dry chlorine gas over dry slaked lime [Ca(OH)₂].
- **A — Sodium hypochlorite (NaOCl):** The active ingredient in liquid household bleach solutions, not solid bleaching powder.
- **B — Calcium chloride (CaCl₂):** A hygroscopic salt used as a laboratory drying agent and road de-icer.
- **C — Calcium hydroxide [Ca(OH)₂]:** Known as slaked lime or limewater, which serves as the reactant (not the product) in bleaching powder manufacture.
- **Exam Extra:** Besides bleaching cotton and wood pulp, bleaching powder is widely used to disinfect drinking water by releasing active chlorine.

### Q397 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

What colour does blue litmus paper turn when dipped into an acidic solution?

A. Red
B. Green
C. Yellow
D. Colourless

**Answer:** A. Red
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Red (Correct):** Litmus is a natural acid-base indicator extracted from lichens; acidic solutions turn blue litmus paper red, whereas basic solutions turn red litmus paper blue.
- **B — Green:** Shown by universal indicator paper around neutral pH 7, not by blue litmus in acid.
- **C — Yellow:** Shown by methyl orange in basic solutions (and by turmeric in neutral/acidic media), not by litmus in acid.
- **D — Colourless:** Characteristic of phenolphthalein indicator in acidic and neutral solutions.
- **Exam Extra:** In its neutral purple form, litmus dye stays purple in distilled water (pH 7).

### Q398 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which type of chemical bond is formed by the mutual sharing of electron pairs between two non-metal atoms?

A. Ionic bond
B. Covalent bond
C. Metallic bond
D. Hydrogen bond

**Answer:** B. Covalent bond
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Covalent bond (Correct):** A covalent bond is formed by the mutual sharing of one or more pairs of valence electrons between two atoms (typically non-metals) so that both achieve a stable noble-gas configuration.
- **A — Ionic bond:** Formed by the complete transfer of electrons from an electropositive metal to an electronegative non-metal, creating oppositely charged ions.
- **C — Metallic bond:** Formed by the electrostatic attraction between positively charged metal ion cores and a surrounding sea of delocalised valence electrons.
- **D — Hydrogen bond:** A weak intermolecular attraction between a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to F, O, or N and a nearby lone pair on another electronegative atom.
- **Exam Extra:** Carbon forms four covalent bonds (tetravalency) and shows catenation, giving rise to millions of organic compounds.

### Q399 · Bonding, Acids, Bases, pH & Salts

Which acid is naturally present in tomatoes and spinach and is used to remove rust stains from clothes?

A. Formic acid
B. Ascorbic acid
C. Oxalic acid
D. Maleic acid

**Answer:** C. Oxalic acid
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Oxalic acid (Correct):** Oxalic acid (H₂C₂O₄) occurs naturally in tomatoes and spinach and reacts with insoluble ferric oxide (rust) to form soluble iron-oxalate complexes, making it effective for removing rust stains.
- **A — Formic acid:** Methanoic acid (HCOOH), found in ant and nettle stings rather than tomatoes or spinach.
- **B — Ascorbic acid:** Vitamin C, found in amla and citrus fruits; it is an antioxidant rather than the primary rust-removing acid in spinach.
- **D — Maleic acid:** A synthetic unsaturated dicarboxylic acid used in resins and polymers, not a major natural acid of tomatoes.
- **Exam Extra:** Excess intake of oxalic acid-rich foods can contribute to calcium oxalate kidney stones in susceptible individuals.

### Q400 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Which metal is liquid at ordinary room temperature?

A. Gallium
B. Sodium
C. Lead
D. Mercury

**Answer:** D. Mercury
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Mercury (Correct):** Mercury (Hg) is the only metallic element that is liquid at ordinary room temperature (20–25 °C), melting at −38.83 °C due to relativistic effects weakening its metallic bonding.
- **A — Gallium:** A solid metal at 25 °C that melts slightly above room temperature at 29.76 °C (it melts when held in a warm hand).
- **B — Sodium:** A soft silvery solid alkali metal at room temperature with a melting point of 97.8 °C.
- **C — Lead:** A dense, malleable solid metal at room temperature with a melting point of 327.5 °C.
- **Exam Extra:** Because of its high density (13.6 g/cm³) and uniform thermal expansion, mercury is traditionally used in barometers and clinical/laboratory thermometers.

### Q401 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Rusting of iron requires oxygen and:

A. Water or moisture
B. Carbon dioxide
C. Nitrogen
D. Argon

**Answer:** A. Water or moisture
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Water or moisture (Correct):** Rusting is the electrochemical oxidation of iron to hydrated iron(III) oxide (Fe₂O₃ · xH₂O), which strictly requires the simultaneous presence of both oxygen (from air) and water or moisture.
- **B — Carbon dioxide:** Reacts with moist copper to form green basic copper carbonate patina, but iron can rust in CO₂-free oxygenated water.
- **C — Nitrogen:** An unreactive atmospheric gas that dilutes air and does not participate in the corrosion of iron.
- **D — Argon:** A chemically inert noble gas present in air that cannot oxidise or corrode iron.
- **Exam Extra:** Salt (electrolytes) or acidic pollutants in moisture dramatically accelerate rusting by increasing water's electrical conductivity.

### Q402 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Which process involves coating iron or steel with a protective layer of zinc to prevent rusting?

A. Electroplating with tin
B. Galvanisation
C. Anodising
D. Vulcanisation

**Answer:** B. Galvanisation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Galvanisation (Correct):** Galvanisation coats iron or steel with a thin layer of zinc; because zinc is more reactive than iron, it provides sacrificial protection even if the coating gets scratched.
- **A — Electroplating with tin:** Known as tinning (used in food cans), which protects iron only as long as the less-reactive tin layer remains unbroken.
- **C — Anodising:** An electrolytic process that thickens the protective oxide layer (Al₂O₃) on aluminium, not zinc-coating on iron.
- **D — Vulcanisation:** A chemical process in which natural rubber is heated with sulfur to improve its elasticity and strength.
- **Exam Extra:** The process is named after Italian scientist Luigi Galvani, whose bioelectricity work inspired electrochemical metal protection.

### Q403 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Brass is an alloy primarily composed of which two metals?

A. Copper and Tin
B. Lead and Tin
C. Copper and Zinc
D. Aluminium and Copper

**Answer:** C. Copper and Zinc
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Copper and Zinc (Correct):** Brass is a widely used copper alloy consisting primarily of Copper (typically ~60–70%) and Zinc (~30–40%).
- **A — Copper and Tin:** The two primary constituents of Bronze, not Brass.
- **B — Lead and Tin:** The two primary constituents of traditional electrical Solder.
- **D — Aluminium and Copper:** The main metals in Duralumin (along with small amounts of magnesium and manganese), used in aircraft bodies.
- **Exam Extra:** Because of its acoustic resonance and malleability, brass is extensively used in musical instruments, decorative hardware, and cartridge casings.

### Q404 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Bronze is an alloy of:

A. Copper and Zinc
B. Iron and Chromium
C. Nickel and Copper
D. Copper and Tin

**Answer:** D. Copper and Tin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Copper and Tin (Correct):** Bronze is one of humanity's oldest alloys, made primarily of Copper (around 88–90%) and Tin (around 10–12%).
- **A — Copper and Zinc:** Forms Brass, which has a brighter yellow-gold appearance than reddish-brown bronze.
- **B — Iron and Chromium:** The essential base components of Stainless Steel.
- **C — Nickel and Copper:** Forms Cupronickel (used in coins) and Monel alloys.
- **Exam Extra:** Bell metal, used to cast temple and church bells, is a special high-tin bronze (~78% Cu, ~22% Sn) prized for its clear ringing tone.

### Q405 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Bauxite is the principal ore of which metal?

A. Aluminium
B. Iron
C. Copper
D. Zinc

**Answer:** A. Aluminium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Aluminium (Correct):** Bauxite (hydrated aluminium oxide, Al₂O₃ · 2H₂O) is the primary commercial ore from which aluminium is extracted via the Bayer and Hall-Héroult processes.
- **B — Iron:** Extracted mainly from oxide ores such as Haematite (Fe₂O₃) and Magnetite (Fe₃O₄).
- **C — Copper:** Extracted mainly from sulfide and oxide ores such as Chalcopyrite (CuFeS₂), Copper glance (Cu₂S), and Cuprite (Cu₂O).
- **D — Zinc:** Extracted mainly from Zinc blende (ZnS) and Calamine (ZnCO₃).
- **Exam Extra:** Odisha is India's largest bauxite-producing state, accounting for over half of the country's reserves.

### Q406 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Cinnabar (HgS) is an ore of which metal?

A. Lead
B. Mercury
C. Silver
D. Tin

**Answer:** B. Mercury
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Mercury (Correct):** Cinnabar is bright red mercury(II) sulfide (HgS), the most important ore of mercury; roasting cinnabar in air converts it to mercury metal and sulfur dioxide.
- **A — Lead:** Primarily extracted from Galena (PbS), a shiny grey sulfide ore.
- **C — Silver:** Extracted from ores such as Argentite / Silver glance (Ag₂S) and Horn silver (AgCl).
- **D — Tin:** Primarily extracted from Cassiterite (SnO₂), also known as tinstone.
- **Exam Extra:** Traditionally, vermilion (sindoor) pigment was prepared using finely ground cinnabar, though modern formulations avoid toxic mercury compounds.

### Q407 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Which metal has the highest electrical and thermal conductivity at room temperature?

A. Copper
B. Gold
C. Silver
D. Aluminium

**Answer:** C. Silver
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Silver (Correct):** Silver (Ag) possesses the highest electrical conductivity and highest thermal conductivity of all metals at room temperature due to its high density of highly mobile free electrons.
- **A — Copper:** Ranks second in electrical and thermal conductivity among pure metals, making it the standard material for power cables.
- **B — Gold:** Ranks third in electrical conductivity among pure metals and is prized in microchip contacts for resisting tarnish and oxidation.
- **D — Aluminium:** Has roughly 61% of copper's conductivity by volume, though its low density makes it ideal for long-distance overhead transmission lines.
- **Exam Extra:** Copper is used instead of silver in household electrical wiring because copper offers ~94% of silver's conductivity at a tiny fraction of the cost.

### Q408 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Which two metals are soft enough to be cut with a knife and are stored under kerosene oil to prevent vigorous reaction with air and moisture?

A. Calcium and Magnesium
B. Zinc and Aluminium
C. Copper and Silver
D. Sodium and Potassium

**Answer:** D. Sodium and Potassium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sodium and Potassium (Correct):** Sodium (Na) and Potassium (K) are soft alkali metals easily cut with a knife; they react explosively with moisture and oxygen in air, so they are immersed in kerosene oil for safe storage.
- **A — Calcium and Magnesium:** Harder alkaline earth metals that develop a protective oxide film in air and do not require storage under kerosene.
- **B — Zinc and Aluminium:** Moderately hard structural metals that form stable surface oxide coatings in air.
- **C — Copper and Silver:** Unreactive, malleable coinage metals positioned below hydrogen in the reactivity series.
- **Exam Extra:** Lithium is even lighter (density 0.53 g/cm³) and floats on kerosene, so it is usually stored wrapped in paraffin wax or mineral oil.

### Q409 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Traditional solder, used for joining electrical wires, is an alloy of:

A. Lead and Tin
B. Copper and Nickel
C. Zinc and Aluminium
D. Iron and Carbon

**Answer:** A. Lead and Tin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Lead and Tin (Correct):** Traditional electrical solder is an alloy of Lead (Pb) and Tin (Sn)—commonly in a 37:63 eutectic ratio—chosen for its low melting point (~183 °C) and good electrical conductivity.
- **B — Copper and Nickel:** Form Constantan and Cupronickel, used for resistors, thermocouples, and coinage rather than low-melting solder.
- **C — Zinc and Aluminium:** Form Zamak alloys used in high-pressure die-casting of hardware and toys.
- **D — Iron and Carbon:** Form Steel and Cast Iron, high-melting structural alloys rather than solder.
- **Exam Extra:** Because lead is toxic, modern electronics increasingly use lead-free solders made of tin, silver, and copper (SAC alloys).

### Q410 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Haematite (Fe₂O₃) and Magnetite (Fe₃O₄) are important ores of:

A. Manganese
B. Iron
C. Chromium
D. Cobalt

**Answer:** B. Iron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Iron (Correct):** Haematite (Fe₂O₃) and Magnetite (Fe₃O₄) are the chief iron oxide ores reduced with coke and limestone in a blast furnace to produce pig iron.
- **A — Manganese:** Primarily extracted from Pyrolusite (MnO₂).
- **C — Chromium:** Primarily extracted from Chromite (FeCr₂O₄).
- **D — Cobalt:** Extracted from ores such as Cobaltite (CoAsS) and Smaltite, or as a by-product of copper and nickel mining.
- **Exam Extra:** Magnetite (Fe₃O₄) has the highest iron content (~72.4%) and is naturally magnetic (lodestone).

### Q411 · Metals, Ores, Alloys & Metallurgy

Which metal is alloyed with iron, carbon, and chromium to manufacture corrosion-resistant stainless steel?

A. Lead
B. Zinc
C. Nickel
D. Tungsten

**Answer:** C. Nickel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Nickel (Correct):** Stainless steel is an alloy of iron, carbon, chromium (at least ~10.5% to form a passive Cr₂O₃ film), and nickel (which enhances corrosion resistance, ductility, and non-magnetic austenitic structure).
- **A — Lead:** Added occasionally in tiny amounts only for free-machining steels, not for corrosion-resistant stainless steel.
- **B — Zinc:** Applied as an external surface coating (galvanisation) on steel rather than alloyed into stainless steel.
- **D — Tungsten:** Alloyed with steel to create ultra-hard high-speed tool steels (HSS) that retain hardness at high cutting temperatures.
- **Exam Extra:** Common kitchen-grade "18/8 stainless steel" contains 18% chromium and 8% nickel.

### Q412 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which allotrope of carbon is the hardest naturally occurring substance and a poor conductor of electricity?

A. Graphite
B. Fullerene
C. Coke
D. Diamond

**Answer:** D. Diamond
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Diamond (Correct):** Diamond is a crystalline allotrope of carbon in which each carbon atom is covalently bonded to four others in a rigid 3D tetrahedral (sp³) network, making it the hardest natural substance and an electrical insulator.
- **A — Graphite:** A soft, slippery carbon allotrope with layered hexagonal sheets that conducts electricity well.
- **B — Fullerene:** A hollow cage-like molecular allotrope of carbon (such as C₆₀ Buckminsterfullerene) resembling a football.
- **C — Coke:** A porous, impure amorphous form of carbon obtained by destructive distillation of coal, used in metallurgy.
- **Exam Extra:** Diamond has an extremely high refractive index (~2.42), which causes total internal reflection and gives cut diamonds their brilliance.

### Q413 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which allotrope of carbon is a good conductor of electricity due to free delocalised electrons between hexagonal layers?

A. Graphite
B. Diamond
C. Charcoal
D. Lampblack

**Answer:** A. Graphite
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Graphite (Correct):** Graphite consists of flat hexagonal layers of sp²-hybridised carbon atoms; the fourth valence electron of each carbon atom is delocalised across the layers, making graphite a good electrical conductor.
- **B — Diamond:** All four valence electrons are locked in rigid sp³ covalent bonds, leaving no free electrons to conduct electricity.
- **C — Charcoal:** An amorphous, highly porous form of carbon used for adsorption (water filters, gas masks) and fuel.
- **D — Lampblack:** Fine carbon soot produced by incomplete combustion of hydrocarbons, used in black ink, shoe polish, and tyres.
- **Exam Extra:** Weak van der Waals forces between adjacent layers allow them to slide over one another, making graphite useful as a dry lubricant and pencil "lead."

### Q414 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG) used for domestic cooking consists mainly of:

A. Methane and Ethane
B. Propane and Butane
C. Ethylene and Acetylene
D. Carbon monoxide and Hydrogen

**Answer:** B. Propane and Butane
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Propane and Butane (Correct):** Domestic Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG) consists primarily of a pressurised liquid mixture of Propane (C₃H₈) and Butane (C₄H₁₀), including isobutane.
- **A — Methane and Ethane:** The primary light hydrocarbons found in Natural Gas and Compressed Natural Gas (CNG), not LPG.
- **C — Ethylene and Acetylene:** Unsaturated hydrocarbons used respectively for polymer/fruit-ripening and oxy-acetylene welding.
- **D — Carbon monoxide and Hydrogen:** The gaseous fuel mixture known as Water Gas or Synthesis Gas (Syngas).
- **Exam Extra:** In India, domestic LPG cylinders are generally filled with a higher proportion of butane, while colder regions use more propane for better vaporisation.

### Q415 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which sulfur-containing compound is added to odourless LPG cylinders to detect gas leaks by its pungent smell?

A. Hydrogen sulfide
B. Sulfur dioxide
C. Ethyl mercaptan
D. Carbon disulfide

**Answer:** C. Ethyl mercaptan
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Ethyl mercaptan (Correct):** Pure LPG hydrocarbons are odourless, so trace amounts of Ethyl mercaptan (ethanethiol, C₂H₅SH) are added as a warning odorant to detect leaks immediately.
- **A — Hydrogen sulfide:** A highly toxic, corrosive gas with a rotten-egg smell that is removed from fuels rather than added.
- **B — Sulfur dioxide:** A pungent acidic gas formed when sulfur burns, responsible for respiratory irritation and acid rain.
- **D — Carbon disulfide:** A volatile, flammable non-polar solvent used in viscose rayon production, not as an LPG odorant.
- **Exam Extra:** Mercaptans are sulfur analogues of alcohols (-SH thiol group) detectable by the human nose even at parts-per-billion concentrations.

### Q416 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which two atmospheric pollutant gases are primarily responsible for Acid Rain?

A. Carbon monoxide and Methane
B. Ozone and CFCs
C. Carbon dioxide and Argon
D. Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides

**Answer:** D. Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides (Correct):** Acid rain (pH below 5.6) is caused primarily by industrial and vehicular emissions of sulfur dioxide (SO₂) and nitrogen oxides (NOₓ), which react with atmospheric moisture to form sulfuric acid (H₂SO₄) and nitric acid (HNO₃).
- **A — Carbon monoxide and Methane:** CO is a toxic pollutant that binds haemoglobin, and CH₄ is a potent greenhouse gas; neither forms acid rain.
- **B — Ozone and CFCs:** Tropospheric ozone forms photochemical smog, while CFCs deplete stratospheric ozone rather than producing acid rain.
- **C — Carbon dioxide and Argon:** CO₂ causes mild natural carbonic acid in normal rain (~pH 5.6) and global warming, while argon is chemically inert.
- **Exam Extra:** Acid rain reacts with calcium carbonate in marble monuments like the Taj Mahal ("marble cancer"), turning the surface yellowish and pitted.

### Q417 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

The monomer used in the manufacture of Polythene (polyethylene) is:

A. Ethene (Ethylene)
B. Propene
C. Vinyl chloride
D. Tetrafluoroethene

**Answer:** A. Ethene (Ethylene)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Ethene (Ethylene) (Correct):** Polythene (polyethylene) is an addition polymer produced by polymerising thousands of Ethene (ethylene, CH₂=CH₂) monomer molecules under high pressure/temperature or Ziegler-Natta catalysis.
- **B — Propene:** Polymerises to form Polypropylene (PP), used in ropes, carpets, and sturdy containers.
- **C — Vinyl chloride:** Polymerises to form Polyvinyl chloride (PVC), widely used for water pipes, raincoats, and electrical cable insulation.
- **D — Tetrafluoroethene:** Polymerises to form Polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE / Teflon), used for non-stick cookware coatings.
- **Exam Extra:** German chemist Karl Ziegler and Italian chemist Giulio Natta won the 1963 Nobel Prize in Chemistry for catalysts enabling high-density polyethylene and polypropylene.

### Q418 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Bakelite, a thermosetting plastic used for electrical switches and utensil handles, is formed by the condensation of phenol with:

A. Acetaldehyde
B. Formaldehyde
C. Acetone
D. Methanol

**Answer:** B. Formaldehyde
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Formaldehyde (Correct):** Bakelite (phenol-formaldehyde resin), invented by Leo Baekeland in 1907, is a cross-linked thermosetting polymer synthesised by condensing phenol with formaldehyde.
- **A — Acetaldehyde:** Used to produce acetic acid, pentaerythritol, and pyridine derivatives rather than Bakelite.
- **C — Acetone:** A common solvent and precursor for bisphenol-A (polycarbonate) and PMMA (acrylic glass).
- **D — Methanol:** Used industrially to manufacture formaldehyde itself, as well as biodiesel and acetic acid.
- **Exam Extra:** Because thermosetting plastics do not soften upon heating and are poor electrical conductors, Bakelite is ideal for electrical switches, plug housings, and pressure-cooker handles.

### Q419 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which gas is known as "Marsh gas" because it is produced by anaerobic bacterial decay in swamps and paddy fields?

A. Ethane
B. Propane
C. Methane
D. Butane

**Answer:** C. Methane
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Methane (Correct):** Methane (CH₄) is called "Marsh gas" because it is naturally generated in marshy wetlands, swamps, and waterlogged paddy fields by anaerobic methanogenic bacteria decomposing organic matter.
- **A — Ethane:** The second simplest alkane (C₂H₆), a minor constituent of natural gas not generated primarily as marsh gas.
- **B — Propane:** A three-carbon alkane (C₃H₈) extracted from petroleum/natural gas and bottled as LPG.
- **D — Butane:** A four-carbon alkane (C₄H₁₀) widely used in LPG cylinders and disposable pocket lighters.
- **Exam Extra:** Methane is also called "firedamp" in coal mines, where it poses an explosion hazard when mixed with air.

### Q420 · Carbon Allotropes, Polymers, Fuels, Everyday Chemicals & Acid Rain

Which chemical compound, commonly known as Laughing Gas, is used as a mild anaesthetic?

A. Nitric oxide (NO)
B. Nitrogen dioxide (NO₂)
C. Ammonia (NH₃)
D. Nitrous oxide (N₂O)

**Answer:** D. Nitrous oxide (N₂O)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Nitrous oxide (N₂O) (Correct):** Nitrous oxide (N₂O) is known as Laughing Gas because inhaling small doses produces mild euphoria and analgesia; it is used as a mild dental/medical anaesthetic.
- **A — Nitric oxide (NO):** A colourless, reactive free-radical gas important in biological vasodilation and smog formation, not laughing gas.
- **B — Nitrogen dioxide (NO₂):** A reddish-brown, toxic, pungent gas that contributes to acid rain and photochemical smog.
- **C — Ammonia (NH₃):** A colourless, alkaline gas with a sharp pungent odour, primarily used to manufacture nitrogenous fertilisers.
- **Exam Extra:** Beyond medicine, nitrous oxide is a potent greenhouse gas and is also used as an oxidiser in rocket engines and racing cars.

---

## Chapter 7: Biology

### Q421 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which organelle is known as the powerhouse of the cell?

A. Nucleus
B. Mitochondrion
C. Ribosome
D. Golgi apparatus

**Answer:** B. Mitochondrion
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Mitochondrion (Correct):** Mitochondria generate most of the cell's adenosine triphosphate (ATP) through aerobic cellular respiration, earning them the title 'powerhouse of the cell'.
- **A — Nucleus:** Houses the cell's chromosomal DNA and controls gene expression and cell division.
- **C — Ribosome:** Synthesizes polypeptide chains (proteins) by translating messenger RNA.
- **D — Golgi apparatus:** Modifies, sorts, and packages proteins and lipids for secretion or transport.
- **Exam Extra:** Mitochondria are semi-autonomous organelles that possess their own circular DNA and 70S ribosomes.

### Q422 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which cell organelle is the primary site of protein synthesis in both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells?

A. Ribosome
B. Lysosome
C. Centrosome
D. Vacuole

**Answer:** A. Ribosome
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Ribosome (Correct):** Ribosomes translate mRNA sequences into amino acid chains to synthesize proteins and are present in both prokaryotic (70S) and eukaryotic (80S) cells.
- **B — Lysosome:** Digests worn-out organelles and foreign particles using hydrolytic enzymes.
- **C — Centrosome:** Organizes microtubules during animal cell division via its pair of centrioles.
- **D — Vacuole:** Stores water, nutrients, and waste products and maintains turgor pressure in plant cells.
- **Exam Extra:** Ribosomes are non-membrane-bound organelles discovered by George Palade in 1955.

### Q423 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which cell organelle is popularly called the 'suicidal bag' of the cell due to its hydrolytic enzymes?

A. Peroxisome
B. Plastid
C. Lysosome
D. Endoplasmic reticulum

**Answer:** C. Lysosome
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Lysosome (Correct):** Lysosomes contain powerful digestive (hydrolytic) enzymes that can break down the entire cell during autolysis if the organelle membrane ruptures, so they are called 'suicidal bags'.
- **A — Peroxisome:** Breaks down fatty acids and detoxifies hydrogen peroxide using catalase.
- **B — Plastid:** Plant and algal organelle specialized for photosynthesis (chloroplast) or pigment/starch storage.
- **D — Endoplasmic reticulum:** Network of membranes involved in protein folding (rough ER) and lipid synthesis (smooth ER).
- **Exam Extra:** Lysosomes were discovered by Christian de Duve, who also discovered peroxisomes.

### Q424 · Cell Biology & Genetics

The cell wall of a typical plant cell is primarily composed of which polysaccharide?

A. Chitin
B. Peptidoglycan
C. Glycogen
D. Cellulose

**Answer:** D. Cellulose
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cellulose (Correct):** Plant cell walls are primarily composed of cellulose, a structural polysaccharide made of beta-D-glucose units that provides rigidity and mechanical strength.
- **A — Chitin:** Structural polysaccharide forming the cell walls of fungi and the exoskeleton of arthropods.
- **B — Peptidoglycan:** Mesh-like polymer of sugars and amino acids forming bacterial cell walls.
- **C — Glycogen:** Branched storage polysaccharide of glucose found in animal liver and muscle cells.
- **Exam Extra:** Cellulose is the most abundant organic polymer on Earth and is indigestible by human digestive enzymes.

### Q425 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Who coined the term 'cell' in 1665 after observing thin slices of cork under a microscope?

A. Antonie van Leeuwenhoek
B. Robert Hooke
C. Robert Brown
D. Matthias Schleiden

**Answer:** B. Robert Hooke
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Robert Hooke (Correct):** Robert Hooke coined the term 'cell' in his 1665 book Micrographia after observing honeycomb-like dead cork compartments under a compound microscope.
- **A — Antonie van Leeuwenhoek:** First observed living single-celled microorganisms and red blood cells under a simple microscope.
- **C — Robert Brown:** Discovered and named the cell nucleus in orchid cells in 1831.
- **D — Matthias Schleiden:** German botanist who co-formulated the cell theory with Theodor Schwann in 1838–1839.
- **Exam Extra:** Antonie van Leeuwenhoek was the first to observe and describe living cells (bacteria and protozoa) in 1674.

### Q426 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which type of cell division produces four genetically distinct haploid gametes from a single diploid cell?

A. Mitosis
B. Binary fission
C. Meiosis
D. Amitosis

**Answer:** C. Meiosis
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Meiosis (Correct):** Meiosis is a reductional division consisting of two successive nuclear divisions (Meiosis I and II) that produces four genetically distinct haploid daughter cells from one diploid cell.
- **A — Mitosis:** Equational cell division that produces two genetically identical diploid daughter cells for growth and repair.
- **B — Binary fission:** Asexual reproduction in prokaryotes like bacteria where a parent cell splits into two equal halves.
- **D — Amitosis:** Direct nuclear division without spindle formation or distinct chromosome condensation, seen in some protozoa.
- **Exam Extra:** Crossing over during pachytene of prophase I in meiosis creates genetic recombination and variation.

### Q427 · Cell Biology & Genetics

How many chromosomes are normally present in a human somatic (body) cell?

A. 44
B. 46
C. 48
D. 23

**Answer:** B. 46
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 46 (Correct):** A normal human somatic (body) cell is diploid and contains 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 pairs (22 pairs of autosomes and 1 pair of sex chromosomes).
- **A — 44:** Number of autosomes (non-sex chromosomes) in a human somatic cell, excluding the 2 sex chromosomes.
- **C — 48:** Normal diploid chromosome number of chimpanzees, gorillas, and potatoes.
- **D — 23:** Haploid chromosome number present in human gametes (sperm and egg cells).
- **Exam Extra:** Human gametes (sperm and ovum) are haploid and carry 23 chromosomes each.

### Q428 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which pair of scientists proposed the double-helix model of DNA in 1953?

A. James Watson and Francis Crick
B. Gregor Mendel and Thomas Morgan
C. Har Gobind Khorana and Marshall Nirenberg
D. Frederick Griffith and Oswald Avery

**Answer:** A. James Watson and Francis Crick
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — James Watson and Francis Crick (Correct):** James Watson and Francis Crick proposed the double-helix model of DNA in 1953, building on Rosalind Franklin's X-ray diffraction data and Erwin Chargaff's base-pairing rules.
- **B — Gregor Mendel and Thomas Morgan:** Mendel discovered laws of inheritance; Morgan proved genes lie on chromosomes using fruit flies (Drosophila).
- **C — Har Gobind Khorana and Marshall Nirenberg:** Deciphered the genetic code and triplet codons for amino acids.
- **D — Frederick Griffith and Oswald Avery:** Demonstrated bacterial transformation and identified DNA as the genetic material.
- **Exam Extra:** Watson, Crick, and Maurice Wilkins shared the 1962 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine for this discovery.

### Q429 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Which nitrogenous base is found in RNA instead of thymine, which occurs in DNA?

A. Adenine
B. Guanine
C. Cytosine
D. Uracil

**Answer:** D. Uracil
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Uracil (Correct):** In RNA, the pyrimidine base uracil replaces thymine and pairs with adenine during transcription.
- **A — Adenine:** Purine nitrogenous base present in both DNA and RNA, as well as in ATP.
- **B — Guanine:** Purine nitrogenous base present in both DNA and RNA, pairing with cytosine.
- **C — Cytosine:** Pyrimidine nitrogenous base present in both DNA and RNA, pairing with guanine.
- **Exam Extra:** Adenine and guanine are double-ring purines, whereas cytosine, thymine, and uracil are single-ring pyrimidines.

### Q430 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Who is regarded as the 'Father of Genetics' for his hybridization experiments on garden pea (Pisum sativum)?

A. Charles Darwin
B. Gregor Mendel
C. Hugo de Vries
D. Jean-Baptiste Lamarck

**Answer:** B. Gregor Mendel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Gregor Mendel (Correct):** Gregor Johann Mendel established the fundamental laws of heredity (Dominance, Segregation, and Independent Assortment) through cross-breeding experiments on Pisum sativum.
- **A — Charles Darwin:** Proposed the theory of evolution by natural selection in On the Origin of Species (1859).
- **C — Hugo de Vries:** Co-rediscovered Mendel's laws in 1900 and proposed the mutation theory of evolution.
- **D — Jean-Baptiste Lamarck:** Proposed the theory of inheritance of acquired characteristics (use and disuse of organs).
- **Exam Extra:** Mendel studied seven contrasting traits in garden pea plants between 1856 and 1863.

### Q431 · Cell Biology & Genetics

Down syndrome in humans is caused by the trisomy of which chromosome?

A. Chromosome 13
B. Chromosome 18
C. Chromosome 21
D. X chromosome

**Answer:** C. Chromosome 21
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Chromosome 21 (Correct):** Down syndrome results from trisomy 21, meaning an individual has three copies of chromosome 21 instead of the normal two (47 chromosomes total).
- **A — Chromosome 13:** Trisomy of chromosome 13 causes Patau syndrome.
- **B — Chromosome 18:** Trisomy of chromosome 18 causes Edwards syndrome.
- **D — X chromosome:** Monosomy X (45,XO) causes Turner syndrome, while XXY causes Klinefelter syndrome.
- **Exam Extra:** Down syndrome was first clinically described by John Langdon Down in 1866.

### Q432 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Who proposed the Five-Kingdom classification system (Monera, Protista, Fungi, Plantae, Animalia) in 1969?

A. Carl Linnaeus
B. Ernst Haeckel
C. R.H. Whittaker
D. Carl Woese

**Answer:** C. R.H. Whittaker
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — R.H. Whittaker (Correct):** R.H. Whittaker proposed the Five-Kingdom classification in 1969, dividing organisms into Monera, Protista, Fungi, Plantae, and Animalia based on cell structure, body organization, and mode of nutrition.
- **A — Carl Linnaeus:** Introduced the two-kingdom classification (Plantae and Animalia) and modern binomial nomenclature.
- **B — Ernst Haeckel:** Proposed a three-kingdom classification adding Protista in 1866.
- **D — Carl Woese:** Proposed the three-domain system (Bacteria, Archaea, Eukarya) based on 16S rRNA sequencing.
- **Exam Extra:** Carl Linnaeus introduced binomial nomenclature (Genus species) in Systema Naturae (1758).

### Q433 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

The cell walls of most fungi are made of which tough structural polysaccharide?

A. Cellulose
B. Chitin
C. Suberin
D. Lignin

**Answer:** B. Chitin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Chitin (Correct):** Fungal cell walls are primarily composed of chitin, a nitrogen-containing polysaccharide (N-acetylglucosamine polymer) also found in arthropod exoskeletons.
- **A — Cellulose:** Structural polysaccharide forming the cell walls of plants and green algae.
- **C — Suberin:** Waxy lipid polymer present in the cork cells and Casparian strips of plant roots.
- **D — Lignin:** Complex phenolic polymer that thickens and waterproofs secondary plant cell walls such as xylem vessels.
- **Exam Extra:** Fungi store reserve food mainly in the form of glycogen and oil droplets rather than starch.

### Q434 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Which group of plants is called the 'amphibians of the plant kingdom' because they live on land but require water for sexual reproduction?

A. Algae
B. Pteridophytes
C. Gymnosperms
D. Bryophytes

**Answer:** D. Bryophytes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Bryophytes (Correct):** Bryophytes (mosses and liverworts) grow in moist terrestrial habitats but require external water for flagellated antherozoids to swim to the archegonium for fertilization.
- **A — Algae:** Primarily aquatic, thalloid photosynthetic organisms classified under Thallophyta.
- **B — Pteridophytes:** First terrestrial vascular plants (ferns, horsetails) that possess true roots, stems, and leaves.
- **C — Gymnosperms:** Vascular plants that bear naked seeds not enclosed within a fruit (e.g., Pinus, Cycas).
- **Exam Extra:** Sphagnum moss is widely used as packing material for living plants due to its high water-holding capacity.

### Q435 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Which plant group was the first terrestrial group to possess vascular tissues (xylem and phloem) while lacking seeds?

A. Pteridophytes
B. Bryophytes
C. Thallophytes
D. Angiosperms

**Answer:** A. Pteridophytes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Pteridophytes (Correct):** Pteridophytes (such as ferns and horsetails) are the earliest terrestrial plants to evolve specialized vascular tissues (xylem and phloem) and reproduce via spores rather than seeds.
- **B — Bryophytes:** Non-vascular land plants lacking true xylem and phloem, such as mosses and liverworts.
- **C — Thallophytes:** Simple aquatic plants (algae) with an undifferentiated thallus body and no vascular tissue.
- **D — Angiosperms:** Seed-bearing flowering plants whose seeds develop inside an ovary (fruit).
- **Exam Extra:** Azolla, an aquatic fern, forms a nitrogen-fixing symbiosis with the cyanobacterium Anabaena in rice paddies.

### Q436 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Lichens are symbiotic associations between which two groups of organisms?

A. Fungi and mosses
B. Algae (or cyanobacteria) and fungi
C. Bacteria and higher plant roots
D. Protozoa and green algae

**Answer:** B. Algae (or cyanobacteria) and fungi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Algae (or cyanobacteria) and fungi (Correct):** Lichens are mutualistic associations between a fungal partner (mycobiont) that provides shelter and water/minerals, and an algal or cyanobacterial partner (phycobiont) that performs photosynthesis.
- **A — Fungi and mosses:** Mosses are bryophytes that grow alongside fungi in soil, not in a lichen symbiosis.
- **C — Bacteria and higher plant roots:** Rhizobium bacteria and legume roots form root nodules for nitrogen fixation, not lichens.
- **D — Protozoa and green algae:** Some ciliates harbor endosymbiotic green algae (zoochlorellae), which is not a lichen.
- **Exam Extra:** Lichens are sensitive to sulfur dioxide (SO2) and serve as natural bioindicators of air pollution.

### Q437 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Which is the largest phylum in the animal kingdom by number of described species, characterized by jointed appendages and an exoskeleton?

A. Mollusca
B. Annelida
C. Arthropoda
D. Chordata

**Answer:** C. Arthropoda
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Arthropoda (Correct):** Arthropoda is the largest animal phylum (accounting for over two-thirds of all named animal species, mostly insects), distinguished by jointed appendages and a chitinous exoskeleton.
- **A — Mollusca:** Second-largest animal phylum featuring soft unsegmented bodies, a muscular foot, and usually a calcareous shell.
- **B — Annelida:** Segmented worms (such as earthworms and leeches) with a true coelom and closed circulatory system.
- **D — Chordata:** Animals possessing a notochord, dorsal hollow nerve cord, and pharyngeal gill slits at some life stage.
- **Exam Extra:** Mollusca is the second-largest phylum in the animal kingdom.

### Q438 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Sponges belong to which animal phylum characterized by porous bodies and a water canal system?

A. Cnidaria
B. Platyhelminthes
C. Echinodermata
D. Porifera

**Answer:** D. Porifera
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Porifera (Correct):** Members of phylum Porifera (sponges) have cellular-level organization, tiny body pores (ostia), a central spongocoel, and a water canal system lined by choanocytes.
- **A — Cnidaria:** Aquatic radial animals (e.g., Hydra, jellyfish) possessing stinging cells called cnidoblasts.
- **B — Platyhelminthes:** Dorsoventrally flattened worms (flatworms such as tapeworm and Planaria) with flame cells for excretion.
- **C — Echinodermata:** Spiny-skinned marine animals (e.g., starfish) with a water vascular system and tube feet.
- **Exam Extra:** Spongilla is a freshwater sponge, while Sycon and Euspongia (bath sponge) are marine sponges.

### Q439 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Starfish and sea urchins belong to which exclusively marine phylum possessing a water vascular system?

A. Echinodermata
B. Mollusca
C. Coelenterata
D. Annelida

**Answer:** A. Echinodermata
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Echinodermata (Correct):** Echinodermata (spiny-skinned animals like Asterias/starfish and Echinus/sea urchin) are exclusively marine and use a hydraulic water vascular system with tube feet for locomotion and feeding.
- **B — Mollusca:** Soft-bodied animals (e.g., octopus, snail) with a mantle cavity and radula for feeding.
- **C — Coelenterata:** Alternative name for Cnidaria, featuring a central gastrovascular cavity and stinging nematocysts.
- **D — Annelida:** Metameric segmented worms (e.g., Nereis, earthworm) using nephridia for excretion.
- **Exam Extra:** Adult echinoderms exhibit pentaradial symmetry, whereas their larvae are bilaterally symmetrical.

### Q440 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Among reptiles, which group exceptionally possesses a four-chambered heart instead of a three-chambered heart?

A. Snakes
B. Crocodiles
C. Lizards
D. Turtles

**Answer:** B. Crocodiles
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Crocodiles (Correct):** While most reptiles have an incompletely divided three-chambered heart, crocodiles (along with birds and mammals) possess a completely divided four-chambered heart.
- **A — Snakes:** Possess a three-chambered heart with two atria and one partially divided ventricle.
- **C — Lizards:** Possess a three-chambered heart typical of non-crocodilian reptiles.
- **D — Turtles:** Possess a three-chambered heart allowing blood shunting during prolonged diving.
- **Exam Extra:** Fishes have a two-chambered heart, whereas amphibians have a three-chambered heart.

### Q441 · Five Kingdoms, Plant & Animal Phyla

Which of the following is an egg-laying mammal (monotreme)?

A. Kangaroo
B. Bat
C. Platypus
D. Whale

**Answer:** C. Platypus
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Platypus (Correct):** The duck-billed platypus (Ornithorhynchus anatinus) is a monotreme—an oviparous (egg-laying) mammal native to eastern Australia that still nourishes its young with milk.
- **A — Kangaroo:** Marsupial (pouched) mammal that gives birth to underdeveloped live young.
- **B — Bat:** Placental flying mammal that gives birth to live young.
- **D — Whale:** Placental aquatic mammal that gives birth to live calves.
- **Exam Extra:** The echidna (spiny anteater) is the only other surviving group of egg-laying mammals.

### Q442 · Human Systems

Which blood group is commonly called the universal red-cell donor?

A. AB positive
B. O negative
C. O positive
D. AB negative

**Answer:** B. O negative
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — O negative (Correct):** Group O negative red blood cells lack A, B, and Rh (D) surface antigens, so they do not trigger acute immune agglutination in recipients of other ABO/Rh blood types.
- **A — AB positive:** Universal red-cell recipient because its plasma lacks anti-A, anti-B, and anti-Rh antibodies.
- **C — O positive:** Most common blood group overall, but can only be safely transfused to Rh-positive recipients.
- **D — AB negative:** Can receive red cells from any Rh-negative ABO group, but is not a universal red-cell donor.
- **Exam Extra:** Karl Landsteiner discovered the ABO blood group system in 1900–1901 and co-discovered the Rh factor in 1940.

### Q443 · Human Systems

Which blood group is known as the universal recipient for red blood cell transfusions?

A. AB positive
B. O negative
C. A positive
D. B negative

**Answer:** A. AB positive
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — AB positive (Correct):** Individuals with AB positive blood have A, B, and Rh antigens on their red blood cells and lack anti-A, anti-B, and anti-Rh antibodies in their plasma, allowing them to receive red cells from any ABO/Rh group.
- **B — O negative:** Universal red-cell donor, but can only receive O negative red blood cells.
- **C — A positive:** Can receive only A or O red blood cells (Rh-positive or Rh-negative).
- **D — B negative:** Can receive only B negative or O negative red blood cells.
- **Exam Extra:** For plasma transfusions, group AB is the universal donor because AB plasma contains neither anti-A nor anti-B antibodies.

### Q444 · Human Systems

Which blood component is primarily responsible for blood clotting (coagulation) at the site of an injury?

A. Erythrocytes
B. Leukocytes
C. Platelets
D. Albumin

**Answer:** C. Platelets
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Platelets (Correct):** Platelets (thrombocytes) aggregate at vascular injury sites to form a platelet plug and release clotting factors that trigger the conversion of fibrinogen into insoluble fibrin threads.
- **A — Erythrocytes:** Red blood cells containing haemoglobin that transport oxygen and some carbon dioxide.
- **B — Leukocytes:** White blood cells that defend the body against pathogens and foreign substances.
- **D — Albumin:** Major plasma protein that maintains blood osmotic pressure and transports hormones and drugs.
- **Exam Extra:** Calcium ions (Ca2+) and Vitamin K are essential cofactors in the blood coagulation cascade.

### Q445 · Human Systems

Which instrument is used to measure human arterial blood pressure?

A. Electrocardiograph
B. Spirometer
C. Stethoscope
D. Sphygmomanometer

**Answer:** D. Sphygmomanometer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sphygmomanometer (Correct):** A sphygmomanometer uses an inflatable cuff and pressure gauge to measure systolic and diastolic arterial blood pressure, normally around 120/80 mmHg in healthy adults.
- **A — Electrocardiograph:** Records the electrical activity of the heart over time as an electrocardiogram (ECG).
- **B — Spirometer:** Measures lung volumes and airflow rates during inhalation and exhalation.
- **C — Stethoscope:** Acoustic device invented by René Laennec to listen to internal heart and lung sounds.
- **Exam Extra:** Blood pressure is commonly measured in the brachial artery of the upper arm.

### Q446 · Human Systems

Which structure in the human heart acts as its natural pacemaker by initiating rhythmic electrical impulses?

A. Sinoatrial (SA) node
B. Atrioventricular (AV) node
C. Purkinje fibres
D. Bundle of His

**Answer:** A. Sinoatrial (SA) node
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Sinoatrial (SA) node (Correct):** The sinoatrial (SA) node in the upper wall of the right atrium spontaneously generates action potentials at 70–75 beats per minute, setting the heart's normal rhythm.
- **B — Atrioventricular (AV) node:** Delays the impulse briefly so atria empty completely before ventricular contraction; acts only as a secondary pacemaker.
- **C — Purkinje fibres:** Conduct impulses rapidly through the ventricular myocardium to coordinate contraction.
- **D — Bundle of His:** Transmits electrical impulses from the AV node down the interventricular septum to the bundle branches.
- **Exam Extra:** Normal resting cardiac output in a healthy human adult is approximately 5 litres per minute.

### Q447 · Human Systems

Which blood vessel carries deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle of the heart to the lungs?

A. Pulmonary vein
B. Aorta
C. Pulmonary artery
D. Superior vena cava

**Answer:** C. Pulmonary artery
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Pulmonary artery (Correct):** The pulmonary artery is the sole artery in the adult systemic/pulmonary circuit that carries deoxygenated blood, pumping it from the right ventricle to the lungs for oxygenation.
- **A — Pulmonary vein:** Carries oxygenated blood from the lungs back into the left atrium of the heart.
- **B — Aorta:** Largest artery in the body, carrying oxygenated blood from the left ventricle to systemic tissues.
- **D — Superior vena cava:** Large vein returning deoxygenated blood from the head, neck, and upper limbs to the right atrium.
- **Exam Extra:** Conversely, the pulmonary veins are the only veins in adults that carry oxygenated blood, returning it to the left atrium.

### Q448 · Human Systems

Which enzyme present in human saliva begins the digestion of starch into maltose in the mouth?

A. Pepsin
B. Salivary amylase
C. Lipase
D. Trypsin

**Answer:** B. Salivary amylase
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Salivary amylase (Correct):** Salivary amylase (also called ptyalin) hydrolyzes about 30% of dietary starch into the disaccharide maltose in the slightly acidic-to-neutral environment (pH ~6.8) of the mouth.
- **A — Pepsin:** Gastric protease secreted in the stomach that breaks proteins into peptones at low pH (~1.8).
- **C — Lipase:** Enzyme that hydrolyzes triglycerides (fats) into fatty acids and glycerol, mainly in the small intestine.
- **D — Trypsin:** Pancreatic protease activated in the small intestine to digest proteins in an alkaline medium.
- **Exam Extra:** Saliva also contains lysozyme, an antibacterial enzyme that helps protect the oral cavity against microbes.

### Q449 · Human Systems

Bile juice, which emulsifies fats in the small intestine, is produced by which organ?

A. Gallbladder
B. Pancreas
C. Stomach
D. Liver

**Answer:** D. Liver
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Liver (Correct):** Hepatocytes in the liver synthesize and secrete bile juice, which is subsequently stored and concentrated in the gallbladder before release into the duodenum.
- **A — Gallbladder:** Muscular sac beneath the liver that stores and concentrates bile but does not produce it.
- **B — Pancreas:** Secretes pancreatic juice containing amylase, trypsinogen, and lipase into the duodenum.
- **C — Stomach:** Secretes hydrochloric acid, mucus, and pepsinogen for protein digestion.
- **Exam Extra:** Bile contains bile salts (which emulsify fats) and bile pigments (bilirubin and biliverdin), but no digestive enzymes.

### Q450 · Human Systems

In which part of the human alimentary canal does the maximum absorption of digested nutrients take place?

A. Small intestine
B. Stomach
C. Large intestine
D. Esophagus

**Answer:** A. Small intestine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Small intestine (Correct):** The small intestine (especially the jejunum and ileum) has circular folds, finger-like villi, and microvilli that enormously increase surface area for absorbing glucose, amino acids, fatty acids, and vitamins.
- **B — Stomach:** Primarily performs mechanical churning, protein digestion by pepsin, and absorbs only small amounts of water, alcohol, and some drugs.
- **C — Large intestine:** Primarily absorbs remaining water, electrolytes, and vitamin K produced by gut bacteria.
- **D — Esophagus:** Muscular tube that transports swallowed food to the stomach via peristalsis without digestion or absorption.
- **Exam Extra:** The three sequential segments of the small intestine are the duodenum, jejunum, and ileum.

### Q451 · Human Systems

Which tiny balloon-like structures in the human lungs are the primary sites of gaseous exchange?

A. Bronchi
B. Trachea
C. Alveoli
D. Pleura

**Answer:** C. Alveoli
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Alveoli (Correct):** Alveoli are microscopic, thin-walled, vascularized air sacs at the ends of bronchioles where oxygen diffuses into pulmonary capillaries and carbon dioxide diffuses out.
- **A — Bronchi:** Cartilaginous airways branching from the trachea that conduct air into each lung without gas exchange.
- **B — Trachea:** Windpipe supported by C-shaped cartilaginous rings connecting the larynx to the bronchi.
- **D — Pleura:** Double-layered serous membrane enclosing the lungs and reducing friction during breathing.
- **Exam Extra:** Human lungs contain roughly 300 million alveoli, providing a respiratory surface area of about 70–100 square metres.

### Q452 · Human Systems

What is the structural and functional unit of the human kidney?

A. Neuron
B. Nephron
C. Hepatocyte
D. Osteon

**Answer:** B. Nephron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nephron (Correct):** Each human kidney contains about one million nephrons, which carry out ultrafiltration (in Bowman's capsule), selective reabsorption, and tubular secretion to form urine.
- **A — Neuron:** Structural and functional unit of the nervous system that transmits electrical nerve impulses.
- **C — Hepatocyte:** Principal parenchymal cell of the liver responsible for metabolism, detoxification, and bile synthesis.
- **D — Osteon:** Cylindrical structural unit (Haversian system) of compact bone tissue.
- **Exam Extra:** Urea, the main nitrogenous waste excreted by human kidneys, is synthesized in the liver via the ornithine (urea) cycle.

### Q453 · Human Systems

Which part of the human brain primarily coordinates voluntary muscle movements, posture, and balance?

A. Cerebrum
B. Medulla oblongata
C. Hypothalamus
D. Cerebellum

**Answer:** D. Cerebellum
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cerebellum (Correct):** The cerebellum (part of the hindbrain) integrates sensory input from the inner ear and proprioceptors to coordinate smooth voluntary movement, posture, and equilibrium.
- **A — Cerebrum:** Largest part of the forebrain responsible for memory, reasoning, intelligence, and conscious perception.
- **B — Medulla oblongata:** Hindbrain centre controlling involuntary autonomic functions such as heartbeat and respiration.
- **C — Hypothalamus:** Forebrain region regulating body temperature, hunger, thirst, and pituitary hormone secretion.
- **Exam Extra:** Alcohol consumption disproportionately impairs the cerebellum, causing slurred speech and loss of balance.

### Q454 · Human Systems

Which part of the brainstem regulates involuntary vital functions such as breathing, heart rate, and blood pressure?

A. Medulla oblongata
B. Cerebellum
C. Corpus callosum
D. Thalamus

**Answer:** A. Medulla oblongata
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Medulla oblongata (Correct):** The medulla oblongata contains vital autonomic reflex centres for respiration, heart rate, blood vessel tone, swallowing, and vomiting.
- **B — Cerebellum:** Coordinates motor precision, muscle tone, and physical balance.
- **C — Corpus callosum:** Thick band of nerve fibres connecting the left and right cerebral hemispheres.
- **D — Thalamus:** Relay station in the forebrain that directs sensory signals (except smell) to the cerebral cortex.
- **Exam Extra:** The brainstem consists of the midbrain, pons, and medulla oblongata.

### Q455 · Human Systems

How many bones are present in a normal adult human skeleton?

A. 208
B. 206
C. 212
D. 300

**Answer:** B. 206
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 206 (Correct):** A normal adult human skeleton comprises 206 bones divided into the axial skeleton (80 bones) and the appendicular skeleton (126 bones).
- **A — 208:** Incorrect count; only 206 bones remain distinct in a typical adult skeleton.
- **C — 212:** Incorrect count exceeding the normal adult human bone total.
- **D — 300:** Approximate number of bones/cartilaginous elements present in a newborn infant before fusion.
- **Exam Extra:** A newborn baby has approximately 270 to 300 bones, many of which fuse together during childhood growth.

### Q456 · Human Systems

Which is the smallest bone in the human body, located in the middle ear?

A. Malleus
B. Incus
C. Stapes
D. Hyoid

**Answer:** C. Stapes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Stapes (Correct):** The stapes (stirrup bone), located in the middle ear cavity, measures about 2.5–3 mm and transmits sound vibrations from the incus to the oval window of the inner ear.
- **A — Malleus:** Hammer-shaped middle ear ossicle attached to the eardrum, larger than the stapes.
- **B — Incus:** Anvil-shaped middle ear bone situated between the malleus and the stapes.
- **D — Hyoid:** U-shaped bone in the neck supporting the tongue and larynx, unique for not articulating directly with any other bone.
- **Exam Extra:** The femur (thigh bone) is the longest and strongest bone in the human body.

### Q457 · Human Systems

Which type of lens is prescribed to correct myopia (nearsightedness)?

A. Convex lens
B. Cylindrical lens
C. Bifocal lens
D. Concave lens

**Answer:** D. Concave lens
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Concave lens (Correct):** In myopia, light rays from distant objects focus in front of the retina; a diverging (concave) lens spreads the incoming rays slightly so they focus accurately on the retina.
- **A — Convex lens:** Used to correct hypermetropia (farsightedness) where images focus behind the retina.
- **B — Cylindrical lens:** Used to correct astigmatism caused by irregular curvature of the cornea or lens.
- **C — Bifocal lens:** Used to correct presbyopia when both near and distant vision need separate correction.
- **Exam Extra:** Hypermetropia (farsightedness) is corrected using a convex (converging) lens.

### Q458 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Insulin is produced by which organ?

A. Pancreas
B. Liver
C. Thyroid gland
D. Adrenal gland

**Answer:** A. Pancreas
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Pancreas (Correct):** Insulin is a peptide hormone secreted by the beta cells of the islets of Langerhans in the pancreas to lower blood glucose levels by promoting cellular glucose uptake and glycogenesis.
- **B — Liver:** Stores excess glucose as glycogen under insulin's influence and produces bile, but does not secrete insulin.
- **C — Thyroid gland:** Secretes thyroxine (T4), triiodothyronine (T3), and calcitonin to regulate metabolism and calcium balance.
- **D — Adrenal gland:** Secretes adrenaline, noradrenaline, cortisol, and aldosterone to manage stress, metabolism, and electrolyte balance.
- **Exam Extra:** Insufficient insulin secretion or insulin resistance leads to diabetes mellitus.

### Q459 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which endocrine gland, located at the base of the brain, is often called the 'master gland' because it regulates several other endocrine glands?

A. Pineal gland
B. Pituitary gland
C. Thymus gland
D. Parathyroid gland

**Answer:** B. Pituitary gland
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Pituitary gland (Correct):** The pituitary gland (hypophysis) is called the 'master gland' because its tropic hormones (TSH, ACTH, FSH, LH) stimulate and regulate most other endocrine glands.
- **A — Pineal gland:** Secretes melatonin to regulate circadian rhythms and sleep-wake cycles.
- **C — Thymus gland:** Secretes thymosin to promote T-lymphocyte maturation and cell-mediated immunity.
- **D — Parathyroid gland:** Secretes parathormone (PTH) to increase blood calcium levels independently of pituitary tropic hormones.
- **Exam Extra:** The pituitary gland itself is regulated by releasing and inhibiting hormones from the hypothalamus.

### Q460 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which element is essential for the synthesis of thyroxine hormone by the thyroid gland?

A. Iron
B. Calcium
C. Iodine
D. Potassium

**Answer:** C. Iodine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Iodine (Correct):** Iodine is an essential trace element required by the thyroid gland to synthesize thyroxine (T4, containing four iodine atoms) and triiodothyronine (T3).
- **A — Iron:** Essential component of haemoglobin and myoglobin for oxygen transport and storage.
- **B — Calcium:** Essential mineral for bone mineralization, muscle contraction, nerve transmission, and blood clotting.
- **D — Potassium:** Major intracellular cation necessary for nerve impulse conduction and osmotic balance.
- **Exam Extra:** Dietary iodine deficiency causes simple goitre, marked by enlargement of the thyroid gland.

### Q461 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which hormone is known as the 'fight-or-flight' hormone because it prepares the body for emergency stress responses?

A. Insulin
B. Melatonin
C. Oxytocin
D. Adrenaline

**Answer:** D. Adrenaline
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Adrenaline (Correct):** Adrenaline (epinephrine), secreted by the adrenal medulla during acute stress, increases heart rate, blood pressure, respiratory rate, and blood glucose to prepare the body for 'fight-or-flight'.
- **A — Insulin:** Pancreatic hormone that lowers elevated blood glucose levels after meals.
- **B — Melatonin:** Pineal hormone that induces drowsiness and regulates circadian sleep cycles.
- **C — Oxytocin:** Pituitary hormone that triggers uterine contractions during labour and milk let-down.
- **Exam Extra:** The adrenal glands sit directly on top of the kidneys and are also called suprarenal glands.

### Q462 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which hormone secreted by the pineal gland regulates the human sleep-wake cycle (circadian rhythm)?

A. Melatonin
B. Prolactin
C. Glucagon
D. Aldosterone

**Answer:** A. Melatonin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Melatonin (Correct):** Melatonin is synthesized and secreted by the pineal gland predominantly in darkness, synchronizing the body's circadian (24-hour) sleep-wake rhythm.
- **B — Prolactin:** Anterior pituitary hormone that stimulates milk production in mammary glands.
- **C — Glucagon:** Pancreatic alpha-cell hormone that increases blood sugar levels during fasting.
- **D — Aldosterone:** Adrenal cortex mineralocorticoid that promotes sodium and water reabsorption in kidneys.
- **Exam Extra:** Exposure to bright blue light at night suppresses melatonin secretion from the pineal gland.

### Q463 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which pituitary hormone stimulates uterine contractions during childbirth and milk ejection during lactation?

A. Vasopressin
B. Oxytocin
C. Calcitonin
D. Somatotropin

**Answer:** B. Oxytocin
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Oxytocin (Correct):** Oxytocin, synthesized in the hypothalamus and released from the posterior pituitary, stimulates forceful uterine contractions during parturition and contraction of mammary myoepithelial cells for milk ejection.
- **A — Vasopressin:** Antidiuretic hormone (ADH) that promotes water reabsorption in kidney collecting ducts to prevent dehydration.
- **C — Calcitonin:** Thyroid hormone that lowers blood calcium levels by inhibiting bone resorption.
- **D — Somatotropin:** Growth hormone (GH) from the anterior pituitary that stimulates skeletal and tissue growth.
- **Exam Extra:** Vasopressin (ADH) is the other hormone released by the posterior pituitary lobe.

### Q464 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which pancreatic hormone raises blood glucose levels by stimulating the breakdown of glycogen in the liver?

A. Insulin
B. Somatostatin
C. Glucagon
D. Cortisol

**Answer:** C. Glucagon
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Glucagon (Correct):** Glucagon is secreted by the alpha cells of the pancreatic islets during fasting or low blood sugar, stimulating hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis to raise blood glucose.
- **A — Insulin:** Secreted by pancreatic beta cells to decrease blood glucose levels.
- **B — Somatostatin:** Secreted by pancreatic delta cells (and hypothalamus) to inhibit both insulin and glucagon release.
- **D — Cortisol:** Glucocorticoid secreted by the adrenal cortex that raises blood sugar and modulates stress and inflammation.
- **Exam Extra:** Insulin and glucagon act as antagonistic hormones to maintain normal fasting blood glucose (70–100 mg/dL).

### Q465 · Endocrine Glands & Hormones

Which endocrine gland regulates blood calcium and phosphate levels primarily through parathormone (PTH)?

A. Adrenal cortex
B. Thymus
C. Pineal gland
D. Parathyroid gland

**Answer:** D. Parathyroid gland
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Parathyroid gland (Correct):** The four parathyroid glands located behind the thyroid secrete parathormone (PTH), which raises blood calcium levels by stimulating bone resorption, renal calcium reabsorption, and intestinal absorption.
- **A — Adrenal cortex:** Secretes steroid hormones (cortisol, aldosterone, androgens) regulating metabolism and electrolytes.
- **B — Thymus:** Secretes thymosin to drive T-cell maturation and immune development.
- **C — Pineal gland:** Secretes melatonin to regulate circadian rhythms and seasonal biological cycles.
- **Exam Extra:** Calcitonin from the thyroid gland acts opposite to parathormone by lowering blood calcium levels.

### Q466 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Which vitamin is produced in the skin with adequate sunlight exposure?

A. Vitamin D
B. Vitamin A
C. Vitamin C
D. Vitamin K

**Answer:** A. Vitamin D
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Vitamin D (Correct):** Ultraviolet-B (UVB) rays in sunlight convert 7-dehydrocholesterol in human skin into cholecalciferol (Vitamin D3), which is essential for calcium absorption and bone health.
- **B — Vitamin A:** Fat-soluble vitamin (retinol) obtained from carrots, dairy, and liver, essential for vision and epithelial health.
- **C — Vitamin C:** Water-soluble vitamin (ascorbic acid) found in citrus fruits and amla, essential for collagen synthesis.
- **D — Vitamin K:** Fat-soluble vitamin (phylloquinone/menaquinone) obtained from leafy greens and gut bacteria, required for blood clotting.
- **Exam Extra:** Vitamin D deficiency causes rickets in children and osteomalacia in adults.

### Q467 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Deficiency of Vitamin C (ascorbic acid) causes which disease characterized by bleeding gums and delayed wound healing?

A. Rickets
B. Scurvy
C. Beriberi
D. Pellagra

**Answer:** B. Scurvy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Scurvy (Correct):** Scurvy arises from severe Vitamin C (ascorbic acid) deficiency, which impairs collagen synthesis and leads to spongy bleeding gums, skin spots, and poor wound healing.
- **A — Rickets:** Bone-softening disease in children caused by Vitamin D, calcium, or phosphate deficiency.
- **C — Beriberi:** Neurological and cardiovascular disorder caused by Vitamin B1 (thiamine) deficiency.
- **D — Pellagra:** Disease characterized by dermatitis, diarrhoea, and dementia caused by Vitamin B3 (niacin) deficiency.
- **Exam Extra:** Amla (Indian gooseberry), guava, and citrus fruits are among the richest natural sources of Vitamin C.

### Q468 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Deficiency of which vitamin leads to night blindness (nyctalopia) and xerophthalmia?

A. Vitamin B1
B. Vitamin E
C. Vitamin A
D. Vitamin K

**Answer:** C. Vitamin A
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Vitamin A (Correct):** Vitamin A (retinol) is needed to form rhodopsin (visual purple) in retinal rod cells; its deficiency impairs dim-light vision (night blindness) and dries the cornea (xerophthalmia).
- **A — Vitamin B1:** Thiamine; its deficiency causes beriberi and Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome.
- **B — Vitamin E:** Tocopherol; an antioxidant vitamin whose deficiency can cause neuromuscular weakness and haemolytic anaemia.
- **D — Vitamin K:** Phylloquinone; its deficiency impairs blood clotting and causes haemorrhage.
- **Exam Extra:** Vitamins A, D, E, and K are fat-soluble, whereas Vitamins B-complex and C are water-soluble.

### Q469 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Which fat-soluble vitamin is essential for synthesizing clotting factors required for blood coagulation?

A. Vitamin D
B. Vitamin E
C. Vitamin B12
D. Vitamin K

**Answer:** D. Vitamin K
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Vitamin K (Correct):** Vitamin K serves as an essential cofactor in the liver for synthesizing clotting factors II (prothrombin), VII, IX, and X needed for normal blood coagulation.
- **A — Vitamin D:** Calciferol; regulates intestinal calcium and phosphorus absorption for bone mineralization.
- **B — Vitamin E:** Tocopherol; protects cell membranes from lipid peroxidation as a fat-soluble antioxidant.
- **C — Vitamin B12:** Cobalamin; required for red blood cell maturation, DNA synthesis, and nerve myelin maintenance.
- **Exam Extra:** Symbiotic bacteria (such as Escherichia coli) in the human colon synthesize Vitamin K2.

### Q470 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Which water-soluble vitamin contains cobalt in its chemical structure and prevents pernicious anaemia?

A. Vitamin B12
B. Vitamin B1
C. Vitamin B3
D. Vitamin B6

**Answer:** A. Vitamin B12
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Vitamin B12 (Correct):** Vitamin B12 (cyanocobalamin/cobalamin) features a central cobalt ion inside a corrin ring and is indispensable for DNA synthesis and red blood cell formation; deficiency causes pernicious (megaloblastic) anaemia.
- **B — Vitamin B1:** Thiamine; contains sulfur and prevents beriberi.
- **C — Vitamin B3:** Niacin (nicotinic acid); forms NAD+/NADP+ coenzymes and prevents pellagra.
- **D — Vitamin B6:** Pyridoxine; acts as a coenzyme in amino acid metabolism and neurotransmitter synthesis.
- **Exam Extra:** Absorption of Vitamin B12 in the ileum requires intrinsic factor secreted by gastric parietal cells.

### Q471 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Deficiency of which mineral in the diet most commonly causes anaemia due to reduced haemoglobin synthesis?

A. Zinc
B. Iron
C. Magnesium
D. Sodium

**Answer:** B. Iron
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Iron (Correct):** Iron is the central oxygen-binding atom in the haem group of haemoglobin; insufficient dietary iron reduces haemoglobin production and leads to microcytic iron-deficiency anaemia.
- **A — Zinc:** Trace mineral essential for immune function, DNA repair, and over 300 enzymes including carbonic anhydrase.
- **C — Magnesium:** Mineral required for ATP stabilization, neuromuscular function, and plant chlorophyll synthesis.
- **D — Sodium:** Major extracellular electrolyte controlling fluid balance, blood volume, and action potentials.
- **Exam Extra:** One haemoglobin molecule contains four haem groups and can bind up to four oxygen molecules.

### Q472 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Malaria is caused by a protozoan parasite of which genus, transmitted by the female Anopheles mosquito?

A. Leishmania
B. Trypanosoma
C. Plasmodium
D. Entamoeba

**Answer:** C. Plasmodium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Plasmodium (Correct):** Malaria is caused by single-celled protozoan parasites of the genus Plasmodium (such as P. falciparum and P. vivax) transmitted through the bite of an infected female Anopheles mosquito.
- **A — Leishmania:** Flagellated protozoan transmitted by sandflies that causes kala-azar (visceral leishmaniasis).
- **B — Trypanosoma:** Flagellated protozoan transmitted by the tsetse fly that causes African sleeping sickness.
- **D — Entamoeba:** Amoebic protozoan (E. histolytica) spread via contaminated food/water that causes amoebic dysentery.
- **Exam Extra:** Ronald Ross discovered the mosquito transmission of malaria in Secunderabad in 1897 and won the 1902 Nobel Prize.

### Q473 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Dengue fever is a viral disease transmitted primarily by which mosquito species?

A. Culex quinquefasciatus
B. Anopheles culicifacies
C. Mansonia uniformis
D. Aedes aegypti

**Answer:** D. Aedes aegypti
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Aedes aegypti (Correct):** Dengue fever is caused by an arbovirus (DENV) and transmitted primarily by day-biting Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, which also spread chikungunya and Zika virus.
- **A — Culex quinquefasciatus:** Primary mosquito vector for lymphatic filariasis (elephantiasis) and Japanese encephalitis.
- **B — Anopheles culicifacies:** Major rural vector of malaria in India.
- **C — Mansonia uniformis:** Mosquito vector associated with Brugian filariasis in coastal and marshy regions.
- **Exam Extra:** Severe dengue causes a sharp drop in blood platelet count (thrombocytopenia) and plasma leakage.

### Q474 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Tuberculosis is a bacterial infectious disease caused by which organism?

A. Mycobacterium tuberculosis
B. Vibrio cholerae
C. Salmonella typhi
D. Clostridium tetani

**Answer:** A. Mycobacterium tuberculosis
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Mycobacterium tuberculosis (Correct):** Tuberculosis (TB) is caused by the acid-fast bacterium Mycobacterium tuberculosis, discovered by Robert Koch in 1882, and primarily affects the lungs.
- **B — Vibrio cholerae:** Comma-shaped bacterium transmitted by contaminated water/food that causes cholera.
- **C — Salmonella typhi:** Rod-shaped bacterium transmitted via the faecal-oral route that causes typhoid fever.
- **D — Clostridium tetani:** Anaerobic spore-forming bacterium whose neurotoxin causes tetanus (lockjaw).
- **Exam Extra:** The BCG (Bacillus Calmette-Guérin) vaccine is administered to protect children against severe forms of tuberculosis.

### Q475 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

The Widal test is a standard serological diagnostic test used to detect which bacterial disease?

A. Cholera
B. Typhoid
C. Tuberculosis
D. Diphtheria

**Answer:** B. Typhoid
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Typhoid (Correct):** The Widal test detects agglutinating antibodies against the O and H antigens of Salmonella enterica serotype Typhi in a patient's serum to aid in diagnosing typhoid fever.
- **A — Cholera:** Diagnosed via stool culture on TCBS agar or dark-field microscopy for Vibrio cholerae.
- **C — Tuberculosis:** Screened using the Mantoux tuberculin skin test, sputum smear microscopy, or CBNAAT/GeneXpert.
- **D — Diphtheria:** Assessed for susceptibility using the Schick skin test and diagnosed by throat swab culture.
- **Exam Extra:** Typhoid spreads primarily through contaminated food and water and causes sustained high fever and intestinal inflammation.

### Q476 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Which diagnostic test is widely used as an initial screening tool to detect HIV antibodies in blood?

A. Mantoux test
B. Schick test
C. ELISA
D. VDRL test

**Answer:** C. ELISA
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — ELISA (Correct):** ELISA (Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay) is a rapid, sensitive serological screening test used to detect antibodies against HIV (Human Immunodeficiency Virus) in blood samples.
- **A — Mantoux test:** Intradermal tuberculin skin test used to screen for latent or active Mycobacterium tuberculosis infection.
- **B — Schick test:** Skin test used historically to determine susceptibility or immunity to diphtheria toxin.
- **D — VDRL test:** Venereal Disease Research Laboratory blood test used to screen for syphilis.
- **Exam Extra:** Western blot or HIV RNA PCR tests are commonly used to confirm positive HIV screening results.

### Q477 · Vitamins, Minerals & Diseases

Who developed the world's first successful vaccine (against smallpox) in 1796?

A. Louis Pasteur
B. Robert Koch
C. Alexander Fleming
D. Edward Jenner

**Answer:** D. Edward Jenner
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Edward Jenner (Correct):** In 1796, English physician Edward Jenner inoculated a boy with cowpox matter and demonstrated immunity against smallpox, pioneering vaccination.
- **A — Louis Pasteur:** Developed vaccines for anthrax and rabies and established pasteurization and germ theory.
- **B — Robert Koch:** Formulated Koch's postulates and identified the causative bacteria of anthrax, tuberculosis, and cholera.
- **C — Alexander Fleming:** Discovered penicillin, the world's first widely used antibiotic, from Penicillium notatum in 1928.
- **Exam Extra:** Smallpox became the first human disease to be completely eradicated globally in 1980.

### Q478 · Plant Physiology & Biological Branches

Which pigment enables plants to absorb light for photosynthesis?

A. Chlorophyll
B. Anthocyanin
C. Lycopene
D. Melanin

**Answer:** A. Chlorophyll
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Chlorophyll (Correct):** Chlorophyll (especially chlorophyll-a) inside chloroplasts absorbs red and blue wavelengths of sunlight to drive the light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis.
- **B — Anthocyanin:** Water-soluble vacuolar pigment giving red, purple, or blue hues to flowers and fruits; does not drive photosynthesis.
- **C — Lycopene:** Bright red carotenoid pigment abundant in tomatoes and watermelons, functioning as an antioxidant rather than a primary photosynthetic reaction-centre pigment.
- **D — Melanin:** Dark pigment found in animal skin, hair, and eyes (and some fungi) that protects against UV radiation.
- **Exam Extra:** Chlorophyll reflects green light, which gives healthy plant leaves their characteristic green colour.

### Q479 · Plant Physiology & Biological Branches

Which metal ion sits at the centre of the porphyrin ring of a chlorophyll molecule?

A. Iron
B. Magnesium
C. Copper
D. Zinc

**Answer:** B. Magnesium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Magnesium (Correct):** Magnesium (Mg2+) occupies the central position of the tetrapyrrole porphyrin ring in every chlorophyll molecule, stabilizing its structure for light capture.
- **A — Iron:** Central metal ion in animal haemoglobin and a cofactor for chlorophyll synthesis enzymes, though not part of chlorophyll itself.
- **C — Copper:** Component of plastocyanin in the photosynthetic electron transport chain and cytochrome c oxidase.
- **D — Zinc:** Cofactor required for auxin (IAA) biosynthesis and carbonic anhydrase activity in plants.
- **Exam Extra:** Magnesium deficiency in plants causes interveinal chlorosis (yellowing between leaf veins).

### Q480 · Plant Physiology & Biological Branches

Which plant vascular tissue transports water and dissolved minerals upward from the roots to the stems and leaves?

A. Phloem
B. Parenchyma
C. Xylem
D. Collenchyma

**Answer:** C. Xylem
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
