World Wetlands Day is observed every year on February 2 to mark the adoption of which international treaty?

A. Stockholm Convention
B. Basel Convention
C. Montreal Protocol
D. Ramsar Convention

**Answer:** D. Ramsar Convention
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ramsar Convention (Correct):** World Wetlands Day is observed on February 2 to commemorate the adoption of the Ramsar Convention on Wetlands on 2 February 1971 in Ramsar, Iran.
- **A — Stockholm Convention:** Adopted in 2001 to eliminate or restrict the production and use of Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs).
- **B — Basel Convention:** Adopted in 1989 to control transboundary movements of hazardous wastes and their disposal.
- **C — Montreal Protocol:** Signed on 16 September 1987 (World Ozone Day) to phase out substances that deplete the ozone layer.
- **Exam Extra:** Chilika Lake (Odisha) and Keoladeo National Park (Rajasthan) were designated as India's first Ramsar sites in 1981.

### Q645 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

National Science Day (February 28) is celebrated in India to commemorate which milestone?

A. Birth anniversary of Sir C. V. Raman
B. Discovery of the Raman Effect
C. India's first nuclear test at Pokhran
D. Launch of India's first satellite Aryabhata

**Answer:** B. Discovery of the Raman Effect
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Discovery of the Raman Effect (Correct):** National Science Day is celebrated on February 28 to commemorate Sir C. V. Raman's announcement of the discovery of the Raman Effect on 28 February 1928.
- **A — Birth anniversary of Sir C. V. Raman:** Sir C. V. Raman was born on 7 November 1888 in Tiruchirappalli, not 28 February.
- **C — India's first nuclear test at Pokhran:** Pokhran-I ('Smiling Buddha') occurred on 18 May 1974; Pokhran-II (11 May 1998) is observed as National Technology Day.
- **D — Launch of India's first satellite Aryabhata:** India's first satellite was launched on 19 April 1975.
- **Exam Extra:** Sir C. V. Raman received the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1930 for this discovery, becoming the first Asian to win a Nobel Prize in any branch of science.

### Q646 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

On which date is National Panchayati Raj Day celebrated in India to mark the enforcement of the 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act?

A. April 7
B. April 21
C. April 22
D. April 24

**Answer:** D. April 24
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — April 24 (Correct):** National Panchayati Raj Day is celebrated on April 24 to mark the day the 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1992 came into force on 24 April 1993.
- **A — April 7:** Observed globally as World Health Day, marking the founding of the World Health Organization (WHO) in 1948.
- **B — April 21:** Observed as National Civil Services Day, marking Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel's 1947 address to the first batch of IAS probationers.
- **C — April 22:** Observed globally as Earth Day (International Mother Earth Day) since 1970.
- **Exam Extra:** The 73rd Amendment added Part IX (Articles 243 to 243O) and the 11th Schedule (29 functional items) to the Constitution of India.

### Q647 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

On which date is World Environment Day observed globally, commemorating the 1972 Stockholm Conference?

A. May 22
B. June 5
C. June 21
D. September 16

**Answer:** B. June 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — June 5 (Correct):** World Environment Day is observed globally on June 5, marking the opening day of the 1972 United Nations Conference on the Human Environment in Stockholm.
- **A — May 22:** Observed as International Day for Biological Diversity, commemorating the 1992 adoption of the Convention on Biological Diversity.
- **C — June 21:** Observed as International Day of Yoga, adopted by the UN General Assembly in 2014 on India's proposal.
- **D — September 16:** Observed as World Ozone Day, commemorating the signing of the Montreal Protocol in 1987.
- **Exam Extra:** The 1972 Stockholm Conference also led to the establishment of the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya.

### Q648 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

In July 2024, the Government of India notified June 25 to be observed annually as which day?

A. Loktantra Raksha Diwas
B. Samvidhan Hatya Diwas
C. Sankalp Diwas
D. Sadbhavana Diwas

**Answer:** B. Samvidhan Hatya Diwas
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Samvidhan Hatya Diwas (Correct):** On 11 July 2024, the Ministry of Home Affairs notified June 25 to be observed annually as 'Samvidhan Hatya Diwas' to mark the imposition of the Emergency on 25 June 1975.
- **A — Loktantra Raksha Diwas:** Not an officially notified Government of India observance for June 25.
- **C — Sankalp Diwas:** Not the name notified by the Ministry of Home Affairs for the June 25 observance.
- **D — Sadbhavana Diwas:** Observed annually on August 20 to mark the birth anniversary of former Prime Minister Rajiv Gandhi.
- **Exam Extra:** The 1975 National Emergency was proclaimed under Article 352 of the Constitution on grounds of 'internal disturbance', later replaced by 'armed rebellion' via the 44th Amendment (1978).

### Q649 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

National Sports Day is celebrated in India on August 29 to honour the birth anniversary of which sporting legend?

A. Milkha Singh
B. K. D. Jadhav
C. Major Dhyan Chand
D. C. K. Nayudu

**Answer:** C. Major Dhyan Chand
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Major Dhyan Chand (Correct):** National Sports Day is celebrated in India on August 29 to honour the birth anniversary of field hockey legend Major Dhyan Chand (born 29 August 1905).
- **A — Milkha Singh:** Born on 20 November 1929; known as 'The Flying Sikh' and India's first athletics CWG Gold medalist.
- **B — K. D. Jadhav:** Born on 15 January 1926; Independent India's first individual Olympic medalist (1952 Helsinki).
- **D — C. K. Nayudu:** Born on 31 October 1895; served as India's first Test cricket captain in 1932.
- **Exam Extra:** Known as 'The Wizard', Major Dhyan Chand helped India win three consecutive Olympic Hockey Gold medals (1928, 1932, and 1936), and India's highest sporting honour is named after him.

### Q650 · Important National & International Days (Jan-Dec)

National Space Day is celebrated every year in India on August 23 to commemorate which achievement?

A. Launch of Aryabhata satellite
B. Mars Orbiter Mission entering Mars orbit
C. Launch of Aditya-L1 solar mission
D. Chandrayaan-3 soft landing near the Lunar South Pole

**Answer:** D. Chandrayaan-3 soft landing near the Lunar South Pole
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Chandrayaan-3 soft landing near the Lunar South Pole (Correct):** National Space Day is celebrated every year on August 23 to commemorate the historic soft landing of Chandrayaan-3's Vikram lander near the Lunar South Pole on 23 August 2023.
- **A — Launch of Aryabhata satellite:** India's first satellite Aryabhata was launched on 19 April 1975.
- **B — Mars Orbiter Mission entering Mars orbit:** ISRO's Mangalyaan successfully entered Martian orbit on 24 September 2014.
- **C — Launch of Aditya-L1 solar mission:** India's first solar observatory mission was launched on 2 September 2023.
- **Exam Extra:** The Chandrayaan-3 landing site was named 'Shiv Shakti Point', making India the fourth country to soft-land on the Moon and the first near the Lunar South Pole.

# Section IV: Environment & Technology

## Chapter 11: Environment, Ecology & Protected Areas

### Q651 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

According to Lindeman's ecological law of energy transfer, approximately what percentage of energy is passed from one trophic level to the next?

A. 50%
B. 25%
C. 10%
D. 1%

**Answer:** C. 10%
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 10% (Correct):** Raymond Lindeman proposed the 10% Law in 1942, stating that only about 10% of the energy entering one trophic level is transferred to the next higher trophic level, while the rest is lost mainly as metabolic heat.
- **A — 50%:** 50% law: Not a recognized ecological law; half of the energy is not retained across trophic levels.
- **B — 25%:** 25% law: Incorrect percentage; energy transfer efficiency between consecutive trophic levels averages roughly one-tenth, not one-fourth.
- **D — 1%:** 1% law: Roughly 1% of incident solar radiation (or ~2–10% of PAR) is captured by green plants during photosynthesis, not transferred between consumers.
- **Exam Extra:** Because roughly 90% of energy is lost at every transfer, terrestrial food chains rarely extend beyond 4 or 5 trophic levels.

### Q652 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Who coined the term "Ecosystem" in 1935?

A. A.G. Tansley
B. Ernst Haeckel
C. Charles Elton
D. Eugene Odum

**Answer:** A. A.G. Tansley
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — A.G. Tansley (Correct):** British botanist Arthur George Tansley coined the term "ecosystem" in 1935 to describe the integrated system of biotic communities and their abiotic environment.
- **B — Ernst Haeckel:** German biologist who coined the term "ecology" (Oekologie) in 1866.
- **C — Charles Elton:** British ecologist famous for introducing ecological pyramids and the concept of the ecological niche in 1927.
- **D — Eugene Odum:** American ecologist who authored the classic textbook Fundamentals of Ecology and advanced ecosystem energetics, but did not coin the term.
- **Exam Extra:** Eugene Odum is widely regarded as the "Father of Modern Ecology" (and of Ecosystem Ecology), whereas Ramdeo Misra is known as the "Father of Indian Ecology".

### Q653 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Who coined the term "Ecology" in 1866?

A. A.G. Tansley
B. Ernst Haeckel
C. Norman Myers
D. Carl Linnaeus

**Answer:** B. Ernst Haeckel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Ernst Haeckel (Correct):** German biologist Ernst Haeckel coined the term "ecology" (originally Oekologie, from Greek oikos meaning house) in 1866 to denote the study of organisms in relation to their environment.
- **A — A.G. Tansley:** Coined the term "ecosystem" in 1935.
- **C — Norman Myers:** British environmentalist who introduced the concept of "biodiversity hotspots" in 1988.
- **D — Carl Linnaeus:** Swedish naturalist who developed binomial nomenclature and modern taxonomy.
- **Exam Extra:** In 1969, Robert Whittaker proposed the widely used Five-Kingdom classification system (Monera, Protista, Fungi, Plantae, Animalia).

### Q654 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Which ecological pyramid is always upright and can never be inverted in any ecosystem?

A. Pyramid of biomass
B. Pyramid of numbers
C. Pyramid of age structure
D. Pyramid of energy

**Answer:** D. Pyramid of energy
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Pyramid of energy (Correct):** The pyramid of energy is always upright because energy flows unidirectionally from producers to consumers and a large portion is lost as heat at each trophic level according to the second law of thermodynamics.
- **A — Pyramid of biomass:** Can be inverted in marine/aquatic ecosystems where tiny phytoplankton support a larger standing biomass of zooplankton and fish.
- **B — Pyramid of numbers:** Can be inverted in a single-tree parasitic food chain where one tree supports many insects and parasites.
- **C — Pyramid of age structure:** Represents population distribution across pre-reproductive, reproductive, and post-reproductive age groups, not trophic energy flow.
- **Exam Extra:** Even in ecosystems where biomass or number pyramids are inverted (such as a tree ecosystem or marine food chain), the energy pyramid remains strictly upright.

### Q655 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

In which ecosystem is the pyramid of biomass generally inverted?

A. Grassland ecosystem
B. Forest ecosystem
C. Marine ecosystem
D. Desert ecosystem

**Answer:** C. Marine ecosystem
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Marine ecosystem (Correct):** In marine (aquatic) ecosystems, the pyramid of biomass is typically inverted because the standing crop biomass of microscopic phytoplankton at a given moment is smaller than that of the zooplankton and fishes feeding on them.
- **A — Grassland ecosystem:** Has an upright pyramid of biomass because grass biomass far exceeds that of herbivores and carnivores.
- **B — Forest ecosystem:** Has an upright pyramid of biomass because large trees hold much greater biomass than forest herbivores and predators.
- **D — Desert ecosystem:** Has an upright pyramid of biomass with sparse producer biomass supporting even smaller consumer biomass.
- **Exam Extra:** Phytoplankton can support a larger consumer biomass because they have an extremely rapid reproduction and turnover rate.

### Q656 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Which type of biological interaction benefits one species while the other is neither harmed nor benefited?

A. Commensalism
B. Mutualism
C. Amensalism
D. Parasitism

**Answer:** A. Commensalism
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Commensalism (Correct):** Commensalism is a (+, 0) biological interaction in which one species benefits while the other species is neither harmed nor benefited, such as an orchid growing as an epiphyte on a mango tree or barnacles on a whale.
- **B — Mutualism:** A (+, +) interaction where both participating species derive mutual benefit, such as Rhizobium bacteria and leguminous plants.
- **C — Amensalism:** A (-, 0) interaction where one species is harmed or inhibited while the other remains unaffected, such as Penicillium mold secreting penicillin that kills bacteria.
- **D — Parasitism:** A (+, -) interaction where the parasite benefits at the expense of the host, such as Cuscuta (dodder) on hedge plants.
- **Exam Extra:** Clownfish living among sea anemone tentacles is a classic textbook example of commensalism (the clownfish gains protection while the anemone is unaffected).

### Q657 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Lichen is a symbiotic association between which two groups of organisms?

A. Bryophytes and bacteria
B. Algae and fungi
C. Fungi and pteridophytes
D. Algae and gymnosperms

**Answer:** B. Algae and fungi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Algae and fungi (Correct):** Lichens represent a classic mutualistic symbiotic association between a photosynthetic partner (green algae or cyanobacteria, the phycobiont) and a fungal partner (the mycobiont).
- **A — Bryophytes and bacteria:** Not lichens; mosses are bryophytes, though some bryophytes like Anthoceros host Nostoc cyanobacteria separately.
- **C — Fungi and pteridophytes:** Not lichens; Azolla (a water fern/pteridophyte) has a symbiosis with Anabaena cyanobacteria.
- **D — Algae and gymnosperms:** Not lichens; cycad coralloid roots associate with nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria, not algae.
- **Exam Extra:** In a lichen, the algal partner prepares food through photosynthesis while the fungal partner provides shelter, water, and mineral absorption.

### Q658 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Which organisms occupy the first trophic level (T1) in a grazing food chain?

A. Primary consumers
B. Secondary consumers
C. Decomposers
D. Producers

**Answer:** D. Producers
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Producers (Correct):** Autotrophs (green plants and photosynthetic organisms, called producers) occupy the first trophic level (T1) because they capture solar energy to synthesize organic food from inorganic substances.
- **A — Primary consumers:** Herbivores that feed directly on producers and occupy the second trophic level (T2).
- **B — Secondary consumers:** Carnivores that feed on herbivores and occupy the third trophic level (T3).
- **C — Decomposers:** Saprotrophic fungi and bacteria that break down dead organic matter from all trophic levels in the detritus food chain.
- **Exam Extra:** Herbivores (primary consumers) occupy the second trophic level (T2), and primary carnivores (secondary consumers) occupy the third trophic level (T3).

### Q659 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

Which step of decomposition releases inorganic nutrients from humus into the soil?

A. Humification
B. Leaching
C. Mineralization
D. Fragmentation

**Answer:** C. Mineralization
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Mineralization (Correct):** Mineralization is the final step of decomposition in which microbes degrade humus to release inorganic nutrients (such as nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium) back into the soil.
- **A — Humification:** The process of forming dark-colored, amorphous, nutrient-rich colloidal organic matter called humus, which resists rapid microbial breakdown.
- **B — Leaching:** The downward movement of water-soluble inorganic nutrients into deeper soil horizons where they precipitate as unavailable salts.
- **D — Fragmentation:** The physical breakdown of detritus into smaller particles by detritivores such as earthworms.
- **Exam Extra:** The five sequential steps of decomposition are fragmentation, leaching, catabolism, humification, and mineralization.

### Q660 · Ecosystems, 10% Law, Pyramids & Interactions

In ecosystem energetics, which quantity equals Gross Primary Productivity minus plant respiratory losses (GPP - R)?

A. Net Primary Productivity
B. Gross Primary Productivity
C. Secondary Productivity
D. Standing Crop

**Answer:** A. Net Primary Productivity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Net Primary Productivity (Correct):** Net Primary Productivity (NPP) equals Gross Primary Productivity (GPP) minus respiratory losses (R) by producers: NPP = GPP - R. It represents the biomass available for consumption by heterotrophs.
- **B — Gross Primary Productivity:** The total rate of organic matter production during photosynthesis, before subtracting plant respiration losses.
- **C — Secondary Productivity:** The rate of formation of new organic matter by consumers (heterotrophs) rather than producers.
- **D — Standing Crop:** The total mass of living material present in a trophic level at a particular time, not a rate equation.
- **Exam Extra:** Tropical rainforests, coral reefs, and estuaries have the highest net primary productivity among Earth's ecosystems, whereas open oceans and deserts have very low NPP per unit area.

### Q661 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Who introduced the concept of "Biodiversity Hotspots" in 1988?

A. Edward O. Wilson
B. Norman Myers
C. Paul Ehrlich
D. G. Evelyn Hutchinson

**Answer:** B. Norman Myers
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Norman Myers (Correct):** British ecologist Norman Myers coined and developed the concept of "biodiversity hotspots" in 1988 to identify regions with exceptional endemism that face severe habitat loss.
- **A — Edward O. Wilson:** American biologist known as the "Father of Biodiversity" for popularizing the term "biodiversity" in the late 1980s.
- **C — Paul Ehrlich:** American biologist famous for the "Rivet Popper Hypothesis" explaining how species loss affects ecosystem stability.
- **D — G. Evelyn Hutchinson:** Anglo-American ecologist celebrated for developing the multidimensional niche concept.
- **Exam Extra:** To qualify as a biodiversity hotspot, a region must contain at least 1,500 endemic vascular plant species and must have lost at least 70% of its original primary vegetation.

### Q662 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which of the following regions in India is recognized as a global Biodiversity Hotspot?

A. Thar Desert
B. Deccan Plateau
C. Indo-Gangetic Plain
D. Western Ghats

**Answer:** D. Western Ghats
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Western Ghats (Correct):** The Western Ghats (shared with Sri Lanka as a hotspot) is one of India's four global biodiversity hotspots, alongside the Himalaya, Indo-Burma, and Sundaland (covering the Nicobar Islands).
- **A — Thar Desert:** An arid biome in northwestern India with specialized xerophytic life, not designated as a global biodiversity hotspot.
- **B — Deccan Plateau:** A vast semi-arid peninsular plateau; while ecologically important, it is not one of the 36 global biodiversity hotspots.
- **C — Indo-Gangetic Plain:** A fertile alluvial plain heavily altered by agriculture, not classified as a global biodiversity hotspot.
- **Exam Extra:** There are 36 recognized global biodiversity hotspots in the world, and India shares 4 of them.

### Q663 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which international organization publishes the Red Data Book (Red List of Threatened Species)?

A. UNEP
B. WWF
C. IUCN
D. CITES

**Answer:** C. IUCN
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — IUCN (Correct):** The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), headquartered in Gland, Switzerland, compiles and publishes the Red List of Threatened Species (Red Data Book) to assess the extinction risk of species worldwide.
- **A — UNEP:** The United Nations Environment Programme, headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya, coordinates UN environmental activities and publishes the Global Environment Outlook.
- **B — WWF:** The World Wide Fund for Nature, an international conservation NGO known for the Giant Panda logo and the Living Planet Report.
- **D — CITES:** The Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (1973), which regulates wildlife trade across borders.
- **Exam Extra:** IUCN was founded in 1948 in Fontainebleau, France, and the Red Data Book system began in 1964.

### Q664 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which IUCN Red List category represents wild species facing an extremely high risk of extinction in the immediate future?

A. Critically Endangered
B. Vulnerable
C. Near Threatened
D. Least Concern

**Answer:** A. Critically Endangered
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Critically Endangered (Correct):** In the IUCN Red List, "Critically Endangered" (CR) is the highest risk category for wild species, indicating an extremely high risk of extinction in the immediate future.
- **B — Vulnerable:** Indicates a high risk of extinction in the medium term—two tiers below Critically Endangered.
- **C — Near Threatened:** Applies to species likely to qualify for a threatened category in the near future, not currently facing extreme extinction risk.
- **D — Least Concern:** Applies to widespread and abundant species with low extinction risk.
- **Exam Extra:** In India, the Great Indian Bustard, Gharial, and Pygmy Hog are classified as Critically Endangered on the IUCN Red List.

### Q665 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which endangered deer species is endemic to Dachigam National Park in Jammu and Kashmir?

A. Sangai
B. Hangul
C. Nilgiri Tahr
D. Lion-tailed Macaque

**Answer:** B. Hangul
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Hangul (Correct):** The Hangul (Kashmir Stag, Cervus hanglu hanglu) is a critically endangered subspecies of red deer endemic to Dachigam National Park in Jammu and Kashmir.
- **A — Sangai:** The brow-antlered "dancing deer" endemic to Keibul Lamjao National Park on Loktak Lake in Manipur.
- **C — Nilgiri Tahr:** An endangered mountain ungulate endemic to the Nilgiri Hills and southern Western Ghats of Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
- **D — Lion-tailed Macaque:** An Old World monkey endemic to the evergreen rainforests of the Western Ghats.
- **Exam Extra:** The Hangul is the state animal of Jammu and Kashmir and is protected under Schedule I of the Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972.

### Q666 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which of the following is an example of ex-situ biodiversity conservation?

A. National Park
B. Biosphere Reserve
C. Wildlife Sanctuary
D. Seed Bank

**Answer:** D. Seed Bank
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Seed Bank (Correct):** A seed bank is an ex-situ ("off-site") conservation method because seeds are preserved away from their natural habitat in controlled, low-temperature facilities.
- **A — National Park:** An in-situ ("on-site") conservation area where species are protected inside their natural ecosystem.
- **B — Biosphere Reserve:** An in-situ conservation landscape comprising core, buffer, and transition zones within natural habitats.
- **C — Wildlife Sanctuary:** An in-situ protected area focused on conserving wild fauna in their native habitat.
- **Exam Extra:** Other ex-situ methods include botanical gardens, zoological parks, tissue culture, and cryopreservation of gametes at -196 °C in liquid nitrogen.

### Q667 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

In which year was the Ganges River Dolphin declared the National Aquatic Animal of India?

A. 2005
B. 2012
C. 2009
D. 2015

**Answer:** C. 2009
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 2009 (Correct):** The Ganges River Dolphin (Platanista gangetica) was declared the National Aquatic Animal of India in October 2009 to strengthen river conservation efforts.
- **A — 2005:** The year the National Tiger Conservation Authority (NTCA) was recommended/constituted following the Wildlife (Protection) Act amendment process (established 2005/2006), not the dolphin declaration.
- **B — 2012:** Not the declaration year; Project Dolphin was later announced in 2020.
- **D — 2015:** The year the Paris Climate Agreement was signed, unrelated to the National Aquatic Animal declaration.
- **Exam Extra:** October 5 is observed annually in India as National Dolphin Day; the Gangetic dolphin is locally called "Susu" and is virtually blind, navigating by echolocation.

### Q668 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Among the "Evil Quartet" causes of biodiversity loss, which is the primary and most significant driver?

A. Habitat loss and fragmentation
B. Co-extinction
C. Alien species invasion
D. Over-exploitation

**Answer:** A. Habitat loss and fragmentation
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Habitat loss and fragmentation (Correct):** Habitat loss and fragmentation—mainly caused by deforestation, agriculture expansion, and urbanization—is the single largest driver of global biodiversity loss among the "Evil Quartet."
- **B — Co-extinction:** Occurs when the extinction of one species triggers the loss of an obligately dependent species (such as a host-specific parasite), representing a secondary cause.
- **C — Alien species invasion:** Non-native species (such as Water Hyacinth or Nile Perch) outcompeting native species, an important but secondary driver compared to habitat loss.
- **D — Over-exploitation:** Unsustainable hunting, fishing, or harvesting of species (such as the Dodo or Steller's sea cow), ranking after habitat loss overall.
- **Exam Extra:** The four major causes of biodiversity loss, collectively termed the "Evil Quartet" by Jared Diamond, are habitat loss & fragmentation, over-exploitation, alien species invasions, and co-extinctions.

### Q669 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

Which endemic deer species is known as the "dancing deer" of Manipur?

A. Swamp Deer (Barasingha)
B. Sangai (Brow-antlered deer)
C. Chinkara
D. Blackbuck

**Answer:** B. Sangai (Brow-antlered deer)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sangai (Brow-antlered deer) (Correct):** The Sangai (Rucervus eldii eldii), also known as the brow-antlered deer or "dancing deer," is endemic to the floating phumdis of Keibul Lamjao National Park in Manipur.
- **A — Swamp Deer (Barasingha):** Found chiefly in Kanha National Park (Madhya Pradesh) and Dudhwa National Park (Uttar Pradesh), not endemic to Manipur.
- **C — Chinkara:** The Indian gazelle inhabiting arid plains and deserts of Rajasthan and Gujarat.
- **D — Blackbuck:** An antelope native to open grasslands across mainland India, notably Velavadar in Gujarat.
- **Exam Extra:** The Sangai is the state animal of Manipur and relies on floating biomass mats called "phumdis" for food and shelter.

### Q670 · Biodiversity Hotspots, IUCN & Endemic Species

According to the India State of Forest Report (ISFR), which Indian state has the largest forest cover by total area?

A. Arunachal Pradesh
B. Chhattisgarh
C. Mizoram
D. Madhya Pradesh

**Answer:** D. Madhya Pradesh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Madhya Pradesh (Correct):** According to the India State of Forest Report (ISFR), Madhya Pradesh consistently holds the largest total forest cover by geographical area among all Indian states.
- **A — Arunachal Pradesh:** Ranks second in India in total forest cover area after Madhya Pradesh.
- **B — Chhattisgarh:** Ranks third in India in total forest cover area.
- **C — Mizoram:** Has the highest percentage of forest cover relative to state area, not the largest total forest area.
- **Exam Extra:** While Madhya Pradesh leads in total forest area, Mizoram holds the highest percentage of forest cover relative to its total geographical area among Indian states.

### Q671 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

In which year was Project Tiger launched in India?

A. 1972
B. 1973
C. 1980
D. 1992

**Answer:** B. 1973
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1973 (Correct):** Project Tiger was launched by the Government of India on 1 April 1973 at Jim Corbett National Park (Uttarakhand) to ensure a viable population of Bengal tigers in their natural habitats.
- **A — 1972:** The year the Wildlife (Protection) Act was enacted in India, laying the legal groundwork before Project Tiger.
- **C — 1980:** The year the Forest (Conservation) Act was enacted in India.
- **D — 1992:** The year Project Elephant was launched in India.
- **Exam Extra:** Kailash Sankhala, known as the "Tiger Man of India," served as the first director of Project Tiger.

### Q672 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which was the first National Park established in India (originally named Hailey National Park in 1936)?

A. Jim Corbett National Park
B. Kaziranga National Park
C. Kanha National Park
D. Gir National Park

**Answer:** A. Jim Corbett National Park
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Jim Corbett National Park (Correct):** Jim Corbett National Park in Uttarakhand was established in 1936 as Hailey National Park, making it India's oldest national park.
- **B — Kaziranga National Park:** Declared a reserve forest in 1908 and a wildlife sanctuary in 1950, and upgraded to a national park in 1974.
- **C — Kanha National Park:** Located in Madhya Pradesh and established as a national park in 1955.
- **D — Gir National Park:** Located in Gujarat and declared a wildlife sanctuary in 1965 and a national park in 1975.
- **Exam Extra:** It was renamed Ramganga National Park briefly before being named after hunter-conservationist Jim Corbett in 1956.

### Q673 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which is the only floating National Park in the world?

A. Sundarbans National Park
B. Bhitarkanika National Park
C. Keibul Lamjao National Park
D. Gulf of Mannar Marine National Park

**Answer:** C. Keibul Lamjao National Park
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Keibul Lamjao National Park (Correct):** Keibul Lamjao National Park, located on the southeastern shore of Loktak Lake in Manipur, is the world's only floating national park, resting on thick mats of vegetation called phumdis.
- **A — Sundarbans National Park:** Located in West Bengal; it is the largest mangrove tidal forest and tiger habitat, not a floating park.
- **B — Bhitarkanika National Park:** Located in Odisha; famous for mangrove ecosystems and saltwater crocodiles.
- **D — Gulf of Mannar Marine National Park:** Located off Tamil Nadu; consists of coastal islands and coral reefs, not floating vegetation mats.
- **Exam Extra:** Loktak Lake is the largest freshwater lake in Northeast India and is a Ramsar site placed on the Montreux Record.

### Q674 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which was the first Biosphere Reserve established in India in 1986?

A. Nanda Devi Biosphere Reserve
B. Sundarbans Biosphere Reserve
C. Nokrek Biosphere Reserve
D. Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve

**Answer:** D. Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve (Correct):** The Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve, spanning Tamil Nadu, Kerala, and Karnataka in the Western Ghats, was established in 1986 as India's first biosphere reserve under UNESCO's Man and the Biosphere (MAB) framework.
- **A — Nanda Devi Biosphere Reserve:** Established in 1988 in Uttarakhand, two years after Nilgiri.
- **B — Sundarbans Biosphere Reserve:** Established in 1989 in West Bengal.
- **C — Nokrek Biosphere Reserve:** Established in 1988 in the Garo Hills of Meghalaya.
- **Exam Extra:** India has 18 notified Biosphere Reserves, of which 12 are included in UNESCO's World Network of Biosphere Reserves.

### Q675 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Kaziranga National Park in Assam is globally famous for conserving which species?

A. Asiatic Lion
B. Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros
C. Snow Leopard
D. Indian Wild Ass

**Answer:** B. Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros (Correct):** Kaziranga National Park in Assam (a UNESCO World Heritage Site) hosts the world's largest population of the Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros (Rhinoceros unicornis).
- **A — Asiatic Lion:** Found in the wild exclusively in the Gir forest region of Gujarat.
- **C — Snow Leopard:** Inhabits high-altitude Himalayan parks such as Hemis (Ladakh), Pin Valley (Himachal Pradesh), and Gangotri (Uttarakhand).
- **D — Indian Wild Ass:** Wild Ass: Conserved in the Indian Wild Ass Sanctuary in the Little Rann of Kutch, Gujarat.
- **Exam Extra:** Pobitora Wildlife Sanctuary in Assam has the highest population density of one-horned rhinoceroses in the world.

### Q676 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which protected area in India is the only natural habitat of the Asiatic Lion?

A. Gir National Park
B. Ranthambore National Park
C. Bandipur National Park
D. Dachigam National Park

**Answer:** A. Gir National Park
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Gir National Park (Correct):** Gir National Park and Wildlife Sanctuary in Gujarat is the sole remaining natural habitat of the endangered Asiatic Lion (Panthera leo leo).
- **B — Ranthambore National Park:** Located in Rajasthan and famous for Bengal tigers, not Asiatic lions.
- **C — Bandipur National Park:** Located in Karnataka as part of the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve, known for tigers and Asian elephants.
- **D — Dachigam National Park:** Located in Jammu and Kashmir and known for the Hangul (Kashmir stag).
- **Exam Extra:** Kuno National Park in Madhya Pradesh was prepared as an alternative second home for Asiatic lions and later became India's cheetah reintroduction site in 2022.

### Q677 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which is the largest National Park in India by total area?

A. Desert National Park
B. Similipal National Park
C. Hemis National Park
D. Gangotri National Park

**Answer:** C. Hemis National Park
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Hemis National Park (Correct):** Hemis National Park in Ladakh covers roughly 4,400 sq km, making it the largest national park in India by area and the only national park in India north of the Himalayas.
- **A — Desert National Park:** Located in Rajasthan (~3,162 sq km), making it the second-largest national park in India.
- **B — Similipal National Park:** Located in Odisha (~845.7 sq km core national park area), much smaller than Hemis.
- **D — Gangotri National Park:** Located in Uttarakhand (~2,390 sq km), the third-largest national park in India.
- **Exam Extra:** Hemis National Park is globally renowned for its high density of snow leopards.

### Q678 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which is the smallest National Park in India by area?

A. Fossil National Park
B. Guindy National Park
C. Silent Valley National Park
D. South Button Island National Park

**Answer:** D. South Button Island National Park
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — South Button Island National Park (Correct):** South Button Island National Park, located in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, is the smallest national park in India, covering an area of about 0.03 sq km (5 sq km including surrounding waters in some records, with land area ~0.03 sq km).
- **A — Fossil National Park:** Fossil National Park (Ghughua): Located in Madhya Pradesh (~0.27 sq km), one of the smallest mainland parks but larger than South Button Island.
- **B — Guindy National Park:** Located inside Chennai, Tamil Nadu (~2.70 sq km), one of the smallest urban national parks.
- **C — Silent Valley National Park:** Located in Kerala (~89.5 sq km), famous for tropical rainforests and lion-tailed macaques.
- **Exam Extra:** Andaman and Nicobar Islands also contain Middle Button Island and North Button Island National Parks, forming part of the Rani Jhansi Marine National Park region.

### Q679 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which Indian state has the highest number of National Parks?

A. Assam
B. Madhya Pradesh
C. Uttarakhand
D. Rajasthan

**Answer:** B. Madhya Pradesh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Madhya Pradesh (Correct):** Madhya Pradesh has the highest number of National Parks among all Indian states and Union Territories, and is often referred to as the "Tiger State of India" as well.
- **A — Assam:** Has 7 national parks (including Kaziranga, Manas, Nameri, Orang, Dibru-Saikhowa, Raimona, and Dehing Patkai).
- **C — Uttarakhand:** Has 6 national parks (Corbett, Rajaji, Nanda Devi, Valley of Flowers, Gangotri, and Govind Pashu Vihar).
- **D — Rajasthan:** Has 5 national parks (Ranthambore, Sariska, Keoladeo, Desert, and Mukundra Hills).
- **Exam Extra:** Among Union Territories, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands have the highest number of national parks (9).

### Q680 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

In which year was Project Elephant launched by the Government of India?

A. 1992
B. 1973
C. 1986
D. 2002

**Answer:** A. 1992
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1992 (Correct):** Project Elephant was launched by the Government of India in February 1992 as a centrally sponsored scheme to protect elephants, their habitats, and migratory corridors.
- **B — 1973:** The year Project Tiger was launched in India.
- **C — 1986:** The year the Environment (Protection) Act was enacted and the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve was established.
- **D — 2002:** The year the Biological Diversity Act was enacted in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The Asian Elephant was declared India's "National Heritage Animal" in 2010.

### Q681 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which wetland, alongside Keoladeo National Park, was designated as India's first Ramsar site in 1981?

A. Wular Lake
B. Loktak Lake
C. Chilika Lake
D. Sambhar Lake

**Answer:** C. Chilika Lake
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Chilika Lake (Correct):** Chilika Lake in Odisha (along with Keoladeo National Park in Rajasthan) was designated on 1 October 1981 as India's first Ramsar Wetland of International Importance.
- **A — Wular Lake:** Located in Jammu and Kashmir; designated as a Ramsar site later in March 1990.
- **B — Loktak Lake:** Located in Manipur; designated as a Ramsar site later in March 1990.
- **D — Sambhar Lake:** Located in Rajasthan; designated as a Ramsar site later in March 1990.
- **Exam Extra:** Chilika Lake is Asia's largest brackish water lagoon and hosts the endangered Irrawaddy dolphin.

### Q682 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

World Wetlands Day on February 2 commemorates the adoption of which international convention?

A. Basel Convention
B. Stockholm Convention
C. Bonn Convention
D. Ramsar Convention

**Answer:** D. Ramsar Convention
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Ramsar Convention (Correct):** World Wetlands Day is observed every year on 2 February to mark the adoption of the Ramsar Convention on Wetlands in Ramsar, Iran, on 2 February 1971.
- **A — Basel Convention:** Adopted in 1989 to control transboundary movements of hazardous wastes and their disposal.
- **B — Stockholm Convention:** Adopted in 2001 to eliminate or restrict Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs).
- **C — Bonn Convention:** Bonn Convention (CMS): Adopted in 1979 for the conservation of migratory species of wild animals.
- **Exam Extra:** India became a party to the Ramsar Convention in 1982 (following designations in 1981) and now has over 80 Ramsar sites across the country.

### Q683 · National Parks, Tiger Reserves, Biosphere Reserves & Ramsar Sites

Which is the largest Tiger Reserve in India by area?

A. Jim Corbett Tiger Reserve
B. Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve
C. Sundarbans Tiger Reserve
D. Periyar Tiger Reserve

**Answer:** B. Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve (Correct):** Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve, spread across Andhra Pradesh and Telangana in the Nallamala Hills, is the largest tiger reserve in India by total area.
- **A — Jim Corbett Tiger Reserve:** Located in Uttarakhand; India's first tiger reserve (1973) and one with very high tiger density, but not the largest by area.
- **C — Sundarbans Tiger Reserve:** Located in West Bengal; the largest mangrove tiger habitat, smaller in total notified area than Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam.
- **D — Periyar Tiger Reserve:** Located in Kerala; famous for elephants and tigers around Periyar Lake, not the largest by area.
- **Exam Extra:** Bor Tiger Reserve in Maharashtra is the smallest tiger reserve in India by area.

### Q684 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Which gas is the largest contributor to human-caused global warming by total emissions?

A. Nitrogen
B. Oxygen
C. Carbon dioxide
D. Argon

**Answer:** C. Carbon dioxide
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Carbon dioxide (Correct):** Carbon dioxide (CO2) is the single largest contributor to human-caused (anthropogenic) global warming by total emissions, primarily from burning fossil fuels, cement production, and deforestation.
- **A — Nitrogen:** Makes up about 78% of Earth's dry atmosphere and is not a greenhouse gas.
- **B — Oxygen:** Makes up about 21% of Earth's dry atmosphere and does not trap outgoing infrared radiation.
- **D — Argon:** An inert noble gas making up about 0.93% of the atmosphere with no greenhouse warming effect.
- **Exam Extra:** While methane has a higher Global Warming Potential (GWP) per molecule over 100 years, CO2 accounts for roughly three-quarters of total anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions.

### Q685 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

The protective ozone layer is mainly found in which atmospheric layer?

A. Stratosphere
B. Troposphere
C. Mesosphere
D. Thermosphere

**Answer:** A. Stratosphere
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Stratosphere (Correct):** About 90% of atmospheric ozone resides in the stratosphere (roughly 15 to 35 km above Earth's surface), where it forms the ozone layer that absorbs harmful solar UV-B radiation.
- **B — Troposphere:** The lowest atmospheric layer where weather phenomena occur; ground-level ozone here is a harmful pollutant rather than the protective ozone shield.
- **C — Mesosphere:** The middle layer above the stratosphere (about 50–85 km) where meteors burn up; it contains negligible ozone.
- **D — Thermosphere:** The high upper atmospheric layer (above ~85 km) characterized by auroras and high temperatures due to solar X-ray/EUV absorption.
- **Exam Extra:** The remaining ~10% of ozone in the troposphere acts as a secondary air pollutant and greenhouse gas, forming photochemical smog.

### Q686 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Minamata disease is caused by poisoning from which heavy metal?

A. Cadmium
B. Arsenic
C. Lead
D. Mercury

**Answer:** D. Mercury
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Mercury (Correct):** Minamata disease is a severe neurological syndrome caused by methylmercury poisoning, first discovered in 1956 in Minamata City, Japan, after industrial wastewater contaminated local seafood.
- **A — Cadmium:** Causes Itai-Itai disease, characterized by painful bone softening (osteomalacia) and kidney damage.
- **B — Arsenic:** Chronic groundwater contamination causes Blackfoot disease and skin lesions (arsenicosis).
- **C — Lead:** Causes plumbism (lead poisoning), leading to anemia, abdominal colic, and neurological impairment in children.
- **Exam Extra:** Organic methylmercury bioaccumulates and biomagnifies up aquatic food chains, reaching toxic levels in predatory fish and shellfish.

### Q687 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Itai-Itai disease is caused by chronic contamination of water and crops with which metal?

A. Mercury
B. Cadmium
C. Fluoride
D. Chromium

**Answer:** B. Cadmium
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Cadmium (Correct):** Itai-Itai disease ("it hurts, it hurts") was caused by mass cadmium poisoning in Toyama Prefecture, Japan, through rice irrigated with river water contaminated by mining effluents.
- **A — Mercury:** Causes Minamata disease, affecting the central nervous system.
- **C — Fluoride:** Excess intake causes dental and skeletal fluorosis ("knock-knee" syndrome).
- **D — Chromium:** Hexavalent chromium exposure is associated with skin ulcers, nasal septum perforation, and lung cancer, not Itai-Itai disease.
- **Exam Extra:** Cadmium toxicity primarily damages the kidneys and causes severe osteoporosis and osteomalacia (softening of bones).

### Q688 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Blue Baby Syndrome (Methemoglobinemia) in infants is caused by excess levels of which ion in drinking water?

A. Nitrate
B. Fluoride
C. Sulfate
D. Chloride

**Answer:** A. Nitrate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Nitrate (Correct):** Blue Baby Syndrome (infantile methemoglobinemia) occurs when excess nitrate in drinking water is converted to nitrite in an infant's gut, oxidizing hemoglobin into methemoglobin that cannot carry oxygen.
- **B — Fluoride:** Excess fluoride in drinking water causes mottled teeth (dental fluorosis) and bone deformities (skeletal fluorosis).
- **C — Sulfate:** High sulfate levels in drinking water primarily have a laxative effect and cause gastrointestinal irritation.
- **D — Chloride:** High chloride levels impart a salty taste to water and corrode pipes, rather than causing methemoglobinemia.
- **Exam Extra:** The World Health Organization (WHO) and BIS drinking water guideline limit for nitrate (NO3-) is 45–50 mg/L to prevent methemoglobinemia.

### Q689 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Blackfoot disease is caused by prolonged consumption of groundwater contaminated with which element?

A. Selenium
B. Copper
C. Arsenic
D. Nickel

**Answer:** C. Arsenic
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Arsenic (Correct):** Blackfoot disease is a severe peripheral vascular disease (causing gangrene of the extremities) linked to long-term consumption of groundwater contaminated with high levels of arsenic.
- **A — Selenium:** Excess selenium causes selenosis, marked by hair loss, brittle nails, and garlic-scented breath.
- **B — Copper:** Excess copper accumulation causes Wilson's disease (genetic) or gastrointestinal distress, not Blackfoot disease.
- **D — Nickel:** Primarily causes allergic contact dermatitis and respiratory tract irritation rather than peripheral vascular gangrene.
- **Exam Extra:** Chronic arsenic exposure in the Ganga-Brahmaputra basin also causes hyperpigmentation and palmar-plantar keratosis (arsenicosis).

### Q690 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Which unit is used to measure the thickness of the atmospheric ozone layer?

A. Decibel
B. Becquerel
C. Sievert
D. Dobson Unit

**Answer:** D. Dobson Unit
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Dobson Unit (Correct):** The thickness of the atmospheric ozone column is measured in Dobson Units (DU), named after British physicist G.M.B. Dobson, who built the first ozone spectrophotometer.
- **A — Decibel:** The logarithmic unit used to measure sound intensity and noise pollution.
- **B — Becquerel:** The SI unit of radioactivity, equal to one nuclear decay per second.
- **C — Sievert:** The SI unit used to measure the biological health effect of ionizing radiation dose.
- **Exam Extra:** One Dobson Unit equals a layer of pure ozone 0.01 mm thick at standard temperature and pressure (STP); normal atmospheric column ozone averages around 300 DU, while an "ozone hole" is defined below 220 DU.

### Q691 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Which toxic gas leaked during the 1984 Bhopal Gas Tragedy?

A. Phosgene
B. Methyl isocyanate
C. Carbon monoxide
D. Sulfur hexafluoride

**Answer:** B. Methyl isocyanate
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Methyl isocyanate (Correct):** The 1984 Bhopal Gas Tragedy at the Union Carbide India Limited pesticide plant was caused by the accidental leakage of toxic Methyl Isocyanate (MIC, CH3NCO) gas.
- **A — Phosgene:** Used as an intermediate in producing MIC, though the primary lethal gas cloud released over Bhopal was Methyl Isocyanate.
- **C — Carbon monoxide:** A colorless, odorless toxic gas from incomplete combustion, not the pesticide precursor leaked in Bhopal.
- **D — Sulfur hexafluoride:** An extremely potent synthetic greenhouse gas used in electrical insulation, not involved in the Bhopal disaster.
- **Exam Extra:** The tragedy occurred on the night of December 2–3, 1984, and prompted India to enact the comprehensive Environment (Protection) Act in 1986.

### Q692 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Which pair of gases is the primary cause of Acid Rain?

A. Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides
B. Methane and Ozone
C. Carbon monoxide and Hydrogen sulfide
D. Ammonia and Argon

**Answer:** A. Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Sulfur dioxide and Nitrogen oxides (Correct):** Acid rain is primarily caused by emissions of sulfur dioxide (SO2) and nitrogen oxides (NOx), which react with atmospheric water vapor and oxygen to form sulfuric acid (H2SO4) and nitric acid (HNO3).
- **B — Methane and Ozone:** Greenhouse and photochemical gases that contribute to warming and smog rather than acid rain.
- **C — Carbon monoxide and Hydrogen sulfide:** Incomplete combustion and anaerobic decay gases that do not form the main nitric/sulfuric acid precipitation.
- **D — Ammonia and Argon:** Ammonia is an alkaline gas that neutralizes acids, while argon is an inert noble gas.
- **Exam Extra:** Normal rainwater is slightly acidic (pH ~5.6) due to dissolved CO2 forming carbonic acid; precipitation is classified as acid rain when its pH falls below 5.6.

### Q693 · Pollution Diseases, AQI & Climate Change

Lichens are widely used as natural bioindicators of which type of pollution?

A. Thermal pollution
B. Noise pollution
C. Air pollution
D. Radioactive pollution

**Answer:** C. Air pollution
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Air pollution (Correct):** Lichens are highly sensitive to sulfur dioxide (SO2) in the atmosphere because they absorb water and nutrients directly from the air without protective cuticles, making them reliable bioindicators of air pollution.
- **A — Thermal pollution:** Excess heat discharged into water bodies; commonly monitored using dissolved oxygen levels and sensitive aquatic macroinvertebrates.
- **B — Noise pollution:** Excessive sound measured in decibels (dB), not monitored by biological lichen growth.
- **D — Radioactive pollution:** Monitored with Geiger-Müller counters or dosimeters, not specifically indicated by lichen sensitivity.
- **Exam Extra:** Areas with heavy industrial air pollution become "lichen deserts," whereas clean air regions support diverse fruticose and foliose lichens.

### Q694 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

Which international agreement was signed in 1987 to phase out ozone-depleting substances like CFCs?

A. Basel Convention
B. Kyoto Protocol
C. Rotterdam Convention
D. Montreal Protocol

**Answer:** D. Montreal Protocol
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Montreal Protocol (Correct):** The Montreal Protocol on Substances that Deplete the Ozone Layer (signed 16 September 1987) phases out ozone-depleting substances (ODS) such as chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) and halons to protect the stratospheric ozone layer.
- **A — Basel Convention:** Regulates transboundary movement and disposal of hazardous wastes (1989).
- **B — Kyoto Protocol:** Sets legally binding emission reduction targets for greenhouse gases (1997).
- **C — Rotterdam Convention:** Promotes shared responsibility in international trade of certain hazardous chemicals and pesticides (1998).
- **Exam Extra:** World Ozone Day is celebrated every year on September 16 to commemorate the signing of the Montreal Protocol; the 2016 Kigali Amendment later added HFC phase-down under it.

### Q695 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

The Kyoto Protocol (1997) primarily aims to limit and reduce emissions of which category of pollutants?

A. Persistent organic pollutants
B. Greenhouse gases
C. Ozone-depleting chlorofluorocarbons
D. Marine plastic debris

**Answer:** B. Greenhouse gases
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Greenhouse gases (Correct):** The Kyoto Protocol (adopted in 1997 in Kyoto, Japan) operationalizes the UNFCCC by committing industrialized countries to legally binding targets to reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
- **A — Persistent organic pollutants:** Controlled under the 2001 Stockholm Convention rather than the Kyoto Protocol.
- **C — Ozone-depleting chlorofluorocarbons:** Regulated under the 1987 Montreal Protocol, not the Kyoto Protocol.
- **D — Marine plastic debris:** Addressed through marine conventions such as MARPOL and ongoing UN plastics treaty negotiations, not Kyoto.
- **Exam Extra:** The Kyoto Protocol originally covered six greenhouse gases: CO2, CH4, N2O, HFCs, PFCs, and SF6 (with NF3 added in the second commitment period).

### Q696 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

The landmark Paris Agreement on climate change (2015) was adopted at which UNFCCC Conference of the Parties (COP)?

A. COP21
B. COP15
C. COP24
D. COP28

**Answer:** A. COP21
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — COP21 (Correct):** The Paris Agreement on climate change was adopted on 12 December 2015 at COP21 (21st Conference of the Parties to the UNFCCC) in Paris, aiming to limit global warming well below 2 °C and pursue 1.5 °C.
- **B — COP15:** Held in Copenhagen in 2009, producing the non-binding Copenhagen Accord.
- **C — COP24:** Held in Katowice, Poland, in 2018 to finalize the Paris Agreement Rulebook.
- **D — COP28:** Held in Dubai, UAE, in 2023, concluding the first Global Stocktake of the Paris Agreement.
- **Exam Extra:** At COP26 in Glasgow (2021), India announced its "Panchamrit" climate targets, including reaching Net Zero emissions by 2070.

### Q697 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

The 1992 United Nations Earth Summit (UNCED) was held in which city?

A. Stockholm
B. Johannesburg
C. Rio de Janeiro
D. Nairobi

**Answer:** C. Rio de Janeiro
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Rio de Janeiro (Correct):** The United Nations Conference on Environment and Development (UNCED), popularly known as the Earth Summit, was held in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, in June 1992.
- **A — Stockholm:** Hosted the 1972 UN Conference on the Human Environment (the first major UN environment conference, where UNEP was born).
- **B — Johannesburg:** Hosted the 2002 World Summit on Sustainable Development (Rio+10).
- **D — Nairobi:** Headquarters of UNEP and host of the 1982 Stockholm+10 session, not the 1992 Earth Summit.
- **Exam Extra:** The 1992 Rio Earth Summit produced Agenda 21, the UNFCCC, the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), and led to the UNCCD.

### Q698 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

In which year was the Wildlife (Protection) Act enacted in India?

A. 1974
B. 1980
C. 1981
D. 1972

**Answer:** D. 1972
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 1972 (Correct):** The Wildlife (Protection) Act was enacted by the Parliament of India in 1972 to provide legal protection to wild animals, birds, and plants and establish protected areas.
- **A — 1974:** The year the Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act was enacted in India.
- **B — 1980:** The year the Forest (Conservation) Act was enacted in India.
- **C — 1981:** The year the Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act was enacted in India.
- **Exam Extra:** Following the 2022 Amendment, the Wildlife (Protection) Act now has 4 streamlined schedules (reducing the earlier 6 schedules) and aligns with CITES implementation.

### Q699 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

In which year was India's umbrella Environment (Protection) Act enacted?

A. 1972
B. 1986
C. 1991
D. 2002

**Answer:** B. 1986
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1986 (Correct):** The Environment (Protection) Act (EPA) was enacted in 1986 under Article 253 of the Indian Constitution as an umbrella legislation for environmental protection following the 1984 Bhopal Gas Tragedy.
- **A — 1972:** The year the Wildlife (Protection) Act was enacted.
- **C — 1991:** The year the Public Liability Insurance Act was enacted in India.
- **D — 2002:** The year the Biological Diversity Act was enacted in India.
- **Exam Extra:** The Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC), which evaluates GM crops in India, functions under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.

### Q700 · Conventions, COP Summits, Indian Laws & Movements

The Chipko Movement (1973) for forest conservation originated in which present-day Indian state?

A. Uttarakhand
B. Himachal Pradesh
C. Karnataka
D. Jharkhand

**Answer:** A. Uttarakhand
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Uttarakhand (Correct):** The Chipko Movement began in 1973 in the Chamoli district (notably Reni village) of present-day Uttarakhand (then part of Uttar Pradesh), where villagers hugged trees to stop commercial logging.
- **B — Himachal Pradesh:** A Himalayan neighbor, but the Chipko Movement originated in the Garhwal region of Uttarakhand.
- **C — Karnataka:** Birthplace of the 1983 Appiko Movement (led by Pandurang Hegde), inspired by Chipko.
- **D — Jharkhand:** Known for tribal forest rights movements, not the 1973 Chipko agitation.
- **Exam Extra:** Key leaders of the Chipko Movement included Chandi Prasad Bhatt, Sunderlal Bahuguna, and Gaura Devi; it later inspired the Appiko Movement in Karnataka (1983).

---

## Chapter 12: Computer Knowledge, Space & Inventions

### Q701 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who is credited with inventing the World Wide Web (WWW) in 1989 at CERN?

A. Vint Cerf
B. Tim Berners-Lee
C. Ray Tomlinson
D. Charles Babbage

**Answer:** B. Tim Berners-Lee
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Tim Berners-Lee (Correct):** Tim Berners-Lee invented the World Wide Web in 1989 while working at CERN in Switzerland.
- **A — Vint Cerf:** Co-designed the TCP/IP protocol suite with Bob Kahn and is known as a 'Father of the Internet'.
- **C — Ray Tomlinson:** Sent the first networked email in 1971 and introduced the '@' symbol in email addresses.
- **D — Charles Babbage:** Designed the Difference Engine and Analytical Engine, earning the title 'Father of the Computer'.
- **Exam Extra:** Tim Berners-Lee also created the first web browser (WorldWideWeb, later renamed Nexus) along with the HTTP and HTML standards.

### Q702 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who invented the first mechanical printing press with movable metal type in Europe around 1440?

A. Johannes Gutenberg
B. James Watt
C. Thomas Edison
D. Samuel Morse

**Answer:** A. Johannes Gutenberg
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Johannes Gutenberg (Correct):** Johannes Gutenberg introduced the mechanical movable-type printing press in Europe around 1440 in Mainz, Germany.
- **B — James Watt:** Improved the steam engine in the 1760s–1770s by adding a separate condenser.
- **C — Thomas Edison:** Invented the phonograph, motion picture camera, and a practical long-lasting incandescent light bulb.
- **D — Samuel Morse:** Co-developed the single-wire telegraph system and Morse code.
- **Exam Extra:** The Gutenberg Bible, printed in the 1450s, was the first major European book produced using mass-produced movable metal type.

### Q703 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Which Indian physicist was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1930 for discovering the scattering of light?

A. Satyendra Nath Bose
B. C. V. Raman
C. Meghnad Saha
D. Homi J. Bhabha

**Answer:** B. C. V. Raman
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — C. V. Raman (Correct):** Sir C. V. Raman won the 1930 Nobel Prize in Physics for discovering the inelastic scattering of light, known as the Raman Effect.
- **A — Satyendra Nath Bose:** Formulated Bose-Einstein statistics with Albert Einstein; the class of particles called bosons is named after him.
- **C — Meghnad Saha:** Developed the Saha ionization equation used to describe physical conditions in stars.
- **D — Homi J. Bhabha:** Nuclear physicist who served as the chief architect of India's nuclear energy programme.
- **Exam Extra:** India celebrates National Science Day every year on 28 February to commemorate C. V. Raman's discovery of the Raman Effect in 1928.

### Q704 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who invented dynamite in 1867 by stabilizing nitroglycerin?

A. Robert Oppenheimer
B. Michael Faraday
C. Alfred Nobel
D. Otto Hahn

**Answer:** C. Alfred Nobel
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Alfred Nobel (Correct):** Alfred Nobel invented dynamite in 1867 by stabilizing volatile nitroglycerin with an inert absorbent material (kieselguhr).
- **A — Robert Oppenheimer:** Theoretical physicist who directed the Manhattan Project that built the first nuclear weapons.
- **B — Michael Faraday:** Discovered electromagnetic induction and formulated the laws of electrolysis.
- **D — Otto Hahn:** Discovered nuclear fission of heavy atomic nuclei in 1938 alongside Fritz Strassmann.
- **Exam Extra:** Alfred Nobel left his fortune from 355 patents, including dynamite, in his 1895 will to establish the Nobel Prizes.

### Q705 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Which Indian scientist invented the Crescograph to measure minute growth in plants?

A. Prafulla Chandra Ray
B. Birbal Sahni
C. Har Gobind Khorana
D. Jagadish Chandra Bose

**Answer:** D. Jagadish Chandra Bose
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Jagadish Chandra Bose (Correct):** Jagadish Chandra Bose invented the Crescograph, an instrument capable of magnifying and recording tiny plant growth movements.
- **A — Prafulla Chandra Ray:** Chemist regarded as the father of Indian chemistry who founded Bengal Chemicals & Pharmaceuticals.
- **B — Birbal Sahni:** Paleobotanist who studied fossils of the Indian subcontinent and founded the Birbal Sahni Institute of Palaeosciences.
- **C — Har Gobind Khorana:** Nobel laureate in Physiology or Medicine (1968) for helping decipher the genetic code.
- **Exam Extra:** J. C. Bose also pioneered millimeter-wave radio optics in the 1890s and invented the mercury coherer for detecting radio waves.

### Q706 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who discovered X-rays in 1895, earning the first Nobel Prize in Physics in 1901?

A. Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen
B. Henri Becquerel
C. Pierre Curie
D. J. J. Thomson

**Answer:** A. Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen (Correct):** Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen discovered X-rays on 8 November 1895 and received the inaugural Nobel Prize in Physics in 1901.
- **B — Henri Becquerel:** Discovered spontaneous radioactivity in uranium salts in 1896.
- **C — Pierre Curie:** Co-discovered polonium and radium with Marie Curie and pioneered piezoelectricity research.
- **D — J. J. Thomson:** Discovered the electron in 1897 through cathode-ray tube experiments.
- **Exam Extra:** Röntgen named the mysterious rays 'X-rays' to denote an unknown type of radiation and refused to patent his discovery.

### Q707 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who invented the smallpox vaccine in 1796, laying the foundation of immunology?

A. Louis Pasteur
B. Alexander Fleming
C. Edward Jenner
D. Robert Koch

**Answer:** C. Edward Jenner
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Edward Jenner (Correct):** Edward Jenner developed the smallpox vaccine in 1796 using cowpox material, founding modern immunology.
- **A — Louis Pasteur:** Developed vaccines for anthrax and rabies and introduced the process of pasteurization.
- **B — Alexander Fleming:** Discovered the world's first broadly effective antibiotic, penicillin, in 1928.
- **D — Robert Koch:** Identified the specific causative bacteria for tuberculosis, cholera, and anthrax.
- **Exam Extra:** Jenner inoculated eight-year-old James Phipps with cowpox (Variolae vaccinae), which gave rise to the word 'vaccination'.

### Q708 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who is widely known as the 'Father of the Indian Space Programme'?

A. Satish Dhawan
B. Vikram Sarabhai
C. A. P. J. Abdul Kalam
D. U. R. Rao

**Answer:** B. Vikram Sarabhai
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Vikram Sarabhai (Correct):** Dr. Vikram Sarabhai is revered as the 'Father of the Indian Space Programme' for initiating space research and founding ISRO's predecessor INCOSPAR.
- **A — Satish Dhawan:** Succeeded Sarabhai as ISRO Chairman in 1972 and guided India's early launch vehicle era including SLV-3.
- **C — A. P. J. Abdul Kalam:** Served as Project Director of SLV-3 and is celebrated as the 'Missile Man of India'.
- **D — U. R. Rao:** Former ISRO Chairman who led the development of India's first satellite Aryabhata.
- **Exam Extra:** Vikram Sarabhai founded the Physical Research Laboratory (PRL) in Ahmedabad in 1947 and chaired INCOSPAR when it was set up in 1962.

### Q709 · Famous Inventions & Indian Scientists

Who applied for the first recorded patent for a refracting telescope in 1608?

A. Galileo Galilei
B. Johannes Kepler
C. Isaac Newton
D. Hans Lippershey

**Answer:** D. Hans Lippershey
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Hans Lippershey (Correct):** Hans Lippershey, a German-Dutch spectacle maker, filed the earliest recorded patent application for a refracting telescope in 1608.
- **A — Galileo Galilei:** Greatly improved the refracting telescope in 1609 to make landmark astronomical discoveries, though he did not invent it first.
- **B — Johannes Kepler:** Formulated the laws of planetary motion and designed the Keplerian telescope using convex lenses.
- **C — Isaac Newton:** Built the first practical reflecting telescope using a curved mirror in 1668.
- **Exam Extra:** Galileo Galilei improved Lippershey's design in 1609 and used it to discover Jupiter's four largest moons and the phases of Venus.

### Q710 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

What was the name of India's first artificial satellite, launched on 19 April 1975?

A. Bhaskara-I
B. Rohini RS-1
C. Aryabhata
D. APPLE

**Answer:** C. Aryabhata
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Aryabhata (Correct):** Aryabhata was India's first artificial satellite, designed by ISRO and launched on 19 April 1975.
- **A — Bhaskara-I:** India's first experimental remote-sensing satellite, launched in June 1979.
- **B — Rohini RS-1:** First satellite placed into orbit by an indigenous Indian rocket (SLV-3) in July 1980.
- **D — APPLE:** Ariane Passenger Payload Experiment, India's first geostationary experimental communication satellite launched in 1981.
- **Exam Extra:** Aryabhata was launched from Kapustin Yar in the Soviet Union aboard a Kosmos-3M rocket and named after the 5th-century Indian mathematician-astronomer.

### Q711 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

Which launch vehicle carried ISRO's Chandrayaan-3 mission toward the Moon in July 2023?

A. PSLV-XL
B. GSLV Mk II
C. LVM3
D. SSLV

**Answer:** C. LVM3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — LVM3 (Correct):** ISRO's heavy-lift Launch Vehicle Mark-3 (LVM3, specifically LVM3-M4) launched Chandrayaan-3 on 14 July 2023.
- **A — PSLV-XL:** Extended Polar Satellite Launch Vehicle variant used for Chandrayaan-1 (2008) and Mangalyaan (2013).
- **B — GSLV Mk II:** Three-stage launcher using an indigenous cryogenic upper stage for medium geostationary payloads.
- **D — SSLV:** Small Satellite Launch Vehicle built for rapid, low-cost launches of small satellites up to 500 kg into low Earth orbit.
- **Exam Extra:** Chandrayaan-3's Vikram lander achieved the first soft landing near the lunar south pole on 23 August 2023 at Shiv Shakti Point; 23 August is observed as National Space Day.

### Q712 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

What is the name of India's first dedicated space-based solar observatory mission launched in September 2023?

A. AstroSat
B. Aditya-L1
C. XPoSat
D. Mangalyaan

**Answer:** B. Aditya-L1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Aditya-L1 (Correct):** Aditya-L1 is India's first space-based solar observatory, launched in September 2023 to study the Sun from the Sun-Earth L1 point.
- **A — AstroSat:** India's first dedicated multi-wavelength space astronomy observatory, launched in September 2015.
- **C — XPoSat:** X-ray Polarimeter Satellite launched in January 2024 to study polarization of cosmic X-ray sources.
- **D — Mangalyaan:** India's Mars Orbiter Mission launched in November 2013, making India the first Asian nation to reach Mars orbit.
- **Exam Extra:** Aditya-L1 was launched aboard PSLV-C57 and orbits in a halo orbit around Lagrange point L1, roughly 1.5 million km from Earth.

### Q713 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

Which of the following is an indigenous Indian surface-to-surface ballistic missile developed under the IGMDP?

A. Akash
B. Nag
C. Trishul
D. Prithvi

**Answer:** D. Prithvi
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Prithvi (Correct):** Prithvi is India's indigenous tactical surface-to-surface short-range ballistic missile developed by DRDO under the IGMDP.
- **A — Akash:** Indigenous medium-range surface-to-air missile defence system.
- **B — Nag:** Third-generation 'fire-and-forget' anti-tank guided missile developed by DRDO.
- **C — Trishul:** Short-range surface-to-air missile system developed under IGMDP.
- **Exam Extra:** The five missile systems developed under Dr. A. P. J. Abdul Kalam's Integrated Guided Missile Development Programme (PATNA) are Prithvi, Agni, Trishul, Nag, and Akash.

### Q714 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

Who was the first Indian citizen to travel into space, in April 1984 aboard Soyuz T-11?

A. Rakesh Sharma
B. Kalpana Chawla
C. Sunita Williams
D. Ravish Malhotra

**Answer:** A. Rakesh Sharma
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Rakesh Sharma (Correct):** Wing Commander Rakesh Sharma became the first Indian citizen in space on 3 April 1984 aboard the Soviet Soyuz T-11 spacecraft.
- **B — Kalpana Chawla:** First woman of Indian origin in space, flying aboard Space Shuttle Columbia in 1997 (STS-87) and 2003 (STS-107).
- **C — Sunita Williams:** NASA astronaut of Indian origin who completed multiple long-duration expeditions aboard the International Space Station.
- **D — Ravish Malhotra:** Indian Air Force pilot who trained as Rakesh Sharma's backup cosmonaut for the Soyuz T-11 mission.
- **Exam Extra:** When Prime Minister Indira Gandhi asked Rakesh Sharma how India looked from space, he famously replied, 'Saare Jahaan Se Achcha'.

### Q715 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

In which year was the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) established?

A. 1962
B. 1969
C. 1972
D. 1975

**Answer:** B. 1969
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1969 (Correct):** The Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) was formally established on 15 August 1969, replacing INCOSPAR.
- **A — 1962:** Year the Indian National Committee for Space Research (INCOSPAR) was set up under the Department of Atomic Energy.
- **C — 1972:** Year the Government of India established the Space Commission and the Department of Space.
- **D — 1975:** Year India launched its first satellite, Aryabhata.
- **Exam Extra:** ISRO is headquartered in Bengaluru, Karnataka, and operates under the Department of Space formed in 1972.

### Q716 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

Where is the Satish Dhawan Space Centre (SDSC), ISRO's primary orbital launchport, located?

A. Thumba, Kerala
B. Mahendragiri, Tamil Nadu
C. Chandipur, Odisha
D. Sriharikota, Andhra Pradesh

**Answer:** D. Sriharikota, Andhra Pradesh
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sriharikota, Andhra Pradesh (Correct):** The Satish Dhawan Space Centre (SDSC-SHAR), ISRO's primary orbital rocket launchport, is located on the barrier island of Sriharikota in Andhra Pradesh.
- **A — Thumba, Kerala:** Location of the Thumba Equatorial Rocket Launching Station (TERLS), used primarily for sounding rockets.
- **B — Mahendragiri, Tamil Nadu:** Location of the ISRO Propulsion Complex (IPRC) for testing liquid and cryogenic rocket engines.
- **C — Chandipur, Odisha:** Location of DRDO's Integrated Test Range (ITR) used for testing strategic and tactical missiles.
- **Exam Extra:** Sriharikota's east-coast proximity to the equator maximizes Earth's rotational boost for eastward launches and ensures safe over-water trajectories over the Bay of Bengal.

### Q717 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

Which joint India-Russia missile is a supersonic cruise missile capable of launch from land, sea, and air?

A. BrahMos
B. Nirbhay
C. Shaurya
D. Pralay

**Answer:** A. BrahMos
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — BrahMos (Correct):** BrahMos is a joint India-Russia supersonic cruise missile capable of being launched from land, ships, submarines, and aircraft.
- **B — Nirbhay:** Indigenous long-range subsonic cruise missile developed by DRDO.
- **C — Shaurya:** Land-based surface-to-surface hypersonic tactical missile derived from the K-15 Sagarika.
- **D — Pralay:** Indigenous short-range surface-to-surface quasi-ballistic missile developed by DRDO.
- **Exam Extra:** The name BrahMos combines the names of two rivers—India's Brahmaputra and Russia's Moskva—and it flies at Mach 2.8 to 3.0.

### Q718 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

What is the name of India's indigenous multi-barrel rocket launcher (MBRL) system developed by DRDO?

A. Pinaka
B. Arjun
C. Tejas
D. Dhanush

**Answer:** A. Pinaka
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Pinaka (Correct):** Pinaka is an indigenous multi-barrel rocket launcher (MBRL) artillery system designed and developed by DRDO for the Indian Army.
- **B — Arjun:** Indigenous third-generation main battle tank developed by DRDO's Combat Vehicles Research and Development Establishment.
- **C — Tejas:** Indigenous supersonic Light Combat Aircraft (LCA) developed by ADA and manufactured by HAL.
- **D — Dhanush:** Indigenous 155 mm / 45-calibre towed howitzer artillery gun.
- **Exam Extra:** Named after Lord Shiva's bow, the Pinaka Mk-I launcher can fire a salvo of 12 rockets in 44 seconds.

### Q719 · ISRO Missions & DRDO Defence Systems

What is the name of the humanoid robot developed by ISRO for uncrewed test flights of the Gaganyaan mission?

A. Vyommitra
B. Indrajaal
C. Daksha
D. NETRA

**Answer:** A. Vyommitra
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Vyommitra (Correct):** Vyommitra is the female-looking half-humanoid robot developed by ISRO to fly on uncrewed Gaganyaan test missions prior to human spaceflight.
- **B — Indrajaal:** Autonomous wide-area counter-drone defence system developed in India by Grene Robotics.
- **C — Daksha:** Remotely operated tracked robot developed by DRDO to detect and neutralize improvised explosive devices (IEDs).
- **D — NETRA:** ISRO's Network for Space Object Tracking and Analysis (also used for DRDO's airborne early warning radar aircraft).
- **Exam Extra:** 'Vyommitra' translates from Sanskrit as 'friend in space' and is built to monitor cabin parameters, operate control switches, and simulate life-support interactions.

### Q720 · Computer Generations, CPU, Memory & Hardware I/O

Which electronic component formed the core technology of First Generation computers?

A. Transistors
B. Vacuum tubes
C. Integrated circuits
D. Microprocessors

**Answer:** B. Vacuum tubes
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Vacuum tubes (Correct):** First Generation computers (1940s–1950s) relied on vacuum tubes for circuitry and magnetic drums for memory.
- **A — Transistors:** Core switching component of Second Generation computers (late 1950s–1960s).
- **C — Integrated circuits:** Core component of Third Generation computers (1960s–1970s), packing many transistors onto silicon chips.
- **D — Microprocessors:** Very Large Scale Integration (VLSI) chips that defined Fourth Generation computers (1970s onward).
- **Exam Extra:** ENIAC (1945) and UNIVAC I (1951) were landmark First Generation computers using tens of thousands of vacuum tubes.

### Q721 · Computer Generations, CPU, Memory & Hardware I/O

What does CPU stand for?

A. Central Processing Unit
B. Control Processing Unit
C. Central Programming Unit
D. Computer Processing Unit

**Answer:** A. Central Processing Unit
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Central Processing Unit (Correct):** CPU stands for Central Processing Unit, the primary hardware component that fetches, decodes, and executes program instructions.
- **B — Control Processing Unit:** Incorrect expansion; the CPU contains a Control Unit inside it, not 'Control Processing Unit'.
- **C — Central Programming Unit:** Incorrect expansion; programming refers to writing software, whereas the CPU executes instructions.
- **D — Computer Processing Unit:** Incorrect expansion; 'Central' emphasizes its role as the main processing hub of the system.
- **Exam Extra:** A CPU comprises three main parts: the Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU), the Control Unit (CU), and high-speed Registers.

### Q722 · Computer Generations, CPU, Memory & Hardware I/O

Which memory is volatile?

A. ROM
B. PROM
C. EEPROM
D. RAM

**Answer:** D. RAM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — RAM (Correct):** RAM (Random Access Memory) is volatile memory because it requires continuous electrical power to retain stored data.
- **A — ROM:** Read-Only Memory; non-volatile memory that permanently stores firmware such as boot instructions.
- **B — PROM:** Programmable Read-Only Memory; non-volatile memory that can be programmed once after manufacturing.
- **C — EEPROM:** Electrically Erasable Programmable Read-Only Memory; non-volatile memory used in firmware and microcontrollers.
- **Exam Extra:** RAM is divided into Static RAM (SRAM, used for fast CPU cache) and Dynamic RAM (DRAM, used for main system memory).

### Q723 · Computer Generations, CPU, Memory & Hardware I/O

Which high-speed memory sits between the CPU and main RAM to reduce average data-access time?

A. Virtual memory
B. Secondary memory
C. Flash memory
D. Cache memory

**Answer:** D. Cache memory
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Cache memory (Correct):** Cache memory is ultra-fast SRAM placed between the CPU and main RAM to store frequently accessed instructions and data.
- **A — Virtual memory:** Technique where the operating system uses secondary disk/SSD space via paging to extend apparent RAM.
- **B — Secondary memory:** Permanent non-volatile storage such as hard disk drives (HDDs) and solid-state drives (SSDs).
- **C — Flash memory:** Non-volatile solid-state storage commonly used in USB pen drives, memory cards, and SSDs.
- **Exam Extra:** Modern processors use multi-level caches (L1, L2, and L3), where L1 is the smallest and fastest directly inside each core.

### Q724 · Computer Generations, CPU, Memory & Hardware I/O
